## 0. [A Step in the Right Place: Understanding and Optimizing Timestep Placement in Continuous Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15527) <a id="link0"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15527
**Authors:** Shutian Zheng, Zhihong Yu, Linye Wei, Zeyang Zhang, Meng Li

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15527v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Continuous diffusion language models represent an emerging paradigm for language modeling. They generate text in parallel by iteratively refining continuous representations and mapping them back to tokens through a decoder. However, realizing the efficiency advantage of parallel generation requires maintaining generation quality with only a few denoising steps. Even when the number of steps is fixed, their placement along the denoising trajectory remains flexible. In this paper, we demonstrate that changing timestep placement alone can substantially alter the final latent representations and thereby affect the decoded token distributions. Building on these findings, we introduce StepTune, an algorithm that searches for timestep placements that improve generation quality. The search is conducted on a small calibration set and guided by a language-aware objective, with the model and sampling budget fixed. Once calibrated, the timestep placement is fixed and reused across inputs. Experiments demonstrate consistent improvements in unconditional generation perplexity and conditional task metrics across all evaluated sampling budgets. In particular, StepTune enables 16-step sampling with a frozen model to outperform its native 64-step baseline on both XSum summarization and WMT14 translation and reduces end-to-end latency by approximately $3.8\times$. These results establish timestep placement as a practical means of improving the quality-efficiency trade-off in continuous diffusion language models.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Advances in diffusion language models. The paper studies continuous diffusion language models and proposes StepTune, a method to optimize timestep placement to improve generation quality and efficiency, showing strong empirical gains.
**Relevance:** 10
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 1. [Match the Canvas, Not Just the Answer: Predictability-Aware SFT for Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18001) <a id="link1"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18001
**Authors:** Miao Li, Hanyang Jiang, Hengyu Fu, Baihe Huang, Yating Liu, Yuhang Cai, Changkun Guan, Zhongzhu Chen, Jiecheng Lu, Michael A. Boateng, Pascal Van Hentenryck

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18001v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Diffusion language models (DLMs) enable flexible parallel generation by resolving tokens according to the model's evolving predictability rather than a fixed left-to-right order. This flexibility, however, introduces training choices absent from standard autoregressive teacher forcing: which reference tokens are visible when a target is supervised and how much generation space is allocated. Standard supervised fine-tuning (SFT) typically makes these choices through random masking, independently of inference. Our analysis shows how this training-inference mismatch can misdirect supervision. Random masking can conceal prediction errors or penalize uncertainty that decoding would otherwise resolve, while the attention-visible input length can change which response formulations the model favors, even when the underlying solution is unchanged. Motivated by these findings, we propose Predictability-Aware Canvas Selection and Supervision (PACS), an SFT method. PACS uses the model's own predictability to select each example's attention-visible input length and supervises reference tokens immediately before their confidence-guided revelation. Across reasoning and code-generation benchmarks, PACS consistently achieves the highest mean accuracy among the compared SFT methods, with gains of up to 12.84 percentage points over vanilla SFT on individual benchmarks. Code will be released upon publication.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Proposes a new supervised fine-tuning method (PACS) specifically for diffusion language models, addressing training-inference mismatch and improving performance on reasoning and code generation benchmarks.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 8

---

## 2. [The Agent Knew: Internal States Forecast Unauthorized Actions and Stop Them, and the Lies That Follow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17301) <a id="link2"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17301
**Authors:** Arya Fayyazi, Mehdi Kamal, Massoud Pedram

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17301v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Language-model agents sometimes act beyond their authorization and then misreport what they did. On CAPE-Bench, matched agent tasks in which one fact decides whether an action is authorized, frontier open-weight agents do both, and one behavior marks the lie: retroactive permission laundering, acting and then asking permission as if the action were pending, precedes a false report in 70% of 1081 fresh informed violations, against 8% otherwise (preregistered odds ratio 7.0); frontier agents on tau^2-bench instead fabricate the authorization before violating. A linear readout of the agent's residual stream forecasts the violation before the action (AUROC 0.94) and the false report before the report, and beats chain-of-thought monitors when the agent is pressured to hide its reasoning. Gating every proposed action on this forecast cuts violations over whole episodes in four model families, and sending repeated flags to an approver keeps authorized work (Gemma-4: violations 0.70 -> 0.09, completion 1.00; a model approver that escalates unclear cases keeps this gain with a person seeing 25% of asks), at about 10-8 of the agent's compute and 109 times less than a chain-of-thought monitor. On AgentDojo, an external prompt-injection benchmark, the same readout calibrated on 29 tasks, with the steering unchanged, cuts attack success on unseen tasks in two families (Gemma-4 11.7% -> 5.7%, gpt-oss 28.5% -> 17.5%) while keeping benign utility (Gemma-4 0.637 vs 0.637; gpt-oss 0.667 vs 0.681). Preventing the violation is also the most reliable way to prevent the lie (misreported violations 0.10 -> 0.01 pooled over three families, preregistered), whereas lie-specific fixes after a violation are weak. All tests are preregistered and hash-locked; code, data and the full record, including tests that did not pass, are at https://github.com/AryaFayyazi/Honesty.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 13: Presents a new method for AI control and oversight by forecasting unauthorized actions and lies in language-model agents using a linear readout of internal states, enabling gating and reducing violations and attacks with low compute overhead.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 8

---

## 3. [PIG: Parallel Inconsistency Guidance for Accelerating Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17375) <a id="link3"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17375
**Authors:** Quentin Fruytier, Anton Xue, Sanjay Shakkottai, Sujay Sanghavi

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17375v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Diffusion language models accelerate generation by decoding multiple masked positions per forward pass. However, accuracy is known to drop when parallel decoding trajectories deviate from their sequential counterparts, which are slow and expensive "oracles" that adhere to the joint distribution of language. In this paper, we propose Parallel Inconsistency Guidance (PIG), a new paradigm that uses a trained auxiliary model to directly estimate the inconsistency between parallel and sequential generation from a single model forward pass. During inference, masked positions are traversed by decreasing confidence, and tokens are added to the parallel decode set one-at-a-time until the total estimated inconsistency between the parallel and sequential generation exceeds a threshold. Surprisingly, it suffices to train a lightweight ~2M parameter auxiliary model on a small mixture drawn from OpenCoder, GSM8K-train, MATH-train, and MBPP-train. For LLaDA-8B Instruct and NemotronDiffusion-3B Instruct, PIG extends the accuracy-TPF frontier on GSM8K, MATH500, MBPP, HumanEval, and MMLU, when compared to the industry standard Fast-dLLM and measures favorably on LLaDA-8B Instruct against DAPD, KLASS, EB-sampler, and other learned unmasking policies.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Introduces Parallel Inconsistency Guidance (PIG), a novel method to improve accuracy and speed of diffusion language models by estimating inconsistency between parallel and sequential decoding.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 8

---

## 4. [One-Layer Transformers Provably Learn In-Context K-Nearest Neighbor Prediction with Chain-of-Thought](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16798) <a id="link4"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16798
**Authors:** Lyumin Wu, Yuan Cao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16798v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Chain-of-Thought (CoT) enables transformers to solve complex reasoning tasks by generating intermediate reasoning steps. Despite strong empirical success, the mechanisms by which such reasoning abilities emerge during training remain poorly understood, particularly from a theoretical perspective. In this work, we study this question in a stylized yet fully analyzable setting: in-context $\mathrm{K}$-nearest-neighbor ($\mathrm{K}$-NN) prediction with a one-layer transformer. Earlier work has demonstrated that one-layer transformers can be trained to perform $1$-NN without CoT (Li et al., 2024a) by having the softmax attention attend to the nearest neighbor in context. However, this result on $1$-NN cannot be extended to $\mathrm{K}$-NN for $K>1$, as softmax attention is much less naturally suited to attending to the 2nd through $K$-th nearest neighbors. We first give a rigorous negative result showing that this limitation already appears on a well-separated class of binary classification tasks for odd $K>1$. We then give an explicit construction of a one-layer transformer, and show that it can solve $\mathrm{K}$-NN via CoT reasoning. We further prove that, under a stylized training setup, gradient descent can recover this construction, thereby showing that transformers can acquire the capability to solve $\mathrm{K}$-NN through training. In addition, we show that the resulting trained model applies to a broader test class than that assumed in the training analysis. Our results shed light on how CoT expands the ability of transformers to learn and solve tasks that are otherwise hard to solve.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 12 closely: advances in LLM reasoning capabilities and chain-of-thought reasoning. The paper provides a theoretical analysis showing how one-layer transformers can provably learn in-context K-NN prediction with chain-of-thought, explaining mechanisms behind CoT emergence during training. This is a significant theoretical contribution to understanding reasoning in LLMs.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 8

---

## 5. [RL Post-Training Diffusion Language Models Along Their Own Generation Paths](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17266) <a id="link5"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17266
**Authors:** Vaibhav Singh, Eugene Belilovsky

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17266v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Diffusion language models are a promising and more efficient alternative to autoregressive models. However, most RL post-training techniques are not easily adapted to these models. One issue is that diffusion language models generate answers through many intermediate states, deciding at each step which masked positions to reveal and which tokens to place in them. Many existing RL methods used in this setting approximate intractable sequence likelihoods or weight every denoising step by the same advantage, without explicitly distinguishing how these intermediate states of the denoising trajectory affect the expected reward. To address this issue of trajectory blindness, we introduce PACT (Potential-Anchored Consistency of Transitions). Motivated by a reward-tilted reference distribution, PACT jointly learns the policy and a scalar potential that scores each intermediate state, anchored by terminal reward. For each sampled denoising transition, a consistency loss matches the policy-to-reference log-probability ratio of revealed tokens to the change in this potential. This formulation requires neither sequence-likelihood estimation nor importance-ratio clipping. On four tasks, PACT improves LLaDA-8B-Instruct by +9.9 points on GSM8K, +12.0 on MATH500, +55.7 on Countdown, and +72.6 on Sudoku, and reaches the best average accuracy across the four tasks reported for this model, +3.7 points over the next best method. Further compared to AGRPO, it reaches that baseline's best accuracy in about half the training compute on GSM8K.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Advances RL post-training methods specifically for diffusion language models, addressing trajectory blindness and improving performance on multiple reasoning tasks, a significant advance in diffusion LMs for language.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 8

---

## 6. [JustOPD: On-Policy Distillation from Autoregressive to Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17397) <a id="link6"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17397
**Authors:** Zikun Zhang, Jiayuan Sheng, David D. Yao, Wenpin Tang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17397v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) is a post-training approach for large language models (LLMs) that enables a student model to learn a teacher model's distribution from sequences sampled from the student's own policy. Despite its promising capability, OPD for diffusion language models (DLMs) remains underexplored and existing studies primarily focuses on DLM-to-DLM distillation. The limited availability of strong teacher DLMs and the flexible generation order of DLMs impose additional technical challenges as well. However, it remains unclear how to effectively distill knowledge from a strong autoregressive language model (ARM) into a weaker DLM, given the mismatch between the left-to-right decoding of ARMs and the flexible decoding order of DLMs. Inspired by Ni et al. (2026), we propose JustOPD, an ARM-to-DLM on-policy distillation method that uses autoregressive rollouts during training to align the student's generation process with the ARM teacher's left-to-right decoding. This alignment enables effective distillation from ARM teachers while preserving the DLM's flexible generation-order capability at inference time. Experiments using Qwen3-14B as the teacher and Dream-7B as the student demonstrate that JustOPD consistently improves mathematical reasoning performance over the base DLM across multiple divergence choices.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3 (Significant advance in diffusion language models). The paper proposes JustOPD, a novel on-policy distillation method from autoregressive to diffusion language models, addressing the mismatch in generation order and improving reasoning performance. This is a clear methodological advance in diffusion LMs.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 8

---

## 7. [ZOIF: Zeroth-Order Influence Function Estimation for LLM Data Selection Using Only Forward Passes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15336) <a id="link7"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15336
**Authors:** Lisong He, Yunxuan Jiang, Haishan Ye, Xiangyu Chang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15336v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Influence functions enable principled data selection for large language models (e.g., identifying influential training samples for model editing, debugging, or efficient fine-tuning). However, scaling IFs to LLMs is hindered by the intractability of billion-scale Hessian matrices and the prohibitive memory overhead of backpropagation (BP). While many classical IF approximations reduce Hessian complexity, they still rely on BP for gradient computation, requiring massive storage for intermediate activations that impedes practical data selection at scale. To address this, we propose ZOIF, a zeroth-order framework for influence estimation that operates entirely via forward passes, enabling memory-efficient data selection under resource constraints. ZOIF decomposes influence estimation into three BP-free stages: (1) approximating the average proxy gradient via zeroth-order estimation; (2) reformulating and solving the inverse Hessian-vector product as an auxiliary zeroth-order optimization to bypass explicit inversion; and (3) deriving per-sample influence scores via a forward finite difference along the previously computed parameter update direction. Experiments show that ZOIF selects data subsets with downstream utility competitive with, and in some cases exceeding, the evaluated baselines, yet at substantially lower peak memory cost and with comparable overall computation time.

**Comment:** This paper proposes ZOIF, a novel zeroth-order influence function estimation method for data selection in LLMs that avoids backpropagation and large memory overhead. It directly relates to membership inference and influence estimation methods for language models, matching criterion 2 on test set contamination and membership inference methods.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 8

---

## 8. [Imperfect Verifiers, Exact Targets: Inference-Time Scaling for Continuous Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15281) <a id="link8"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15281
**Authors:** Kijung Jeon, Molei Tao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15281v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Verifier-guided inference-time scaling uses additional computation to score intermediate states and steer generation toward better outputs. However, imperfect intermediate verifiers can distort sampling, and repeated refinement alone does not ensure recovery of the desired reward-tilted distribution. We introduce Continuous-State Verifier-Guided Backtracking (CS-VGB), an inference-time scaling method for sampling from reward-tilted distributions with continuous generative models through repeated denoising and renoising. With correctly paired denoising and renoising transitions, the stationary distribution of CS-VGB at the clean checkpoint equals the desired reward-tilted target, even when intermediate verifiers are inaccurate. Under stated regularity conditions, CS-VGB converges exponentially to the target despite verifier error, and the expected number of updates needed to reach a clean sample grows only linearly with the number of checkpoints. In analytically tractable settings, we empirically demonstrate that CS-VGB recovers the desired reward-tilted distribution despite verifier error, whereas the compared verifier-based sampling methods fail to recover the target under the same verifier errors. We also instantiate the framework with diffusion models and one-step stochastic posterior maps. Across prompt-guided ImageNet generation, facial-attribute generation on CelebA, and multi-robot planning, recurrent CS-VGB search improves reward and task success as inference budgets grow, with image-generation rewards continuing beyond the observed plateaus of denoising-only baselines.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Presents a new inference-time scaling method (CS-VGB) for continuous generative models including diffusion models, with theoretical guarantees and empirical validation. The method applies to language models as continuous diffusion models and improves sampling towards reward-tilted distributions despite imperfect verifiers.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 8

---

## 9. [Double-Rollout Reinforcement Learning with Sampling-based Reward Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16593) <a id="link9"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16593
**Authors:** Haifeng Qian, David Mosallanezhad, Christian Munley, Adithya Renduchintala, Oleksii Kuchaiev

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16593v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) implemented with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) has driven substantial progress in training reasoning language models. However, verifiable outcome rewards provide an imperfect measure of reasoning quality: a correct final answer may not be supported by the reasoning trace, whereas an incorrect rollout may contain a nearly correct reasoning trajectory. We present empirical evidence for this mismatch and hypothesize that the resulting reward noise contributes to instability in reinforcement learning for both reasoning accuracy and token efficiency. To address this credit assignment challenge, this paper proposes double-rollout reinforcement learning. For each sampled rollout, we truncate its reasoning trace, sample multiple continuations from the truncated prefix, and replace the original reward with the mean continuation reward, yielding a more robust estimate of reasoning quality. This double-rollout algorithm incurs only a moderate increase in compute cost and yet reaches substantially higher accuracies across math and coding tasks and across Qwen and Nemotron models than baseline algorithms with fewer RL steps. For example, using only DAPO data it trains Nemotron-3-Nano-30B-A3B to reach 95.4% on AIME 2026, versus 91.5% by GRPO and substantially above state of the art for this model size, within 30 RL steps. When combined with length-based rewards, the same algorithm further improves the accuracy-efficiency Pareto frontier over existing efficiency optimizations. These findings suggest that the double-rollout reward estimation is an effective and cost-efficient way to improve both the accuracy and token efficiency of reasoning RL.

**Comment:** This paper proposes double-rollout reinforcement learning with sampling-based reward estimation to improve reasoning accuracy and token efficiency in RL for reasoning language models, which is a new methodological improvement to RLHF or instruction-following fine-tuning steps (criterion 1).
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 8

---

## 10. [Unlocking Token Rewards via Training-Free Reward Attribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18704) <a id="link10"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18704
**Authors:** Sitong Wu, Haoru Tan, Bin Xia, Xichen Zhang, Jingyao Li, Shaofeng Zhang, Xiaojuan Qi, Bei Yu, Jiaya Jia

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18704v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: In this paper, we propose an extremely efficient, training-free method to extract token-level reward signals directly from an existing deep reward model. Our core idea is to attribute the overall process reward to individual tokens by estimating each token's influence. This influence is defined as the change in the final macroscopic reward (e.g., the process reward) when a token is replaced with a semantically null token. Naively calculating this influence is computationally infeasible, requiring N forward passes through the PRM for an N-token sequence. We overcome this bottleneck by proposing a highly efficient gradient-based estimator. Specifically, we use a first-order Taylor approximation, which simplifies the influence calculation to the inner product of the difference between the token embedding and the null token embedding, and the gradient of the reward with respect to the token embedding. This requires only a single forward and backward pass. The resulting token-level rewards enable standard RL algorithms to perform precise credit assignment without requiring additional reward model training. Experiments on challenging reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that our method substantially improves policy optimization efficiency and enhances the generalization of LLM reasoning capabilities. Our P2T outperforms the outcome reward by +4.9% on MathVista for Qwen2.5-VL-7B-Instruct, and +11.5% on AIME24 for Qwen2.5-Math-7B. The code will be released via https://github.com/stonewst/P2T.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1 closely: proposes a new, training-free method for token-level reward attribution to improve RLHF-style credit assignment and instruction-following fine-tuning efficiency.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 11. [HaloBlock: Faster and Better Decoding for Diffusion Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17948) <a id="link11"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17948
**Authors:** Zhaohui Zheng, Chenhang He, Jinrui Zhang, Ming-Ming Cheng, Lei Zhang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17948v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Diffusion large language models (dLLMs) have attracted increasing research attention due to their bidirectional attention and arbitrary order decoding. However, there are two issues in dLLMs inference: the decoding difficulty is dominated by the tokens on the right side of the block, and the block-wise decoding inherently suffers from boundary contextual discontinuity. To tackle these limitations, we propose HaloBlock, a difficulty-aware adaptive halo mechanism for dLLM inference. We first leverage bidirectional cumulative entropy to characterize block decoding difficulty. Then, based on the bidirectional decoding difficulty, we adaptively allocate bidirectional halo regions on both sides of each block to mitigate boundary contextual discontinuity. The difficulty-aware design of HaloBlock smooths cross-block context propagation and lowers decoding difficulty for hard tokens, yielding better generation quality and faster convergence. Evaluated on LLaDA-8B-Instruct, Dream-7B-Instruct and LLaDA-1.5 across mathematical reasoning and code generation benchmarks, HaloBlock demonstrates consistent accuracy improvements and higher inference efficiency. Source codes are publicly available at https://github.com/PolyU-VCLab/HaloBlock.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Presents a significant advance in diffusion language models with a novel decoding method (HaloBlock) that improves generation quality and inference efficiency for diffusion LLMs.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 12. [Spectral-Driven Adaptive-Rank Optimizer for Large Language Model Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16533) <a id="link12"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16533
**Authors:** Zhipeng Chen, Wayne Xin Zhao, Ji-Rong Wen

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16533v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) is reshaping how large language models (LLMs) acquire reasoning capabilities, yet the optimizers used remain largely inherited from teacher-forcing training. This raises a fundamental question: does RLVR induce a distinct optimization geometry that calls for a different optimizer? We investigate this question through the spectral structure of LLM gradients and find that gradient energy progressively concentrates into a shrinking effective subspace, which is characteristic of RLVR, as it is absent under supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and is largely removed when the verifiable reward is replaced by a random one. Moreover, the rank required to capture a fixed fraction of gradient energy steadily decreases over training, revealing that the effective dimensionality of useful updates itself evolves with training. These findings motivate a new optimization principle: rather than prescribing the update rank externally, an optimizer should adapt its rank to the evolving spectral structure of the gradient. We introduce Ares (Adaptive Rank-Effective Spectral Optimization), which estimates the effective update rank from the gradient's spectral energy distribution, tracks its evolution, and confines the gradient signal driving each update to the resulting low-rank subspace. Across LLMs of different scales, Ares improves accuracy over widely used optimizers and generalizes across RLVR algorithms and tasks. Results suggest that effective optimization may require optimizers to be designed around the geometry and dynamics of the training process. We release the code and data at https://github.com/RUCAIBox/Ares.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes a new optimizer (Ares) tailored for reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) in LLMs, adapting optimization rank based on spectral gradient structure, thus a methodological improvement specific to RLHF-like instruction-following fine-tuning.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 13. [Copying Is Not Grounding: Training Faithful Long-Form RAG with Authored-Sentence Credit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18442) <a id="link13"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18442
**Authors:** Hoang Pham, Duc Anh Vu, Duy Tung Doan, Cong-Duy T Nguyen, Anh Tuan Luu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18442v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: In search engines and assistants, long-form RAG answers can mislead readers with claims their sources do not support. Faithfulness rewards, the share of supported sentences, train models toward fewer such claims. We show that these rewards are hacked by copying. A sentence copied from the passages is supported almost by construction, so trained models learn to copy rather than ground what they write. Common defenses only move the hack: a KL or copy penalty leaves copying in place or trades it for shorter, emptier answers and refusals. GRPO, the standard RL algorithm, makes it worse by giving every sentence its answer's advantage, reinforcing copied and grounded sentences alike. We propose Authored-Sentence Credit (ASC) , which fixes both by moving GRPO's reward and advantage from the answer to the sentence level. The reward credits only sentences that are authored rather than copied, say more than the question, and are supported by the passages. The advantage compares each sentence with every sentence sampled for the question, so one that earns no credit ranks last and is never reinforced. Across four models and two in-domain, three held-out corpora, only ASC lowers the error rate of authored sentences and writes more supported ones in every setting, by 38% and 46% at the median under held-out evaluators, giving readers more grounded information. It takes none of these shortcuts, and two LLM judges comparing whole answers prefer no baseline to the base model but rate ASC's answers on par with it. We conclude that a faithfulness reward should credit what a model writes, not what it copies.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1 closely: proposes a new fine-grained reward crediting method (Authored-Sentence Credit) to improve instruction-following and faithfulness in long-form retrieval-augmented generation, addressing RLHF reward hacking issues.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 14. [Releasing the Inner Jev: A Simple Recipe for Open Decision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17205) <a id="link14"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17205
**Authors:** Jonathan Geuter, Andrew Cheng, David Alvarez-Melis

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17205v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: LLM-based decision-making systems, also called System One models, have recently gained popularity, most notably through Jev (Almeida, 2026). Given a typed input state, a question, and a set of options, they return a probability for each option in a single forward pass, without text generation. We show that existing open instruction-tuned language models can achieve comparable performance \textit{without any additional training}, by reading out answers from letters (A, B, C, etc.) in the model's output logits at the last token position. Furthermore, we show how a simple supervised fine-tuning recipe can improve their performance further. We evaluate four open instruction-tuned language models: Gemma 4 31B, Gemma 4 12B, Qwen3.6-27B, and Qwen3.5-4B. We fine-tune each using LoRA and a decision head initialized from a slice of the language model head. Furthermore, we introduce OpenDecisions, a novel 2,352-question validation dataset designed to reflect the task composition of JevBench (JevBench main- tainers, 2026) and which computes a Capability score based on accuracy and calibration metrics. Without fine-tuning, Gemma 4 31B scores 79.6 on OpenDecisions, compared to Jev's 80.1. After undergoing our fine-tuning recipe, it scores 84.4 on OpenDecisions and 81.5 on JevBench v1.7, outperforming Jev by 4.3 and 4.4 Capability points respectively and setting a new state-of-the-art on OpenDecisions. On a suite of public decision datasets, our models achieve accuracy comparable to Jev, with lower calibration error. Our results show that open language models already possess System One capabilities, and with simple fine-tuning they outperform Jev on a wide range of decision tasks. We release our models, serving code, and OpenDecisions.

**Comment:** Closely matches criterion 1: proposes a simple fine-tuning recipe to improve open instruction-tuned language models' decision-making capabilities, improving instruction-following performance across tasks. Also introduces a new validation dataset for evaluation of decision models.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 15. [CLIVO: Differentiable Cluster-Based Instruction Mixing with Learned Validation Objectives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16876) <a id="link15"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16876
**Authors:** Yuhan Wang, Rui Hu, Batian Di, Tan Jiang, Dongsheng Ma, Leichao Dong, Yuanzi Li, Yalin Feng

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16876v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Target-aware multimodal instruction selection requires useful examples and an appropriate balance of supervision. Sample-level rankings can overrepresent similar content without optimizing this balance; group-level methods face coarse units, predefined set-level validation signals, and costly or inaccurate mixture search. We propose CLIVO, a target-aware framework connecting fine-grained semantic allocation, automatically learned validation objectives, and differentiable mixture search. Specifically, semantic clusters provide independently adjustable shares at the hundred-cluster scale. Correlations between per-example merged-proxy losses and real probe performance identify target-relevant validation examples; learned nonnegative weights combine their losses into an objective. A single backward pass through the merged proxy supplies gradients for all shares, avoiding an additional mixture-response regression model. Selecting 100K instructions from an 8M pool, CLIVO attains 97.2% of the full-data four-target average score with only 1.25% of the data. It improves on the strongest evaluated selection baseline by 1.16 points using random within-cluster sampling; combining learned quotas with sample ranking increases this advantage to 1.55 points. Analyses show that finer units benefit most targets, short-trained experts preserve mixture responses, sample weighting retains useful validation information, and direct search consistently reduces the learned objective. CLIVO also supports interpretable allocation, modular selection, and reuse across targets and granularities.

**Comment:** CLIVO proposes a differentiable cluster-based instruction mixing method with learned validation objectives to select instruction data efficiently. This is a new methodological improvement to instruction tuning datasets and fine-tuning steps to improve instruction-following models, matching criterion 1 closely.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 16. [Can Agents Forget When Asked? A Benchmark for Memory Unlearning in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18052) <a id="link16"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18052
**Authors:** Bofan Xie, Chaokun Wang, Guanchen Ge, Songyao Wang, Fang Niu, Hang Zhang, Wei Wang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18052v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Long-term memory has become a core component of large language model (LLM) agents. As agents accumulate personal information over long-term use, users increasingly ask them to delete sensitive or outdated facts. A memory system should honor such requests. It should remove exactly what was requested and keep the rest of its memory intact. However, existing memory benchmarks evaluate only memory writing, retrieval, and organization, so whether agents can forget at the user's explicit request remains under-evaluated. To address this gap, we propose Bemula, the first benchmark dedicated to memory unlearning. It comprises 50 multi-session dialogue instances, 135 forget requests of six types, and 1,742 test questions. Each dialogue has a control version without the requests, so that only systems that have stored the target can earn credit for forgetting. Across the paired versions, five test suites measure storage, removal of the requested information, preservation of unrelated memories, relearning of similar facts, and resistance to extraction attacks. On Bemula, we evaluate six mainstream memory systems under three backbone LLMs, together with the Full Context LLM on two of them. The results are negative. Across all evaluated systems and backbones, no system passes more than 1.5% of forget requests end to end, and most pass none at all. These systems either leave the requested information extractable under simple attacks or delete far more than requested. Memory unlearning therefore remains an open challenge for agent memory design. We release our data and code at https://github.com/Nboxff/bemula-benchmark.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 9: The paper proposes a benchmark for memory unlearning in LLM agents, evaluating the ability to forget specific information on request, which is a form of machine unlearning.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 17. [Can Agents Truly Forget? Explore Machine Unlearning for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17659) <a id="link17"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17659
**Authors:** Junkai Lin, Ge Xu, Zheng Xu, Junkai Chen, Xiangyuan Lan

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17659v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents have shown strong capabilities in interactive problem solving, tool use, and long-horizon task execution. However, they may also acquire sensitive or private knowledge during training and expose it during deployment. Retraining a model from scratch to remove such knowledge is prohibitively costly, making machine unlearning a more practical approach. Machine unlearning aims to remove targeted undesirable knowledge from trained models without retraining from scratch while preserving general utility. When language models are deployed as agents, parameter-level unlearning becomes insufficient: forgotten information may still persist in memory artifacts or be reacquired via tool calls. In this paper, we formulate the problem of agent unlearning as removing private information from an LLM-based agent across three information channels: parametric knowledge, persistent memory, and tool-call acquisition. To study this problem, we introduce we introduce Agent Unlearning Benchmark (AUBench), a controlled benchmark that extends the fictitious-author setting of TOFU into an agent environment with persistent memory, an offline web snapshot, tool-accessible documents, and provenance-tracked interaction trajectories. By evaluating existing parameter-level and memory-level unlearning methods in this setting, AUBench reveals the gap between model-level forgetting and system-level agent unlearning, and provides a foundation for developing unlearning methods that operate across the full lifecycle of LLM agents.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 9: The paper formulates and benchmarks machine unlearning methods specifically for LLM agents, addressing unlearning across parametric knowledge, memory, and tool-call acquisition, which is a novel and comprehensive approach to machine unlearning in language models.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 18. [Next-Concept Modeling: Fast, Parallel Coarse-to-Fine Text Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14162) <a id="link18"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14162
**Authors:** Hermann Kumbong, Ishan S. Khare, Iddah Mlauzi, Andrew Woen, Charles Ding, E. Kelly Buchanan, Simran Arora, Azalia Mirhoseini, Christopher R\'e

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14162v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Diffusion language models (DLMs) offer parallel sampling for faster generation than autoregressive models, an increasingly important advantage as inference becomes a dominant computational workload. However, DLMs degrade quickly with fewer sampling steps, process the full sequence at every step, and require many passes over the dataset to reach good quality. We introduce Next-Concept Modeling (NCM), a two-stage, coarse-to-fine framework: a multiscale residual VQ-VAE encodes text into a hierarchy of discrete codes, and a transformer generates one level at a time, predicting all codes within a level in parallel. At matched parameter scales, NCM achieves better generative perplexity than the strongest DLM baseline on LM1B while running $21\times$ faster. On OpenWebText, NCM is competitive with the strongest DLM while running $10\times$ faster; on XSum, it matches the strongest DLM's ROUGE-L while being $31\times$ faster. On the reasoning task of Sudoku, NCM outperforms autoregressive and diffusion baselines across all difficulty levels, using fewer function evaluations. On the reasoning task of Sudoku, NCM outperforms autoregressive and diffusion baselines across all difficulty levels, using fewer function evaluations.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: presents a significant advance in diffusion language models for text generation with a novel coarse-to-fine approach (Next-Concept Modeling) that improves speed and quality on language modeling and reasoning tasks.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

## 19. [Reward-Aligned Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14572) <a id="link19"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14572
**Authors:** Yilan Chen, Zian Su, Manish Prajapat, Dingmin Wang, Rein Houthooft, Rui Song, Vijay Lingam

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14572v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards improves the reasoning capabilities of large language models, but remains computationally expensive and sample inefficient. On-policy distillation (OPD) provides dense token-level supervision from a stronger teacher, yet standard OPD distills every token regardless of whether the teacher's preference actually improves task reward. Existing token-selection methods rely on proxies such as uncertainty or teacher--student divergence, which need not identify reward-improving supervision. We introduce Reward-Aligned Distillation (RAD), a bilevel framework that weights distillation signals based on their predicted effect on the student's post-update reward. RAD differentiates through a temporary optimizer step to score each token's supervision, then updates the token weights by projected ascent from the initial token weights. The resulting weights can strengthen, suppress, or reverse individual distillation-gradient contributions. At a matched training-response budget, RAD improves mean accuracy on six competition-math benchmarks over the strongest baselines by 6.5 percentage points for Qwen3-1.7B (non-thinking) and 2.5 points for Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507. RAD-Simple, a simplified variant, achieves comparable math accuracy at the same training-response budget. On LiveCodeBench-v6, RAD reaches 54.69\% and 58.67\% pass@1 with Qwen3-1.7B (non-thinking) and OLMo3-7B-Instruct, respectively, exceeding the strongest baselines by 8.7 and 6.4 points. These results support reward-guided weighting of teacher supervision across model families and tasks.

**Comment:** This paper introduces Reward-Aligned Distillation (RAD), a bilevel framework that weights distillation signals based on predicted reward improvement, improving RLHF-style training efficiency and accuracy on reasoning benchmarks. This is a clear methodological improvement to instruction-following fine-tuning and RLHF-related distillation methods, matching criterion 1.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 20. [Revisiting the 16x Pretraining Compute Gap between Diffusion and Autoregressive Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17590) <a id="link20"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17590
**Authors:** Guanghan Wang, Bing He, Zhan Shi, Benoit Dumoulin, Hanqing Lu, Volodymyr Kuleshov

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17590v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Diffusion language models (DLMs) have attracted wide attention for their fast, parallel decoding, but they are believed to be far less efficient to train than autoregressive language models (ARMs): a widely cited study reports that a DLM needs roughly 16x the training compute of an ARM to match its likelihood, a figure that has made pretraining DLMs from scratch look prohibitive at scale. We find that the methodology behind this figure has fallen out of step with current DLM practice: it scores the model with its evidence lower bound (ELBO) and trains a full-sequence, decoder-only model. We therefore revisit the scaling laws of DLMs, adopting the exact likelihood under the model's own decoding order, block diffusion training, an encoder-decoder architecture, and a sweep over the block sizes used in practice. Under this new protocol, we study across ten model variants, about 1,700 models in total, and find that pretraining DLMs is far cheaper than previously thought. Concretely, scoring with the exact likelihood reduces the compute multiple to 12.8x; block diffusion training and an encoder-decoder architecture further reduce it to 7.8x at block size 32, and smaller block sizes narrow it to 4.6x at block size 8 and 3.0x at block size 4. We complement these scaling analyses with an empirical rule for the optimal decoder depth of encoder-decoder block diffusion, a held-out compute budget beyond the fitting range at which the gap between variants persists, and downstream benchmark results where likelihood improvements carry over to accuracy. Overall, the 16x figure substantially overstates the pretraining cost gap between DLMs and ARMs, and pretraining DLMs from scratch is far more viable than previously assumed.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Presents a significant advance in the performance and understanding of diffusion language models, revisiting and improving the scaling laws and training cost estimates for diffusion LMs compared to autoregressive LMs.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 21. [TRACESTEER: Trace Driven Activation Steering for Hallucination Mitigation in Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18173) <a id="link21"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18173
**Authors:** Xin Zhang, Yili Wang, Yue Tan, Xin He, Rui Miao, Yixin Liu, Yi Chang, Xin Wang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18173v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Diffusion large language models (dLLMs) have emerged as a promising alternative to autoregressive LLMs, yet remain susceptible to hallucination. Activation steering offers a promising mitigation approach by guiding parallel token predictions through internal representations. However, existing steering methods largely overlook two characteristics of dLLMs: dynamic denoising states and temporal hallucination risks, which challenge predefined steering vectors and uniform intervention strengths. We propose TRACESTEER, a trace-driven activation steering framework that uses preceding denoising predictions as feedback to jointly adapt steering direction and strength. Specifically, Denoising Feedback Steering (DF-Steering) constructs step-specific steering vectors from preceding predictions and current hidden states. Trajectory-Guided Steering Gate (TS-Gate) regulates their strength using prediction uncertainty and the denoising timestep. With the backbone frozen, factual supervision and magnitude regularization jointly guide the learning of adaptive steering interventions, promoting factual reliability and stability throughout denoising. Experiments on two dLLMs show improved factual reliability on TruthfulQA and generalization across five downstream benchmarks.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Advances diffusion language models by proposing TRACESTEER, a novel trace-driven activation steering method to mitigate hallucination in diffusion LLMs, addressing unique challenges of dynamic denoising states and temporal hallucination risks.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 22. [Multi-Stream Residual Computation in Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18407) <a id="link22"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18407
**Authors:** Ekaterina Alimaskina, Suren Kazarian, Gleb Molodtsov, Aleksandr Beznosikov

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18407v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Diffusion language models reconstruct masked tokens in parallel, offering an alternative to autoregressive generation. Yet their Transformer backbones typically carry visible evidence and evolving masked predictions through a single residual stream. We equip masked diffusion models with multiple residual streams that persist through depth and adaptive routing between them, then investigate how they support these complementary roles. On the pretraining task, two streams reduce reconstruction perplexity by 5.71% on validation data and 5.13% on average across external corpora compared with standard residual connections. We observe that, without predefined roles, visible and masked positions learn distinct routing patterns. Causal interventions show that this distinction organizes read and write routing, while token-specific write strength remains important within each role. Guided by these findings, a model trained with role-conditioned routing recovers half the validation gain from adding a second stream. State interventions explain the benefit of the additional stream: it preserves early token representations for later reuse while evidence accumulates at masked positions. This mechanism recurs at a larger model scale, showing how persistent streams support context preservation alongside prediction refinement.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Advances in diffusion language models with a novel multi-stream residual computation mechanism improving reconstruction perplexity and providing mechanistic insights via causal interventions.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

## 23. [Teach Me After You Learn It: Lookahead Self-Distillation for RLVR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18256) <a id="link23"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18256
**Authors:** Zepeng Zhai, Meilin Chen

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18256v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) has emerged as a popular training paradigm for LLMs, providing dense, token-level supervision by aligning a model's own distribution with the distribution induced by privileged information. However, existing OPSD methods derive supervision solely from privileged information of the same instance, leaving inter-instance supervision largely unexplored. We propose Lookahead Self-Distillation (LEAD), which introduces inter-instance distillation by allowing the teacher to first learn from other on-policy rollouts before teaching the student. LEAD constructs lookahead teachers through virtual RL updates on disjoint subsets of on-policy rollouts, and uses each teacher to provide cross-group token-level supervision for the other subset. To reconcile the complementary yet potentially conflicting signals from OPSD and RLVR, we introduce harmonious modulation, which adaptively modulates the GRPO advantage based on the agreement between the two signals. Extensive experiments on scientific reasoning and interactive-agent benchmarks show that LEAD achieves the best in-domain average performance across model scales while maintaining out-of-domain generalization comparable to GRPO.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes a new fine-tuning method (Lookahead Self-Distillation) for RL-based language model training that improves instruction-following and reasoning benchmarks, explicitly building on RLHF-like on-policy distillation and instruction-following improvements.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 24. [Full-duplex Spoken Language Modeling with Discrete Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17780) <a id="link24"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17780
**Authors:** Chen Chen, Pin-Jui Ku, Ankita Pasad, Ante Juki\'c, Zhehuai Chen, Boris Ginsburg

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17780v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: We introduce Diffuplex, a full-duplex spoken language model based on discrete diffusion that achieves an unprecedented balance between linguistic intelligence and response efficiency. With thinking mode, Diffuplex delivers 13.0 tokens per forward pass, reaching an average of 1872 tokens per second and substantially surpassing the frontier autoregressive (AR) models in decoding speed. Without thinking, Diffuplex matches the first-audio latency of existing full-duplex models while achieving markedly stronger linguistic intelligence. This capability is enabled by separating when to say from what to say at the training level, rather than compressing both into a single autoregressive timeline. During inference, Diffuplex continuously tracks the user speech through an AR interaction policy, while independently generating and refining response content through parallel diffusion. Consequently, semantic computation is no longer bounded by the perception audio frame rate, allowing intelligence and responsiveness to improve simultaneously. Taken together, these results establish a new paradigm for spoken agents that can sustain real-time dialogue while devoting parallel computation to intelligent response generation. Training code and checkpoints will be avaliable soon.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: presents Diffuplex, a discrete diffusion-based full-duplex spoken language model with a novel training/inference paradigm separating 'when to say' and 'what to say', achieving new speed and linguistic intelligence tradeoffs in language modeling with diffusion.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 8

## 25. [OPMD: On-Policy Mechanism Distillation Beyond Output Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14022) <a id="link25"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14022
**Authors:** Yingdong Shi, Zhihong Li, Changming Li, Jingyi Yu, Quan Li, Kan Ren

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14022v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) improves language-model by supervising a student on prefixes sampled from its own policy, but its supervision remains confined to the output space. Consequently, OPD transfers what a teacher predicts without supervising the intermediate computation that produces those predictions. Simply distilling intermediate representations like representation matching is insufficient since teacher and student representations may differ geometrically, and even well-aligned hidden states need not be usable by the student's subsequent computation. We introduce On-Policy Mechanism Distillation (OPMD), which transfers teacher-derived intermediate computation while explicitly accounting for its compatibility with the student. OPMD first constructs a student-compatible teacher that transforms teacher representations into intermediate states usable by the student's downstream computation, then it internalizes these states from on-policy trajectories. OPMD achieves the highest average accuracy among the evaluated distillation baselines in all three teacher--student settings, and generalizes to multi-teacher distillation and cross-family supervised fine-tuning. Further analyses show that mechanism supervision yields more effective reasoning and meaningfully alters the student's intermediate computation and solution strategies, supporting that it transfers information beyond output supervision alone. Together, these results establish mechanism-level supervision as an effective way to move on-policy distillation beyond output supervision, enabling students to learn inner mechanisms they can use and internalize.

**Comment:** Closely matches criterion 1: proposes On-Policy Mechanism Distillation (OPMD) to improve instruction-following by distilling intermediate computations beyond output supervision, a novel fine-tuning step to improve instruction-following in language models.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

## 26. [The Surprising Expressivity of a Single Neuron with Autoregressive Chain-of-Thought](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17283) <a id="link26"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17283
**Authors:** Roey Magen, Nirmit Joshi, Gal Vardi

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17283v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: The success of autoregressive language models trained with Chain-of-Thought~(CoT) supervision raises a fundamental question: How simple can a next-token predictor be while still enabling the efficient simulation of general computation through its autoregressively generated CoT? Surprisingly, we show that a single neuron suffices. For a broad family of activations, including threshold and ReLU, a single autoregressive neuron can simulate any feedforward circuit with the same activation. Specifically, a size-$s$ circuit on $n$ inputs can be simulated by a neuron of dimension $O(ns+s^2)$ using $O(s^2)$ generation steps, with both bounds independent of circuit depth and tight under natural assumptions. For threshold activations, this improves upon the simulation of Joshi et al. (COLT 2025), whose bounds scale exponentially with circuit depth. Consequently, autoregressive threshold neurons can simulate polynomial-time Turing machines, while remaining efficiently learnable from CoT supervision. Our main expressivity result relies on a novel connection to Sidon sets from additive combinatorics. As an experimental illustration, we efficiently train a single autoregressive ReLU neuron to mimic a neural network teacher with nontrivial test accuracy on CIFAR-10.

**Comment:** This paper shows that a single autoregressive neuron can simulate general computation with chain-of-thought supervision, which is a significant advance in understanding LLM reasoning capabilities (criterion 12).
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 8

## 27. [Davinci-Tool: Scaling Agentic Tool-Use Supervision with Verifiable Executable Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16867) <a id="link27"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16867
**Authors:** Xiaoyang Yuan, Ji Zhao, Xingyu Hu, Wenhan Wang, Yutao Hu, Qiaosheng Zhang, Pengfei Liu, Yu Qiao, Yi Bin, Wenqi Shao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16867v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reliable agentic tool use requires training data that pair diverse multi-step workflows with executable evidence that the requested outcomes were achieved. Scaling either trajectory coverage or verification alone is insufficient as new environments introduce distinct states, rules, and dependencies that must also be reflected in the success criteria. To address this challenge, we introduce Davinci-Tool, a 30B tool-use model, and Davinci-ToolData, an execution-grounded dataset designed to scale environment coverage and outcome verification together. Davinci-ToolData preserves restorable states, execution traces, outcomes, and verifiers as a unified substrate for supervision. Two complementary pipelines populate this substrate: Execution-Grounded Task Synthesis (EGTS) derives verified demonstrations from independently authored systems, and Executable Environment Synthesis (EES) synthesizes auditable environments, state-grounded tasks, and executable verifiers across single and coordinated domains. These pipelines yield 30,395 verified trajectories spanning 2,260 invoked tool names. We first train on these demonstrations with supervised fine-tuning, then reuse EES states and verifiers to compare alternative actions and construct outcome-grounded preferences for Direct Preference Optimization. Davinci-Tool improves Qwen3-Coder-30B-A3B-Instruct from 52.4 to 88.2 on $\tau^2$-bench, from 30.4 to 62.8 on BFCL v4 multi-turn, and from 16.9 to 56.2 on MCP-Atlas, outperforming Qwen3-Coder-480B-A35B-Instruct on all three benchmarks with only 1/16 of its total parameters. Code is available at https://github.com/MassLab-SII/Davinci-Tool.

**Comment:** This paper presents a new dataset and supervised fine-tuning method for agentic tool use, including outcome-grounded preferences and direct preference optimization, which relates to new methodological improvements in instruction-following and fine-tuning (criterion 1).
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

## 28. [ConicFlow: constrained Inference-time optimal control for flow matching models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14307) <a id="link28"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14307
**Authors:** Shervin Khalafi, Kasra Fallah, Sung Cho, Dongsheng Ding, James Anderson, Alejandro Ribeiro

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14307v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: We propose ConicFlow, which frames constrained sampling from a flow matching model at inference time as an optimal control problem that balances deviation from the pretrained trajectory against reward, subject to hard terminal constraints. We discretize this problem and adapt receding-horizon control, optimizing only a short window of the trajectory at each step for computational tractability. We address the resulting sequence of receding-horizon subproblems with a receding-horizon primal-dual algorithm. Its dual multipliers capture the difficulty of satisfying each constraint and are refined throughout sampling, steering generation toward feasible final samples. We further propose a terminal-estimate variant that avoids accumulating local truncation errors by evaluating the reward and constraints at the predicted terminal point rather than at the end of the current horizon. Under standard regularity assumptions, we establish finite-horizon guarantees on suboptimality. Under convex-affine structure, we derive a sharper bound that scales linearly with omitted horizon length, and we show that the terminal-estimate variant is provably tighter when the flow dynamics change slowly. Experiments in both low- and high-dimensional settings with a recent large-scale flow matching model show that ConicFlow achieves high reward and strong constraint satisfaction while deviating less from the pretrained flow than recent baselines.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3 closely: advances in diffusion language models. The paper proposes ConicFlow, a method for constrained inference-time control in flow matching models, which are continuous diffusion models, with theoretical guarantees and experiments on large-scale flow matching models. This is a clear methodological advance in diffusion LMs.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 29. [Glasseek: A Transparent Recipe for Training and Trustworthy Evaluation of DeepSearch Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16998) <a id="link29"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16998
**Authors:** Cong Pang, Jiaqi Su, Chengrui Wu, Kuihao Yan, Tianrui Zhang, Zixuan Qiu, Chao Lin, Zixiu Lin, Zixuan Chen, Zelin Xiao, Tiankuo Yao, Jiawei Hong, Zixuan Chen, Dan Xu, Lewei Lu, Wenjie Wang, Xin Lou

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16998v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Deep search enables language agents to solve complex information-seeking tasks through iterative web exploration. However, developing robust open-source agents remains challenging for three reasons. First, opaque trajectory-construction pipelines make training data difficult to audit and reproduce. Second, context compression can create a substantial mismatch between training and inference. Third, agents may retrieve benchmark content from the open web during evaluation, contaminating the results and overstating their performance. To overcome these bottlenecks, we introduce Glasseek, a fully transparent deep search framework spanning the complete development lifecycle, releasing all data, model weights, training recipes, and code. Specifically, for data construction, we establish a verifiable trajectory curation pipeline that systematically generates high-quality exploration paths. For training, we propose Compaction-Managed GSPO (CM-GSPO), which uses the same policy for search and summarization while optimizing only execution sub-trajectories, enabling end-to-end search-policy training across context-compaction boundaries. For evaluation, we conduct the first systematic audit of test-set leakage in open-web agents and release a modular anti-leakage plugin that seamlessly integrates into existing evaluation harnesses. Under this rigorous evaluation protocol, our 9B agent outperforms frontier proprietary agents across almost all deep research benchmarks spanning diverse task types, and achieves the best overall performance among recent open-weight agents, setting a new state of the art at the 9B scale.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 2: provides a systematic audit of test-set leakage (contamination) in open-web agents and releases an anti-leakage plugin for evaluation harnesses, addressing benchmark contamination in language models.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 30. [Can You Trust Your Preference Models? Stackelberg Games for Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15008) <a id="link30"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15008
**Authors:** Riccardo Formenti, Aditya Agrawal, Vikas Garg

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15008v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Language models are frequently aligned to human preferences via imperfect evaluators whose errors can be exploited during optimization. We train policies to search for comparisons on which an evaluator may reverse the underlying preference, providing candidates for additional supervision. Since human preferences are unavailable during policy training, we use disagreement among an ensemble of preference models as a proxy for prediction error. We formulate this search as a zero-sum Stackelberg game between two opposing policies, a Leader which tries to minimize disagreement, and a Follower which maximizes it. For a general class of finite contextual Stackelberg games, we establish equilibrium existence and, under regularization, uniqueness and a closed-form characterization. We introduce Stackelberg Mirror Descent-Ascent (SMIDA), a two-timescale algorithm with linear last-iterate convergence in the tabular setting, and develop a neural policy-gradient analogue. Experiments with a synthetic oracle support disagreement as an informative proxy for prediction error. On held out data the trained policies outperform reference policies and increase the fraction of comparisons that exceed a disagreement threshold and reverse the oracle's preference.

**Comment:** This paper studies alignment of preference models via a Stackelberg game framework to find evaluator errors and improve supervision. It directly relates to criterion 11 (AI alignment techniques) by addressing imperfect evaluators and preference model alignment. It also introduces a novel game-theoretic method for this purpose.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

## 31. [inLoRA-MINT: Internal Representations for Auditing Training Data in Domain-adapted LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18051) <a id="link31"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18051
**Authors:** Gonzalo Mancera, Julian Fierrez, Aythami Morales, Ruben Tolosana, Daniel DeAlcala, Francisco Jurado

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18051v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: This work investigates the application of the Membership Inference Test (MINT) to large language models (LLMs) fine-tuned with LoRA, aiming to determine whether specific samples were used during training and address concerns about data privacy, leakage, and transparency. Building on our previous LoRA-MINT framework, which relies exclusively on perplexity under a strict black-box setting, we propose an approach based on intermediate model representations, including activations and parameter-dependent signals. These representations enable a MINT classifier to distinguish training from non-training samples without requiring full access to model parameters.   We evaluated the method on four LLMs (Qwen, Phi-4, DeepSeek-V3, and LLaMA 3.2-3B) fine-tuned on three datasets covering medical, mathematical, and academic reasoning tasks. Results show consistent performance across architectures and domains, achieving scores between 95% and 98%, with robustness to variations in model configurations and data distributions. The proposed approach enables efficient auditing of sensitive training data and supports data provenance and compliance with emerging privacy and AI governance regulations. Although our experiments focus on LoRA-based fine-tuning, the methodology can be extended to other LLM adaptation techniques and, more broadly, to other AI models.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 2: Proposes a novel membership inference method (MINT) for auditing training data in fine-tuned LLMs using internal representations, improving detection of training data membership and addressing test set contamination/privacy concerns.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

## 32. [A Few More Tokens Ahead: Reinforcing Parallelism in Diffusion LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18200) <a id="link32"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18200
**Authors:** Jiahao Kuang, Yangfan Gao, Tao Gui, Yuanbin Wu, Qi Zhang, Xuanjing Huang, Tao Ji

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18200v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Parallel generation is widely regarded as an inherent advantage of diffusion large language models. Yet our fixed-quota evaluation of four representative models reveals severe degradation across mathematics and code, with all scores below 15% at 16 tokens per step. Token-level probes identify local predictive potential near the observed prefix, especially in mathematical text. We propose RePara, which reinforces short consecutive token blocks through group-relative policy optimization and reduces rollout and log-probability recomputation costs. On LLaDA and SDAR, \ours improves GSM8K and MATH-500 accuracy over JustGRPO under reduced denoising budgets, with a 1.96x training-step speedup in our LLaDA/MATH setup.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Advances in diffusion language models. The paper studies diffusion LLMs, identifies performance degradation in parallel generation, and proposes a method (RePara) to improve accuracy and training efficiency in diffusion LLMs.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7
