## 33. [The Optimal Decoder is Tilted Temperature](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15058) <a id="link33"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15058
**Authors:** Nikolaos Papagiannis, Ananth Grama, Wojciech Szpankowski

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15058v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Larger sample budgets benefit from higher sampling temperatures in LLMs, but what temperature tuning optimizes and why it works remain poorly understood. We seek to answer these questions: we pose decoding as pass@$k$ maximization subject to two constraints that encode critical decoding failure modes: an entropy floor preventing diversity collapse, and a cross-entropy ceiling preventing implausibility. We prove that this maximization takes the form of a single closed-form conditional sampling rule, the \emph{tilted temperature} $q^\star \propto \bigl(p e^{\eta V}\bigr)^{1/T}$. Here, the tilt comes from a verifier potential $V$ that quantifies how likely a partial generation is to eventually pass. Ordinary temperature sampling, verifier-greedy decoding, and reward-tilted policies represent special cases. We further show that, under a natural rule for scaling the diversity budget with sample size $k$, the resulting optimal temperature increases with $k$, turning empirical choice into a theorem. The optimum still needs the unknown verifier potential, which we estimate using two design approaches. In the online setting, our J-EOS algorithm jointly tracks the two constraint multipliers and learns the verifier potential from binary pass/ fail feedback alone. In the offline setting, we show that when the model is calibrated, its log-probability tracks correctness up to an affine map. Under this condition, the tilt collapses into the exponent and the optimum reduces to ordinary temperature sampling. Calibrated Temperature Decoding (CTD) then fits one scalar offline and samples with zero additional inference-time overhead relative to temperature sampling. Building on these decision rules, we design a practical decoding algorithm for code generation and evaluate it alongside CTD on benchmarks including HumanEval and MBPP, showing improvements over standard and adaptive decoding baselines.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4: Proposes a new theoretical framework for decoding in LLMs optimizing pass@k with constraints, introducing the 'tilted temperature' sampling rule. This fundamentally rethinks decoding quality and sampling temperature, with provable optimality and practical algorithms, thus a new paradigm in evaluating and improving open-ended generation quality.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 8

---

## 34. [Growing and Transferring Pathways for LLM Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16489) <a id="link34"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16489
**Authors:** Haolin Tian, Yikang Li, Tonghan Wang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16489v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) can substantially improve the reasoning capabilities of large language models, but an effective training run requires considerable resources. Reusing the computation acquired through RL across models would extend the value of that training. We propose GrowRL, an RL method that organizes learned updates into compact computational pathways through structural growth. GrowRL freezes the pretrained backbone and adds an independent causal attention branch and a feed-forward network branch at each Transformer layer. Zero-initialized output projections preserve the pretrained policy at initialization, and RL updates only the added pathways. The learned pathways remain explicit after training, allowing transfer to structurally compatible target models and subsequent adaptation with the target backbone frozen. The architecture also supports sequential transfer of additional pathways. By keeping the learned attention and feed-forward computation separate from the pretrained weights, GrowRL provides an explicit structure for preserving and reusing the outcome of an RL run. Experiments across five backbones show competitive mathematical reasoning and out-of-distribution performance with a small number of trainable parameters. Learned pathways provide immediate gains across all three transfer directions, with performance after only 10 target-side updates already close to that after 100 updates in every case. These results support computational pathways as parameter-efficient and reusable units of reinforcement learning adaptation.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1 closely: proposes GrowRL, a new RL method for LLMs that organizes learned updates into compact computational pathways, freezing pretrained backbones and adding trainable branches, enabling parameter-efficient RL fine-tuning and transfer. This is a novel structural approach to RLHF or instruction-following fine-tuning improvements for LLMs.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 35. [Decoupling Knowledge Forgetting and Refusal Learning in MLLM Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14605) <a id="link35"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14605
**Authors:** Haodong Chen, Shengtao Wen, Dong Liang, Lingbing Guo, Xiang Chen

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14605v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Current evaluations of multimodal unlearning typically focus on whether a model stops outputting target information. This surface-level criterion, however, masks two critical failure modes. First, knowledge suppression techniques often induce confabulation, leading the model to hallucinate facts about the unlearned subject. Second, refusal training merely alters observable behavior without erasing the underlying knowledge; such models might decline open queries but still identify correct answers under multiple-choice probing. To overcome these limitations, we propose Forget-Aware Self-Distillation (FASD). This method is designed to achieve honest ignorance, a state where the model genuinely loses the target knowledge and learns to acknowledge its absence. FASD separates these goals into two complementary channels. The behavior channel distills appropriate refusals from an ignorance-prompted copy of the model itself. Crucially, target tokens are masked from the teacher distribution to avoid accidentally reteaching the forgotten concepts. Simultaneously, the knowledge channel directly suppresses ground-truth answer spans independently of the model's generated responses. Experiments demonstrate that FASD successfully unlearns target knowledge, prevents information leakage in open-ended tasks, and preserves performance on retained data. The resulting model not only forgets the specified information but accurately recognizes the boundaries of its own knowledge. Code and data will be released upon publication.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 9 closely: proposes a new method (Forget-Aware Self-Distillation) for multimodal unlearning that separates knowledge forgetting from refusal learning, aiming for honest ignorance in models. This is a significant advance in machine unlearning techniques.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 36. [CUSE: Fine-Grained Multimodal Unlearning with Counterfactual Guidance and On-Policy Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14763) <a id="link36"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14763
**Authors:** Chenlu Ding, Jiancan Wu, Yanchen Luo, Tianyu Zhang, Yancheng Yuan, Xiang Wang, Xiangnan He

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14763v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can reproduce sensitive, copyrighted, or unsafe information from their training data. Effective unlearning must remove target information while preserving non-target content, even within the same response. Existing methods typically apply uniform forgetting objectives to complete responses and rely on fixed, off-policy supervision, resulting in coarse token control and misalignment with the model's current behavior. We propose CUSE, a Counterfactual-guided Unlearning framework via on-policy Self-distillation for multimodal large language models. It first constructs counterfactual inputs by removing target-related evidence and uses the resulting changes in token probabilities to identify which tokens require stronger forgetting. It then distills token-level forgetting and retention targets on responses sampled from the current policy, with a frozen copy of the original model serving as the teacher model augmented with the corresponding forgetting- and retention-oriented privileged contexts. This design jointly determines what to forget and what to preserve. On MLLMU-Bench, CUSE reduces forget-set ROUGE-L by 33.6% while achieving 1.2% higher retain-set ROUGE-L than the strongest baseline.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 9: Presents new methods for machine unlearning techniques specifically for multimodal large language models, with a novel counterfactual-guided on-policy self-distillation approach to selectively forget target information while preserving other content.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 37. [A Production-Grade, High-Efficiency Agentic RL Framework for Frontier LLMs with Validated Recipes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16352) <a id="link37"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16352
**Authors:** LoonSage Team, Bowei Song, Caiwei Zhao, Chuwen Liu, Dechuang Gu, Dong Fang, Feiyu Xie, Huaiyu Li, Hui Zhang, Jian Tian, Jian Wang, Jie Zhao, Liguang Li, Maoyun Wang, Meiyan Yang, Minghan Zhu, Pai Liu, Pandeng Yao, Renbao Tian, Ru Yang, Shuai Jia, Shuai Tan, Shuailong Li, Shuliang Li, Teng Jin, Tianze Yang, Wenbo Cui, Wenjing Bai, Wenkang Wu, Xiaowei Jiang, Xiaoyi Dong, Yanpeng Wang, Yanwen Wang, Yaohui Ma, Yawen Wang, Yihang Zhang, Yingjie Yan, Zhanbiao Lian, Zhenyu Lu, Zhida Hu, Zhihui Hao, Zhihui Hu, Zhiwen Xiao, Zhiyi Gao, Zhongwei Qi, Zhuangzhuang Wu, Ziqian Liu, Ziyue Zheng

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16352v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) has emerged as a critical phase in the post-training pipeline of large language models (LLMs). As the frontier of LLM capabilities shifts from static reasoning to agentic tasks--characterized by external tool invocation, multi-turn interactions, and long-horizon decision-making--existing mainstream RL frameworks have grown increasingly unwieldy, raising the overhead of adoption, usage, and debugging. To address these challenges, we present LoongSage, an agent-native, fully asynchronous RL framework with a lightweight core of roughly 14K lines of code. Built upon Megatron for distributed training and SGLang for high-throughput inference, LoongSage centralizes orchestration within a single controller process and encapsulates all GPU-resident components as Ray actors. It introduces AgentFlow, a unified execution paradigm that orchestrates both white-box and black-box agents. For asynchronous execution, it integrates sliding-window strategies that bound trajectory staleness, and employs TransferMesh, a topology-aware synchronization mechanism that routes weights after each step over the fastest transport available to each receiver. Beyond agentic RL, LoongSage incorporates a memory-efficient Teacher Manager that decouples the scaling of teacher models from GPU memory constraints, natively supporting Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation. Extensive evaluations across four complete training runs, spanning complex reinforcement learning and multi-teacher distillation, demonstrate that task rewards improve steadily while the train-inference gap is kept under continuous control, and the fully asynchronous pipeline removes synchronous idle bubbles, sustaining hardware utilization.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Presents a new reinforcement learning framework (LoongSage) designed for agentic RL with asynchronous execution, multi-teacher distillation, and improved training efficiency for large language models, directly relevant to RLHF and instruction-following fine-tuning improvements.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

## 38. [Decouple to Forget: Semantic Mediation for Sensitive Unlearning in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17654) <a id="link38"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17654
**Authors:** Jingyue Cong, Andy Song, Yang Yu, Tabinda Sarwar, Xinshu Li, Ziqi Xu, Xun Yi, Ke Wang, Bo Han, Estrid He

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17654v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Machine unlearning for large language models seeks to remove specified knowledge while preserving general capability, yet current methods often treat an entire response as the unit of forgetting. This coarse granularity causes unnecessary utility loss when the forget target is only a small set of sensitive facts embedded within otherwise benign explanations. In this paper, we introduce Decouple to Forget (D2F), a targeted unlearning method inspired by semantic mediation that separates sensitive factual content from non-sensitive meaning and surface expression. For each example to be forgotten, D2F extracts a fact-level "mediator" representation from the answer, identifies which parts are sensitive, and removes only those parts while leaving the rest of the structure intact. We then generate counterfactual answers grounded in this mediator that preserve non-sensitive content but exclude sensitive facts, employing preference-based contrastive optimization to reduce the likelihood of information leakage while maintaining helpfulness and fluency. Experiments across various benchmark datasets show that D2F achieves a better balance between forgetting and retention than strong baseline methods. Our code is available at https://github.com/JingyueCong/D2F.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 9 (Advances in machine unlearning techniques). The paper proposes Decouple to Forget (D2F), a fine-grained semantic mediation method for sensitive unlearning in LLMs, removing only sensitive facts while preserving other content, improving the balance between forgetting and retention.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

## 39. [Artificiality's First Exam: An LLM-Referenced C-test for Supraintelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14005) <a id="link39"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14005
**Authors:** Jose Hernandez-Orallo

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14005v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Under the accelerated pace of AI development, any benchmark whose difficulty is calibrated with humans or current AI systems becomes circular, or saturates soon. How can we then evaluate machines that may become more capable than the current machines and the people who write the tests? One answer to the question hinges on inverse problems, inductive inference questions for which difficulty depends on the complexity of the underlying mechanism, given the observations and the previous knowledge. Levin's Kt, a computable variant of Kolmogorov complexity, puts a theoretical upper bound to that difficulty, balancing the simplest representation encoding for the mechanism and the logarithm of the time steps to run that mechanism. Previous tests derived from Levin's Kt, such as the original C-test, are based on minimalistic Universal Turing Machines (UTM), capable of very limited representations and no previous knowledge. Reasoning large language models (LLM) are UTMs that capture a compendium of human culture as knowledge, using prompts as natural language programs. This paper presents the first test based on Levin's Kt with LLMs as representational machines. Fresh test items can be produced at high difficulties, estimated in a ratio scale measured in bits, before any human or frontier model can solve them. We formalise the measure, devise a lightweight generation and certification pipeline, explore the effect of the power of the reference machine and the gap between examiner and examinee. We produce a first battery of certified items administered to twelve LLMs. The resulting agent characteristic curves decrease with difficulty, down to the uncharted high-difficulty region, the supra-intelligence window of items we can certify, yet beyond generations of frontier intelligence.

**Comment:** This paper proposes a new benchmark based on Levin's Kt complexity for evaluating LLMs on supraintelligence-level tasks, addressing fundamental evaluation challenges for future AI systems. It matches criterion 4 on new paradigms for evaluating open-ended text generation by fundamentally rethinking evaluation difficulty and calibration.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 8

---

## 40. [Learning to Detect Shifts in Unstructured Data Streams via Monitoring-by-Betting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17399) <a id="link40"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17399
**Authors:** Siyu Zhou, Jialin Ouyang, Fan Xia, Adarsh Subbaswamy, Drew Prinster, Suchi Saria, Augusto Garcia-Agundez, Sarah Pollet, Jinoos Yazdany, Patrick Vossler, Jean Feng

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17399v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Continuous monitoring of generative AI systems requires detecting distribution shifts in the unstructured data they ingest and generate. Existing monitoring methods typically control false alarms by requiring the discrepancy measure used to detect shifts to be *pre-specified*. However, for unstructured data, pre-specification creates a sensitivity--breadth trade-off: a narrowly defined measure is sensitive to the anticipated shift but may miss unexpected changes, whereas a broadly defined measure may detect a wider range of shifts but with reduced sensitivity. Although adaptively hypothesizing the shift direction can overcome this limitation, such data-dependent adaptation generally invalidates conventional false-alarm guarantees. We reconcile adaptivity with statistical validity through testing-by-betting, reframing sequential changepoint detection as a betting game in which the bettor continually updates its hypothesized shift direction, or witness function, as new observations arrive. We introduce *asymptotic e-detectors*, which approximate the pre-change distribution using a calibration set of size $m$, and prove that they asymptotically control the average run length to a false alarm as $m$ increases. Empirically, calibration sets as small as $m=30$ provide effective control. We then develop adaptive monitoring strategies ranging from black-box bettors that learn from embeddings to interpretable bettors based on LLM-proposed concepts. Across three real-world datasets, including data from a clinical AI scribe deployment, adaptive monitors detect shifts faster than fixed monitors at the same false-alarm level. Further, when the true shifts are sparse and interpretable, LLM-concept monitors provide the fastest and most interpretable detection, while also guiding root-cause diagnosis for complex shifts.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 13 closely: The paper introduces a new adaptive monitoring method (testing-by-betting) for detecting distribution shifts in generative AI systems, with statistical guarantees controlling false alarms. This is a novel AI oversight/control method relevant to deployment monitoring of LLMs and generative models.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 8

---

## 41. [Prefilling-dLLM v2: Learned Chunk Routing for Long-Context Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17868) <a id="link41"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17868
**Authors:** Qi Han, Jing Xiong, Ziyuan Zhang, Ngai Wong

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17868v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Diffusion language models apply bidirectional attention over the whole context at every denoising step, so a longer context makes each step substantially more expensive. Sparsifying only at inference leaves the pretrained weights unchanged, so the model is never trained under the sparse pattern it runs with. We propose Prefilling-dLLM v2, which continues training a diffusion language model so that it routes with its native mask token. One mask is appended at the end of each chunk, and a learned role offset marks it as a routing slot. On every fourth layer, a query keeps a bidirectional local window and the top-K chunks scored from that mask; the other layers use a bidirectional sliding window. Starting from Dream-v0-Base-7B extended to 16K with YaRN, we compare Prefilling-dLLM v2 with bidirectional NSA, DSA, and SWA under the same layout, key budget, and dense initialization, using the same Fast-dLLM decoder. With the YaRN factor scaled to the evaluation length, Prefilling-dLLM v2 leads the other sparse methods on all three RULER tasks from 16K to 128K. Its mean accuracy at 128K is 7.4 points above NSA, the next sparse model, while DSA and SWA remain near zero. At the same length, decoding is 172x faster than with full dense attention.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Advances in diffusion language models. The paper proposes Prefilling-dLLM v2, a method to train diffusion language models with learned chunk routing to improve efficiency and performance on long contexts, showing significant empirical improvements over prior sparse methods.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 42. [WildTrace: Evaluating Training Data Detection in Realistic Large-Scale LLM Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16152) <a id="link42"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16152
**Authors:** Hengxiang Zhang, Junxian Liu, Hongxin Wei

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16152v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Although post-training significantly enhances model performance, the use of massive undisclosed data raises concerns about benchmark contamination and copyright infringement. This drives growing interest in training data detection for post-trained models. Existing evaluations for training data detection often leverage benchmarks constructed post hoc, which can introduce distribution shifts. Alternatively, controlled evaluations rely on small-scale training setups that diverge from realistic post-training settings. As a result, their reported performance may not faithfully reflect the effectiveness of detection methods in real-world scenarios. To this end, we introduce \textsc{\method}, a reliable benchmark designed to evaluate detection methods in realistic, large-scale post-training settings. Built on a multi-stage pipeline, \method\space encompasses stage-specific models throughout the post-training lifecycle, spanning SFT, DPO, and RL. To perform a reliable evaluation, we construct model-specific evaluation sets across four diverse tasks, featuring distribution-matched members and non-members with verifiable membership labels. Our results reveal that even the best-performing method achieves an average AUC of only 0.529 on our benchmark, which approaches random-guessing level (0.5). Furthermore, our analysis suggests that evaluation protocols can significantly affect measured detection performance. These findings highlight the significant potential for developing effective, practical, and robust detection methods under realistic scenarios.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 2: Presents a new benchmark and evaluation for training data detection in large-scale post-trained LLMs, revealing near-random detection performance and highlighting challenges in realistic contamination detection.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 43. [LoOPSD: On-Policy Self-Distillation with Looped Teachers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18162) <a id="link43"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18162
**Authors:** Wei Fan, Qingyue Yang, Chao-Hong Tan, Qian Chen, Wen Wang, Xiangang Li, Kejiang Chen, Weiming Zhang, Nenghai Yu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18162v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: On-policy self-distillation improves a model using guidance from the model itself, but it still requires a teacher that provides more useful predictions than the student. Existing approaches obtain such guidance by giving the teacher additional context or instructions. We ask whether it can instead come from changing how the same model uses its own layers. To address this, we introduce LoOPSD, which searches over a model's layer execution order to construct a stronger teacher and distills its advantage into the student. Specifically, we keep the model weights unchanged while searching for layers to repeat, and use the resulting teacher's predictions on student-generated prefixes to guide learning. We then alternate search and distillation, rebuilding the teacher from the updated student in each round so that the student progressively absorbs the gains from alternative layer execution. Experiments on mathematical and logical reasoning tasks show improvements across models. On GSM8K with a 400-token thinking budget, LoOPSD raises Qwen3-1.7B accuracy from 42.30% to 79.61%; subsequent distillation rounds yield further gains beyond the first round. Together, these findings show that by searching layer execution to construct teachers, we can turn a model's additional computation into improved student capabilities, opening a new avenue for self-distillation to derive supervision from the model's own computation.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes a novel self-distillation method (LoOPSD) that improves instruction-following and reasoning capabilities by searching layer execution orders to create stronger teachers without changing weights.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 44. [Target-ESS Tempered Policy Optimization (TETPO): Smooth Variance Control via Bridge Importance Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14440) <a id="link44"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14440
**Authors:** Shayan Mohajer Hamidi, Kayhan Behdin, Wenhui Zhu, Aida Rahmattalabi, Jincheng Cao, Jelena Markovic-Voronov, Alborz Geramifard

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14440v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Asynchronous reinforcement-learning post-training of large language models is computationally efficient but inherently off-policy: the learner updates on rollouts generated by a stale policy, while correcting this mismatch requires importance weights that can be extremely heavy-tailed. A small number of trajectories may then dominate an update, collapsing the effective sample size (ESS) and increasing the variance and instability of the resulting gradient estimates. Existing stabilizers apply fixed interventions regardless of the realized severity of weight concentration, either discarding selected samples or reweighting them without adapting the correction strength. We introduce \emph{Target--ESS Tempered Policy Optimization} (TETPO), which replaces each importance weight $\rho$ by $\rho^\alpha$ for a data-adaptive exponent $\alpha\in[0,1]$: $\alpha=1$ preserves the original importance weights, whereas $\alpha=0$ assigns uniform coefficients. For each prompt group, TETPO selects the largest $\alpha$ whose tempered weights satisfy a target ESS fraction $\kappa$, applying little tempering to healthy groups and stronger tempering when weight degeneracy is severe. At the population level, the tempered coefficients are exact importance weights for a geometric bridge between the sampler and learner policies. The exponent is efficiently found by one-dimensional bisection, while the resulting weights satisfy deterministic bounds on normalized-weight dispersion and weighted-gradient norm that are independent of policy drift, together with a fixed-$\alpha$ bound on bridge-target discrepancy. Power tempering also introduces no explicit zero-weight mask. On mathematical-reasoning benchmarks under high staleness, TETPO outperforms strong on- and off-policy baselines.

**Comment:** This paper proposes Target-ESS Tempered Policy Optimization (TETPO), a new method for stabilizing off-policy RL updates in large language models, which is a new methodological improvement to RLHF or instruction-following fine-tuning steps (criterion 1).
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 45. [Batch-Paced: Fixed-Budget Batch Decoding in Discrete Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17917) <a id="link45"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17917
**Authors:** Shuai Zhang, Zhanchao Zhou, Huachuan Qiu, Anqi Li, Zhenzhong Lan

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17917v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: As generative language models become more capable and widely used, inference demand grows, making efficient serving increasingly important. Discrete diffusion language models (dLLMs) offer a promising path to more efficient inference through parallel token generation. Prior dLLM decoding methods, however, focus on improving inference efficiency for individual sequences. We formulate fixed-budget dLLM batch decoding and introduce Batch-Paced, a training-free method that jointly optimizes the allocation of decoding compute across requests to improve overall batch quality. Across two dLLM backbones, five benchmarks and their representative mixtures, Batch-Paced improves batch performance under matched budgets; gains generally grow with batch size and largely persist across decoding configurations. Analysis of requests' decoding trajectories suggests that these gains arise largely by raising the committed-token confidence of lower-confidence requests. We further show that Batch-Paced's batch decode time remains competitive and that adding a decoding-depth allowance during Batch-Paced decoding can shorten it further at a modest performance cost.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Presents a new method (Batch-Paced) for batch decoding in discrete diffusion language models, improving inference efficiency and batch quality. This is a significant advance in diffusion LMs applied to language.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

## 46. [CoCA: Criterion-Aware Credit Assignment for Rubric-Based Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17733) <a id="link46"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17733
**Authors:** Guangcheng Zhu, Yingfan Ma, Xing Zheng, Haobo Wang, Shenzhi Yang, Kai Tang, Zhengqing Zang, Bowen Song, Weiqiang Wang, Gang Chen

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17733v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Rubric-based reinforcement learning (RL) evaluates responses against explicit criteria to construct rewards, offering a viable way to train large language models on open-ended tasks. However, standard methods compress criterion-level feedback into scalar rewards and assign uniform token advantages, leaving structured rubric supervision underutilized. Recent work seeks finer-grained guidance by treating rubrics as privileged information to derive self-distillation targets, but the resulting learning signal is not grounded in criterion outcomes and can lead to reward degradation. This motivates us to reframe the problem as criterion-aware credit assignment, where privileged rubric information serves as a relevance cue for credit allocation rather than a source of imitation targets. Accordingly, we propose CoCA, which derives criterion-level credits from group-relative outcomes and allocates them across tokens based on criterion-induced distribution shifts, yielding both outcome-grounded and fine-grained learning signals. Empirically, CoCA achieves an average gain of +4.2% over standard rubric-based RL across medicine and science benchmarks, demonstrating more effective learning on open-ended tasks.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes a new methodological improvement for rubric-based reinforcement learning, a form of RLHF/instruction-following fine-tuning with criterion-aware credit assignment to improve instruction-following performance on open-ended tasks.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 47. [Scaling Laws for Multimodal Data Mixtures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14423) <a id="link47"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14423
**Authors:** Aditi Khandelwal, Ayush K. Tarun, Yixuan Xu, Imanol Schlag, Golnoosh Farnadi, Siva Reddy, Luke Zettlemoyer, Antoine Bosselut

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14423v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Frontier AI systems are increasingly natively multimodal, jointly pretrained on multiple modalities, such as text, vision, and audio. However, the scientific understanding of the multimodal data mixtures used to pretrain these systems --- how much of each modality to mix, and at what potential interference cost to the others --- is largely absent or gatekept. In this work, we derive data mixing scaling laws for three modalities: text, vision, and audio. Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) increasingly adopt Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures, as MoEs scale efficiently and partially limit cross-modal interference. We therefore use MoEs to conduct 268 experiments ranging from 457M to 8.3B parameters, on up to 150 billion tokens. Our work leads to multiple actionable insights into multimodal data mixing for training MLLMs. First, compute optima are modality-specific: audio modality is model-heavy, favoring scaling parameters over tokens, while text and vision require balanced scaling of model size and data. Second, repeating vision or audio data beyond 2x yields negligible benefits. Finally, cross-modal interactions are highly asymmetric: vision strongly interferes with audio, whereas audio's interference with learning text and vision is negligible. Overall, we provide a principled foundation for understanding the multimodal data mixtures needed to train frontier MLLMs.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 6: Studies scaling laws for multimodal data mixtures in large models, providing principled insights into data mixing and compute allocation across modalities.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 48. [Autoregressive Decoding as a Martingale: Characterizing the Stochastic Dynamics of LLM Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16041) <a id="link48"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16041
**Authors:** Xiahui Zhu, Zhenyi Qi, Wanwei Liu, Haoang Chi, Yang Tan, Xinyu Sun, Ji Wang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16041v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable success across a wide range of language tasks. At their core, autoregressive LLMs are conditional probability models that learn to approximate the next-token distribution given the preceding context. Despite this probabilistic nature, the stochastic dynamics of autoregressive decoding have rarely been investigated from a rigorous probabilistic perspective. In this work, we define the per-step prediction error as the difference between the sampled token indicator and the model's conditional predictive distribution, and prove that the cumulative error process is a martingale with respect to the decoding filtration, implying that its conditional first-order moment is identically zero. Beyond this first-order property, we derive a closed-form expression for the second-order behavior of the martingale sequence: its predictable quadratic variation reduces to $\sum_t\left(1-p(y_t\mid y_{<t})\right)$, computable from realized token probabilities alone. This identity establishes a direct theoretical connection between sequence fluctuations and predictive uncertainty during autoregressive decoding. White-box experiments on Llama-3.x and Qwen2.5 families (1B to 72B parameters) verify that the empirically measured second-order moments closely match the theoretical predictions derived from our martingale analysis. These results provide a probabilistic framework for analyzing autoregressive LLM decoding, reveal intrinsic probabilistic fingerprints of different language models, and establish a rigorous mathematical foundation for LLM-generated text detection and uncertainty quantification.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 2: Provides a rigorous probabilistic framework analyzing autoregressive decoding as a martingale, enabling theoretical understanding of sequence fluctuations and uncertainty quantification, which can underpin test set contamination detection and membership inference methods for LLMs.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 49. [Truncation Samplers Are Nested: Comparing Decoding Rules Is a Measurement Problem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15570) <a id="link49"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15570
**Authors:** Rasul Khanbayov, Hasan Kurban

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15570v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Settling one comparison between two truncation samplers has cost six thousand A100-hours, and nothing says in advance how far apart two samplers can be. We observe that almost every deployed truncation rule keeps a prefix of the probability-sorted vocabulary, so any two such rules are nested at every decoding step. Nesting yields a closed-form per-step distance and a coupling under which both samplers emit identical text until they first disagree. Accumulated over a generation, this gives a separation certificate that bounds the difference between two samplers on any bounded outcome, is computable from logs a comparison already produces, and equals their sequence-level total variation distance exactly when neither rule ever overtakes the other. Two consequences follow without measurement: exactness is fragile, and certifying a small difference requires the two retained masses to agree to very high precision at every step. On six open-weight models over four math and science benchmarks, the certificate for every hyperparameter grid we tested is at least 0.998, so a bound alone rarely settles a comparison, although two members of one family can be far closer. What remains useful is the coupling, which gives an unbiased paired estimator that cuts variance $1.2$-$3.8\times$ against prompt-level pairing. Read against benchmark sizes at the default pair's variance and our point estimate of its between-prompt component, resolving a one-point difference needs 8 to 32 samples per prompt on GSM8K, MATH-500 and GPQA-Diamond and about 210 on AIME 2025; at that component's upper confidence bound GPQA-Diamond needs more than 32 and AIME 2025 cannot be resolved at any sample count. Choosing a truncation sampler is a measurement problem at least as much as a design one. The code is available at https://github.com/KurbanIntelligenceLab/truncation-samplers.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4 closely: proposes a novel statistical measurement framework for comparing truncation samplers in language model decoding, providing exact per-step distance and coupling-based separation certificates, which fundamentally rethink evaluation of open-ended text generation.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 50. [Multimodal Co-Generation With Continuous Bitstream Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14349) <a id="link50"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14349
**Authors:** Georgios Batzolis, Georgios Kontos, Bruno Trentini, Pranam Chatterjee, Micha Livne, Mark Girolami, Luca Ambrogioni

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14349v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: We show that continuous bitstream diffusion (CoBit), originally developed for language modeling, provides a unified framework for joint and conditional multimodal generation using the same training and sampling protocol. Across image-text, speech-text and protein sequence-structure modeling, models are trained separately from scratch, but each uses a single input projection, transformer backbone, bitwise prediction head and binary score-matching objective shared across its modalities. Within each setting, a single checkpoint supports joint generation and the conditional directions included in training by changing which parts of the bitstream are observed. We first validate the approach on MNIST-Sum, a controlled image-text task pairing raw binary images with equations encoded as text, achieving near-perfect conditional accuracy and high joint consistency without learned tokenizers. We then apply the same protocol at larger scale to speech-text and protein sequence-structure modeling. Trained without text pretraining, a single speech-text model performs speech synthesis, recognition, continuation, and unconditional co-generation. Jointly generated speech and text achieve 3.5% word error rate between the generated text and automatic transcriptions of the generated speech. In protein modeling, a single CoBit checkpoint supports forward folding, inverse folding, and joint sequence-structure generation. For jointly generated proteins spanning 100-500 residues, computational refolding of CoBit's sequences yields structures that agree with the generated backbones at a mean self-consistency TM-score of 0.911. Together, these results show that CoBit provides a common generative framework for substantially different modalities and forms of cross-modal dependence, without introducing a domain-specific objective or sampling procedure.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3 closely: presents continuous bitstream diffusion (CoBit) for language modeling and multimodal generation, including text and speech, showing advances in diffusion language models with a unified framework and strong empirical results.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 51. [Geometric Calibration of Supervised Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18634) <a id="link51"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18634
**Authors:** Jianzhu Bao, Jia-Chen Gu, Qingyuan Liu, Haozhen Zhang, Kai-Wei Chang, Nanyun Peng, Wenya Wang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18634v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Recent studies investigate the weight geometry through its spectral structure, which characterizes how the weights transform different input directions. Within this view, supervised fine-tuning (SFT) is shown to induce dense updates that perturb the spectral geometry of pre-SFT weights along their principal directions, potentially disrupting the model's prior knowledge. These observations motivate using the geometry of pre-SFT weights as a reference for refining learned SFT updates. We study this perspective as Geometric Calibration and develop SPACE Subspace Projection for Adapted Checkpoint Enhancement), a lightweight post-hoc method that selectively reverts SFT update components within the principal subspace of the pre-SFT weights while preserving those in its orthogonal complement. SPACE is a data-free, training-free method that requires only the pre- and post-SFT checkpoints. We evaluate SPACE on Qwen2.5, Qwen3, Qwen3.5, Llama-3.1, and Olmo-3 models ranging from 2B to 14B, across mathematical, coding, and medical SFT, as well as several SFT variants. Overall, SPACE mitigates out-of-domain (OOD) capability regression while further improving in-domain (ID) performance over the uncalibrated SFT checkpoints. For math SFT, SPACE yields average ID/OOD gains of 2.85/2.26 points on Qwen2.5-7B, and 2.41/1.52 points on Qwen3-14B-Base, respectively. These results highlight how the geometric relationship between SFT updates and pre-SFT weights shapes the adapted model's capabilities and, more broadly, motivate geometry-aware parameter updates for continual model improvement.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: New methodological improvements to supervised fine-tuning (SFT) for instruction-following. The paper proposes a geometry-aware post-hoc method (SPACE) to calibrate SFT updates, improving in-domain and out-of-domain performance without additional training or data.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 52. [What Do Sparse Autoencoder Feature Discovery Scores Identify? From Activations to Interventions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15850) <a id="link52"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15850
**Authors:** Yifeng Guo, Yanran Li, Xiang Chen

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15850v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Sparse autoencoder (SAE) features are typically discovered from how their activations co-vary with behaviour, yet they are often used through interventions such as steering or ablation. We take intervention attribution, the first-order effect of a specified intervention on the model output, as our estimand. For a broad class of response functions, we show that observational feature-discovery evidence generally does not identify this attribution, and derive an exact gradient-based decomposition of the gap between linear-probe predictions and intervention attribution. Across four model-SAE pairs, median probe-to-attribution magnitude ratios range from tens to hundreds, while attributions closely track small-step steering. For single-latent ablations with nonzero effects, gradient predictions match the realized sign in 91-97% of cases, compared with 17-35% for probe-implied changes. Attribution-selected sets also produce larger absolute mean ablation effects on held-out prompts than sets selected by correlation, rank tests or probe coefficients. Together, these results establish intervention attribution as a principled, gradient-estimable target for selecting SAE features for intervention.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 7: sparse autoencoders in neural network interpretability. The paper analyzes sparse autoencoder feature discovery scores and their relationship to intervention attribution, providing a gradient-based decomposition and showing how to select features for interventions. This is a novel interpretability method related to sparse feature extraction.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 53. [The Sharp Directions Are Against You: Curvature Analysis of Activation Steering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16084) <a id="link53"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16084
**Authors:** Seyedarmin Azizi, Arya Fayyazi, Parsa Razmara, Erfan Baghaei Potraghloo, Souvik Kundu, Massoud Pedram

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16084v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Activation steering adds a vector to a language model's internal representations at inference time. It is a lightweight alternative to fine-tuning for behavioral control. The standard construction, contrastive activation addition (CAA), is fragile, as on many inputs it shifts behavior in the wrong direction. To explain this fragility, we first study the geometry of the steering vector. We introduce steering Hessian, the matrix of second order derivative of the model's behavioral loss with respect to the steering vector. It captures the output behavior response sensitivity of the model under small perturbations of the steering vector along each direction. Its top eigenvectors are sharp directions: tiny perturbations along them can cause large, erratic, input-dependent behavioral swings. Bottom eigenvectors are flat directions: behavior is robust along them. Removing the sharp component of a steering vector approximately preserves its direction and magnitude (cosine > 0.974, norm reduction < 3% for typical settings) while reducing the vector's directional curvature by up to 12x (median 4x across 36 conditions). We evaluate on three model families (Qwen2.5-7B, Llama-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B), with four behavioral tasks, and 36 token-level conditions. Steering along sharp directions reverses the intended behavior in every condition tested, with anti-steer rates of 53-100%. Sharp directions account for only 1-5% of the CAA vector's squared norm. However, they drive a disproportionate share of the gap between the steered and unsteered next-token distributions during autoregressive generation. We term this one-line correction as sharp removal; it outperforms standard CAA in open-ended generation across all 25 conditions tested. A jailbreaking experiment further shows that sharp directions act as noise rather than signal: they disrupt coherent behavioral control regardless of steering polarity.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 8: mechanistic interpretability techniques. The paper studies the geometry of activation steering vectors in language models, introduces the steering Hessian matrix, identifies sharp directions causing erratic behavior, and proposes a method to remove these sharp directions to improve behavioral control. This is a new mechanistic interpretability insight into how steering vectors affect model behavior internally.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 54. [Instructions Are Evidence, Not Objectives: Measuring Outcome Alignment of Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17757) <a id="link54"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17757
**Authors:** Kaiming Liu, Yuxuan Liu, Jinrui Ju, Minyuan Ruan, Xuanyu Lei, Peng Li, Yang Liu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17757v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language model agents can follow complex human instructions to solve real-world tasks. In practice, however, initial instructions are often incomplete. In our analysis of 100 user-agent trajectories from SWE-chat, 56\% of initial instructions omit requirements that later emerge as necessary, and 52\% of the useful follow-up requirements could have been inferred from the project. A user states requirements from their own understanding of the task, and these \textbf{stated requirements} often differ from the \textbf{true requirements} that completing the task imposes. An agent should therefore treat the instruction as evidence rather than as the objective, inferring the true requirements from the instruction and the environment and satisfying them even when the user is not yet aware of all of them. We call this ability \textbf{outcome alignment}. In this work, we construct \textbf{TiGanEval}, a benchmark of software engineering and research tasks. In every task the user genuinely lacks part of the requirements, each requirement stays discoverable from the code and runtime behaviour of the environment, and requirements the agent infers are separated from those the user adds after observing delivered work. These properties together make outcome alignment measurable. Evaluating eight frontier models including GPT-5.6-Sol, we find outcome alignment broadly missing: the models lose 53 to 67 points of success rate when moving from the full specification to the partial instruction, and consequence feedback across four submissions does not close the gap. Furthermore, a plan-mode harness cannot compensate for the missing ability of the model itself, which places outcome alignment among the abilities the next generation of models should be developed for.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Discusses a new conceptual approach to instruction-following by treating instructions as evidence rather than objectives, introducing the notion of outcome alignment and measuring it on a new benchmark TiGanEval.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 55. [RELAY: Selective Self-Distillation with Adaptive Hand-off for Rubric-Based Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17623) <a id="link55"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17623
**Authors:** Kunwang Zheng, Xinyu Sun, Yu Cao, Shuocheng Li, Lei Cheng, Ying Liu, Qilong Zhang, Jinlong Chen, Hua Yang, Qianglong Chen

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17623v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Rubric-based reinforcement learning grades a response against many written criteria but collapses them into one reward, which discards the detail the rubric carries and gives no GRPO reward gradient when a group's responses tie. A frozen teacher that reads the rubric as privileged context provides token-level supervision, but we observe an early plateau in the gains over GRPO. RELAY uses advantages and rewards to select positive-advantage and low-scoring zero-advantage rollouts, and monitors the distillation loss to decide when to retire the teacher. Stage 1 combines selective distillation from a frozen teacher with GRPO; a plateau detector then removes the teacher, and Stage 2 continues with GRPO alone. Calibrated on one model-domain pair and reused unchanged in three others, the plateau detector fires between steps 122 and 171. RELAY scores highest in-domain among the compared methods in all four settings, ahead of GRPO on RaR-Medicine by 4.62 points with Qwen3-1.7B and 6.02 with Qwen3-4B. On Qwen3-1.7B/Medicine, removing the teacher from a shared checkpoint outperforms keeping, weakening or refreshing it. Distilling selected rollouts covering about half the response tokens also outperforms distilling all rollouts at a matched average coefficient.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes RELAY, a selective self-distillation method combined with rubric-based reinforcement learning, including a teacher retirement mechanism based on plateau detection. This is a novel fine-tuning approach to improve instruction-following and reinforcement learning with rubrics.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 56. [Teacher-Guided Tool Learning for Small Language Models via Bidirectional KL Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18459) <a id="link56"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18459
**Authors:** Haoxuan Gao, Qin-Wen Luo, Xiang Chen, Yu Tian, Zequn Sun, Xiaofeng Cao, Jiangchao Yao, Sheng-Jun Huang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18459v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: External tools can extend the capabilities of small language models (SLMs), enabling their deployment in resource constrained environments. However, their limited capabilities make it difficult for SLMs to discover effective tool use behaviors through reinforcement learning alone, while direct teacher supervision suffers from teacher student behavioral distribution mismatch and may propagate erroneous preferences. To address these challenges, we propose Selective Teacher guided Policy Optimization (SeTPO), which coordinates student exploration with selective teacher guidance and persistent demonstration supervision. Specifically, SeTPO incorporates reverse KL derived token level teacher feedback into policy advantages on low reward trajectories, while auxiliary forward KL supervision preserves teacher demonstrated behaviors throughout training. Under an idealized bidirectional KL objective, we theoretically establish that the optimal policy achieves expected reward no lower than the teacher's while retaining a guaranteed fraction of the teacher's probability mass. Extensive experiments on BFCL v4 and API Bank demonstrate that SeTPO achieves superior overall performance over supervised fine tuning, reinforcement learning, and distillation baselines across all three student scales.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: proposes a new fine-tuning method (SeTPO) combining bidirectional KL constraints for improving small language models' instruction-following and tool use via selective teacher guidance and reinforcement learning.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 57. [Only What You've Signed up For: Secure Workflows with Constrained Diffusion Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15177) <a id="link57"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15177
**Authors:** Michael Cardei, Anjun Hu, Chenrui Ma, Francesco Pinto, Chawin Sitawarin, Chulin Xie, Ferdinando Fioretto

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15177v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: As agentic AI systems take on increasingly complex and consequential tasks, securing them against prompt injection becomes critical. Existing defenses often rely on detecting or reasoning about injected malicious instructions and can therefore remain vulnerable to adaptive attacks that optimize the injection itself. Diffusion language models (dLLMs), an emerging framework for language generation, offer a new opportunity for security enforcement: by iteratively refining their sequence wide predictions, they enable broader-scope constraint enforcement throughout generation. This paper introduces Policy-Constrained Diffusion Agents (CoDA), a framework that enforces task dependent security policies directly within the denoising process of diffusion language models. At each denoising step, CoDA projects token distributions onto a policy-compliant set, maintaining constraint satisfaction throughout generation. We evaluate CoDA across state-of-the-art diffusion language model families under both static and adaptive prompt-injection attacks. Results demonstrate substantial reductions in attack success rate with competitive task utility and practical inference overhead.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 10 (tamper resistance, robustness against adversarial modifications) by proposing Policy-Constrained Diffusion Agents (CoDA) that enforce security policies during diffusion LM generation to defend against prompt injection attacks.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 58. [Chain of Thought Monitoring Can Fail Even When CoT Is Necessary](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18105) <a id="link58"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18105
**Authors:** Lennart B\"urger, Piotr Komorowski, Moritz Weckbecker, Evgenii Kortukov, Aakriti Jain, Sebastian Lapuschkin, Wojciech Samek

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18105v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Chain of Thought (CoT) monitoring is used by frontier labs to detect misbehaviour of AI systems. Yet, its reliability remains poorly understood. We attribute this to the lack of a successful organizing principle that leads to generalizable conclusions about monitoring reliability in different regimes. Recent work attributes monitoring failures to the CoT not being necessary for the answer, and argues that CoT becomes monitorable when it is necessary. We show that this binary necessary/not-necessary organizing principle is too coarse by demonstrating that monitoring failures persist even if the CoT is necessary for the answer. In response, we introduce a more fine-grained organizing principle based on a hierarchy of four CoT necessity levels. Building on this hierarchy, we develop a framework for measuring CoT monitorability at the three lowest levels via intervention experiments. We demonstrate monitoring failures at all three of these levels on realistic, hard prediction tasks. On a difficult medical-diagnosis task, patient sex and a clinical attribute influence the answer in at least 19% and 24% of correct responses with a necessary CoT, respectively, yet their influence is almost never articulated. These results show that even when CoT is necessary, it is not guaranteed to be monitorable.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 14: presents a new paradigm to evaluate chain-of-thought monitoring reliability, introducing a hierarchy of CoT necessity levels and intervention experiments to show monitoring failures even when CoT is necessary.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 59. [Loose Lips: Understanding Privacy Leakage in Agentic Dialogue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14071) <a id="link59"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14071
**Authors:** Soumya Sharma, Ayana Hussain, Heber Hwang Arcolezi, Ulrich A\"ivodji, Spandana Gella, Adriana Romero-Soriano, Golnoosh Farnadi

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14071v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: As LLM agents are increasingly deployed in high-stakes enterprise settings, understanding how they handle sensitive information in agentic dialogue has become critical. Privacy leakage in such settings requires reasoning about whether information flows respect the contextual norms under which they originated. We extend CIMemories to CIMEMORIES DIALOGUE, a benchmark of 4,410 two-agent dialogue instances grounded in Contextual Integrity theory. Using this benchmark, we systematically evaluate 7 open-weight models from the Qwen3 and Llama 3.1 families across conversation lengths of 1 and 6 turns under three prompting conditions. We find that task capability and privacy leakage are fundamentally coupled: more capable models are systematically leakier, with no model in our evaluation able to decouple the two. Thinking models consistently leak more than their non-thinking counterparts, a pattern we attribute to the extended reasoning surface created by chain-of-thought. Leakage compounds with conversation length, rising up to 3x from 1 to 6 turns. Our results suggest that the coupling between task capability and privacy leakage is not an artifact of misconfiguration but a structural feature of current agentic systems.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 5: Provides a new benchmark (CIMEMORIES DIALOGUE) and systematic evaluation of privacy leakage in agentic dialogue with LLMs, analyzing real-world usage and safety properties related to privacy leakage and capability coupling.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 60. [SLDBench v2: Active Scaling Law Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16297) <a id="link60"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16297
**Authors:** Lingbei Meng, Rui Huang, Zhirong Shen, Jinxin Liu, Lingxiao Jing, Haorui Chen, Yaosong Du, Haowei Lin

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16297v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Scaling laws predict large-model performance from small experiments, but finding a reliable law itself requires costly experiments. SLDBench tests whether language-model agents can discover scaling laws when all experimental results are given upfront. We introduce SLDBench v2 with two extensions. First, we expand the corpus from 8 to 127 scenarios spanning language-model pretraining, downstream performance, and vision-language transfer, and select 30 challenging scenarios on which a reference power-law fit reaches $R^2 \le 0.8$; they represent at least $1.09 \times 10^{23}$ FLOPs of source-reported training compute. Second, we introduce active scaling law discovery: an agent chooses which experiments to run, pays each one's recorded cost (e.g., training FLOPs), and revises its law as outcomes arrive, and is scored on the same hidden extrapolation targets as in the passive setting. Across 8 frontier agent systems, median $R^2$ drops from 0.72-0.75 (passive) to 0.24-0.57 (active), and prediction error rises 1.5-4.7 times, depending on the system. Agents favor cheap experiments, spending 8-44% of the full-pool cost on average; low-cost acquisition can yield strong predictions, but cheap purchases also leave experimental conditions needed for extrapolation unobserved. Experimental planning thus remains a key open challenge for agents that assist model development in the prevailing AI-for-AI study.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 6: advances scaling laws by introducing active scaling law discovery with agents selecting experiments to fit power-law relationships, including language model pretraining scenarios.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 61. [ACED-Bench: Evaluating How LLM Agents Acquire and Act on Causal Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14381) <a id="link61"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14381
**Authors:** Yiwei Chen, Amila Weerasinghe, Siddharth Biswal, Utkrisht Rajkumar, Olcay Boz, Taylor Berg-Kirkpatrick, Rose Yu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14381v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language models are increasingly used as scientific assistants, yet we still lack reliable ways to evaluate whether they can investigate a system rather than merely answer a prompt. A useful evaluation must let agents choose experiments, interpret finite evidence, and make causal decisions, while keeping the environment coherent across repeated observations and interventions and preserving objective ground truth. We introduce ACED-BENCH (Active Causal Evidence and Decision Benchmark), a neuro-symbolic benchmark built around this requirement. The key idea is to separate the readable scientific surface from the formal causal world: LLMs generate natural-language scenarios and task text, while hidden probabilistic graphical models define the causal graph, data-generating process, intervention semantics, validation checks, and oracle answers. This yields scientific worlds that are natural for agents to investigate but exact enough for scalable, programmatic evaluation. The main leaderboard ACED-CORE contains 180 tasks: 60 set-valued causal ancestry/descendancy questions and 120 constrained causal-decision tasks. We also report ACED-QUAL, a 150-question yes/no qualification check using the same hidden-PGM interface. We evaluate seven contemporary LLMs and two earlier-generation models across three active agent designs. ACED-QUAL checks whether agents can use the interface to draw conclusions from evidence, while ACED-CORE tests whether they can decide what evidence to collect and when to commit to an answer. Evidence-ledger analysis localizes failures within the investigation: agents test the wrong intervention, lose relevant evidence, or continue after sufficient evidence has appeared. These results suggest that reliable AI co-scientists need explicit support for experiment design, evidence retention, answer commitment, and stopping.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4: Proposes a new paradigm for evaluating open-ended text generation by testing LLM agents' ability to acquire and act on causal evidence with a neuro-symbolic benchmark.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 62. [VH-GPO: Verified Handoff for LLM Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14000) <a id="link62"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14000
**Authors:** Haoze Li, Peng Zhou, Peiyuan Wu, Weijie Zhao, Yinan Xin

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14000v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Group-relative reinforcement learning methods such as GRPO train large language model (LLM) agents by comparing rollouts for the same task. When every trajectory in a group fails with the same reward, relative advantages collapse toward zero, leaving no useful learning signal. A frozen teacher can recover from a student-visited state, but letting it complete the task makes the recovered suffix entirely teacher-generated. We ask: after repairing a student failure, when should control return to the student? We propose outcome-verified teacher-student handoff, instantiated in Verified Handoff for Group Policy Optimization (VH-GPO). The teacher provides enough support to reach a state from which the student can succeed, then returns control so that successful experience is again student-generated. Given an environment-verified teacher recovery, VH-GPO selects the earliest independently confirmed handoff point found within a bounded search budget. With Qwen2.5-1.5B, VH-GPO increases success rates over GRPO from 59.38% to 94.79% on ALFWorld and from 71.62% to 77.34% on WebShop. Improvements also hold with the 7B student, for which additional teacher and student output tokens amount to 1.77% and 2.61% of regular student rollout output tokens on ALFWorld and WebShop, respectively. Ablation studies demonstrate the effectiveness of the key components. These results support using verified teacher assistance to repair failures while reinforcing the student's ability to complete tasks independently.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes a new reinforcement learning method (VH-GPO) for LLM agents that improves instruction-following by verified teacher-student handoff, a specific fine-tuning step to improve instruction-following capabilities.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 63. [Learning $\omega$-Powered Conditional Distributions with Importance-Weighted Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16419) <a id="link63"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16419
**Authors:** Maciej Falkiewicz, Frantzeska Lavda, Jason Ramapuram, Alexandros Kalousis

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16419v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Classifier-Free Guidance (CFG) with guidance scale $\gamma$ is commonly motivated by the $\omega$-powered conditional distribution obtained by setting $\omega=\gamma$, but CFG does not, in general, sample from this distribution exactly. We study whether the powered target can instead be learned explicitly. We introduce Importance-Weighted Conditional Flow Matching (IW-CFM), which reweights conditional flow-matching examples using density ratios obtained from frozen conditional and unconditional flow models. With exact normalized weights, the resulting objective has the same gradient as flow matching on the powered target; in practice, we use self-normalized estimation and a regularized empirical approximation for high-dimensional data. On synthetic distributions, IW-CFM recovers analytical powered targets for which numerically matched CFG, $\gamma=\omega$, exhibits a visible mismatch. On ImageNet, an amortized lightweight corrector achieves better alignment than fixed- and matched-guidance CFG with the corresponding weighted and filtered reference distributions. We additionally present an exploratory CLIP analysis of powered-density rankings on real ImageNet data. Aggregate shifts across selected prompt-specific contrasts coexist with heterogeneous classwise responses, including nonmonotonic trajectories. These results provide a practical route for studying and approximating model-defined powered conditional distributions.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Proposes Importance-Weighted Conditional Flow Matching (IW-CFM) to explicitly learn powered conditional distributions related to classifier-free guidance in diffusion models, improving alignment and sampling accuracy.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 64. [TextualRL: Textual Reinforcement Learning for Context Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18216) <a id="link64"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18216
**Authors:** Tianyu Huai, Jie Zhou, Bo Mao, Qin Chen, Liang He, Xipeng Qiu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18216v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: LLM agents can adapt to task feedback by revising the textual context that guides their behavior, without changing their model parameters. However, turning execution rewards into reusable context updates is challenging: individual trajectories offer limited comparative evidence about what to change, while task-specific fixes may overgeneralize when incorporated into a shared context. We introduce TextualRL, a gradient-free reinforcement learning framework that treats a group of rollouts from the same task under the same context, rather than an isolated trajectory, as the unit of policy critique. Groupwise Policy Critique contrasts successful and failed rollouts within a task; when outcomes are uniform, it draws on cross-task evidence to identify behaviors worth preserving or formulate repair hypotheses. Cross-Group Policy Update reviews the resulting proposals against evidence pooled across groups, including observations that yield no proposed edit, to refine their content and applicability. Reviewed proposals form a bounded textual patch, with adoption of the candidate context determined by held-out validation rewards. Together, these components establish a reward-driven policy optimization loop in which multiple attempts jointly inform reusable context updates. We evaluate TextualRL with five target agents on six benchmarks. Averaged over the six benchmarks, TextualRL improves test accuracy over the base model by 24.4 and 15.3 percentage points for GPT-5.5 and Qwen3.8-27B, respectively.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes a new gradient-free RL method (TextualRL) for improving instruction-following behavior of LLM agents by updating textual context based on groupwise policy critique and cross-group policy update.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 65. [StartLux-Decision: AutoThink Decision Models with Reinforcement Learning for Calibrated Deliberative Decisions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17309) <a id="link65"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17309
**Authors:** Xialie Zhuang, Zunhai Su, Jia Pan, Ngai Wong, Guanhua Chen, Chuan-Wei Kuo

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17309v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: We introduce StartLux-Decision, decision models from 0.8B to 27B parameters that combine efficient single-pass prediction with adaptive, confidence-guided reasoning. The Instant models use bidirectional attention in full-attention layers to improve decision accuracy while keeping single-pass inference. The AutoThink models invoke explicit reasoning for uncertain decisions and answer confident cases directly. A three-stage training pipeline establishes shared decision capabilities, specializes the two variants, and equips AutoThink with reasoning through reasoning supervision and reinforcement learning. We introduce Reinforcement Learning for Calibrated Deliberative Decisions (RLCD$^2$), which uses a frozen model and proper scoring rules to reward improvements in decision probability quality and applies a soft penalty to overlong reasoning traces. RLCD$^2$ raises the share of reasoning traces that terminate within the generation limit by up to 48 percentage points and cuts average reasoning length by up to 65%, while largely preserving decision accuracy. On the public subset of Decision Index 0.3, the Instant models outperform the strongest evaluated comparable-scale open-weight decision models by 7.4-16.9 points, and the 27B AutoThink model reaches 73.4. We also introduce DecisionPivot, a programmatically verifiable benchmark of computational decisions built from contrast pairs that differ in a single answer-changing detail. On DecisionPivot, AutoThink outperforms its Instant counterparts by 11.5-18.4 points in pair accuracy and leads among evaluated systems. The models run on vLLM, llama.cpp and MLX, with quantized variants for lower memory use.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1 (new methodological improvements to RLHF or instruction-following). The paper introduces a three-stage training pipeline including reinforcement learning (RLCD^2) to improve calibrated deliberative decisions with reasoning supervision and RL, which is a specific fine-tuning step to improve instruction-following and reasoning in language models.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7
