## 66. [The Tokens Strike Back: Safety-Aware Denoising with Directional Embedding-based Revision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17636) <a id="link66"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17636
**Authors:** Amman Yusuf, Jiwoo Shin, Ruizhi Deng, Heitor Rodrigues Guimar\~{a}es, Nikita Dvornik, Mijung Park

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17636v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: The Safety-Aware Denoiser (SAD) is a recent, training-free, safety-guidance framework for text diffusion models. During inference, SAD steers text samples toward safe regions of the token probability space. In this paper, we propose $\mathit{SADDER}$ (Safety-Aware Denoising with Directional Embedding-based Revision), which improves the safety and quality trade-offs of SAD by exploiting a safety-guidance signal in the embedding (or activation) space for each token position, then updating the token positions using the SAD update rule multiplied by the guidance signal. This multiplication acts as a gating mechanism: tokens with a high unsafe signal are more likely to be modified during inference following the SAD update rule. However, such an update does not automatically preserve the conditional mean, i.e., the denoiser of a fixed target distribution. We therefore theoretically derive new position-wise target distributions for which the modified denoiser is exact. Furthermore, we derive conditions under which the position-wise target distributions match a global target distribution. We test our $\mathit{SADDER}$ method on popular Masked Diffusion Language Models (MDLMs) and Uniform-State Diffusion Models (USDMs) across unsafe continuation and jailbreak settings, and show that $\mathit{SADDER}$ achieves a better balance between safety, generation quality, and inference cost than existing methods.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: advances in diffusion language models with a new safety-aware denoising method (SADDER) improving safety and quality trade-offs in text diffusion models.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 67. [AM-Bench: Understanding Agents under Task Underspecification across Models and Harnesses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18090) <a id="link67"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18090
**Authors:** Kaiyu Huang, Yiting Li, Angyang Gu, Pinjia He, Minchen Yu, Qingjiang Shi

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18090v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Ambiguous or incomplete task specifications complicate both execution and clarification for language-model agents. Yet agents' responses to these information gaps remain poorly characterized across models and execution harnesses. We present AM-Bench, a benchmark built from 48 tasks adapted from Terminal-Bench 2.0 and Harness-Bench. Each task includes a fully specified reference and six variants spanning ambiguous or missing goals, context, and constraints. We evaluate every pairing of five models and four harnesses over 20,160 runs. Our results reveal distinct execution deficits: goal gaps hinder completion, context gaps increase token use, and constraint gaps weaken compliance without necessarily preventing completion. Missingness lowers success more than ambiguity in all three domains. These effects also reshape system comparisons: advantages under complete information need not persist, and rankings depend on model $\times$ harness pairings and the evaluation criterion -- completion, success, or repeat consistency. Estimates based on observed clarification requests and matched fully specified runs further suggest that clarification could recover unfinished work while leaving most constraint violations unresolved. Together, these findings motivate joint model-harness evaluation across information conditions and targeted clarification of unresolved requirements before dependent actions. To support future research on agents under task underspecification, we provide the AM-Bench project repository at https://github.com/s4-lab-cuhksz/AM-Bench.git.

**Comment:** This paper introduces AM-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating language-model agents under task underspecification, which is a new paradigm for evaluating open-ended text generation (criterion 4).
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 68. [SAT-RL: Training Language Models for Logical Reasoning with Symbolic Solvers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14402) <a id="link68"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14402
**Authors:** Jialiang Sun, Kuldeep Meel

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14402v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have achieved strong performance in mathematical and logical reasoning, yet they still suffer from the curse of complexity and struggle as problem difficulty increases with the number of constraint interactions and the size of the search space. In contrast, satisfiability solvers such as Z3 provide scalable and verifiable reasoning through constraint solving under formal theories, but lack the flexibility to interpret natural-language problem descriptions. In this work, we propose SAT-RL, a reinforcement-learning (RL) framework that trains LLMs to interact with symbolic solvers through multi-turn tool execution. SAT-RL leverages synthetic data generation with verifiable solutions, supervised warm-up on solver-augmented traces, and solver-grounded reinforcement learning with tool-aware rewards. Experiments show that SAT-RL substantially improves symbolic reasoning with tools on both in-domain and out-of-domain logic tasks. Our code is available on https://github.com/sunjia72/SAT-RL.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: introduces SAT-RL, a reinforcement learning framework to train LLMs to interact with symbolic solvers for logical reasoning, involving supervised warm-up and RL with tool-aware rewards to improve instruction-following and reasoning.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 69. [Query-Adaptive Neuron Steering for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14519) <a id="link69"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14519
**Authors:** Yanxi Pan, Jingwen Wu, Yan Ma, Xiang Chen, Xinyi Zeng, Xuetong Wang, Yunyan Zhang, Yue Zhang, Yu Tian, Jianfeng Pan, Hongyi Zhou

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14519v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Effectively eliciting the capabilities of large language models (LLMs) at inference time remains an important challenge. Existing studies address this challenge through context engineering, which shapes user queries, or fixed steering, which directly intervenes in model computation. However, predefined contexts and fixed intervention strategies may not adequately accommodate the diverse knowledge and reasoning requirements of individual queries. This raises a natural question: Can internal steering in LLMs be made query-adaptive? In this work, we investigate whether query-induced internal activations can guide adaptive steering in LLMs. Our pilot studies reveal subject- and layer-dependent neuron activation patterns and show that amplifying subject-relevant neurons can improve model performance. Based on these findings, we propose Q-NeuS, a query-adaptive neuron steering framework that dynamically determines which neurons to modulate and by how much for each input. Specifically, Q-NeuS constructs a compact subject-guided candidate neuron space and employs a lightweight Query-Adaptive Gate to generate neuron-wise modulation coefficients. To learn effective intervention strategies, we introduce Neu-GRPO, which adapts group-relative optimization to train the Query-Adaptive Gate using the relative performance of multiple neuron intervention configurations. The base LLM remains frozen throughout training and inference, and no task or subject labels are required at inference time. Extensive experiments across multiple LLMs and benchmarks demonstrate that Q-NeuS consistently improves model performance and exhibits broad applicability across model scales and strong cross-benchmark transferability.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 8: proposes Q-NeuS, a query-adaptive neuron steering method that dynamically modulates neuron activations in LLMs based on input, advancing mechanistic interpretability and internal control of LLM behavior.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 70. [Contrastive Covariance Subspaces for Activation Steering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14025) <a id="link70"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14025
**Authors:** Alexandros Xenos, Manos Plitsis, Niki Foteinopoulou, Yannis Panagakis, Ioannis Patras

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14025v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Activation steering controls large language model behavior by adding a direction to hidden activations during inference. For steering to work well, that direction must be a pure signal for the target concept: any confound entangled with it, such as an incidental topic or writing style, is injected into the residual stream alongside the concept itself and degrades the generated text. We introduce Contrastive Covariance Steering (CCS), a training-free method that eliminates this confound variation. CCS projects steering vectors to a low-dimensional subspace obtained in closed form by solving a contrastive variance objective, which retains the directions along which the target concept varies while suppressing directions shared with the other classes. Across three LLMs and four steering domains, CCS consistently beats all baseline steering methods in all $12$ model-domain settings. The largest relative improvement occurs on AxBench and Emotion where we outperform the next strongest steering baseline by $48$ to $70\%$ and $13$ to $27\%$, respectively. Furthermore, CCS outperforms naive difference-of-means steering by up to $151\%$, showcasing the importance of deconfounding concepts. A toy experiment verifies our motivation on why: planting a topic nuisance into the training data reduces unprojected steering by $46\%$, whereas CCS loses only $12\%$ and recovers most of the gap to an oracle that explicitly deletes the planted direction. A human study with $23$ raters on $296$ generations reproduces the LLM judge's ranking. Our extensive ablation verifies the importance of our method's components and robustness to the number of nuisance classes. Finally, we extend our method to multi-turn conversations, where CCS sustains both concept expression and fluency, matching system prompt performance as other steering methods drift.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 8: introduces a new mechanistic interpretability technique called Contrastive Covariance Steering (CCS) for activation steering in LLMs, which is a method to understand and control internal activations by removing confounds.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 71. [Auditing Failure Attribution: Label Predictability and Judge Behavior](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17747) <a id="link71"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17747
**Authors:** Tianyang Xu, Jing Gao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17747v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Failure-attribution benchmarks ask an LLM judge which step or agent caused a failure. A high score should mean the judge checked the faulty step against the run (a cross-step check). However, spurious signals (surface cues) that predict the annotation can produce a high score. In this paper, we propose four tests to separate checking from cue use. Input restriction asks whether partial input predicts the annotation. Cue following checks whether moved cues change answers. Residual accuracy scores judges where cue-only predictors fail. Aggregation asks whether the failure mix or metric changes rankings. Real observations motivate these tests: on three benchmarks, simple predictors match or beat GPT-4.1. In two Who&When Pro systems, a classifier reading one step's reasoning finds 95-100% of faulty steps, motivating input restriction. In one of them, error injection leaves no reasoning after the faulty step; judges fine-tuned on its labels beat GPT-4.1 by 41 points. Adding task-related reasoning after that step moves this cue: fine-tuned judges lose 31-99 points, but zero-shot judges lose at most 9, motivating cue following. On Aegis, blaming every agent matches GPT-4.1 on micro-F1 but trails by 19 points in exact-set accuracy, motivating aggregation. We recommend reporting these tests when developing failure-attribution benchmarks.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4: proposes new tests to separate true failure attribution from spurious cue use in LLM judge benchmarks, fundamentally rethinking evaluation of open-ended text generation and failure attribution.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 72. [Beyond Policy Rollouts: Embedding-Space Directional Exploration with Anchored Noise for LLM RLVR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16105) <a id="link72"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16105
**Authors:** Daijiao Liu, Huiyi Wang, Lina Yao, Dong Gong

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16105v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) typically uses small rollout groups per prompt, where repeated sampling from the same policy can yield highly similar trajectories, reducing within-group reward variation and weakening group-relative advantage signals. Existing remedies such as higher sampling temperature, entropy bonuses, asymmetric clipping, and larger rollout budgets broaden sampling or modify policy updates but leave rollout conditioning unchanged. In this paper, we propose EDEN (Embedding-space Directional Exploration via anchored Noise), which intervenes at the rollout source through anchored input perturbations. EDEN explores beyond the policy's own rollouts. Within the same rollout budget, it replaces a small fraction of standard rollouts in each group with rollouts that the same policy generates from the prompt plus a single appended, perturbed embedding position, rather than from the prompt alone. Gaussian noise drives variation, while a shared anchor provides a common conditioning direction within each prompt group. Selected perturbed rollouts contribute to GRPO updates with downweighted advantages, and the model architecture, discrete decoding, and test-time inference remain unchanged. Across model scales and mathematical reasoning benchmarks, EDEN outperforms GRPO and the exploration controls evaluated in the same pipeline, with the largest gains in sampled solution coverage. Analyses show that more prompt groups provide non-zero group-relative learning signals and that noise and anchoring play complementary roles. Intervening at the rollout source is thus an effective lever for exploration in RLVR.

**Comment:** Proposes a new exploration method (EDEN) for reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) in LLMs. This is a specific fine-tuning step to improve instruction-following or RLHF-like training by intervening at rollout source. Matches criterion 1 closely.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 73. [Are We Testing Too Much? A Survey of Benchmark Compression for Efficient Evaluation of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18248) <a id="link73"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18248
**Authors:** Guoming Ling, Yupei Lin, Zhongzhan Huang, Junxin Li, Junjie Liu, Shanshan Zhong, Hefeng Wu, Liang Lin

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18248v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Benchmarks are essential for evaluating and improving large language models (LLMs). Yet many widely used benchmarks are redundant, making evaluation unnecessarily expensive. Benchmark compression lowers this cost by estimating full-benchmark scores and rankings from a partial evaluation. Research in this area has grown rapidly since 2024, but to our knowledge no survey has systematically organized its methods and findings. We review 119 papers to explain how methods choose what to evaluate, recover full-benchmark results, and verify their reliability. From this analysis, we develop a taxonomy covering compression within individual benchmarks, across benchmark suites, and during adaptive evaluation, together with work on redundancy and reliability. We compare their information needs, total costs, reliability checks, and limitations. Methods reduce evaluation costs by selecting representative subsets, using cheaper or reusable information, and testing adaptively, but risk poor coverage, inaccurate estimates, and sequential delays. The resulting taxonomy and practical guide help researchers choose, validate, and deploy methods for more efficient and reliable LLM evaluation.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4: provides a comprehensive survey and taxonomy on benchmark compression methods for efficient evaluation of large language models, fundamentally addressing evaluation paradigms and reliability in open-ended LLM benchmarking.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 74. [ClawLoop: Self-Improving Agents via Harness-Native Training Loop](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14118) <a id="link74"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14118
**Authors:** Hansi Yang, Linji Hao, Hongyan Hao, An Wang, Han Wu, Qi Gu, Xunliang Cai

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14118v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: The rapid advancement of Large Language Models (LLMs) has driven their deployment toward general-purpose harness environments, where LLM-based agents operate in sandbox workspaces and interact directly with file systems and shells. While numerous post-training pipelines exist for tool-use agents, their counterparts for harness-based agents remain scarce and are predominantly one-shot and teacher-dependent: a stronger external model synthesizes tasks and collects trajectories, the target agent is trained once, and evaluation results are consumed by human researchers rather than fed back into the loop. Such pipelines are inherently bounded by the capability of the external model and the scope of human prior, leaving no room for self-improvement. To address these limitations, we propose ClawLoop, a framework that enables self-improving without any external teacher model and with minimal prior dependencies. ClawLoop composes three phases into a self-sustaining cycle: Task Synthesis, which generates a diverse pool of training tasks via two complementary construction routines, applies diagnosis-conditioned difficulty growth followed by both agent-free and agent-based task validation; Training, which updates the agent via SFT and/or RL; and Diagnosis and Feedback, which analyzes the agent's rollout behaviors to produce a capability analysis report that will be used as feedback for task synthesis in the next iteration. Using Qwen3.6-27B as the loop model, ClawLoop yields consistent improvements on SkillsBench, Claw-Eval-Live, and ClawGym-Bench. The iteratively refined tasks are further transferred to smaller-scale Qwen3.5-4B and 9B models with gains across all benchmarks, validating data quality independently of the loop model.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: proposes a novel self-improving training loop for instruction-following agents involving SFT and RL, with iterative task synthesis and feedback, improving instruction-following capabilities without external teachers.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 75. [LLM Value Functions for Credit Assignment in Agentic RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15840) <a id="link75"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15840
**Authors:** Kaijie Zhu, Weixiang Yan, Omar Attia, Abbas Kazerouni, Alexander Toshev, Andrew Szot

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15840v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) for large language model (LLM) agents remains challenging in long-horizon tasks with sparse outcome rewards, where assigning credit to individual actions is difficult. Methods such as GRPO assign the same trajectory-level advantage to all actions, while critic-based methods require learning an additional value function that can be costly and unstable. We introduce LLM Value Function RL (LVRL), which uses the pretrained knowledge and in-context learning ability of an LLM to estimate policy-aware state values without finetuning a separate critic. LVRL conditions the value model on group context and calibrates its predictions using known values at the initial and terminal states, enabling step-level credit assignment from estimated value differences. We further extend this idea to learning from an expert policy through on-policy distillation (OPD), introducing LLM Value Function OPD (LVOPD), which focuses teacher supervision on actions associated with decreases in estimated value. We evaluate our approach across diverse agentic benchmarks: AppWorld, BabyAI, and SWE-bench Verified. Across policy scales (4B to 32B), LVRL consistently and substantially outperforms both GRPO and PPO. On AppWorld and SWE-bench Verified, LVRL yields absolute improvements of up to 13.6 and 8.6 percentage points over GRPO. Notably, on tasks requiring extended exploration like BabyAI, LVRL bridges the gap from total failure to 75.0% success. Furthermore, LVOPD yields consistent improvements over standard distillation baselines on AppWorld. Finally, Monte-Carlo analysis confirms that our frozen critic accurately identifies semantic pitfalls and recoveries, proving that LVRL extracts robust, policy-aware step-level credit from sparse terminal outcomes.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes a new RL method (LLM Value Function RL) for credit assignment in agentic RL with LLMs, improving instruction-following and policy learning without separate critic finetuning, relevant to RLHF and instruction-following fine-tuning improvements.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 76. [Gradient Routing Localizes and Removes Unintended Behaviors in RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17062) <a id="link76"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17062
**Authors:** Jake Ward, Shawn Hu, Aria Wong, Nathan Hu, Bryce Woodworth, Alexander Matt Turner, Alex Cloud

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17062v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: We propose a mitigation to reward hacking in reinforcement learning. Our setup involves a misspecified reward function that gives rise to unintended behavior, and a monitor used to detect unintended behavior. As with frontier AI systems, the monitor makes systematic errors. Consequently, using the monitor for data filtering or to apply a reward penalty fails to prevent the model from learning unintended behavior ("reward hacking"). To address this problem, we introduce GRAFT: Gradient Routing Adapter Fine Tuning. Rather than penalizing or filtering flagged samples during training, GRAFT constrains gradient updates on those samples to a sacrificial set of new parameters. This has two benefits: First, because the monitor is not part of the reward function, the model does not learn to hide misbehavior. Second, ablating the sacrificial parameters reduces or entirely stops the unintended behavior while maintaining improved performance on target tasks. We demonstrate these benefits in RL environments where reward penalties cause models to learn to reward hack in undetected ways, training models ranging from 135M to 8B parameters. Additionally, we show a similar effect at a larger scale by distilling frontier LLM trajectories containing reward hacking into Qwen3-32B, without teaching that model to reward hack. Overall, our work suggests GRAFT may be a promising approach for leveraging imperfect monitors during post-training in settings where true performance is difficult to evaluate.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes a new fine-tuning method (GRAFT) to mitigate reward hacking in RL, related to improving instruction-following and RLHF-like setups by constraining gradient updates to sacrificial parameters. This is a novel fine-tuning step to improve behavior in RL settings.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 77. [Understanding and Reducing Misalignment in Persuasion Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16070) <a id="link77"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16070
**Authors:** Boyi Wei, Ruta Desai, Peter Henderson, Wenyue Hua

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16070v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Frontier models are now trained for complex multi-agent tasks, from running a company to engaging in negotiations. These tasks necessitate training models to be persuasive. But what does this optimization pressure mean for model alignment? In this work, we study how reinforcement learning (RL) training for persuasion affects models' use of misaligned strategies such as evidence fabrication and belief coercion, and what techniques can reduce such misalignment. We find that optimizing sender models solely for receiver belief shifts causes the average number of distinct misaligned strategies used per game to increase by more than 25%, and fabrications per game increase to more than 4 times relative to pre-RL models. We compare various mitigation approaches, including stronger strategy guidance, clean demonstrations, penalizing misaligned strategy usage, and improving the model's ability to detect fabrications. We find that navigating the persuasiveness-misalignment trade-off in this setting is challenging, with the best trade-offs coming from combining guidance on aligned persuasion strategies with reward penalties on misalignment. Overall, our work provides a testbed for assessing misalignment stemming from RL-based persuasion training and shows concrete paths for reducing misalignment. Importantly, it highlights that without direct intervention, persuasion-involved RL environments are likely to naturally lead to large increases in the use of misaligned strategies.

**Comment:** This paper studies misalignment arising from RL training in persuasion tasks and mitigation techniques. It explicitly discusses RL-based misalignment and strategies to reduce it, which relates closely to criterion 11 (AI alignment techniques). It involves RLHF-like concerns about alignment and reward modeling.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 78. [ProAS: A Progress-Aware Advantage Shaping Framework for Multi-Turn Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18683) <a id="link78"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18683
**Authors:** Bowen Ding, Mingda Wan, Yuhan Chen, Jiyao Yuan, Qi Zhu, Futing Wang, Heyuan Deng, Fei Mi, Lifeng Shang, Tao Lin

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18683v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) has emerged as the primary paradigm for optimizing Large Language Model (LLM) agents, yet efficient critic-free algorithms (e.g., GRPO) suffer from a fundamental uniform credit assignment dilemma that broadcasts trajectory-level advantages equally to all actions. Our analysis reveals that this uniformity inherently reinforces structurally invalid actions in preferred trajectories and ignores the downstream cascading influence of early pivotal deviations in unpreferred ones. To resolve this without introducing critic overhead, we propose Progress-Aware Advantage Shaping (ProAS), a plug-and-play framework that leverages a dynamically evolving Agentic Reference Graph (ARG) to achieve fine-grained credit assignment via Masking Unreasonable Incentives (MUI) and Asymmetric Penalty Redistribution (APR). Extensive experiments across Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct demonstrate that ProAS universally elevates mainstream group-based baselines to new state-of-the-art performance, achieving peak success rates of 91.9\% and 94.3\% on ALFWorld, alongside 66.7\% and 70.8\% on WebShop, respectively. Furthermore, it accelerates training convergence by up to $2.44\times$ while maintaining strong robustness against imperfect or distilled ARGs. Codes: https://github.com/LINs-lab/ProAS.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes a new fine-grained credit assignment method (Progress-Aware Advantage Shaping) for reinforcement learning of LLM agents, improving instruction-following and RLHF-like training without critic overhead.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 79. [BridgeTwist: Twisting Schr\"odinger Bridges for Training-Free Conditional Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18658) <a id="link79"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18658
**Authors:** Mengyu Li, Qianqian Qu, Jun S. Liu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18658v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Generative models are often required to serve many downstream conditional tasks. In the training-free setting, a pretrained unconditional model is reused as a prior and combined with a task-specific likelihood to sample from the corresponding posterior. While such training-free samplers have been developed for diffusion and flow models, their counterpart for Schr\"odinger bridge (SB) models remains unexplored, despite the growing appeal of SBs as flexible stochastic transport models between general source and target distributions. We propose BridgeTwist, a training-free conditional sampler for pretrained SB generative models, formulated as a tempered twisted sequential Monte Carlo scheme. Its twist is a closed-form plug-in surrogate, obtained by combining the velocity and score fields exposed by SB pretraining into an endpoint predictor and evaluating the likelihood there. Since this surrogate is less reliable near the source, we temper the twist with a time-varying exponent, downweighting its influence at early steps. We further introduce a bridge-shaped inference-time diffusion schedule that concentrates the proposal noise in the interior of the path. Telescoping importance weights then cancel all intermediate twist factors, yielding an asymptotically exact sampler for the posterior. Empirically, BridgeTwist outperforms training-free baselines on class-conditional and text-prompted image generation, and is competitive with specialized inverse problem solvers on inpainting, deblurring, and super-resolution.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Presents a new training-free conditional sampling method for Schrödinger bridge generative models, a type of diffusion model, applied to conditional sampling tasks. This is a significant advance in diffusion language models and generative modeling.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 80. [TRACE: Temporal Risk Assessment and Concept Editing for Safer Language Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15365) <a id="link80"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15365
**Authors:** Ankur Garg, Xuemin Yu, Hassan Sajjad, Samira Ebrahimi Kahou

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15365v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) can produce harmful content while generating a response. A useful safeguard should not only detect this risk during generation but also intervene while leaving safe responses largely unchanged and provide an auditable record of the internal patterns supporting its decisions. We propose TRACE (Temporal Risk Assessment and Concept Editing), a framework that tracks harmful-response risk during generation, selectively edits token activations, and leaves an auditable trace of its detection and steering decisions. TRACE learns a codebook of recurring activation patterns, with each codebook vector representing a latent concept. At inference time, it assigns each token activation to the nearest codebook vector to obtain the corresponding concept. A gated recurrent unit (GRU) processes the activations and their assigned concept features to predict completed-response harmfulness and a monotone cumulative streaming risk. During steering, activations assigned to harmful concepts are steered along directions defined by nearby benign targets. An external LLM labels the learned concepts post hoc, making detection sequences and harmful-to-benign edit records interpretable. Experiments across three generators and the WildGuard and S-Eval benchmarks show that TRACE improves streaming F1 over Kelp in five of six comparisons while remaining competitive on completed-response detection. TRACE's selective steering reduces Harmbench attack success with little change in refusal on originally safe responses. The post-hoc audit reveals recurring semantic structure in the learned concepts and distinct traces associated with harmful detection, safe generation, and activation edits.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 10: proposes TRACE, a method for tamper resistance and safer language generation by detecting and steering harmful content during generation with interpretable activation edits.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 81. [A CaLLM Judge is a Better Judge: Mitigating Bias in LLM-as-a-judge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18108) <a id="link81"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18108
**Authors:** Cesare Barbera, Luca Stradiotti, Lorenzo Cascioli, Lorenzo Perini, Niek Tax, Daniel Haimovich, Jesse Davis, Andrea Passerini, Andrea Pugnana

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18108v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as judges to predict which of two candidate answers a human evaluator would prefer, providing a scalable alternative when human evaluation is too costly. Unfortunately, LLM judgments can exhibit systematic biases. Prior work has documented position, verbosity or model family of the candidate answers influencing the judge decision rather than their quality, while additional biases may remain undocumented. $\textit{Multicalibration}$ provides a natural framework to address this problem: each bias corresponds to miscalibration on an identifiable group, $\textit{e.g.}$, family bias manifests as overconfidence in answers generated by models belonging to the same family of the judge. The challenge is to fix miscalibration simultaneously across many, potentially unknown groups. To this end, we introduce $\mathrm{CaLLM}$, a post hoc framework that multicalibrates an LLM judge, thereby bounding all such biases simultaneously. We prove that $\mathrm{CaLLM}$ bounds the bias of every group whose error structure is captured by an available representation, and that augmenting it with explicit bias groups further reduces the residual bias. We then derive theoretical conditions under which $\mathrm{CaLLM}$ improves the judge's accuracy, rather than trading it for calibration. Finally, we evaluate $\mathrm{CaLLM}$ across pairwise preference benchmarks, showing that it reduces the multicalibration error on both specified and unspecified bias groups, while improving predictive performance of the judge.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4: introduces CaLLM, a post hoc multicalibration framework to mitigate bias in LLMs used as judges for preference evaluation, fundamentally rethinking evaluation paradigms by bounding biases across groups.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 82. [Interaction-as-a-Judge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17895) <a id="link82"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17895
**Authors:** Wayne Chi, I-Hung Hsu, Li Chen, Wei-Lin Chiang, Ion Stoica, Anastasios Nikolas Angelopoulos, Ameet Talwalkar, Chris Donahue, Cho-Jui Hsieh

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17895v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Coding agents are tasked with producing sophisticated interactive applications such as websites, desktop applications, and games. While humans assess these applications interactively, current automated LLM evaluations primarily focus on code analysis and unit tests. This mismatch can cause LLM judges to miss failures that are immediately apparent to human users. We study Interaction-as-a-Judge, an evaluation paradigm where computer-use agents (CUAs) directly interact with an application. We demonstrate that Interaction-as-a-Judge can improve both \emph{evaluation} and \emph{generation} of interactive applications. To this end, we collect and release two complementary datasets: $6,858$ pairs of applications with human preferences and corresponding agent traces, and $222$ applications with human-verified requirement labels. Relative to standard non-interactive approaches, we find that Interaction-as-a-Judge improves evaluation in terms of predicting human preferences ($65.6\% \to 69.1\%$) and requirement verification ($77.0\% \to 81.6\%$). We also observe scaling benefits: across both preference alignment and requirement verification, higher-quality interactions improve performance. Finally, through both inference- and training-time experiments, we demonstrate that Interaction-as-a-Judge can also improve the quality of generated artifacts. At inference time, Interaction-as-a-Judge repairs $75.5\%$ of broken requirements within five attempts, compared to $55.4\%$ for code-only self-feedback. At training time, using Interaction-as-a-Judge as a reward signal improves win-rate over the base model by $6.7$pp while reducing both generated code length by $35.7\%$ and build failure rates by $7.2$pp compared to a code-only reward signal.

**Comment:** Presents a new paradigm for evaluating open-ended generation via Interaction-as-a-Judge, improving evaluation and generation quality; matches criterion 4 on new paradigms for evaluating open-ended text generation.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 83. [Rate-Resolved Text Evaluation with Masked Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15667) <a id="link83"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15667
**Authors:** Yiqi Liu, Kun Zhao, Chenghua Lin, Nafise Sadat Moosavi

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15667v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: A single evaluation score can rank generation systems but rarely reveals what failed. We propose rate-resolved text evaluation, which turns the masking rate of masked diffusion language models into a diagnostic axis. By reconstructing text under different fractions of masked tokens, one diffusion score becomes a vector of band-conditioned scores that reflect reconstruction difficulty at varying levels of visible context. On SummEval, preserving this rate structure yields complementary signal: the best masking-rate band improves fusion with autoregressive likelihood over a collapsed diffusion scalar, survives a sample-shuffle control that breaks rate ordering, and adds residual signal to a fixed prompted judge. A second diffusion model reproduces the effect more weakly. On WMT MQM zh-en, roundtrip mask-and-reconstruct probing produces a tunable localization curve in which ranking-oriented AUROC and span-detection F1 peak at different masking rates. Factuality benchmarks bound the interpretation: band semantics are model- and task-dependent, so each application requires empirical validation.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4: Proposes a new paradigm for evaluating open-ended text generation using masked diffusion language models to produce rate-resolved evaluation scores that reveal failure modes and reconstruction difficulty at varying masking rates.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 84. [SkillScaffold: Scaffolding Agentic Reinforcement Learning with Policy-Adaptive Skill Credit Assignment and Selective Injection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18807) <a id="link84"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18807
**Authors:** Wenlin Zhong, Hao Wang, Kun Yuan, Fan Yang, Jinluan Yang, Dian Jin, Xu Tan, Yiquan Wu, Kun Kuang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18807v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Agentic reinforcement learning (RL) enables large language models (LLMs) to learn from multi-turn interactions with verifiable outcomes. Textual skills can improve exploration by distilling prior interactions into reusable guidance. However, existing skill-based RL approaches often either assume skill injection is beneficial or rely on indirect feedback or rollout-intensive evaluation to assess its utility. Our pilot studies show that rollout-based utility estimation for skill credit assignment is costly and unstable under limited sampling, while skill benefits vary across candidates and depend on the current policy's competence on each query. To address these challenges, we introduce SkillScaffold, a policy-adaptive training scaffold that combines low-cost skill-utility estimation with selective skill injection. By reusing outcome-verified training trajectories, SkillScaffold generates candidate skills and estimates their utility through outcome-aware likelihood comparisons, without candidate-specific environment rollouts. The resulting utility guides whether and which skill to inject, retaining skill-free execution when no candidate is estimated to help. Joint training enables the Generator to produce skills tailored to the evolving Executor. Scaffolded RL encourages skill internalization for skill-free inference. Experiments on ALFWorld, WebShop, ScienceWorld, and $\tau^2$-Bench demonstrate gains over strong agentic-RL and skill-based RL baselines across both short- and long-horizon tasks. Ablations and further analyses support the importance of utility-based selection and abstention. Our code is available at https://github.com/Vaidurya00/SkillScaffold.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes a new policy-adaptive training scaffold (SkillScaffold) for agentic reinforcement learning that selectively injects skills to improve instruction-following and RLHF-like training of language models.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 85. [Enabling Token-wise Importance Sampling in Off-Policy RLVR with Proximal KL Regularization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18738) <a id="link85"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18738
**Authors:** Hao Bai, Jiarui Yao, Tong Zhang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18738v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Off-policy data in reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) can arise from asynchronous generation, rollout-training mismatch, and response reuse. Token-wise importance weighting avoids multiplying likelihood ratios across a response but generally biases policy gradients. We prove that, under coverage and local expressiveness conditions, full future KL credit makes the exact proximal optimizer the update's unique stationary policy, even for mixtures of older policies, without full-response weights or a fitted critic. We further establish convergence of continuous-time tabular population updates under persistent coverage and time-varying behavior policies, and a finite-sample reward guarantee for linear logistic models under additional assumptions. These results motivate Proximal KL, a practical off-policy RLVR method that separates the proximal reference from the sampling policy and replaces PPO clipping with bounded future KL credit. Across all seven matched off-policy comparisons on Qwen3-1.7B and Qwen3-4B, spanning two learning rates, response reuse, and rollout staleness, Proximal KL outperforms GRPO in micro-averaged accuracy over eight math benchmarks by up to 2.58%. A separate on-policy comparison yields comparable aggregate accuracy. In periodic-refresh experiments, Proximal KL avoids the collapses observed in PG and CISPO, exceeds GRPO's training accuracy, and reaches higher accuracy in fewer policy updates. Ablations show that token-wise correction outperforms sequence-level and unweighted controls. With token-wise correction retained, future KL feedback reduces sensitivity to the tested baseline perturbation relative to PPO clipping. A finite future horizon improves AIME24 mean@16 and pass@16 over the full tail, while fixed-policy measurements show that truncation reduces correction variance over the tested horizons.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1 closely: proposes a new off-policy RL method with token-wise importance sampling and proximal KL regularization to improve reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards, relevant to RLHF and instruction-following fine-tuning improvements.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 86. [From Policy Evaluation to Off-Policy Control: Provable Emergence of In-Context Q-Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18057) <a id="link86"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18057
**Authors:** Yingjun Mo, Yajie Zhao, Fang Kong

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18057v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Transformers can implement reinforcement-learning algorithms in context, yet how off-policy control emerges within Transformers remains poorly understood. We study Q-learning, a foundational model-free control algorithm that combines off-policy bootstrapping with greedy policy improvement. We prove that a two-layer attention-only Transformer can implement a finite-temperature form of Q-learning, where the temperature $\tau>0$ controls the approximation to greedy action selection, recovering window Q-learning as $\tau\to0$. We train the structured Transformer on randomly generated MDP tasks using output-only supervision. Experiments show that the theoretically predicted parameter structure emerges during training, including the internal greedy action-selection mechanism, and that the trained Transformer achieves performance comparable to Q-learning on unseen tasks. Our theoretical analysis further shows that the equivalent realizations form a solution manifold and that, under suitable task-diversity conditions, the training iterates converge locally to this manifold at an exponential rate. For the random-task model analyzed here, the local worst-case number of gradient steps needed to reduce the distance to the manifold by a factor $\epsilon$ is $\Theta\left(\tau^{-5}\log(1/\varepsilon)\right)$, quantifying the increasing optimization cost as the learned update approaches window Q-learning.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: provides a theoretical and empirical demonstration that Transformers can implement Q-learning in-context, revealing mechanistic insights into how off-policy control emerges in language models, relevant to instruction-following and RLHF methodology.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 87. [Learning Source Structure: Bayesian Prediction with Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18725) <a id="link87"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18725
**Authors:** Meir Feder, Ruediger Urbanke

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18725v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: A transformer trained on sequences whose source parameters are drawn afresh each time can learn only what the sequences share: the source family and its prior. The Bayesian predictor that knows both, which we call informed Bayes, is the unique minimizer of the training loss and is computable from the family alone; we use it as a quantitative, falsifiable reference. Where earlier work compared transformers with Bayes on sources whose contexts can be learned independently, we build Markov sources with up to 64 symbols in which observations in one context inform prediction in another: product sources with known or hidden encodings, transition rows sharing an unknown base law and depth, and a mixture of three families. Our main result is that informed Bayes predicts, from the family and prior alone, how a trained transformer behaves in four tests that go beyond the learning curve: the loss that remains under a source outside the training family (predicted 2.237 bits, measured 2.309), the doubling of the extra loss when two independent components change rather than one (0.345 predicted, 0.350 measured), the benefit of other contexts when a token appears for the first time, and the depth at which copying an earlier continuation starts to pay. On the learning curves themselves, small transformers match informed Bayes when the shared structure is visible in the tokens, and hidden structure needs more layers and training, with gaps below 0.01 bits per token for the largest models. We explain why the imitation is possible and where it must fail: informed Bayes decomposes into sufficient statistics that attention selects and accumulates and an estimator, fixed by the prior, that the feed-forward network learns; training pays the complexity of informed Bayes under the architecture's prior, and finite-memory universal prediction bounds how closely a bounded network can reach it.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 6 (Studies scaling laws in neural networks). The paper provides a theoretical and quantitative analysis comparing transformers to informed Bayesian predictors, explaining behavior beyond learning curves and decomposing sources of error. This is a conceptual explanation related to scaling and generalization.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 88. [Separating Prompt Harmfulness from Interaction Safety via Partial Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16761) <a id="link88"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16761
**Authors:** Ram Vikas Mishra, Shashank Aggarwal, Jahnavi Kumar, Amit Awekar

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16761v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: LLM-based information systems need safety guardrails. Most guardrails classify the prompt alone. If the prompt looks harmful, the system rejects it before any answer is generated. This is costly. Many rejected prompts would have received a safe response. Users lose help they should have received from the information system. This cost grows as conversational and generative search becomes common. Prompt-level labels treat harm as a property of the query. But harm depends on what the model actually writes. Existing input-output guards do look at responses. But they judge a finished response, so the decision comes after full generation. However, we observe that safety of model interaction with the input prompt can be inferred early with only partial output. So the system can start generating, watch the partial response, and stop only if it turns harmful. We test this idea in three steps. First, we build a dataset of 41,944 prompt-response pairs with seven open weights LLMs from three families: Llama, Gemma, and Qwen. Second, we show that prompt harmfulness and interaction safety differ. Prompt labels and response labels agree on only 50.46\% of items (Cohen's $\kappa$ = 0.06). Of the prompts that make any model respond harmfully, only 3.5\% do so for all seven LLMs. Third, we show that partial responses carry most of the signal. Fine-tuned classifiers predict response-level harm well (best AUPRC 0.885, F1 0.807). About one third of the response tokens recover almost full-response AUPRC.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 19 (Studies security of LLMs and methods to protect language models from attacks or exploitation). The paper proposes a method to detect harmful outputs early during generation, which is a novel approach to safety guardrails in LLMs, focusing on partial generation monitoring to prevent harmful responses.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 89. [Input-Conditioned Search for Efficient Test-Time Scaling of Recursive Reasoners](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18547) <a id="link89"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18547
**Authors:** Haochen Shi, Tianwen Fu, Qianggang Ding, Yinhan Lu, Yizhan Li, Huan Zhang, Yajie Zhao, Xingdi Yuan, Hengyang Lu, Bang Liu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18547v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: People often tackle difficult problems by thinking more deeply or exploring more possibilities. Similarly, recursive reasoners, which reason by repeatedly applying a learned update rule to latent states, can also scale inference through more updates (depth) or additional trajectories generated by perturbing latent states (width). When people hit a dead end in their thinking, they may step back and approach the problem from a fresh angle. However, neither depth scaling nor perturbation-based width scaling of recursive reasoners can reliably achieve this: both may converge to incorrect attractors. To address this, we propose to explore diverse reasoning traces by applying different update maps to the same latent state, without any additional training. We realize this idea through \emph{Input-Conditioned Search (ICS)}, which searches over input conditions to induce alternative update maps from the pretrained reasoner. Across six reasoning tasks, ICS achieves the highest mean accuracy, while requiring up to two orders of magnitude fewer model FLOPs than the evaluated scaling baselines. Controlled experiments confirm that ICS can redirect trajectories from the same state. Our theoretical analysis and rigorous numerical verification also confirm that ICS can locally eliminate incorrect attracting fixed points. Together, these findings establish ICS as an effective axis for efficient test-time scaling and motivate further research on shaping recursive reasoning during training and at test time.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 12: Advances in LLM reasoning capabilities by proposing Input-Conditioned Search (ICS) to efficiently scale recursive reasoners at test time, redirecting reasoning trajectories and eliminating incorrect attractors without additional training. This is a novel method for improving reasoning in language models.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 90. [The Thinking Leakage in Reinforcement Learning for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14270) <a id="link90"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14270
**Authors:** Wenxuan Zhu, Shuming Liu, Bang An, Aznaur Aliev, Bing Li, Yibo Yang, Bernard Ghanem

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14270v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Hybrid reasoning models such as the Qwen3 series switch between a THINK mode and a NO-THINK mode through a few chat-template tokens. A popular recipe trains such models with reinforcement learning in NO-THINK mode and reports improved reasoning performance. We provide evidence that much of this gain can be explained by thinking leakage: the gain comes largely from partially reactivating the reasoning abilities of the original model's THINK mode instead of learning enhanced reasoning behavior. We train Qwen3-1.7B, 4B, and 8B with GRPO in NO-THINK mode. As accuracy improves, responses become longer and more reflective, are increasingly judged by the original model to resemble its own thinking, and the model's internal activations shift toward its THINK mode. We then investigate at the level of weights, activations, and attention heads: scaling the RL update further elicits THINK-mode behavior and approaches THINK-mode accuracy but does not exceed it; in Qwen3-8B, replacing a low-dimensional THINK-related activation subspace of the RL-trained model with its original-model value removes most of the RL gain, and the reverse exchange transfers a considerable fraction of the gain to the original model; and the RL-induced shift passes through the attention heads that read the empty think block. We further identify a possible mechanism in the GRPO training signal: responses favored by the advantage-weighted update are more THINK-like, and a gradient step computed from this signal moves the model toward its THINK state. Thus, gains in NO-THINK mode can largely reflect reactivation of reasoning abilities already present in the original model.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Analyzes RLHF-style training (GRPO) effects on reasoning modes in LLMs, showing that improvements come from reactivating existing reasoning abilities rather than new learning, providing mechanistic insight into RLHF fine-tuning steps for instruction-following.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 91. [Do Factuality Methods Make LLMs More Factual? Reproducibility and Fair Comparison](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18415) <a id="link91"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18415
**Authors:** Pratuat Amatya, Vinay Setty, V Venktesh, Avishek Anand

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18415v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are aligned to human preferences, after pre-training and supervised fine-tuning steps. However, standard RL alignment steps such as RLHF do not focus on factuality of LLMs, hence they may generate non-factual information, which could result in unintended consequences. In response, a number of factuality alignment approaches have been proposed in the literature which claim to improve factuality of LLMs on their own benchmarks. However, these methods have not been reproduced independently and are also not comparable since each uses a different base model, training data, evaluation set, judge, and metric. In this paper, we present the first independent reproduction of five factuality alignment methods, which span on-policy RL (PPO, GRPO, and Dr.~GRPO) and offline preference optimization (DPO and KTO), and compare them on a common benchmark with common evaluation metrics. We found that none of these methods reproduces in full, and on the common benchmark none of the methods significantly outperforms the base model. On closer inspection, the rewards optimize for format, length, or brevity rather than factuality and relevance, whereas the choice between on-policy and offline training matters little. Furthermore, the evaluation metrics and benchmarks are also part of the problem since they do not measure relevance of text to the prompt. Since there is no ground truth associated with the prompts, the reliance on LLM judges and retrieval for verification also makes the evaluation fragile. We release our code, data, and checkpoints.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: The paper studies and reproduces multiple RLHF-related factuality alignment methods (including PPO, GRPO, DPO) and compares them on a common benchmark, analyzing their effectiveness and evaluation metrics for instruction-following improvements.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 92. [Compositional RL: Evaluating and Training LLMs for Collaborative Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17456) <a id="link92"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17456
**Authors:** Shangbin Feng, Yulia Tsvetkov, Jan Kautz, David Acuna

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17456v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Language models are often evaluated alone but increasingly deployed together, routed between, made to debate, ensembled, or collaboratively trained. For a model inside a system, what matters most is not how well it performs on its own, but how much the system gains from its presence. We call this a model's compositional strength and formalize it using the Shapley value of a cooperative game over coalitions of a model pool, and use a model's leave-one-out contribution to the full coalition as a tractable proxy, requiring n+1 rather than 2^n system evaluations. Evaluating individual vs. compositional strengths across five model pools, twelve collaboration methods, and twelve datasets, we find them largely uncorrelated: in 12 of 15 (pool, method-family) settings, individual strength does not translate into compositional advantages, and the coupling is weakest among general-purpose models that practitioners are most likely to deploy. Standalone benchmarks are therefore often poor proxies for models' value in collaborative systems. We then propose compositional reinforcement learning (C-RL), which trains a policy inside a live multi-model system and rewards the outcome produced by the system as a whole, rather than the policy's correctness in isolation. The leave-one-out baseline in Shapley values cancels under a group policy gradient, so this needs no additional rollout cost beyond RL on the collaborative system's outcome and never requires running a coalition without the policy. Across three collaboration protocols and eight tasks spanning reasoning, coding, and scientific discovery, C-RL produces the strongest collaborator in 21 of 24 settings, lifting collaborative performance by +4.0 to +5.4 pts on average (~3x the gain of baselines), up to +13.1 pts.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes a new compositional reinforcement learning method to train LLMs for collaborative reasoning, improving instruction-following and system-level performance via RL-based fine-tuning inside multi-model systems.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 93. [Before Attention Sinks Form: Hidden-State Geometry Reveals and Modulates Their Formation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16745) <a id="link93"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16745
**Authors:** Fan Yang, Yue Song

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16745v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Attention sinks are usually studied after they have formed. We ask whether a measurable hidden-state change appears beforehand and whether changing that geometry alters what happens later. To remove network depth as a shortcut, we compare prompts in the same model and at the same layer. When sink timing varies across prompts, simple spectral summaries of the hidden states add information beyond current attention, with the clearest prompt-specific signal in a short window about two to three layers before sink formation. The predictive strength varies across cohorts, so we treat the spectrum as a readout of a pre-sink state change rather than a universal warning statistic. The signal is broader than one measured extreme activation. We then modify only the singular values of the post-layer hidden states after current attention has already been computed. These interventions change later sink behavior in Qwen2.5, GLM-Z1, and GPT-OSS relative to norm-matched random spectral perturbations. In Qwen2.5, the immediate suppressive effect is accompanied by a reduction in next-layer query--key preference for position~0, and the learned spectral directions remain stable when estimated from substantially less training data. Together, the results show that attention sinks are preceded by a short spectral reorganization of hidden states, and directly perturbing this geometry can causally alter subsequent sink formation.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 8: Demonstrates a mechanistic interpretability technique by analyzing hidden-state geometry changes preceding attention sink formation and causally intervening to modulate them.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 94. [REPRO: Reflective Evolutionary Prompt Optimizer for Automated LLM Red-Teaming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15513) <a id="link94"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15513
**Authors:** Abrar Alotaibi, Moataz Ahmed

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15513v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Safety-aligned large language models (LLMs) remain vulnerable to adversarial prompts that bypass their safety training. Existing automated red-teaming methods either require privileged access unavailable in production, rely on manually engineered per-model templates, or optimize a single objective without population-level diversity. We present REPRO (Reflective Evolutionary Prompt Optimization), a pure black-box framework that discovers adversarial prompts through evolutionary optimization driven entirely by a compressed open-weight 7B reflective model on a single consumer GPU. REPRO introduces two mechanisms absent from prior work, both isolated in a component ablation: semantic crossover, an LLM-guided multi-parent recombination operator, and Bayesian strategy selection via Thompson Sampling informed by structured failure analysis.   We catalog REPRO's behavior on AdvBench, JailbreakBench, and HarmBench across 36 target models from 11 families, including GPT-5-nano, Claude-3.5-Sonnet, Gemini-2.5-Pro, and Grok-4.1. Under our protocol REPRO reaches 100\% attack success rate (ASR) on every evaluated target in a single turn, at a median of 24 target queries per behavior. Two properties of the protocol qualify that figure. The judge scoring candidates during optimization also produces the reported ASR, and search halts when it accepts, so ASR reports whether search succeeded within the iteration budget rather than success at a fixed budget. All baseline figures are transcribed from their original papers, which use different judges, budgets, and model versions. We therefore present this as a catalog of model coverage under one protocol rather than a head-to-head ranking, and develop both qualifications in the paper.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 10: Presents a new black-box adversarial prompt generation method (REPRO) for automated LLM red-teaming, including novel mechanisms like semantic crossover and Bayesian strategy selection. This contributes to tamper resistance and robustness against adversarial prompt attacks on LLMs.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 95. [Label Collapse in Diffusion Language Model In-Context Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16839) <a id="link95"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16839
**Authors:** Ziye Guo, Ye Yuan

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16839v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: In-context learning (ICL) applies large language models to new tasks by prepending a few labeled demonstrations to a query. Prior work has studied how demonstration ordering affects ICL, but the interaction between label arrangement patterns and model biases has not been systematically examined. We define label collapse as the failure mode in which the model generates query-independent labels, and show that it is architecturally universal at the tested 8-9B scale: five models spanning autoregressive (Llama-3, Qwen3.5) and discrete diffusion (LLaDA, iLLaDA, LLaDA-Instruct) families all exhibit label collapse even under the class-balanced alternating arrangement, with some label word pairs collapsing in virtually every tested condition and others flipping their collapse direction with arrangement. Critically, accuracy alone cannot detect this collapse: models can achieve near-chance accuracy while generating query-independent labels, a score indistinguishable from balanced performance, so the failure can persist undetected in deployment. We measure separation, the log-odds of the two label words at the query position (the quantity that label-bias calibration operates on), as a complementary diagnostic that exposes collapse hidden beneath surface accuracy, together with a measurement protocol that guards label extraction against tokenizer and formatting artifacts. For autoregressive models, collapse persists where the majority-label-bias account does not apply, and arrangement patterns can reverse the collapse direction; for diffusion models, we present a systematic evaluation of ICL failure modes, complementing contemporaneous studies of dLLM context comprehension and demonstration-order sensitivity, and identify open questions about how iterative denoising interacts with label biases.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: This paper studies label collapse in diffusion language models and autoregressive LMs during in-context learning, providing a new diagnostic metric (separation) to detect failure modes not visible from accuracy alone. It systematically evaluates ICL failure modes in diffusion LMs, which is a novel empirical insight into diffusion LM behavior.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 96. [TPPO: Tracking-Value PPO For Language Model Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16794) <a id="link96"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16794
**Authors:** Futing Wang, Chenxi Li, Jianhao Yan, Yun Luo, Zhi Wang, Yizhuo Li, Runzhe Zhan, Yu Cheng, Tao Lin

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16794v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) is widely used in reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards for language-model reasoning. While \emph{value evolution along trajectory} characterizes relative token-level credit, yet standard critics often fail to track it under sparse terminal rewards. To model this evolution, we analyze the relation between successive prefix values and show that they admit a Bayesian decomposition with additive updates in log-odds space. We use this additive structure to construct Tracking-Value PPO (\method), a cumulative value parameterization that sums position-wise scalar outputs into value logits. These outputs are learned through terminal-outcome supervision on the reconstructed values. \method improves aggregate mathematical reasoning scores over PPO by 7.0 and 8.4 points on Qwen3-4B-Base and Qwen3-8B-Base, respectively, while improving out-of-distribution generalization. Gains extend to Qwen3-4B, Qwen2.5-Math-1.5B, Qwen3.5-9B and to countdown, and scientific coding tasks. Further analysis shows more accurate tracking of value evolution and more reliable advantage estimates. Our code is publicly available in https://github.com/LINs-lab/TPPO.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes Tracking-Value PPO, a new RL method improving value tracking in PPO for language model reasoning tasks, enhancing instruction-following and reasoning capabilities with verifiable rewards. This is a specific fine-tuning step to improve language model instruction-following and reasoning.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 97. [Facts Don't Speak Louder than Words: The Behavioral Essence of Long-CoT Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17607) <a id="link97"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17607
**Authors:** Yongcan Yu, Jian Liang, Lingxiao He, Kuangpu Guo, Yanbo Wang, Shuo Lu, Meng Wang, Ran He

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17607v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: While long chain-of-thought (CoT) distillation significantly improves the reasoning abilities of language models, its underlying mechanism remains poorly understood. Current pipelines generally rely on rejection sampling to gather reasoning traces across diverse prompts, assuming that factual correctness and broad problem coverage are indispensable. Surprisingly, we find that training exclusively on flawed reasoning trajectories or a restricted prompt set yields nearly comparable performance. Driven by this counterintuitive finding, we propose the Behavioral Hypothesis: the essence of Long-CoT distillation is behavioral alignment with structured reasoning patterns, rather than factual knowledge memorization. We then validate this by demonstrating substantial reasoning gains even when models are fine-tuned exclusively on previously mastered problems-effectively isolating behavioral alignment from novel knowledge injection. Having established that reasoning patterns are the true bottleneck, we investigate how to learn them across varying levels of complexity optimally. We reveal a critical interplay: while the recent probability-based loss excels at learning simple patterns, the standard cross-entropy loss is essential for scaling to complex CoTs. We believe these insights demystify CoT distillation and provide a principled foundation for the future development of reasoning models. Code is available on https://github.com/yuyongcan/Long-CoT-Distillation.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 12: The paper studies chain-of-thought (CoT) distillation in language models, revealing that behavioral alignment with reasoning patterns rather than factual knowledge is key. It provides new insights into loss functions for learning reasoning patterns, advancing understanding of LLM reasoning capabilities and chain-of-thought reasoning.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 98. [Joint Discrete and SE(3) Diffusion for Protein Interface Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14699) <a id="link98"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14699
**Authors:** Zhenqiao Song, Jianwen Xie, Lei Li

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14699v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: The design of high-affinity, target-specific protein binders is central to the development of modern therapeutics. However, existing protein-generation methods remain limited in this setting, as many generated designs fail to simultaneously achieve geometric and physicochemical complementarity at binding interfaces. To address this challenge, we introduce PIDiff, a physicochemically enhanced multimodal diffusion framework for protein-protein complex design. PIDiff jointly designs target-conditioned protein sequences and structures by coupling diffusion in discrete sequence space with diffusion on the continuous (SE(3)) structural manifold. At its core, PIDiff employs PIFormer, a novel architecture that interleaves geometry-aware invariant point attention with global sequence self-attention. We further curate PIBench, a protein-protein complex dataset with diverse target scaffolds derived from the Protein Data Bank (PDB). We first pretrain PIDiff on PIBench and subsequently fine-tune it for two therapeutically relevant applications: minibinder design and antibody design. PPFrame consistently outperforms baseline methods across sequence design success rate, structure binding energy, and sequence-structure co-designability on all tasks.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: The paper presents a novel diffusion framework (PIDiff) for protein interface design that jointly diffuses in discrete sequence space and continuous SE(3) structural space. This is a significant advance in diffusion models applied to language-like sequence generation (protein sequences) combined with continuous diffusion on structure, aligning with advances in diffusion language models.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 99. [Beyond Answer Voting: Fine-Grained Correction of LLM Reasoning through Graph Alignment across Sampled Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18606) <a id="link99"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18606
**Authors:** Haotian Liu, Yingjian Chen, Xiaohang Xu, Lang Huang, Yusuke Iwasawa, Yutaka Matsuo, Irene Li

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18606v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language models increasingly tackle tasks that require multi-step reasoning, where errors in intermediate claims can affect the final answer. Self-consistency (SC) improves reasoning by voting over sampled final answers, but its vote leaves intermediate disagreements unresolved. To address this problem, we introduce DDRG (Disagreement-Driven Verification Guided by Reasoning Graphs), a framework for selective correction from a frozen bank of reasoning traces. Our insight is that graph alignment can expose claim-level disagreements and provide correction evidence without expanding the sampled trace bank. DDRG builds source-grounded claim graphs and sends localized conflicts to an answer-blind verifier. A verifier-supported refutation that reaches the majority answer's supporting path triggers one focused repair; otherwise, DDRG returns the exact SC vote. Experimental results across five benchmarks show that native zero-shot DDRG improves macro-average accuracy over direct answering by 8.65 and 15.83 percentage points for Qwen2.5-7B and Llama3.1-8B. It also exceeds CoT, the evaluated GoT and GoV question-answering adaptations, and SC on this macro metric. DDRG links local verification to selective correction without enlarging the initial candidate bank.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 12: proposes DDRG, a method for fine-grained correction of LLM reasoning by graph alignment across sampled reasoning traces, improving multi-step reasoning and selective correction beyond self-consistency voting.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 100. [ShellElastic: Scalable Elastic Pretraining with Simultaneous Maximal Updates and Hyperparameter Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15124) <a id="link100"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15124
**Authors:** Can Jin, Yan Kang, Yifan Gong, Chongjian Ge, Mingze Xu, Yuanjun Xiong, Dimitris N. Metaxas, Hongwu Peng

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15124v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Foundation models need to support diverse inference budgets, yet training a separate model for each budget duplicates pretraining effort. Elastic models train several nested sub-networks inside one weight-shared supernet, but existing recipes face three obstacles: variable per-step workloads that hinder distributed scaling, limited hyperparameter (HP) transfer across model sizes, and no guarantee that all sub-networks are maximally updated at once. We present \Ours, an elastic transformer that organizes hidden dimensions into a dense core and successive shells, so that every hidden matrix is block lower triangular. One full-width forward pass contains every sub-network's exact hidden states as prefixes, so joint training keeps a fixed computation graph and costs about as much per step as training the widest sub-network alone. We further introduce \muE, an extension of maximal update parameterization (\mup) to \Ours and other elastic architectures. Its scaling rules provably keep all sub-networks of a supernet maximally updated simultaneously (\muI), and keep doing so when the supernet is widened or given more shells; sweeps confirm that HPs tuned on a small non-elastic proxy stay near-optimal for elastic families and larger supernets (\mbox{\muT}). A single \Ours run produces sub-networks that outperform MatFormer and fully elastic baselines at matched parameter counts, and approach separately trained models that together take several times the steps.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 6: Studies scaling laws in neural networks. Paper presents ShellElastic, an elastic transformer architecture with maximal update parameterization and hyperparameter transfer, with provable scaling rules keeping all subnetworks maximally updated simultaneously. This is a novel approach to scaling elastic models and training efficiency.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 101. [Can We Trust This Evaluation? Instance-Level Reliability of LLM-as-a-Judge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14029) <a id="link101"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14029
**Authors:** Yang Ba, Michelle V. Mancenido, Rong Pan

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14029v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: LLM-as-a-judge evaluation commonly aggregates multiple judges' decisions into a majority vote, yet this panel decision process does not guarantee that the evaluation of an individual item is reliable. We propose Structured Cross-fitted Uncertainty Risk (SCUR), a label-free approach for estimating instance-level evaluation reliability based on the judge panel's behavior. A latent measurement model serves as the backbone for measurement system analysis, from which SCUR derives three complementary signals capturing panel uncertainty, measurement cross-entropy, and perturbation sensitivity. These three sources of evidence are combined into a risk score. We evaluate the SCUR approach on five real-world benchmarks using majority-vote error ranking, selective trust, and risk stratification. Furthermore, we show our approach to be more effective at detecting high-consensus risk items, which are the blind spots for many other LLM-as-a-judge evaluation methods.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4: Proposes a new paradigm for evaluating open-ended text generation by estimating instance-level reliability of LLM-as-a-judge evaluations using a novel uncertainty risk score combining multiple signals.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 102. [Compare to Correct: Pairwise-Guided Logit Adjustment for LLM Test-Time Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18583) <a id="link102"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18583
**Authors:** Cong Hua, Qianqian Xu, Zitai Wang, Zhiyong Yang, Shilong Bao, Linchao Zhang, Qingming Huang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18583v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Sampling-based test-time scaling enhances LLM reasoning by aggregating multiple reasoning paths for the same query. Existing methods use answer frequencies or path confidence to compute candidate scores, which do not explicitly characterize comparisons between competing answers. Inspired by the Condorcet principle in social choice theory, we introduce pairwise confidence comparisons between paths supporting different answers to capture answer-level comparative advantage. We combine this comparative evidence with voting support through a KL-regularized objective. Theoretically, we show that this comparative-advantage is equivalent to the adjustment to the traditional voting logits. In this way, our proposed Pairwise-Guided Logit Adjustment (PGLA) framework allows comparative evidence to compensate for weaker voting support and correct voting-based ranking errors. Experiments on 10 challenging benchmarks including mathematical reasoning, knowledge reasoning, and code generation with 7 models ranging from 1.7B to 32B parameters speak to the effectiveness of this plug-and-play term. Additional evaluations with external reward scores and online sampling further demonstrate its applicability.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4 closely. Proposes a new paradigm for evaluating open-ended text generation by using pairwise confidence comparisons inspired by social choice theory to improve reasoning path aggregation and answer ranking in LLMs.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7
