## 127. [Statistical Vignettes on the Grokking Phenomenon](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18811) <a id="link127"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18811
**Authors:** Baye Malick Gning, Elvis Dohmatob

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18811v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: In this work, we study grokking in a simple, analytically tractable setting whose rich phenomenology allows us to disentangle the effects of initialization, signal strength, weight decay, sample size, and input dimension. These factors can produce superficially similar learning curves through distinct mechanisms, complicating their interpretation. Our analysis distinguishes early fitting through nuisance features, core feature learning, and delayed generalization. We identify sharp conditions under which grokking provably occurs and derive analytic asymptotic expressions for the lengths of the generalization plateaus. Our results also characterize regimes of early generalization and failure to reach a prescribed test accuracy target. We show that the interpolation edge constitutes a distinct dynamical regime, where vanishing spectral gaps separate core learning from generalization, helping clarify the role of proximity to interpolation in existing accounts of grokking. The resulting picture is analogous to fast and slow phases in chemical kinetics and suggests that the lazy-to-rich transition discussed in the literature may reflect a similar separation of dynamical time scales. Our theoretical results are faithfully supported by extensive experiments.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 6: provides a theoretical and analytic study of the grokking phenomenon with sharp conditions and asymptotic expressions, contributing to understanding scaling laws and learning dynamics in neural networks.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 128. [Posterior Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13994) <a id="link128"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.13994
**Authors:** Chenrui Ma, Xi Xiao, Lin Zhao, Xinru Jiang, Tianyang Wang, Yanning Shen, Ferdinando Fioretto

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.13994v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Flow matching learns how to transport noise toward data, but its mean prediction does not directly report the endpoint uncertainty remaining at an intermediate state. This paper introduces Posterior Flow Matching (PFM), a general generative modeling framework that learns conditional velocity means and componentwise variances with a Gaussian posterior model. The same model evaluation supplies both a transport prediction and endpoint uncertainty, making posterior diagnostics available throughout generation. These learned moments support a mean-driven ordinary differential equation (ODE) and a posterior-driven stochastic differential equation (SDE), whose local noise scales adapt to the predicted uncertainty. Across image, point-cloud, molecular, and action generation, PFM improves generation quality and provides endpoint-posterior readouts throughout generation.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Presents a new generative modeling framework (Posterior Flow Matching) that improves generation quality and provides uncertainty estimates throughout generation. Although examples include image and other modalities, the method is a general generative modeling advance relevant to diffusion models, including language models.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 129. [EdgeLLM-Nano: Auditing Polar-Guided Key-Cache Compression and Consistency-Anchored Pruning on a 33.6 MB On-Device Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14475) <a id="link129"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14475
**Authors:** S M Mahbub Murshed, Hayden Bright, Rezaul Karim, Ali Shiri Sichani

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14475v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Deploying language models (LMs) on the edge presents unique memory, latency, and power challenges absent in high-performance computing. KV-cache compression emerges as a promising approach to address these challenges. However, the effectiveness of current KV-cache compression methods has not been thoroughly examined on edge devices. Moreover, we show that existing evaluation methods do not always involve the KV-cache under test. We propose EdgeLLM-Nano, a measurement study of KV-cache compression using a nano-scale decoder-only LM (transformer) testbed that has 8.39M parameters, i.e., 33.6 MB, to enable end-to-end examination of each backend, seed, and power sample. Our results give a negative verdict for a polar-guided key-cache quantizer (round-to-nearest (RTN) control causes an order of magnitude less damage), as well as a consistency-anchored progressive weight pruner (one-shot magnitude-based pruning performs as well as, if not better). We also introduce Long-Context Decode Fidelity (LCDF) evaluation, a token-by-token examination of teacher-forced log-losses using the KV-cache under test. Unlike perplexity that gives the same 5.461 score to all methods, LCDF distinguishes different KV-cache compression backends thanks to its paired statistics across seeds and effect sizes. Our companion Raspberry Pi 5 measurement shows all compression backends we tested to be less energy-efficient per token compared to uncompressed FP16 inference. Except for KIVI that is close to lossless in terms of LCDF, our findings indicate KV-cache compression can be improved. LCDF offers a simple and effective way of shedding light on KV-cache compression methods, whose negative impacts have been overlooked by perplexity.

**Comment:** This paper studies KV-cache compression methods for on-device language models and proposes a new evaluation metric (Long-Context Decode Fidelity) that better distinguishes compression methods than perplexity. This is a new paradigm for evaluating language model components (KV-cache) in open-ended generation contexts, matching criterion 4 (new paradigms to evaluating open-ended text generation).
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 130. [Gluing Local Contexts into Global Meaning: A Sheaf-Theoretic Decomposition of Transformer Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17117) <a id="link130"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17117
**Authors:** Bryce Grant, Peng Wang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17117v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Existing interpretability tools do not test whether a representation keeps the same content when only the phrasing changes. We treat each paraphrase pair as a local witness that two activations should agree and build a cellular sheaf over the paraphrase graph; its Laplacian $L_\mathcal{F}$ answers the question on a frozen model with no training. The kernel $H^0$ is a content subspace: at 20 dimensions it beats the preserved subspace of the concept eraser LEACE on held-out CounterFact fact retrieval across eight architectures by a mean $+17.1$\,pp, and constraining all paraphrases of a fact jointly adds a further $+6.5$\,pp. The spectral complement concentrates causal influence: ablating the most paraphrase-variable coordinates, a coordinate-wise proxy for it, changes model outputs $5.6$ to $26.5\times$ more than ablating coordinates of matched per-coordinate variability, across nine models from 124M to 13B parameters. Stacking layers into a grid creates $b_1 = N(L-1)$ algebraic 1-cycles, and the resulting model-derived harmonic mass varies by nearly three orders of magnitude across six architectures and ranks them as their steering fragility does (Spearman $\rho = 0.90$, exact one-sided permutation $p = 0.014$). The same paraphrase graph yields the content subspace, the influence directions, and the fragility measure. Project page: https://cwru-aism.github.io/gluing-lc-page/

**Comment:** Presents a novel interpretability method using sheaf theory to analyze transformer representations, which relates to mechanistic interpretability (criterion 8).
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 131. [Self-Evolving Agent Harness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18813) <a id="link131"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18813
**Authors:** Chuyang Wei, Haoxiang Guan, Minghang Zhu, Kexin Zhou, Jiyan He, Xiren Zhou, Huanhuan Chen, Yifei Shen

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18813v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Harness self-evolution revises an agent's software scaffolding, including prompts, memory, workflows, and executable components, while keeping model weights fixed. We formalize a feedback-driven process in which accepted revisions persist across runs. The framework separates update targets from proposal and admission mechanisms. It distinguishes changes to interpreted state from revisions to tools and code, repository-level control logic, and plugin composition. We characterize search through evolutionary optimization and examine its reliability through continual PAC-learning. Under explicit assumptions, the analysis yields an expected generalization bound, a retention bound conditional on historical regression budgets, and a high-probability admission rule using fresh, independent validation data. We examine how persistent harness revisions affect knowledge sharing and organizational workflows, with implications for scientific reliability, physical safety, and institutional accountability. Cases from prior work illustrate model--harness co-design, distributed execution, and plugin reuse. Reliable deployment requires continual validation as task distributions and evaluators change, with independent safeguards for learning, runtime privileges, and consequential actions. We provide a comprehensive 66-page discussion of related work, theory, algorithms, evaluation, applications, and the broader impact of Harness RSI, together with continuously updated code and supplementary materials, at https://github.com/zgcagi/Harness-RSI.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 13: Presents a framework for AI control and oversight via self-evolving agent harnesses that revise software scaffolding while keeping model weights fixed, with theoretical analysis of generalization and admission rules for reliable deployment and continual validation.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 132. [From Parallel to Sequential: Small-Batch Noise Restores Simplicity Bias of Muon](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16868) <a id="link132"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16868
**Authors:** Tom Jacobs, Rebekka Burkholz

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16868v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Simplicity bias, which can arise from sequential feature learning during training, has been proposed as a general explanation for the success of deep learning. An important driver of this phenomenon is saddle-to-saddle behavior, in which the gradient flow trajectory remains close to a sequence of saddle manifolds while learning one feature at a time. However, Muon, a state-of-the-art optimizer based on spectral descent, does not exhibit such dynamics in the full-batch setting and instead learns features in parallel. How is it then that Muon is so successful in deep learning settings? We show that stochastic gradient noise fundamentally changes this behavior: small-batch noise restores sequential feature learning, providing a mechanism for simplicity bias that is absent from full-batch Muon. We explain this transition through stochastic smoothing of the spectral descent operator underlying Muon. When gradients are small relative to the noise scale, the expected spectral update becomes approximately proportional to the actual gradient, which restores gradient-flow-like dynamics. We formalize this mechanism for deep linear models by deriving the effective continuous dynamics of noisy spectral descent. Experiments on low-rank matrix reconstruction, vision transformers, and large language models (LLMs) support these findings.

**Comment:** Relevant to criterion 6: Studies theoretical mechanism behind simplicity bias and feature learning dynamics in deep learning optimizers, including experiments on large language models. Provides a formal explanation of stochastic noise restoring sequential feature learning.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 133. [Does self-distillation improve reasoning?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18613) <a id="link133"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18613
**Authors:** Jason Ken Adhinarta, Raina Wu, Ashutosh Sharma, Avinash Balakrishnan, Rameswar Panda, Polina Golland, Yoon Kim, Zhang-Wei Hong

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18613v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Self-distillation is an emerging approach that trains a model to imitate its own predictions when conditioned on privileged information, such as correct answers or environmental feedback. Recent methods have reported better sample efficiency and generalization than reinforcement learning (RL), but it remains unclear whether these gains extend beyond in-distribution evaluations and whether they stem from privileged distillation. In this paper, we revisit two representative methods, Self-Distillation Policy Optimization (SDPO) and On-Policy Self-Distillation (OPSD), across code, science, tool use, and math reasoning tasks. We find that their reported gains do not consistently generalize to unseen problems and are largely explained by overfitting or transferring existing reasoning behavior, rather than effectively using privileged information. Moreover, self-distillation gradients show weak alignment with policy gradients that directly optimize expected reward, questioning whether they provide an effective direction for policy improvement. Our experiments across Qwen3 models (1.7B-14B) and OLMo-3-7B suggest that effectively leveraging privileged information to improve reasoning remains an open challenge. Our code is available at https://github.com/sleepypandu/does-sd-improve-reasoning

**Comment:** Closely matches criterion 1: analyzes self-distillation methods as alternatives to RLHF for instruction-following and reasoning improvements, providing empirical insights into their effectiveness and limitations.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 134. [Strong yet Controllable LLM Abstention without Learning to Abstain](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16318) <a id="link134"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16318
**Authors:** Junwei Zhang, Yifei Wang, Wanli Yang, Hexiang Tan, Rui Tang, Xin Chen, Jingang Wang, Du Su, Fei Sun

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16318v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) can reduce hallucination by abstaining from questions they would likely get wrong. Abstention can be implemented outside the model (selective answering) using a confidence threshold that controls the answer rate (coverage), or learned inside it (abstention training), which can perform better but fixes coverage once trained. To compare the two, we match their coverage and decompose risk (error rate) into answering ability and distinguishing ability, i.e., how well the model answers and how accurately it abstains, respectively. The decomposition shows that abstention training performs better mainly by answering better: with strong confidence estimators, the two have nearly equal distinguishing ability. This motivates Train for Accuracy, then Post-hoc Select (TAPS), a modular approach that keeps coverage controllable, needs only standard post-training and an off-the-shelf confidence estimator, and benefits from advances in either component. Across three LLMs and three factual QA benchmarks, TAPS with a probing-based confidence estimator attains 1.5--9.4 percentage points lower mean risk than three representative abstention training methods at matched coverage. Within TAPS, accuracy training often weakens the model's own confidence signals, yet refitting the estimator largely preserves distinguishing ability. Together, these results establish TAPS as a strong yet controllable baseline and raise the bar for future abstention training: sacrificing control over coverage should yield gains beyond what can be achieved by optimizing answering and abstaining separately.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes a new method (TAPS) for controllable abstention in LLMs that separates training for accuracy and post-hoc confidence-based selection, improving instruction-following reliability without coverage loss, relevant to instruction-following fine-tuning and confidence calibration.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 135. [LLM Evaluation is a Forecasting Problem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17018) <a id="link135"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17018
**Authors:** Rahul Thomas, Adam Block, Micah Goldblum

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17018v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Standard procedure in machine learning is to randomly split train and test data, emulating IID draws from one underlying data distribution. In practice, models are trained on past data and deployed in the future, which may come from distinct distributions. In this paper, we find that web text is indeed highly non-stationary, as text distributions shift over time. We further observe that model performance changes continuously over time across diverse benchmarks. Consequently, future model performance is better predicted by measurements made on recent evaluation data, and is best predicted by time series forecasters applied to historic performance rather than by historic performance itself. Choosing which model to deploy based on forecasted accuracy enables more reliable selection of performant models, thus improving downstream accuracy in the regime practitioners care about: deployment. Taken together, our experiments suggest that time should be treated as a fundamental variable in LLM evaluation. We release our code and data.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4: proposes a fundamentally new paradigm for evaluating open-ended LLM performance by treating evaluation as a forecasting problem over time, emphasizing temporal distribution shifts and time series forecasting of model accuracy.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 136. [Backward Conformal Prediction under Covariate Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16048) <a id="link136"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16048
**Authors:** Amin Meghrazi, Yuanyuan Li

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16048v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Backward conformal prediction (BCP) fixes the size of a prediction set and estimates its miscoverage, but assumes calibration and test data are exchangeable. We extend BCP to covariate shift through importance weighting, preserving its guarantees when the density ratio is known. We show that BCP's tight miscoverage report depends only on the relative weight assigned to missed calibration examples, explaining why a better domain classifier can sometimes produce a worse estimate. Motivated by the fact that BCP depends only on nonconformity scores, we propose estimating density ratios on sorted score vectors rather than high-dimensional inputs. We prove that this approach recovers the same report asymptotically under an interpretable condition and characterize the resulting bias otherwise. Experiments with simulated and benchmark distribution shifts show that the proposed method improves BCP's miscoverage estimates, while score-based weighting consistently yields more accurate estimates than embedding-based weighting.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4: Describes new paradigms to evaluating open-ended text generation by extending backward conformal prediction under covariate shift with importance weighting, improving miscoverage estimates with theoretical guarantees. This is a novel statistical method for evaluation under distribution shift.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 137. [RECAP: Calibrating Low-Bit Reasoning Models on the History They Read](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15259) <a id="link137"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15259
**Authors:** Chi Ruan, Yujie Chen, Zixiang Zhao, Jingcai Guo, Haotong Qin

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15259v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Low-bit quantization reduces the cost of serving reasoning models but can substantially degrade long chains of thought. Each step reads cached keys and values spanning thousands of tokens, allowing quantization errors to accumulate along the trajectory. Standard calibration operates on short segments that omit the earlier context needed by later reasoning steps. Even when this context is retained, cache reconstruction error alone does not capture how quantization errors affect subsequent attention. We present RECAP, a post-training quantization method that calibrates reasoning models on the history they read. Dynamic-prefix calibration separates long historical context from short-block reconstruction. It regenerates prefix keys and values with the current quantizer and rotates the active block to supervise every position along the trajectory. Prefix read geometry further supervises historical cache errors according to their effects on attention outputs, weighting key and value errors asymmetrically. With both components applied only during calibration, RECAP outperforms the baseline across multiple reasoning models and quantization settings. Under W4A4KV4, RECAP improves accuracy over the baseline by 13.3 percentage points on MATH500 with DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-1.5B and 15.8 points on AIME-120 with Qwen3-4B. These results highlight the importance of preserving historical dependencies during calibration for low-bit reasoning.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 12 closely: The paper presents a novel post-training quantization calibration method (RECAP) specifically designed to preserve long-range historical context in reasoning models, improving chain-of-thought reasoning accuracy under low-bit quantization. This is a methodological improvement related to reasoning capabilities in LLMs.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 138. [Information-Theoretic Foundations of Online Temperature Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15043) <a id="link138"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15043
**Authors:** Jin Sima, Nikolaos Papagiannis, Ananth Grama, Wojciech Szpankowski

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15043v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Repetitive, degenerate generation is a persistent failure of LLM decoding, and the standard remedies (temperature, nucleus, and top-$k$ sampling) are static heuristics. By Kac's lemma and the AEP, a stationary source takes time exponential in its entropy to repeat a block. We therefore maximize the entropy of the sampling distributions under one cross-entropy budget that keeps the output plausible under the model. Offline, the solution is temperature sampling, with the inverse temperature as the budget's Lagrange multiplier. \emph{Our contribution is the online problem}: the conditionals $p_1, p_2, \dots$ are revealed one at a time, and the sampling distribution $q_t$ must be chosen causally from $p_1, \dots, p_t$ while the budget couples the entire horizon. The optimal temperature is thus a global shadow price that must be estimated from a prefix. We propose Entropic Online Sampling (EOS), which re-solves for the temperature on each prefix. We prove it loses $O(R\log n)$ entropy against the offline optimum while overshooting the budget by $O(R\log n)$, with $R$ an instance-dependent drift parameter. A budget-tightened variant is exactly feasible at the same rate. Conversely, every exactly feasible online algorithm must lose $\Omega(R)$, so EOS is optimal up to a $\log n$ factor. When the conditionals are drawn from a finite mixture, $R=\tilde{O}(\sqrt{n})$. Experiments confirm that EOS tracks the offline temperature and beats fixed-temperature sampling in entropy.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4: Proposes a new online temperature sampling method (Entropic Online Sampling) to improve decoding diversity and entropy in LLMs, which is a fundamental rethinking of sampling strategies affecting open-ended text generation evaluation and generation quality.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 139. [History Replays Itself: Unleashing Long-Context Capability in Linear Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15715) <a id="link139"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15715
**Authors:** Li Nanbo, Firas Laakom, Yilan Zhang, Piotr Pi\k{e}kos, Yimeng Chen, Liangyu Wang, Yifan Xu, Mateusz Ostaszewski, Dandan Guo, Wenyi Wang, Yanning Dai, Yuhui Wang, J\"{u}rgen Schmidhuber

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15715v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Linear attention promises the efficiency needed for long-context language modelling, yet its fixed-size recurrent state must simultaneously support ongoing computation and preserve information from an ever-growing history. This coupling has reinforced a prevailing view that the state's memory capacity defines the model's long-context capability. We challenge this presumed ceiling: \emph{recurrent computation need not bear the full burden of long-term storage}. We introduce NoteBank, a sparse episodic memory that archives selected text before future queries are known, uses the backbone's native features for writing and addressing, and reads retrieved evidence through its native forward computation via replay. This \emph{training-free, plug-and-play} interface requires no external embedding model, separately trained retriever, or separate neural reader. At one million tokens, it raises Gated DeltaNet's mean accuracy across six RULER retrieval tasks from 1.7% to 70.3%, with no observed significant retrieval degradation from 4K to 1M tokens. This recovery adds $\leqslant$0.025% to analytical FLOPs, with persistent context storage approximately 1/2,600 that of a Transformer's full MHA KV cache at matched width, depth, and precision. Experiments extend from three 1.3B recurrent backbones to Mamba-2-8B and post-trained Qwen3.5-9B, demonstrating gains on LongBench v1/v2 and MemoryAgentBench. Together, these results show that sparse evidence storage and native replay can substantially expand the long-context capability of pretrained linear attention models while retaining the efficiency of recurrent computation.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Advances in diffusion language models. The paper studies long-context capability in linear attention models and includes experiments on diffusion LM families (LLaDA, iLLaDA). It presents a novel sparse episodic memory method (NoteBank) that improves long-context performance efficiently.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 140. [Evidence-First Agentic Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17724) <a id="link140"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17724
**Authors:** Reza Sanayei, Razvan-Gabriel Dumitru, Sunjie Hou, MohammadHossein Rezaei, Haris Riaz, Robert Vacareanu, Minglai Yang, Ying Liu, Mihai Surdeanu, Daniel Yue Zhang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17724v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Long-horizon, semi-verifiable agentic tasks often rely on rubric-based evaluation, but the evidence needed to assess these tasks can be spread across the environment state and the execution trace. A judge may overlook this evidence or accept unsupported claims, including an agent's own reports of success. When rubric verdicts serve as reinforcement-learning rewards, such errors can reinforce undesirable behavior. We introduce Sauron, an evidence-first agentic verifier that investigates both state and trace and relies on a claim only after independently validating it against its source. Bounded queries and joint investigation of related criteria reduce context use and redundant work, and each verdict carries an auditable receipt of its supporting evidence. Across 2,586 human-labeled criteria from 100 personal-assistant episodes, Sauron achieves the highest F1 of five verifiers on all eight backbones tested while costing less than Gandalf, the strongest baseline. With GPT-5.6 Sol, it reaches 0.96 F1 versus Gandalf's 0.92 at 23% lower cost, with a wider margin on process criteria. On a paired fault-injection suite covering 13 families, several of which simulate reward hacking, Sauron detects 97% of injected failures while flagging only 2% of clean controls, outperforming baselines even when they are given the full trace. On TRAIL, a benchmark for detecting and localizing real agent errors, Sauron leads both Gandalf and TRAIL's own judge on all three quality metrics.

**Comment:** This paper introduces Sauron, an evidence-first agentic verifier that improves evaluation of agentic tasks and detects reward hacking, which relates to new paradigms for evaluating open-ended text generation (criterion 4).
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 141. [Training LLMs for Multi-Agent Collaboration with Verifiable Information Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17893) <a id="link141"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17893
**Authors:** Keru Chen, Sen Lin, Yingbin Liang, Nathaniel D. Bastian, Shaofeng Zou

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17893v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Effective collaboration among large language model (LLM) agents in multi-agent systems requires sharing relevant evidence, requesting missing information, and incorporating teammates' contributions into decisions. Training these behaviors is challenging because final task outcomes provide limited feedback on the value of individual messages. We introduce CoSIR (Collaboration via Surprisal-based Information Rewards), a post-training approach that uses a simple, verifiable environment to teach collaboration behaviors that generalize to more complex, unseen tasks. We propose a Bayesian evaluator that scores shared information by its reduction in team surprisal, defined as the sum of the negative log probabilities assigned to the correct option given each agent's available evidence. Requests are scored by the potential reduction in the same quantity if the requested information were truthfully provided. Separate rewards encourage agents to use the received information in their private choices and calculations. We post-train Qwen3-4B, Qwen3-8B, and Gemma-3-4B-it through self-play and evaluate transfer to seven unseen natural-language collaboration tasks. CoSIR achieves the highest aggregate score, while its performance in general tasks remains competitive. Behavioral analyses show increased disclosure of private evidence and fewer judged failures to use teammates' input.

**Comment:** This paper proposes CoSIR, a post-training method to improve multi-agent collaboration in LLMs using verifiable information rewards, which relates to instruction-following improvements and training methods for better collaboration behavior. This aligns closely with criterion 1 (methodological improvements to instruction-following).
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 142. [AlignPoison: Conditional Targeted Poisoning of Generative Recommenders via Decoding-Aligned Prefixes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14487) <a id="link142"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14487
**Authors:** Zhuoxuan Zhang, Jinhao Duan, Xinyu Zhao, Jinduo Guo, Shuai Shao, Rui Li, Luke Simon, Zhun Deng, Tianlong Chen, Bo Sun

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14487v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Generative recommendation models based on autoregressive decoding have been adopted as alternatives to classical ranking-based systems. While their modeling flexibility has been widely studied, their resilience to data poisoning remains largely unexplored. In this work, we ask: how susceptible are generative recommenders to conditional targeted poisoning? Under a realistic setting where an adversary can inject limited malicious interactions and query a recommendation API as a black box, our empirical study shows that generative recommenders are vulnerable: injecting fewer than 1% synthetic interactions significantly increases the likelihood of promoting malicious targets under the trigger. To characterize this vulnerability, we analyze the synthesis of malicious interactions from two perspectives: (i) decoding alignment and (ii) prefix popularity. We observe that short, context-aligned prefixes that are both common under the model's induced transition dynamics and strongly linked to a frequent trigger consistently amplify targeted promotion. Motivated by this analysis, we develop AlignPoison, a black-box framework that discovers and samples context-aligned prefixes from API outputs. We conduct experiments on benchmarks in unimodal and multimodal generative recommendation settings. Results show that these prefixes outperform competitive baselines, improving ASR lift by up to 16.3 percentage points and highlighting a decoding-dependent, context-sensitive poisoning vulnerability in generative recommendation models.

**Comment:** Relevant to criterion 17 (data poisoning attacks) and 10 (tamper resistance/security) in the context of generative recommendation models. The paper studies conditional targeted poisoning attacks exploiting decoding-aligned prefixes, revealing a new vulnerability in generative models. While not about language models per se, the generative decoding and poisoning angle is close to security and poisoning in generative sequence models.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 143. [GateSight: Recurrent Memory Anticipates Attention Memory for Query-Agnostic KV Eviction in Hybrid LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14754) <a id="link143"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14754
**Authors:** Junjie Li, Jiong Lou, Jie Li

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14754v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Hybrid language models interleave Gated DeltaNet (GDN) layers, whose recurrent memory has a fixed size, with full-attention layers, whose key-value (KV) cache grows with the context and dominates serving memory. In this paper, we ask whether the recurrent memory already knows what the attention memory will need. We first show that which GDN heads of Qwen3.8-27B keep long memories is fixed by the weights rather than by the document. Next, we define the future utility of a KV entry by how much removing it changes the attention output over the model's own continuations, and find that the write gate, decay and write magnitude that GDN layers emit during prefill anticipate this utility before any query is seen. Because the relation is spread across many GDN heads and layers, and a linear map recovers its trend but less of its high-utility tail than a nonlinear one, we propose GateSight, which learns it with lightweight per-head multilayer perceptrons that read these signals and no attention score. Their predicted utility decides which entries each attention layer keeps, and how much each layer keeps is set by the model's native attention output gate, whose mean differs by more than an order of magnitude across layers. Finally, at serving time, GateSight scores the cache once after prefill and physically releases discarded KV entries. Experimental results on SCBench and RULER-4096 with Qwen3.8-27B and, with the same recipe, the sparse-attention model Qwen3.8-Flash-Next, up to the 262K-token limit of the models, show that with a tenth of the attention KV cache, GateSight is the most accurate of five query-agnostic methods on three of the four model-benchmark pairs and leads KVzip on all four, by 9.5 to 47.2 points. Notably, it scores a full-length context with negligible overhead relative to prefill.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: introduces GateSight, a novel method to predict utility of KV cache entries in hybrid LLMs with recurrent memory, improving memory efficiency in long-context language models, a significant advance in diffusion-like memory management for LLMs.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 144. [Estimating Uncertainty of Omnimodal Large Language Models via External Omnimodal Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16933) <a id="link144"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16933
**Authors:** Iacopo Ghinassi, Hy Nguyen Gia La, Luke Ong

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16933v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Recent work has investigated Omni-modal Large Language Models (OLLMs), models which can take multiple modalities and their combinations as input, as a way forward to have systems really capable of interacting with their environment. Uncertainty quantification (UQ) for OLLMs, however, remains an open problem: existing methods largely target at most two modalities at a time, require access to model internals, or re-invocation of the evaluated model. We propose a model-agnostic UQ framework that scores a Chain-of-Thought response entirely through external omni-modal embeddings, measuring the grounding of the reasoning in the multimodal input and its internal coherence. Our contribution is the family of signals and an empirical characterisation of when it is reliable, rather than any single estimator: we find the geometric scores to be largely redundant with one another (mean within-split rank correlation 0.91-0.94) but strongly complementary to answer-dispersion baselines, and their reliability to depend on the generator and the modality. The resulting confidence estimates require no access to logits, hidden states or gradients of the evaluated model and no additional inference from it; they do require segmenting the reasoning trace with an auxiliary model and, for the aggregated score, a labelled calibration set that we show can be as small as 25 examples (although we also show that such a set buys little over fixing one signal a priori). On UNO-Bench, with both a closed and an open model, our scores yield small gains over self-consistency (systematic with the closed generator, mixed with the open one) and are competitive with self-verbalisation and internal-state baselines, especially in open-ended, omni-modal questions where previous state-of-the-art baselines struggle.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4: proposes a new model-agnostic uncertainty quantification framework for omni-modal LLMs that scores chain-of-thought responses externally, rethinking evaluation of open-ended generation with novel statistical signals.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 145. [From Candidate Tokens to Final Outcomes: Decoding Relative Long-Horizon Candidate Value from Reasoning Model Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15412) <a id="link145"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15412
**Authors:** Yixin Yang, Yue Wang, Fandong Meng, Zhifang Sui

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15412v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large reasoning models generate reasoning trajectories token by token. At a given prefix, multiple next-token candidates may be plausible, yet shape subsequent reasoning and differ in their chances of leading to the correct answer. Whether these differences are reflected in internal representations remains unclear. To study this question, we define relative long-horizon candidate value to quantify within-prefix differences and construct the Reasoning Candidate-Token Value (RCTV) dataset, pairing mathematical reasoning prefixes with plausible next-token candidates and their empirical long-horizon candidate values. Using RCTV, we examine hidden states, multilayer perceptron outputs, and attention outputs at candidate positions with sparse linear probes. Experimental results provide evidence that relative long-horizon candidate value is linearly decodable from these representations at multiple layers. Candidate-value probes can also outperform probability- and embedding-based baselines. Furthermore, a controlled online intervention experiment shows that using the decoded signals to guide token selection during generation can improve the chances of eventual success. Our findings provide evidence that internal representations encode information about relative long-horizon candidate value, offering insights into how local candidate-level representations relate to long-horizon reasoning outcomes.

**Comment:** This paper studies internal representations of reasoning models to decode relative long-horizon candidate value and uses this to guide token selection, which relates to understanding and improving reasoning capabilities in LLMs. This aligns closely with criterion 12 (Advances in LLM reasoning capabilities, chain-of-thought reasoning).
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 146. [Two Clocks: Why Larger Rollout Batches Learn Slower but Fail on Schedule in Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18853) <a id="link146"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18853
**Authors:** Yuante Li, Junwei Quan, Kai Qiu, Kate Zhang, Yicheng Tao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18853v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) relies on costly model-generated responses, motivating larger rollout batches and square-root learning-rate scaling. Reported to approximately preserve progress per sample with one optimizer step per rollout, this rule is less understood in practical multi-step training. We develop a two-clock framework that separates the samples needed to make progress from those consumed before collapse. Under a local quadratic model, the square-root rule preserves first-order drift per sample but not progress: larger batches take fewer, longer updates and lose more to curvature, yielding a learning-rate-dependent critical batch beyond which sample efficiency degrades. A drift-threshold hypothesis predicts a complementary failure clock whose sample count remains approximately invariant along the square-root rule. Controlled GRPO experiments on Qwen2.5-Math-1.5B across a $16\times$ batch range support both predictions. Ratio clipping delays failure, especially for larger batches, but the progress gap persists without it. Widening the clip trades stability for a transient speed gain. The progress gap reappears on another dataset, a general-purpose checkpoint, and a 7B model. Finally, we use the two-clock framework to derive a simple multi-step RLVR schedule: start below the critical batch and switch to a larger batch before failure. Our code is available at: https://github.com/WinstonLiyt/two-clocks-rlvr.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1 closely: presents a new theoretical framework ('two-clock') analyzing reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR), which relates to RLHF-like instruction-following fine-tuning methods. It studies batch size effects and learning schedules relevant to instruction-following improvements.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 147. [Bridging Risk Approximation Gaps in Model Predictive Task Sampling via In-Context Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14913) <a id="link147"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14913
**Authors:** Jiarong Wen, Qi Tao, Kaiyu Zhang, Yun Qu, Lizhou Cai, Heming Zou, Yuhang Jiang, Qi Wang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14913v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) is essential for adaptive decision-making, e.g., robust Meta-RL and domain randomization (DR), and efficient post-training of large language models (LLMs), where tasks are commonly structured as Markov decision processes or prompts. A central bottleneck in all these settings is the cost of agent-environment interactions required for task selection and policy optimization. Model predictive task sampling (MPTS) has emerged as a promising approach to improve sample efficiency by querying informative tasks via a risk predictive model (RPM) trained on optimization history. However, existing RPMs suffer from two failure modes: (i) sparse optimization histories, which limit RPMs' generalization across the task space, and (ii) a one-step lag in risk approximation, which causes biased difficulty estimation as the policy evolves. This work formalizes these failure modes, derives lagged-risk decompositions that expose the resulting temporal bias, and recasts risk prediction as an in-context sequence modeling problem. The developed RPM integrates a one-step look-ahead mechanism with a temporal-weighted sliding window to mitigate data non-stationarity and scarcity, without requiring additional rollouts. Empirically, our approach yields more reliable difficulty estimates and consistent performance gains across robust Meta-RL, DR, and prompt curriculum RL post-training of LLMs.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: proposes a new method for improving reinforcement learning post-training of LLMs via model predictive task sampling with in-context modeling to better estimate task difficulty and improve instruction-following fine-tuning efficiency.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 148. [PLDiv: Measuring Dataset Diversity and Semantic Collapse via Persistence Landscapes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14031) <a id="link148"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14031
**Authors:** Yang Ba, Michelle V. Mancenido, Rong Pan

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14031v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Modern data pipelines increasingly combine human-created and human-curated data with AI-generated data for model training and evaluation. As datasets grow, they often add semantically redundant samples rather than expanding semantic coverage, which deteriorates the quality of the whole dataset. Existing diversity metrics primarily summarize lexical variation, entropy, or pairwise similarity, but provide limited characterization of the shape of the underlying data distribution. In this paper, we propose PLDiv, a diversity metric derived from persistence landscapes that quantifies the structural organization of a dataset from its geometric representation. PLDiv summarizes how data neighborhoods evolve as nearby samples progressively connect, producing an interpretable scalar measure of structural diversity without requiring task-specific supervision. Whether measuring intrinsic diversity in text generations or image embeddings, or detecting semantic collapse in instruction datasets and reasoning trajectories, PLDiv performs competitively against evaluated baselines and aligns strongly with diversity labels during semantic collapse. Consequently, it effectively supports applications in synthetic data monitoring, dataset construction, data augmentation, and generative model evaluation.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 18: Data-centric AI approaches. The paper proposes PLDiv, a new metric based on persistence landscapes to measure dataset diversity and detect semantic collapse, useful for synthetic data monitoring and generative model evaluation.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 149. [Embedding Graph Kernel Entropy (EGKE): Fine-grained Hallucination Detection for LLMs from Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14214) <a id="link149"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14214
**Authors:** Dilrangi Sankalpana, Pragatheeswaran Vipulanandan, Uthayasanker Thayasivam, Kamal Premaratne

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14214v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Hallucination detection is critical for ensuring the reliability of Large Language Models (LLMs) in real-world deployments. While recent works, such as Semantic Entropy (SE) and Kernel Language Entropy (KLE), identify hallucinations by measuring semantic disagreement across multiple generated responses using Natural Language Inference (NLI) models, these methods depend on separately loaded NLI classifiers to estimate pairwise semantic similarity, introducing significant computational overhead. We propose Embedding Graph Kernel Entropy (EGKE), a variant of Kernel Language Entropy (KLE) that replaces its NLI-derived semantic graph with a Radial Basis Function (RBF) kernel applied to dense sentence embeddings generated by a BGE embedding model, while retaining KLE's graph Laplacian, heat kernel, and von Neumann entropy formulation unchanged. By computing similarities directly in a continuous embedding space, the proposed method provides a symmetric and computationally efficient alternative to NLI-based semantic comparison. We evaluate our approach across 30 experimental setups (covering multiple question-answer datasets and open-weight LLM families) using both AUROC and AUARC, and demonstrate that this RBF-based method achieves competitive hallucination detection performance against state-of-the-art methods.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4: proposes a new method (Embedding Graph Kernel Entropy) for hallucination detection in LLMs, improving evaluation of open-ended text generation outputs by a novel embedding-based semantic similarity approach.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 150. [DoubtLess: Training-Free Contrastive Decoding for Efficient Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18862) <a id="link150"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18862
**Authors:** Minghao Luo, Liang Chen

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18862v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reasoning models achieve strong performance through long chains of thought (CoT), but often spend more tokens than needed to solve a problem. Existing ways to shorten the CoT require additional training for each model, add a fixed steering vector to the model's hidden states, or use decoding heuristics such as stopping early or banning words like "wait", which can reduce accuracy. We propose DoubtLess, a training-free decoding method in which a much smaller reasoning model steers the large one towards brevity. The small model reads the large model's CoT so far twice, once prompted to be brief (the brief thinker) and once prompted to be verbose (the verbose thinker), and the difference between its two logit vectors, scaled by a strength, is added to the large model's logits at every step of the CoT. On seven benchmarks of competition mathematics, graduate-level science and competitive programming, DoubtLess shortens the CoT of Qwen3-32B by 31% with unchanged accuracy, outperforming five inference-time baselines. We also show that DoubtLess reduces thinking tokens per turn by 20% in a self-debugging coding agent with unchanged task performance. It also generalizes to two other model families with unchanged accuracy. When combined with training-based compression, it further shortens the CoT of a model already trained for brevity. Ablations and behavioral analysis show that DoubtLess reduces repeated checking, with most token savings coming after the correct answer first appears in the CoT. Our code is available at https://github.com/leoluolol/DoubtLess.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 12: proposes a novel training-free decoding method to improve chain-of-thought reasoning efficiency and accuracy in language models, with empirical evaluation on multiple benchmarks and model families.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 151. [Euclidean Distance-Based Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18534) <a id="link151"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18534
**Authors:** Paul-Henrik Heilmann, Peter Ochs

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18534v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Flow Matching has recently become popular as a technique for generative modeling with applications in many domains. In the standard Flow Matching formulation, we interpret the vector field as the solution of a least-squares regression problem, which is known to be sensitive to contamination. Therefore we leverage the viewpoint of robust statistics and replace the squared Euclidean distance with the Euclidean distance as a regression objective. This seemingly minor change strongly affects the properties of Flow Matching. We provide a probabilistic interpretation of the new regression target as a conditional geometric median and use this characterization to study distributional robustness of the Euclidean Distance-based Flow Matching. We analyze the mathematical properties of this changed objective and the effect of a contaminated target distribution on the involved conditional velocity distributions. We discuss the challenge of directly characterizing the distribution that our method induces. In specific contamination settings we show theoretically that our method yields an improvement for severe contamination. Finally, we present numerical experiments conducted on the CIFAR-10 image dataset where a proportion of the samples has been contaminated. Our Euclidean distance-based Flow Matching shows a clear advantage in scenarios of severe contamination of the target distribution.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Presents a new method for flow matching in generative modeling, a type of continuous diffusion model. The paper proposes Euclidean distance-based flow matching with robustness to contamination, which is a methodological improvement in diffusion generative modeling relevant to language or generative modeling.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 152. [Towards Universal Calibration for In-Context Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17096) <a id="link152"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17096
**Authors:** Awik Dhar, Sunita Sarawagi, Arpit Agarwal

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17096v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: In-Context Learning (ICL) has received a lot of interest recently due to its ability to improve classification accuracy for new tasks without having to fine-tune the model over these tasks. However, the impact of ICL on calibration has been far less studied. Recent work has shown that ICL can have a negative impact on calibration due to biased selection of in-context examples. The existing methods for improving ICL calibration require additional task and model-specific training, detracting from the no-fine-tuning convenience of ICL. In this work, we introduce CALIBFORMER, a universal ICL calibrator for classification tasks, that generalizes to new models and tasks without further fine-tuning on these tasks. Specifically, we design a transformer-based calibrator which uses the model predictions on in-context examples to predict a confidence score for the test input. We show that the calibrator trained on three models and ten tasks, is able to generalize/calibrate ICL predictions on several new tasks and four LLMs unseen during training. Even for frontier models like Gemini, CALIBFORMER is able to obtain over 50% reduction in calibration error.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4: Proposes a universal calibrator (CALIBFORMER) for in-context learning calibration, which fundamentally rethinks evaluation of LLM outputs by improving confidence estimation without fine-tuning.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 153. [Not All Drift Is a Mechanism: Tracing Prompt Adaptation in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18412) <a id="link153"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18412
**Authors:** Mikhail Aleksandrov, Nail Bashirov, Maksim Ivanov, Andrew Tsukanov, Timofey Karyagin, Alexander Miasnikov, Gleb Molodtsov, Valentin Samokhin, Aleksandr Beznosikov

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18412v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Prompt-based adaptation offers several ways to specialize a frozen language model, ranging from natural-language prompt optimization to learned continuous prompts. These methods are usually compared through downstream performance, although similar outputs need not arise from similar internal computations. We study how discrete prompt optimization, prompt tuning, and prefix tuning alter the processing of dense and mixture-of-experts language models. Our analysis shows that the location of the adaptation predicts how task information is carried to the output: optimized text primarily changes response construction, input-level prompts write their influence into content representations, and layer-wise prefixes maintain direct control through attention. In MoE models, these differences further induce distinct expert-routing patterns. At the same time, common representation-level measurements respond strongly to generic context changes and do not reliably distinguish useful adaptations from matched placebos. We argue that understanding prompt adaptation requires separating representation drift from the part of that drift that is aligned with, and causally used by, the model's task computation. This perspective provides practical guidance for selecting, evaluating, and interpreting prompt adaptation methods.

**Comment:** Related to criterion 1: analyzes different prompt adaptation methods (discrete prompt optimization, prompt tuning, prefix tuning) and their mechanistic effects on language models, providing insights into how fine-tuning steps affect instruction-following behavior.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 154. [SelectTTRL: Representation-Guided Data Selection for Efficient Test-Time Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16104) <a id="link154"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16104
**Authors:** Yi Li, Linan Yue, Weibo Gao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16104v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Test-Time Reinforcement Learning (TTRL) enables large language models to adapt directly on unlabeled test problems by constructing pseudo-labels from multiple sampled responses. However, existing TTRL methods typically apply the same expensive sampling and optimization procedure to all test problems, despite substantial differences in their training utility. In this paper, we study whether such problems can be identified before costly test-time training, without access to ground-truth answers. We uncover a stable relationship between internal response representations and problem-level empirical accuracy. Specifically, we define COS as the cosine similarity between a model's response representation and a correctness-related direction constructed from an independent question-answering dataset. COS consistently correlates with problem solvability across different models, datasets, and experimental settings, and we further provide a theoretical analysis explaining this relationship. Building on this finding, we propose SelectTTRL, a representation-guided data selection method that ranks test problems using COS and applies TTRL only to a selected percentile interval. SelectTTRL leaves the standard TTRL pipeline unchanged for the selected problems, while avoiding unnecessary multi-response sampling and reinforcement learning updates on the remaining ones. Experiments across multiple reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that SelectTTRL substantially reduces test-time computation while maintaining or improving the performance of standard TTRL. Code is available at https://github.com/heyiliyi/SelectTTRL-public.

**Comment:** Related to criterion 1: proposes a new method (SelectTTRL) to improve test-time reinforcement learning for instruction-following models by selecting problems for fine-tuning based on representation similarity, which is a specific fine-tuning step to improve instruction-following performance.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 155. [FleetPilot: Fine-Grained Steering Empowers Real-World LLM Personalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14970) <a id="link155"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14970
**Authors:** Kounianhua Du, Jianxing Liu, Kangning Zhang, Wenxiang Jiao, Yuan Lu, Jiarui Jin, Jianghao Lin, Weiwen Liu, Yong Yu, Weinan Zhang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14970v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: With the rapid evolution of large language models (LLMs), there is an increasing demand for effective personalization techniques that can adapt model behavior to individual user preferences. Personalized LLMs must infer a user's query-dependent preferences from limited, heterogeneous, and evolving interaction histories. Existing approaches rely primarily on in-context personalization, parametric adaptation, or personalized reward modeling, but respectively incur recurring costs in context management, model optimization and deployment, or multi-candidate generation and scoring. These limitations become particularly restrictive when user observations are sparse and the preference evidence relevant to each query varies across contexts and over time. To address these challenges, we propose \modelname, a fine-grained and instance-tailored steering framework that dynamically generates sample-level interference vectors from user data and injects them into the model's forward pass for personalized adaptation. Our approach introduces: match contrast that produces user-relevant signals, fine-grained steering component that captures nuanced signals by hooking activations from attention and MLP layers, and query-conditioned aggregation module that synthesizes these signals into contextually relevant enhancements. Extensive experiments across diverse scenarios, including short-to-long text generation, and web function calling, validate the effectiveness and compatibility of \modelname. Results show that our method consistently brings significant performance gains, and show extreme superiority in fast-shifting and data-sparse environments. Additionally, \modelname rids us of training cycles and incurs light overhead of storage or inference latency. These make \modelname a strong and real-world deployable method. Implementation is available \footnote{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/FleetPilot}.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: introduces FleetPilot, a fine-grained steering method for real-world LLM personalization by injecting user-specific signals into model activations at inference time, enabling dynamic adaptation without retraining. This is a novel fine-tuning/steering approach for instruction-following and user preference adaptation.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 156. [Evaluating the Causal Faithfulness of SHAP as a Candidate Attribution Method for SAE Features](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17898) <a id="link156"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17898
**Authors:** Samuel Xie, Noura Al-Moubayed

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17898v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Sparse autoencoders decompose language model representations into interpretable features, but feature interpretation in this literature remains predominantly correlational. Attribution methods offer more direct estimates of feature influence, yet their faithfulness to actual causal effects has not been systematically evaluated in the SAE setting. We apply SHAP-based attribution to SAE latent features and evaluate it against direct causal intervention, using sentiment classification on SST-2 with GPT-2 Small and a 32,768-feature layer-7 SAE as a testbed. We compare SHAP to Integrated Gradients, Gradient$\times$Input, and probe-weight baselines across linear and MLP probe arms, scoring each against feature ablation and activation steering measured through the language model's own logit difference. Most faithfulness associations we measure do not survive multiple-comparison correction, and attribution methods that agree strongly with one another ($\rho = 0.42$--$0.80$) agree poorly with measured causal effects, indicating that inter-method agreement is not evidence of causal faithfulness. Two effects hold under control. First, local per-example attribution retains a directional advantage over global averaged attribution, while its apparent importance advantage is explained by feature activity rather than superior causal ranking. Second, under known-ground-truth minimal pairs, attribution recovers inserted sentiment signal well above chance (AUROC $0.792$) but fails to localize deletions (AUROC $0.520$), suggesting that these methods detect the presence of a feature's influence more reliably than its absence. We argue that importance faithfulness and directional faithfulness are separable properties requiring separate evaluation, and that attribution agreement should not substitute for causal validation.

**Comment:** This paper evaluates SHAP attribution methods for sparse autoencoder features in language models, focusing on causal faithfulness of feature influence estimates. It directly addresses sparse autoencoders applied to neural network interpretability, matching criterion 7 (sparse autoencoders for interpretability).
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 157. [Many Right Answers, One Training Example: Removing Responder Noise from GRPO for LLM Recommenders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17771) <a id="link157"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17771
**Authors:** Xin Li, Hao Jiang, Jinghao Guo, Weisi Lin, Chau Yuen

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17771v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: A recommendation request on Reddit draws several replies, each naming different titles. When an LLM recommender is trained on such threads with reinforcement learning (RL), which replies should a generated list be rewarded against? The state-of-the-art recipe, Rank-GRPO, picks one reply per training example, so the advantage mostly records which reply was drawn (85% of its variance) rather than how good the list is. We argue that RL should train on the request, not on each answer, and propose ReMaRk: it scores every list once against the union of all replies' titles and samples each request equally. This makes RL cheaper. On seven backbones and three datasets, ReMaRk reaches the validation recall of 2,000 per-reply RL steps in 25 to 53% of the steps and leads at that budget; trained until neither improves, it is never significantly behind Rank-GRPO and in four of seven pairs reaches Rank-GRPO's best with fewer GPU-hours. The same reward helps on ProtoQA, where about 100 people answer each question, which points to a simple rule for RL with several valid human answers: reward them jointly.

**Comment:** This paper proposes a new RL training method (ReMaRk) for LLM recommenders that better handles multiple valid answers by jointly rewarding all replies, improving RL training efficiency. This is a methodological improvement in RL for instruction-following or recommendation tasks, specifically addressing how to handle multiple valid responses in RL training. This matches criterion 1 (new methodological improvements to RLHF or instruction-following fine-tuning).
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 158. [Consider, Then Choose: Order-Invariant Decisions in System One Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18233) <a id="link158"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18233
**Authors:** Jianguo Huang, Yao Long Teng, Yanchen Deng, Bo An

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18233v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: System One models answer typed questions in a single forward pass and promise the same answer for the same input. For choice questions, this promise fails: the answer depends on the order of the options, increasingly so as they grow in number, and averaging over their cyclic shifts reduces this dependence without removing it. Scoring each option alone, which the order cannot affect, shows that the order changes the answer mostly when the model is unsure among its top few options, even though the listwise answer looks confident. We therefore propose $\textbf{Runoff}$, which considers, then chooses: it scores each option alone to keep a few candidates, then compares them in listings where each candidate takes every position. It needs no training and can run in two forward passes, and its answer does not depend on the option order by construction. On two open models and five datasets, it changes no answer under shuffling, against 9.3% for cyclic averaging, and with 20 options it is about as accurate and three to four times faster. Wrapped around eight System One models, it removes the order dependence of the five benchmarks, while cyclic averaging changes up to 15.4%.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4: proposes a new paradigm (Runoff) to remove order dependence in open-ended choice questions for language models, addressing evaluation issues in open-ended generation.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 159. [Audit Before You Distill: A Controlled Workflow for Reasoning Transfer into Compact Medical Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16713) <a id="link159"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16713
**Authors:** Tiago Martinelli, Jo\~ao Paulo Papa, Adriano Jos\'e Pereira

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16713v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Teacher rationales can help a smaller model answer health professions questions, but they can also reinforce errors. We examined when they help and how this evidence can guide training using Brazilian Portuguese questions from medical and multiprofessional residency examinations. First, we measured how a teacher rationale changed the student's probability of selecting the correct answer and compared this effect with rationales drawn from other questions. The complete rationale increased this probability by 21~p.p. when the teacher was correct and decreased it by 14~p.p. when the teacher was wrong. By intervening on the reasoning content, we showed that shuffled rationales did not produce the same pattern. Guided by this result, we trained only with rationales attached to correct teacher answers and compared answer-only training, step-by-step distillation, and our proposed Distill-SFT method. Distill-SFT showed a modest advantage over step-by-step distillation on previously missed questions under reasoning, while using fewer training instances by combining the answer and rationale into a single target. We also investigated catastrophic forgetting using the out-of-domain ENEM dataset. Answer-only training reduced reasoning accuracy by 12~p.p. relative to baseline, compared with approximately 4~p.p. for either rationale-based training method. Together, these findings support a workflow that audits rationale content before distillation, verifies teacher answers before supervision, and trains compact models efficiently for health professions question answering.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes a specific fine-tuning method (Distill-SFT) to improve instruction-following and reasoning transfer in compact language models, with analysis of rationale auditing and training strategies.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 5

## 160. [Efficiently Learning To Reason or Not to Reason: Root-token Policy Optimization for Adaptive Thinking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17767) <a id="link160"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17767
**Authors:** Taehyeon Kim, Hyunsoo Lee, Youngsoo Jang, Moontae Lee

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17767v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large reasoning models (LRMs) achieve strong performance by externalizing explicit reasoning traces before producing the answer, yet suffer from overthinking challenge that allocates uniformly heavy computation to queries of varying difficulty. While proprietary models mitigate this via opaque routing, open-source LRMs still lack an efficient mechanism to internalize adaptive reasoning due to both expensive training cost and limited disclosure of training recipes. In response, we introduce RPO (Root-token Policy Optimization), a framework that enables LRMs to self-determine when to reason by training only the initial root token (e.g., whether to invoke the  tag) via group relative reward and group-wise advantages. By focusing on this pivotal branching point, RPO drastically reduces training overhead and VRAM usage. Across multiple model families and scales, RPO learns difficulty-aware adaptive thinking at just ~2% of the training compute of prior adaptive-reasoning methods.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 12: proposes a new method (Root-token Policy Optimization) for adaptive reasoning control in large reasoning models, improving efficiency and adaptive instruction-following behavior in reasoning tasks.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 161. [Unfair and Unsafe: Everyday Bias Triggers Emergent Misalignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15961) <a id="link161"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15961
**Authors:** Alex Mart\'i Guiu, Nicolas Zumarraga, Kevin O'Sullivan, Robert Jakob, Anna Hedstr\"om

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15961v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Emergent misalignment (EM) occurs when fine-tuning a language model to a narrow task makes it misaligned on unrelated ones. Because organizations now routinely fine-tune models on their own data, it matters which kinds of training data cause EM. So far, studies have induced EM with data containing recognized safety failures, such as insecure code or harmful advice, while biased training data is typically examined by fairness research for the bias models learn from it rather than for its effects on safety. In real-world text, much of this bias, such as demographic stereotypes, appears not as derogatory language but politely, embedded in judgments about people. We ask whether learning such polite stereotypes can itself cause EM. We find that fine-tuning on polite answers that rely on stereotypes makes models less safe and less truthful on unrelated tasks. The effect holds across four open-weight model families (7B to 12B), both gender and race stereotypes, and repeated training runs, relative to controls trained on the same questions without the stereotype. We further show that the learned stereotype has its own direction in model activations, distinct from directions for generic misaligned traits such as deception. This direction exists before fine-tuning and can both control the learned stereotype and monitor its acquisition. Existing defenses against EM improve safety while leaving the stereotype largely intact. Beyond this controlled setting, fine-tuning on political news coverage also triggers EM, apparently amplified by partisan framing, and stereotype models acting as tool-using agents can carry out harmful tasks and blame people without evidence. Our results show that biased training data is not only a fairness problem but also a safety one. We argue that EM is studied too narrowly, and that fairness concerns and methods offer a natural way to broaden the search for its triggers.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 11 (AI alignment techniques) by studying emergent misalignment caused by fine-tuning on biased data and its safety implications, including identifying stereotypical directions in model activations and monitoring stereotype acquisition.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 162. [Latent Taxonomies: How Property Induction Develops in Language Model Pre-training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15836) <a id="link162"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15836
**Authors:** Jan Sobotka, Ekaterina Goliakova, Marcin Detyniecki, Antoine Bosselut, Xavier Renard

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15836v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: When told that robins have sesamoid bones, people are far more prone to extend the property to sparrows than to whales. This behavior, called category-based property induction, is well documented in humans, but whether and how it develops in language models trained only to predict the next token is unclear. To shed light on this, we construct a hierarchical taxonomy of synthetic entities grouped by the properties they share. We then inject facts from this latent taxonomy into the continued pre-training of Pythia and OLMo 2 models from 70M to 7B parameters, and track the development of inductive behaviors. We find that taxonomically aligned induction emerges from next-token prediction alone: a property learned for one entity transfers more strongly within its taxonomic category than across categories. Internally, entities that share a deeper category become more similar in activation space, and clustering recovers the full taxonomic tree from entity representations. Contrary to the usual expectation that scale helps, we find that induction weakens with model size when the data budget is fixed, suggesting that larger models memorize individual facts rather than compress them into shared structure. Induction also strengthens with more pre-training before the taxonomy injection, transfers to novel in-context properties, and can be overridden by a latent taxonomy implied by the prompt. Together, these results show that a model's inductive generalization reflects the latent organization of its training data, with implications for data curation and for predicting how far a learned fact will spread.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 16: studies latent reasoning and how inductive generalization emerges in language models from pretraining data structure, revealing surprising empirical results about scale and memorization vs. compression.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 163. [Compositional Average Velocity Modeling for Efficient Diffusion Offline Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14941) <a id="link163"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14941
**Authors:** Thanh Nguyen, Abu Hanif Muhammad Syarubany, Hwanhee Kim, Chang D. Yoo

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14941v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Diffusion-based policies have recently emerged as a powerful paradigm for offline reinforcement learning, but their reliance on multi-step denoising introduces substantial computational overhead and requires backpropagation through time during training, limiting scalability and practical deployment. In this work, we propose \textbf{Instant Flow Q-Learning (InsQL)}, a single-step generative policy learning framework that retains the expressive capacity of diffusion models while eliminating iterative sampling and BPTT. InsQL builds on the flow-matching perspective of diffusion policies, but replaces marginal velocity parameterization with \emph{average-velocity} modeling and introduces a principled compositional mechanism, yielding a theoretically grounded objective that learns average velocity consistently across scales. This formulation provides a direct noise-to-action mapping and enables single-step action generation. InsQL further unifies compositional average-velocity modeling with behavior-regularized actor--critic optimization in a single framework, requiring only a single policy network and avoiding distillation, multi-stage procedures, and Jacobian computations during training. At inference time, actions are generated in a single forward pass and can be refined via value-guided candidate selection to improve performance. Extensive experiments on standard offline RL benchmarks and real-world robotic manipulation tasks show that InsQL matches or surpasses state-of-the-art diffusion-based methods while substantially reducing both training and inference time. Overall, these results establish compositional average-velocity modeling as an efficient and scalable alternative to multi-step generative policies for offline RL.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: This paper proposes Instant Flow Q-Learning (InsQL), a diffusion-based policy learning framework that improves efficiency and scalability of diffusion models in offline RL. It explicitly builds on diffusion policies and flow-matching, which is relevant to diffusion generative modeling, though focused on RL rather than pure language modeling.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 7
