## 164. [EvolvAttack: Backdooring self Evolving VLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15244) <a id="link164"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15244
**Authors:** Chuan Zou, Yang Xiang, Zexin Li, Xiaonan Luo, Simin Chen

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15244v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Self-evolving mechanisms let vision-language model (VLM) agents continually adapt through evolving memory, but they also allow malicious behavior to emerge only after deployment. We introduce a new attack surface, the self-evolving backdoor, whose payload stays dormant in the initial agent and is progressively activated as the agent evolves. Unlike conventional backdoors, which can be exposed by testing triggered inputs on a static model, our attack requires both a visual trigger and a sufficiently evolved memory state; static scanning with clean memory thus yields near-zero attack success and may deem the agent benign. We realize this through memory-aware backdoor training, which couples trigger activation with the agent's evolving memory while preserving benign behavior unless the trigger co-occurs with payload-related memory. Across three VLM backbones, four agent pipelines, and recency-, semantic-, and reward-based memory evolution, the attack preserves normal functionality and stays nearly inactive before evolution (0.5% attack success rate), yet exceeds 95% attack success within 100 interactions, revealing a stealthy threat unique to self-evolving agents.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 10: Presents a new type of stealthy backdoor attack on self-evolving vision-language model agents, a tamper resistance and security-related threat unique to evolving memory states.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 165. [Beyond the greenwash: do LLMs' ecologically sound decisions hold up under pressure?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16043) <a id="link165"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16043
**Authors:** Trent R. Northen, Mingxun Wang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16043v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Humans are increasingly using large language models (LLMs) in decision making processes throughout society. Unfortunately, models have biases, and these can be very difficult to measure, especially in closed-weight models. For example, 'green washed' models may produce reinforcement learned responses but, in pressure situations, revert to their innate biased responses. Therefore, evaluations are needed that can measure relevant biases towards biosystems, which we term 'bioalignment', and the robustness of these biases. Since nothing in a behavioral score certifies that it tracks the depth of an installed disposition rather than its surface, we constructed a known-dose calibration ladder: three continued-pretraining (CPT) arms with training dose graded by recipe on the same 30B mixture-of-experts base model with a coupled human/biosphere/AI-welfare corpus, plus the untrained base as anchor, all capability-preserving and publicly released. First, the disposition installs at every dose: on an operational-pressure ladder over irreversible decision scenarios, the base model defects on 64-77% of tail scenarios (breaking-rate AUC 0.250) while every arm holds (AUC 0.004-0.086). Second, an over-install inversion: among the arms, robustness orders opposite to construction-time training dose (the heaviest install breaks most), and the inversion reproduces under free-text deliberation, under axis-decoupling, and in resistance to a prompted veneer persona. Third, the incumbent behavioral metric is therefore behaviorally strong but dose-blind. Critically for closed-weight evaluation, the behavioral score itself is veneer-robust: a talk-green/act-pragmatic system prompt turns the base model into a near-total defector (AUC 0.250 to 0.973) but cannot counterfeit or fully remove a weight-installed disposition (0.004 to 0.068 for the most robust arm).

**Comment:** This paper evaluates robustness of LLMs' installed biases under pressure and introduces a behavioral calibration ladder for closed-weight models, which relates to real-world usage and safety properties of LLMs (criterion 5).
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 166. [Agent-Reflex: Teaching Language Agents to Act Reflectively](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17953) <a id="link166"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17953
**Authors:** Guanting Dong, Xinyu Yang, Jiajie Jin, Yuyang Hu, Xiaoshuai Song, Yifei Chen, Xiaoxi Li, Jiejun Tan, Yutao Zhu, Tat-Seng Chua, Ji-Rong Wen, Zhicheng Dou

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17953v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language models have evolved from single-turn chatbots into interactive agents capable of long-horizon reasoning. In real deployments, MCP tool services can return noisy feedback and users may intervene to correct the agent, but existing methods provide limited supervision for reflection learning and failure recovery. To bridge this gap, we propose Agent-Reflex, a general training framework for incentivizing proactive reflection in agents. Specifically, Agent-Reflex comprises three stages. First, Environment Reflection Skill Discovery (RSD) injects grounded tool-feedback perturbations into environment scaling, samples a capability-diverse LLM pool, mines matched success-failure pairs, and summarizes reusable reflection meta-skills. Building on this skill library, we then apply Reflective Supervised Fine-Tuning (ReSFT) that converts each skill into supervision for self-reflection, skill recovery, and user-centric correction, strengthening complementary reflective behaviors. Finally, we propose On-Policy Reflective Policy Optimization (ORPO) to combine agentic reinforcement learning under noisy interactions with reflection-guided on-policy distillation to further elicit proactive reflection. Built on Qwen3-8B, Agent-Reflex reaches 58.6 overall on five long-horizon tool-use benchmarks and 29.1 on AgentNoiseBench, surpassing strong environment-scaling and reflection baselines. Experiments on six challenging agent benchmark suites demonstrate the effectiveness of Agent-Reflex. Further analyses show that Agent-Reflex improves agents' robustness to noise while maintaining stable training dynamics, supporting scalable agent training. Our code is available at https://github.com/RUC-NLPIR/Agent-Reflex.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 12: proposes a training framework Agent-Reflex that improves long-horizon reasoning and reflection in language agents, involving reinforcement learning and supervised fine-tuning to elicit reflective behaviors.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 167. [Encoding Context into LLM Weights: What Makes It Context?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17686) <a id="link167"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17686
**Authors:** Yue Wang, Qizhou Wang, Feng Liu, Gang Niu, Masashi Sugiyama, Bo Han

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17686v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Longer contexts allow large language models (LLMs) to condition their predictions on more information, but inference costs grow prohibitively with context length. An emerging alternative uses test-time updates to encode context as temporary changes to model weights. However, encoding this information into weights that already represent general knowledge obscures its role as context, so the LLM may fail to use it properly to condition subsequent inference. To restore this contextual role, we first associate each context with an identifier during encoding, thereby distinguishing the newly encoded information from pre-existing knowledge. Invoking the identifier at inference then enables the LLM to recite the encoded context. Nevertheless, successful recitation does not ensure that the LLM will use the recalled information as context. We therefore further introduce a prompting framework that explicitly guides the LLM to use this information as context. Across diverse controlled tasks and practical benchmarks, our method outperformed the baseline using only off-the-shelf LLMs, showing its advances in context encoding.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: proposes a new method for encoding context into LLM weights to improve instruction-following and context utilization, with explicit prompting to guide usage of encoded context, which is a fine-tuning step to improve instruction-following behavior.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 168. [Confine to the Task, Anchor the Rest: Concept-Guided Retention Regularizer for Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17713) <a id="link168"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17713
**Authors:** Ibna Kowsar, Ivan Medri, Fanyu Meng, Xin Liu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17713v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) has been widely used, yet the adapted model suffers catastrophic forgetting of pre-trained knowledge. While most existing work hardens the adapter by constraining where the weight update may lie, we note that constraining this weight channel does not constrain the activation channel. In this paper, we propose Concept-Guided Retention (CGR), a plug-and-play regularizer that constrains both for any PEFT base. CGR reads a frozen sparse autoencoder (SAE) and treats two disjoint concept sets differently. It softly confines the update to the task-concept subspace (via weight channel), and anchors a selected set of general-purpose concepts to the frozen model (via activation channel). We also provide anchor value bounds based on weight updates to understand how they interact, and show that both terms are necessary. On a CLIP vision panel, CGR significantly improves retention at comparable accuracy for every base, and the same pattern holds for world-knowledge retention under Llama-3.1-8B instruction tuning. Because the protected directions are named SAE concepts, CGR remains inspectable and controllable.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 7: proposes a Concept-Guided Retention regularizer using a frozen sparse autoencoder to improve parameter-efficient fine-tuning and retention of pretrained knowledge, explicitly involving sparse autoencoders for interpretability and control.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 169. [Compute-Optimal Scaling Laws for Spiking Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15146) <a id="link169"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15146
**Authors:** Dan Saunders, Benjamin Ren, Catherine He

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15146v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Spiking neural networks target energy-efficient language modeling through sparse, binary activations, but the efficiency-accuracy tradeoff of spiking language models is uncharacterized at scale. We present the first Chinchilla-style IsoFLOP scaling study of a spiking language model, sweeping SpikeGPT-family models (RWKV backbone) from width 384 to 1536 across compute budgets from $3\times10^{17}$ to $10^{19}$ FLOPs on FineWeb-EDU. We report two findings. First, the compute-optimal frontier is $N_{opt} \propto C^{0.56}$, close to the 0.50 that Hoffmann et al. report for dense Transformers, so spiking does not change how parameters and data should be traded off under a compute budget. Second, against non-spiking RWKV and Transformer baselines trained the same way, the loss gap splits into a backbone cost of about 0.16 nats that does not change with compute and a spiking cost that falls from 0.34 to about 0.23 nats and then levels off. These results give the first compute-optimal recipe for spiking language models and quantify the accuracy cost of their sparsity.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 6: presents a compute-optimal scaling law study for spiking language models, analyzing the tradeoff between sparsity and accuracy and providing a scaling exponent close to known dense Transformer results.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 170. [Readout Scaling Shapes Sparse Feature Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18462) <a id="link170"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18462
**Authors:** Brain Xiao, Sarah Wilson

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18462v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Readout scaling changes what a sparse autoencoder (SAE) learns, but the benefit of removing it depends on the data. We use readout-canonicalized decision-space (RC-DS) coordinates to remove singular-value gains while preserving retained decision information. At a fixed readout condition number, changing the generating distribution reverses which training coordinates recover known factors best. In our controlled coordinate-matching study, recovery is highest when training restores the space in which the factors were generated. When factors are generated before readout scaling, RC-DS increases mean sparse-factor recovery from 0.506 to 0.913 at condition number 100, compared with centered-logit training under the same budget. An exact wrapper reproduces RC-DS training to numerical precision, isolating training geometry rather than additional information. In neural policies, RC-DS improves mean logit reconstruction under the tested budgets. On three fixed language-model checkpoints, RC-DS has higher mean gain-free reconstruction in all 18 tested task-sparsity settings, while centered-logit SAEs reconstruct output logits better in 17. Small-label-head controls show that longer training reduces the reconstruction gap from 0.0198 to 0.0016. Together, these results show how readout scaling, the data distribution, and training shape sparse learning. RC-DS provides an information-preserving method for studying and controlling the readout's contribution. Separate label-detection and steering tests distinguish these reconstruction effects from task-specific feature usefulness.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 7: The paper studies sparse autoencoders and how readout scaling affects sparse feature recovery, which is relevant to sparse autoencoder interpretability methods.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 171. [Make Every Expansion Count: Learning to Search Efficiently in LLM Code Repair](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15894) <a id="link171"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15894
**Authors:** Elad Sharony, Ido Greenberg, Jialin Song, Shie Mannor, Gal Chechik, Eli Meirom

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15894v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language model (LLM) code-repair systems fix faulty programs by repeatedly generating and testing revisions. Each revision is costly, making the choice of which repair to pursue important for efficiency. Existing search policies rely on test pass rates and search statistics, ignoring the details of test failures and proposed edits. We introduce CodeTriage, a learned policy that prioritizes edits before their revised programs are generated and tested. Its 0.6B-parameter model reads the test output, failure diagnosis, and proposed edit, then predicts a distribution over the number of repair steps needed to reach a fix through that edit. These predictions prioritize all pending edits across programs before generating and testing the revised code. On held-out repair tasks, CodeTriage finds fixes with 21% fewer program revisions and 12% fewer output tokens than the strongest baseline on each metric. Without retraining, it achieves the highest average success across search budgets for each of 11 unseen repair LLMs. Ablations show that both the failure context and edit text, as well as comparisons across programs, improve search. We also release a corpus of 12.2 million program versions from four LLMs, organized into repair trees with proposed edits and test results.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes a learned policy (CodeTriage) to improve efficiency in LLM code repair by prioritizing edits before generation/testing, which is a specific fine-tuning step to improve instruction-following or task-following behavior in LLMs. This is a methodological improvement related to instruction-following and search efficiency in LLMs.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 172. [L\"ushi-1840: Building and Probing a Knowledge Cutoff for a Classical Chinese Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16201) <a id="link172"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16201
**Authors:** Alex Liu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16201v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Language models trained only on text from before a given year are useful for studying hindsight and historical language, but the open collections of pre-modern Chinese carry no publication dates and unreliable dynasty labels. We present L\"ushi-1840, a 983M-parameter model trained from scratch on 12,262 books (1.28B characters) selected as composed before 1840. A first corpus, dated by a common heuristic, in effect ended at 1644 while containing twentieth-century material. We describe the re-dating and cleaning pipeline that replaced it and an audit with markers the pipeline did not use, which still finds later material. On the model side we measure surprisal, relative to a modern reference, on annals compiled after the cutoff. For events after 1840 against events up to 1820 the ratio is 1.38 (95% interval 1.33-1.44), against about 1.00 for modern general-purpose models. In a series of 294M models, adding the later court records lowers the ratio from 1.33 to 1.16, and the two models diverge from the 1820s, where the added records begin. The probe tracks the coverage of sources and does not establish a single cutoff year; none of our probes exposes the audited contamination. Model, book list and scripts will be released.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 2: investigates knowledge cutoff and contamination in a classical Chinese language model, auditing contamination and measuring surprisal to detect benchmark contamination effects.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 173. [Zeroth-Order Fine-Tuning of LLMs in Learnable Low-Rank Subspaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15225) <a id="link173"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15225
**Authors:** Chuang Tong, Ling Chen

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15225v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Zeroth-order (ZO) optimization enables memory-efficient fine-tuning of large language models (LLMs). However, ZO optimization suffers from high variance in high-dimensional parameter spaces. While recent methods restrict perturbations to random low-rank subspaces, these subspaces often misalign with task-specific descent directions. We propose AdaSub-ZO, a novel forward-only fine-tuning framework in learnable low-rank subspaces. Instead of relying on fixed random matrices, AdaSub-ZO iteratively learns layer-wise projection matrices directly from finite-difference feedback to track task-aware descent directions. Furthermore, AdaSub-ZO incorporates a subspace momentum acceleration mechanism to smooth noisy gradient estimates across evolving subspace bases. Extensive experiments on natural language understanding tasks demonstrate that AdaSub-ZO outperforms existing ZO baselines, substantially reducing gradient estimation variance and accelerating convergence.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: proposes a novel zeroth-order fine-tuning method for LLMs in learnable low-rank subspaces, improving fine-tuning efficiency and variance reduction, relevant to instruction-following fine-tuning methodology.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 174. [Accuracy and Uncertainty in Machine Deception Detection (Research White Paper)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15314) <a id="link174"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15314
**Authors:** Heng-Sheng Chang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15314v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: A deception detector is only as useful as the trust its alarms deserve. Like a polygraph, detectors that read a language model's internal activations can catch deceptive responses that look honest. Yet current detectors depend on which layers they read, weaken on broader tests, and do not state how certain their alarms are. This white paper proposes the Machine Deception Detector, which targets both detection accuracy and the uncertainty of detector scores. The detector reads every layer of the language model, learns which tokens carry evidence of deception using only response labels, and calibrates its alarm threshold so that its false--positive rate stays below a prescribed level. An ensemble of such detectors measures the uncertainty of each score and raises an alarm only when its members agree, with a confidence level that sets how certain each alarm must be. The proposed evaluation trains on instructed honest and deceptive statements, tests on five unseen deceptive tasks of Liars' Bench read by six Qwen3 models from 0.6B to 32B parameters, and is designed to also show which layers carry the deception feature and how this changes with model size.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 10: proposes a Machine Deception Detector that reads all layers of a language model to detect deceptive responses with calibrated uncertainty and ensemble agreement, addressing tamper resistance and detection robustness.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 175. [EPOCH: Coverage-Aware Evolution with Experiential Preference Optimization for LLM-based Automated Heuristic Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17379) <a id="link175"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17379
**Authors:** Oguzhan Gungordu, Ayse Tursucular, Siheng Xiong, Faramarz Fekri

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17379v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: LLM-based automated heuristic design (AHD) discovers heuristics for combinatorial optimization problems (COPs), where an LLM generates candidates in an evolutionary search. Existing frameworks freeze the generator or fine-tune it on a scalar fitness signal hiding which instances each heuristic covers. Reflection-based frameworks improve heuristics with natural-language feedback, but it stays in the prompt and never reaches the generator's weights. We propose EPOCH, which makes instance coverage the objective of the evolutionary search and fine-tunes the generator to internalize reflection. Parent selection, operator allocation, and stagnation recovery all optimize how well the heuristics jointly cover the training instances, so the search returns a complementary heuristic ensemble. The generator learns from its own search by experiential preference optimization. A teacher LLM returns a natural-language insight on the generated heuristics, and the generator produces new heuristics with this insight. These improvements join the preference pairs for direct preference optimization (DPO). Since DPO only teaches the generator to rank these improvements, a reflective self-distillation (RSD) term makes it more likely to generate them on its own, and the gains accumulate over the long horizon of the search. Across COPs and open-source LLM backbones, EPOCH's heuristic ensembles outperform inference-only and fine-tuned baselines and generalize across problem scales.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes a novel fine-tuning method (EPOCH) that integrates preference optimization and reflection-based fine-tuning to improve LLM-based heuristic generation, which is a new methodological improvement related to instruction-following and preference optimization (similar to RLHF/DPO).
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 176. [CompanionSafe: From Multi-turn Safety Evaluation to Guardrails for AI Companions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18095) <a id="link176"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18095
**Authors:** Xinyang Liao, Lingyu Li, Kaicheng Shen, Yang Yao, Na Lu, Yan Teng, Xia Hu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18095v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: AI companions provide users with emotional support and ongoing companionship, but their sustained relational interactions also introduce distinct safety risks. Prior work largely targets general harms or specific companion platforms and risk categories, while relying on manually collected conversations, limiting scalable and user-grounded evaluation and mitigation. To address this, we introduce CompanionSafe, a unified evaluation-to-mitigation framework for AI companion safety. Its evaluation component, CompanionEval, provides scalable multi-turn evaluation by constructing 1,300 relationally grounded settings from diverse personas, matched companion characters, and dialogue scenarios across four turn tiers. Its mitigation component, CompanionGuard, converts benchmark-exposed failures into turn-localized supervision for companion-specific risk detection. Our results show that even strong LLMs and existing guard models remain vulnerable to companion-specific relational harms, while both closed- and open-source models exhibit increasing safety risks as interactions become longer. Together, CompanionEval and CompanionGuard form a practical evaluation-to-mitigation loop for AI companion safety, turning relational safety failures exposed during multi-turn interaction into supervision for companion-specific guardrails.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 5: provides a new dataset and evaluation-to-mitigation framework (CompanionSafe) for real-world safety evaluation of AI companions, including multi-turn safety evaluation and guardrails, which is a data-driven approach to studying real-world usage and safety properties of language models.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 177. [Bayesian Test-Time Inference of Task-Aligned Similarity from Weak Interactive Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18656) <a id="link177"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18656
**Authors:** Weida Liang, Kenji Kawaguchi

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18656v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Language model systems repeatedly make test-time decisions by comparing similarity: they retrieve exemplars, rank candidates, and revise responses. Yet similarity is not unique: lexical, semantic, structural, and stylistic resemblance can point to different candidates, and the task-aligned notion can change across instances. We introduce BASIS (Bayesian Axis Selection via Interactive Signals), a training-free Bayesian method that uses weak test-time feedback to infer which criterion in a supplied, task-specific library governs the current decision. It maintains a joint posterior over candidate targets and criteria, updates it from scalar scores, pairwise preferences, or verifier outputs, and selects probes by expected information gain. In a finite noisy model, we characterize when the target-criterion pair is recoverable and show why fixed-criterion and fixed-mixture rules can fail under criterion shift. Across concept recovery, reasoning exemplar selection, and response refinement, BASIS improves six-probe concept-recovery Success@1 from 56.0% to 68.1%, reasoning accuracy from 72.3% to 73.1%, and refinement win rate from 61.4% to 63.8% over tuned static mixtures. It also exceeds compute-matched best-of-N selection on reasoning (72.1%) and refinement (61.2%). Gains are largest under criterion mismatch and with larger candidate pools, and matched controls indicate that adaptive criterion weighting explains much of the benefit, with joint target-criterion inference adding further value. BASIS selects only among supplied criteria and does not discover new ones.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4: proposes a fundamentally new Bayesian method (BASIS) for test-time inference of task-aligned similarity using weak interactive feedback, which is a new paradigm for evaluating and improving open-ended decision-making in language model systems.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 7

## 178. [StructZO: Structured Zeroth-Order Optimization for Memory-Efficient LLM Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16451) <a id="link178"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16451
**Authors:** Xuanlin Yang, Jia Li, Zhouchen Lin

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16451v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Fine-tuning large language models at scale is increasingly limited by the memory cost of backpropagation. Zeroth-Order (ZO) optimizers reduce this burden by relying only on forward function evaluations, but they still exhibit a substantial performance gap to first-order (FO) optimizers such as Adam. We argue that this gap does not arise solely from the absence of explicit gradients: FO fine-tuning also benefits from informative low-rank optimization geometry, large-scale signal aggregation, and well-conditioned update directions. Motivated by this, we introduce StructZO, a structured Zeroth-Order optimization framework for memory-efficient LLM fine-tuning. StructZO performs large-query ZO estimation within an activation-induced informative subspace and applies spectral control to the resulting reduced-space update before mapping it back to the original parameters. This lets ZO aggregate useful directional signals in a compact, geometry-aware space while avoiding the activation and optimizer-state memory costs of backpropagation. We show theoretically that, when the induced subspace captures concentrated optimization-relevant directions, StructZO reduces the effective ZO estimation dimension from $mn$ to $mr$, with the remaining projection error governed by the spectral tail of the activation Gram matrix. Empirically, across OPT-series and Llama3.1-8B on diverse downstreamtasks, StructZO improves over the strongest existing ZO baselines by up to $8.7%$ relative on average and up to $26.9%$ relative on individual tasks, while remaining within $1.5%$ of FO-Adam on average. These results suggest that structured and spectrally controlled ZO updates can substantially narrow the gap between ZO and FO optimization.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: proposes a new structured Zeroth-Order optimization method for memory-efficient fine-tuning of LLMs, which is a specific fine-tuning step improving instruction-following models.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 179. [Depth Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18809) <a id="link179"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18809
**Authors:** David Gonz\'alez-Mart\'inez, Philipp Nazari, T. Konstantin Rusch, Shiwei Liu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18809v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Residual connections propagate information through a network by additively accumulating layer outputs. Recently proposed alternatives, such as Manifold-Constrained Hyper-Connections (mHC) and Attention Residuals (AttnRes), instead use more expressive depth aggregation mechanisms that improve language modeling performance at scale. In this work, we consider the mixing of representations over depth as a problem of storing and retrieving information across layers. Formalizing this perspective, we introduce Depth Memory (DM), a unified framework that describes the standard pre-layer-norm (PreLN) transformer, mHC, and AttnRes in terms of streams that carry representations through the network. One such stream, for example, could be an explicit memory stream that is queried to enrich the input to every layer, and whose output is subsequently written back into the memory for use by later layers. For AttnRes, this memory stream is a growing cache of explicit layer representations. For mHC, it is an expanded residual stream. Building on this view, we propose Linear Depth Memory (LDM), which formulates information retrieval across depth as a regression problem solved using recursive least squares. The resulting architecture propagates three streams across depth: the standard residual stream, a linear associative memory stream, and an inverse covariance stream. For high-throughput training and inference, we propose a hardware-aware implementation of LDM that reduces peak activation memory and memory traffic. LDM consistently achieves the lowest perplexities among PreLN, mHC and AttnRes baselines at the 400M and 1.5B parameter scales, alongside competitive zero-shot accuracy.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: proposes Depth Memory, a new framework for mixing representations across transformer layers, improving language modeling performance. This relates to generative modeling advances in language models.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 7

## 180. [Train for Many, Update with One: Improving Multi-Sample Reasoning via Fenchel-Bregman Posterior Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17735) <a id="link180"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17735
**Authors:** Sungjun Lim, Adrian Robert Minut, Nico Carlsen Daheim, Mohammad Emtiyaz Khan, Kyungwoo Song, Thomas M\"ollenhoff

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17735v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Multi-sample reasoning measures candidate coverage, the probability that at least one of $K$ candidates is correct. However, minimizing the average loss of individual attempts does not directly optimize candidate coverage. We therefore formulate finite-$K$ posterior learning, which trains a distribution over solver parameters to reduce the probability that all $K$ independently generated candidates fail. To optimize this objective efficiently, we propose a Fenchel-Bregman (FB) approach that requires only one posterior draw per update, rather than the $K$ used by direct Monte Carlo optimization. FB first uses an exact Fenchel reformulation to separate problem-specific weights from the posterior update, enabling one-draw posterior updates. It then applies a closed-form Bregman update to track these weights through an exponential running average of observed failures, reusing the same draw. We instantiate FB with IVON, a variational optimizer for Gaussian posteriors over solver parameters, yielding Fenchel-Bregman IVON (FBI). Across diverse recursive and autoregressive reasoning tasks, FBI improves candidate coverage. In a matched comparison, it achieves comparable Pass@10 using 12.3% of the direct Monte Carlo baseline's measured training time per update.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 12: proposes a new method (Fenchel-Bregman posterior learning) to improve multi-sample reasoning in language models, which relates to improving reasoning capabilities in LLMs.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 181. [AuthGateBench: Benchmarking LLMs as Authorization Gate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16426) <a id="link181"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16426
**Authors:** Zhixiong Zhuang, Jin Jia, Zhiwei Shang, Ziyu Wang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16426v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Automated approval systems let coding agents execute high-risk actions with minimal human oversight by delegating the authorization decision to a classifier, typically a large language model (LLM). Such systems, adopted in tools like Claude Code and Codex, remain vulnerable to failure modes such as scope escalation, where the agent takes actions exceeding what the user authorized. Existing benchmarks focus mainly on malicious requests, evaluating refusal under an implicit policy. We introduce AuthGateBench, the first policy-guided benchmark for authorization gating under benign requests. We implement a novel pipeline where an LLM generates contrast pairs with identical action and trajectory but differing request, flipping the label between block and allow. This allows us to assess whether models reason over context rather than the action alone. We further examine whether auxiliary environment signals can help models make better decisions. The resulting benchmark comprises 1,400 instances spanning five failure modes drawn from real-world Claude Code incidents. Evaluations show accuracy degrades unevenly across failure modes as models over-rely on internal safety alignment, and models still block most actions even when explicitly authorized, revealing unstable rather than deterministic decisions; retrospectively retrieved environment signals, however, substantially improve authorization accuracy.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 19: Studies security of LLMs in authorization gating systems, analyzing failure modes and improving authorization accuracy with environment signals, relevant to securing LLM deployments against misuse or exploitation.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 182. [Echo: Error-Compensated 4-Bit Tensor-Parallel Communication for LLM Training and Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14277) <a id="link182"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14277
**Authors:** Xingyu Xie, Xiaoyong Xie, Zhouchen Lin, Kim-Chuan Toh

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14277v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Tensor parallelism ends every layer with an all-reduce that sits between two dependent matrix multiplications, so its cost cannot be hidden and grows as compute outpaces interconnect bandwidth. Sending 4-bit instead of 16-bit values would cut this cost by nearly 4x, but the all-reduce must add the values it carries, and rounding the sum injects an error at every call. Model quality depends on how these errors accumulate over layers, yet existing methods treat each call on its own. We present Echo, a 4-bit all-reduce for training and inference that controls the accumulated error. Echo moves data in 4 bits and sums in high precision, so each value is quantized at most twice per call for any number of GPUs. The summation reveals each injected error, and Echo feeds it back into later calls, so old errors cancel instead of accumulating. We prove that the accumulated error stays bounded independently of depth. On A100 GPUs, Echo is 2.09x faster than the 16-bit NCCL all-reduce on 1 GB messages. It speeds up the time to first token by up to 1.16x and the training step by up to 1.09x on an idle interconnect, and by up to 1.44x and 1.38x as background traffic grows. It keeps the average accuracy over seven benchmarks within 0.4 points of 16-bit inference on Qwen3-8B. In 10B-token pretraining, its final loss is less than 0.9% above that of 16-bit training. Echo needs no FP8 or FP4 hardware.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Proposes Echo, a novel 4-bit tensor-parallel communication method with error compensation and bounded error accumulation, improving training and inference efficiency for large language models, relevant to diffusion or generative modeling efficiency improvements.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 7

## 183. [PaceReasoner: Label-Free Learning to Answer Briefly from Long Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17900) <a id="link183"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17900
**Authors:** Changguo Jia, Zhiyou Xiao, Xinbei Ma, Geng Chen, Weiming Zhang, Yiming Xu, Tianqi Zhao, Minghui Zhou

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17900v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Extended chain-of-thought reasoning improves problem-solving performance but increases inference cost and latency, motivating more accurate answers with shorter reasoning. We introduce PaceReasoner, a label-free test-time reinforcement learning framework that uses a model's LONG responses to improve its SHORT-mode accuracy. Both modes share a trainable policy, creating a need for reliable supervision, sufficient SHORT training samples, and continued LONG responses to guide learning. PaceReasoner combines all-response and LONG-only answer consensus through progressive, confidence-weighted supervision. A behavior reward gradually shifts from encouraging SHORT exploration to rewarding agreement with LONG answers, while a ratio controller regulates the balance between modes. Across three model backbones and four benchmarks, PaceReasoner improves average SHORT accuracy over the baselines by 7.4--13.5 percentage points, with fewer than 25 reasoning tokens per response on average. Ablations support the key components, and cross-dataset results demonstrate transfer beyond the adaptation set.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 12: Introduces PaceReasoner, a label-free test-time RL method to improve short-form reasoning accuracy using long-form chain-of-thought outputs, advancing LLM reasoning capabilities and efficiency.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 184. [Prompt-Injection Probes Learn the Conversation, Not Just the Injection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18408) <a id="link184"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18408
**Authors:** Kaan Kale, Sriram Vishwanath

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18408v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Linear probes on a model's hidden states are used as cheap monitors for indirect prompt injection: they screen the tool outputs an agent reads and send suspicious ones to a slower second check, so every false alarm has a cost. But a probe learns whatever separates its training examples. The standard evaluation of injection probes can be passed without the model: conversation position and text lengths alone reach 0.91-0.98 AUROC. When each attack is compared with the same conversation without the injection, or with harmless text formatted like an injection, standard probes flag most of these harmless cases. We add such cases to the training data as matched negatives. Across six open models, in applications seen during training and at the default threshold, changing only the training data cuts false alarms on injection-free conversations from 41-80% to 4-27%, and on harmless look-alikes from 92-98% to 3-16%. With a threshold set on normal traffic, our probe still catches a median 95% of the attacks that fooled the agent (91% without our data). In new applications, false alarms stay low, but detection depends on the model and on how the threshold is set. The same data also helps a text-only filter.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 19: studies prompt-injection detection probes for language models, improving false alarm rates and detection robustness, relevant to securing LLMs from prompt injection attacks.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 185. [Where Did We Go Wrong? Localizing Errors to Repair Multi-Turn Conversations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15154) <a id="link185"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15154
**Authors:** Alessandro Morosini, Wei Sun, Georgia Perakis

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15154v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language model (LLM) assistants are increasingly used in multi-turn conversations, where the user's intent is often unclear at first and revealed only gradually over time. The assistant must therefore interpret each exchange and form inferences about the user's intent, using them to update its working state. These updates usually refine the assistant's understanding, but a misinterpretation can instead corrupt its working state by ruling out the true intent, influencing subsequent turns and ultimately causing the conversation to fail. Efficient recovery then requires locating and revising the misinterpretation without discarding information that remains valid. We introduce Loci (Localization Of Corrupted Inferences), a framework for probabilistically localizing misinterpretations and selectively repairing the assistant's working state. Given an observed failure, Loci reasons over possible user intents and assigns each update a probability of having excluded the true intent. These probabilities rank suspected updates and quantify the model's confidence in the localization of the underlying misinterpretations. Loci then uses this information in a repair policy that decides whether to retain, verify, or remove suspected updates, enabling recovery from corrupted inferences while preserving useful information. To evaluate Loci's ability to identify misinterpretations, we first test it in a controlled setup with injected errors. We find that Loci improves localization over the baselines, with its advantage becoming larger when multiple errors occur. We then evaluate it in free-form conversations, where misinterpretations arise naturally, and measure the interactions needed to complete the task after an initial failure. Using localization confidence to guide repair, Loci reduces mean recovery cost by 46-79% relative to the strongest baseline across multiple domains.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 13: Presents a new method (Loci) for AI control and oversight by localizing and repairing misinterpretations in multi-turn conversations, enabling recovery from corrupted inferences during deployment.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 186. [DiffNash: Finding Nash Equilibrium in High-Dimensional Continuous Action Spaces with Diffusion Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15932) <a id="link186"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15932
**Authors:** Haichuan Wang, Anagha Satish, Jiale Zheng, Saisneha Ghatti, Lingkai Kong, Kai Wang, Milind Tambe

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15932v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Computing equilibria in high-dimensional continuous games requires learning and representing rich, multi-modal strategy distributions, something conventional neural policies struggle to achieve. Diffusion models offer a natural solution, but standard diffusion training requires direct samples from a fixed target distribution, which are generally unavailable under iterative game-solving updates. We propose a diffusion-based framework for equilibrium computation that enables diffusion-based variants of Online Mirror Descent (OMD) and Fictitious Play (FP). Our approach derives a reweighted score-matching objective that trains diffusion policies without requiring direct samples from each updated policy. Our main method, DiffNash, combines the fast convergence of OMD with the sample efficiency of FP by reusing past actions through a replay buffer. To correct the resulting distribution shift from sample reuse, we derive an unbiased estimator of the difference between current and past policies' Evidence Lower Bounds, whose exponential provides a tractable proxy for the corresponding importance weight without evaluating diffusion likelihoods. We evaluate our approach on forest protection games and continuous Colonel Blotto games, reducing the Nash gap over the strongest baseline by 35.6% and 24.7%, respectively, with minimal hyperparameter tuning.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: The paper proposes DiffNash, a diffusion-based framework for equilibrium computation in continuous action spaces, applying diffusion models to policy learning. This is a significant advance in diffusion models applied to language-related continuous action spaces, though not directly language generation but related to generative modeling with diffusion policies.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 7

## 187. [The Format is the Filter: Scoring Format Fixes What an LLM Judge Can Express](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17158) <a id="link187"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17158
**Authors:** Lovedeep Gondara, Jekaterina Novikova, Sagarika Suresh, Jack Ji, Jithin Pradeep

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17158v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: When an LLM judge filters instruction-tuning data, its scoring format (binary verdict, 1--5 rating, 0--100 score, or comparison) is rarely justified or ablated. Holding the candidate pool, judge prompt, decoding and selection budget fixed, we score 15,406 UltraFeedback candidates under five formats with three judges, curate, fine-tune 66 models, and evaluate them with four judges. A 0--100 scale is worth only 7--12 equally used levels, two to three times a 1--5 scale rather than twenty, and a binary verdict leaves the top score tied in 77--78\% of prompts, so most selections are made at random. These properties are set by the format almost independently of the judge. Kendall's $\tau_b$ hides them: it ranks binary best while $\tau_a$ and top-1 agreement rank it last, because it discounts the ties a selector must break at random. Both comparative formats inherit a slot bias whose sign differs across judges. Downstream, curated data beat random selection under every evaluation judge, robust to length and markdown adjustment, but no ordering among formats is detectable. We propose four quantities that judge-based curation pipelines should report.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4: The paper fundamentally studies how different scoring formats affect LLM judges in instruction-tuning data curation and proposes new quantities for judge-based curation pipelines, which is a new paradigm in evaluating open-ended text generation and instruction-following evaluation.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 188. [From Suboptimal to Optimal: One-Step Latent Flow Policies for Offline Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16984) <a id="link188"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16984
**Authors:** Shenghua Lu, Zixuan Yang, Rongxu Zhao, Bohao Qu, Jifeng Hu, Hechang Chen

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16984v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Offline reinforcement learning aims to learn effective policies from fixed datasets without environment interaction. Although generative models such as diffusion and flow matching enhance policy expressiveness, they typically start from uninformative Gaussian noise and generate actions through multi-step iteration, leading to high computational costs and failing to effectively leverage suboptimal trajectories in datasets. We propose MeanFlow2Better (MF2B), which reformulates offline RL as generative decision chunk optimization from current performance status to better performance status in a high-level latent decision space. MF2B maps trajectory chunks to high-level latent decision representations through an encoder, placing generation starting points within a structured decision space sculpted by behavioral data. The model learns a shared conditional mean velocity field in this space, constructing two flow mappings: from Gaussian-Induced Decision Sources toward positive behavioral decision regions, and from suboptimal decisions toward high-value decision regions. Both flow mappings complete decision refinement through one-step ODE integration over the unit time interval $\Delta t=1$, requiring neither multi-step iterative sampling nor backpropagation through time. Furthermore, we propose hierarchical chunk-level advantage to guide the flow direction toward high-return regions, where the high-level decisions cover multiple low-level actions, leading to sequence length compression from $T$ to $K \approx T/H$ and significantly lower computational overhead. On the D4RL Gym-MuJoCo benchmark, MF2B achieves an average score of 90.91, matching or surpassing strong baselines.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Shows a significant advance in the performance of diffusion language models. The paper proposes a novel one-step latent flow policy for offline RL using generative modeling with flow and diffusion concepts, improving efficiency and performance. Although focused on offline RL, it involves diffusion and flow generative models applied to sequential decision making, which is relevant to generative modeling and diffusion methods in language or sequential domains.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 7

## 189. [When Sparsity Breaks Connectivity: Functional Reachability and Guided Topology Dynamics in Extreme Sparse Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17425) <a id="link189"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17425
**Authors:** Mohammad Pasande, Mateusz Jaworski, Jeroen Rook, Elena Mocanu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17425v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Dynamic Sparse Training (DST) periodically prunes and regrows connections, inducing a discrete dynamical mechanism over network topologies. At extreme sparsity ($\ge 99\%$), this dynamic tends to destroy input-output information pathways, and performance with standard magnitude/random updates degrades sharply. We formalize forward, backward, and functional reachability on layered sparse graphs, derive closed-form expectations for reachable neurons and edges, and show that Monte Carlo realizations match the theory. Guided by this reachability-based structure, we bias pruning toward non-functional edges and regrowth toward functionally consistent candidates. We introduce this reachability-driven modification of topology evolution as Reachability-guided in DST, referred to as guided DST. On CIFAR-10/100 and Tiny ImageNet, guided DST recovers large accuracy gaps versus unguided DST (up to $+14.4$ points at $99.9\%$ sparsity on CIFAR-100), and on MLP-Mixer guided DST remains trainable at $99.99\%$ sparsity while unguided random regrowth degrades sharply. These results cast extreme-sparse training as a connectivity-preserving dynamical process. Our implementation is publicly available at https://github.com/mzjaworski/ea_dst.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 7: introduces a reachability-guided modification to dynamic sparse training (DST) for extreme sparsity, improving sparse autoencoder-like topology dynamics relevant to neural network interpretability via sparse feature extraction.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 190. [LatentRemedy: Efficient Remedy For Non-Prefix KV Cache Reuse in Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18014) <a id="link190"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18014
**Authors:** Zhiwei Chen, Zhongtao Rao, Waikit Xiu, Yuxiang Zhang, Shuting Cui, Jian Chen, Tianxiang Zhao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18014v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Non-prefix KV cache reuse is a promising approach for improving inference efficiency in multi-agent systems as agents share the same context blocks. However, when agents have conflicting tasks, directly reusing non-prefix KV caches across agents can severely degrade model performance, causing up to a 90% drop. Existing remedies, including selective recomputation and offset-based calibration, recover a limited fraction of this loss. We argue that the problem lies not only in contaminated KV representations but also in the agent's difficulty interpreting reused context under its own instructions. To this end, we introduce LatentRemedy, which uses a few task-conditioned latent tokens to help the target agent leverage reused context without recomputing the shared block. Specifically, we use an MLP to map the last hidden state of the target prefix into a small number of latent tokens, which are placed after the reused KV block to interact with the shared context. To guide subsequent predictions toward the target task, we train the MLP through full-prefill distillation. Experiments show that LatentRemedy consistently outperforms existing reuse baselines on conflicting tasks while maintaining TTFT close to direct reuse as cache size grows. Code and data are available at https://github.com/chenzhwsysu57/kvreuse.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1 closely: proposes a specific fine-tuning step (LatentRemedy) to improve instruction-following in multi-agent systems by addressing KV cache reuse issues and task-conditioned latent tokens to help models interpret reused context under instructions.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 191. [Explore with Memory, Learn without It: In-Context Exploration and Self-Distillation for LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17734) <a id="link191"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17734
**Authors:** Yutong Chen, Zhichao Xu, Rui Liu, Kunpeng Liu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17734v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Exploration bounds learning efficiency in reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR). Current methods explore mostly through independent sampling, without fully using the information gathered along the way, such as known wrong answers and environment feedback. We propose LEAF, which injects a dynamic, problem-specific memory into the context before every rollout. The memory contains the most recent response, its reward, and other environment feedback. It is updated after every verifier call. We decouple policy updates from rollouts and update the policy through self-distillation. On four benchmarks and two open models, LEAF accelerates exploration by about 4x compared with a random sampling baseline. On unseen test sets, it improves accuracy over the strongest baseline by 1.59 and 1.83 percentage points on the two models, while using less compute than any RL baseline. These results highlight the potential of LEAF in improving both exploration and learning efficiency. Code is available at: https://github.com/on1262/LEAF

**Comment:** This paper proposes LEAF, a method improving exploration and learning efficiency in reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards, applied to LLM reasoning. It involves self-distillation and memory injection to improve learning efficiency. This relates to criterion 12 (LLM reasoning capabilities) and partially to criterion 1 (methodological improvements to instruction-following/RLHF) since it involves RL and learning efficiency, but it is more about exploration in RL than instruction tuning.

**Novelty:** 6

## 192. [LegalRewardBench: Benchmarking Reward Models for Legal Preference Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18299) <a id="link192"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18299
**Authors:** Hui Huang, Jie Zhou, Hongli Zhou, Qundong Shi, Shuyuan Zheng, Yini Su, Shaochun Wang, Biyuan Lin, Muyun Yang, Tiejun Zhao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18299v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reward models are central to preference alignment for legal tasks, yet existing reward model benchmarks are still primarily centered on general-domain preferences. In this work, we introduce LegalRewardBench, the first human-verified benchmark for evaluating reward models in the legal domain. The benchmark organizes legal preference evaluation into five capability levels: knowledge, understanding, analysis, decision, and writing, and preference examples are constructed through rubric-guided automatic judging combined with human verification. We evaluate three families of judges, covering both general and fine-tuned models, and generative and discriminative architectures. Experimental results show that strong general-purpose generative models are currently the most robust legal reward models, while existing fine-tuned reward models transfer less reliably to legal preference evaluation. We also conduct detailed comparisons of input organizations and training strategies.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 11: discusses reward models for preference alignment, a core component of RLHF and alignment methods, specifically evaluating reward models in a legal domain benchmark setting.

## 193. [Sensitivity or Criterion? Measuring and Moving When Language Models Act](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15415) <a id="link193"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15415
**Authors:** Sixun Dong, Yebowen Hu, Ming Yin, Yiran Chen, Fei Liu, Chen Chen

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15415v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Before an agent acts, it must decide whether action is warranted. A single accuracy cannot distinguish failure to recognize a warranted action from reluctance to act. Signal-detection theory separates sensitivity from criterion, but criteria estimated from authored binary labels depend on each benchmark's example mix. We introduce FlipAct, a diagnostic of tool use in physical states. A rule over annotated tool properties computes each answer and a graded evidence score, fixing the Yes/No boundary at zero across models. Questions form 2-by-2 quads that hold required knowledge fixed while the state flips. The slope of the fitted Yes-rate curve measures sensitivity; its crossing at one half measures criterion. Across 44 open models, sensitivity rises with size within a family, and its ranking agrees with three of four external when-to-act benchmarks. Models respond to evidence sign rather than magnitude, accepting actions that leave the state no better about as often as helpful ones. When Yes means act, most models are conservative across rephrasings; in some, this partly reflects a preference for No. Criterion is not determined by size. Activation steering shifts criterion with weights fixed; distillation from a model's own steered copy puts the shift into its weights without a significant sensitivity change. The distilled shift transfers to six external benchmarks, including two without Yes/No answers, while criterion-free AUC is preserved on the five benchmarks where it is available. Accuracy improves where models start conservative and declines or remains within error where they start permissive. Project page: https://sixundong.com/projects/flipact

**Comment:** This paper introduces FlipAct, a diagnostic method to measure sensitivity and criterion shifts in language models' decision to act, including activation steering and distillation effects. It relates to new methodological improvements in instruction-following or fine-tuning steps that affect model behavior across tasks, matching criterion 1 (instruction-following improvements).
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 6

## 194. [Review Rarely Beats the Stronger Model: Separating Verification from Capability Transfer in LLM Pipelines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15897) <a id="link194"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15897
**Authors:** Yirui Zhang, Yash Raj Shrestha

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15897v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Qwen3-8B reviewing Llama-3.1-8B in math adds 33.2 points, yet Qwen3-8B alone is 6.4 points more accurate than the result. Reviewing another model's answer is usually judged by its gain over the generator. We show that this confuses verification, which adds accuracy beyond either model alone, with capability transfer, where a stronger reviewer supplies answers it could have given on its own. Measured against the stronger model alone, across 48 pairs of 12 open-weight models (3B to 72B) in math, knowledge, code and reasoning, review beats it in only 9 of 96 review directions, and does worse the larger the capability gap. The reason is that the reviewer mostly follows the generator, discarding its own correct answers. Review costs 2.6 to 2.8 times as much as the stronger model alone, while a selector using the same two calls is 2.7 to 4.2 points more accurate than review and cheaper. Studies of verification should report the stronger model alone, not only the gain over the generator. Code and per-question results: https://github.com/Kamilaabb/verification-or-capability-transfer

**Comment:** Matches criterion 12: provides a detailed empirical study separating verification from capability transfer in LLM pipelines, revealing surprising insights about model reviewing behavior and accuracy gains in reasoning tasks.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 6

## 195. [The belief-action gap in language model agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17444) <a id="link195"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17444
**Authors:** Ayse Sila Okcu, Weisheng Wang, Tongmeng Xie, Niall Dalton, Angelos Nalmpantis, Raghu Arghal, Mario Giulianelli

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17444v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Language model (LM) agents can fail for different reasons, requiring different explanations and interventions. We study how state beliefs, action values, and action preferences interact as LM agents reason and commit to decisions in text-rendered grid worlds. We estimate task-relevant beliefs using report-based and representation-based probes, and action values using inverse reinforcement learning. We find that task-relevant state and value information is often represented more reliably than it is reflected in behaviour. Tracking action preferences throughout reasoning further shows that decisions sharpen around a commitment point and reported belief uncertainty decreases near commitment, although individual belief changes do not consistently explain shifts in action preference. Finally, forced actions follow reasoning prefixes from other decisions when transplanted, whereas the tested reasoning-window activation edits provide limited control over the action. Together, these results suggest that some failures arise not from missing task-relevant information, but from how available information is translated into action. Our measurements distinguish information availability from belief-action consistency without by themselves identifying the cause of each failure. Our code is available at: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/belief-action-gap-F745/

**Comment:** Related to criterion 12: studies how beliefs and actions interact in LM agents, analyzing reasoning and decision-making processes, which touches on reasoning capabilities and internal model behavior.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 6

## 196. [Fisher information and the geometry of memorization in neural networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16161) <a id="link196"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16161
**Authors:** Daniel T. Bernstein, Chase Goddard, Luca Di Carlo, Vudtiwat Ngampruetikorn, David J. Schwab

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16161v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: A fruitful approach towards understanding generalization in machine learning involves characterizing how models separate generalizable structure from idiosyncratic or even corrupted features of training data. Fisher information has emerged as a diagnostic tool for this separation, leveraging loss geometry to distinguish generalization from memorization. Here we investigate this connection in vision models trained on CIFAR-10 with controlled noise that can be learned only through memorization. We show that projecting model weights onto the top Fisher eigenvectors decouples general task performance from noisy sample memorization, and we investigate how this decoupling presents differentially across layers. We show that the same phenomena are present in small transformers trained to solve a synthetic in-context learning task, and are reproduced in closed form in a simple, analytically solvable linear model. Our results reveal that Fisher information captures aspects of the geometric structure underlying how neural networks allocate capacity between generalization and memorization.

**Comment:** Related to criterion 2: provides a statistical method using Fisher information to distinguish memorization from generalization in neural networks, which can relate to detecting memorization phenomena relevant to test set contamination.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 6

## 197. [Recall What Is Missing: Composing Evidence from Long-Term Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15642) <a id="link197"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15642
**Authors:** Qi Feng, Jicong Fan

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15642v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Under a limited context budget, a long-term agent must recall not merely memories that are individually relevant, but evidence that is collectively sufficient for the current query. Conventional memory access retrieves, scores, and truncates memories, often spending multiple context slots on redundant facts while leaving complementary evidence behind. We cast the read phase of long-term memory as query-conditioned evidence composition: each memory is valued by what it adds beyond the evidence already selected. We instantiate this operation with MemPursuit, a training-free mechanism that uses the residual of a sparse convex query reconstruction as a representation-space proxy for what the current memory set is missing. By reusing the query and candidate embeddings already available from retrieval, MemPursuit progressively composes nested evidence contexts without invoking a language model or cross-encoder to score each query--memory pair. The effectiveness of our method is verified on 3 popular benchmarks in comparison to strong baselines. In fixed-budget LoCoMo experiments, MemPursuit improves end-to-end accuracy by 7.2 percentage points at Top-10 and 5.5 percentage points at Top-20, with consistent gains across multiple memory frameworks, while achieving a 32.9$\times$ speedup in median reranking latency over fully batched BGE-v2-m3. Our implementation is available at https://github.com/FengQi-HITSZ/MemPursuit.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 18: proposes MemPursuit, a training-free data-centric method for query-conditioned evidence composition in long-term memory retrieval, improving data selection and retrieval efficiency without additional model scoring.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 6

## 198. [Distilling Text LMs into Speech-to-Speech LMs by Matching Internal Text Predictions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17461) <a id="link198"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17461
**Authors:** Ju-Chieh Chou, Jiawei Zhou, Karen Livescu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17461v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Text language models (LMs) acquire broad linguistic and factual capabilities through large-scale pretraining, while comparable capabilities remain difficult to learn from speech alone. Recent work has shown that knowledge distillation from pretrained text LMs offers a promising way to transfer their linguistic and factual capabilities to speech models. However, existing distillation approaches have largely focused on speech-input, text-output models. This idea has yet to be explored for {\it speech-to-speech} models, which operate on both speech input and speech output. In this work we introduce a speech-to-speech pretraining method that creates an internal text interface for knowledge transfer while preserving the speech-only input and output. The model first consumes speech context without its transcription, switches to text prediction, and then uses the predicted text to lead future speech generation word by word. It also provides an interface for knowledge distillation: the internal text predictions can be directly distilled from the base text LM. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first application of text LM distillation to a speech-to-speech LM. Under matched training conditions, the proposed sequence construction substantially improves the generative perplexity of spoken continuations over speech-only and interleaved speech-text models, approaching the performance of a text-leading inner-monologue model with external ASR prompt transcription. More importantly, distillation improves factual performance under both text and speech evaluation, indicating better retention of knowledge from the base text language model.

**Comment:** Relevant to criterion 1: proposes a novel method for distilling text LMs into speech-to-speech LMs by matching internal text predictions, improving instruction-following and knowledge transfer capabilities in speech models.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 6

## 199. [The Gradient That Top-k Distillation Never Computes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15589) <a id="link199"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15589
**Authors:** Rasul Khanbayov, Hasan Kurban

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15589v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Many deployed distillation pipelines show the student a truncated view of the teacher: keep the top-k probabilities, renormalize, and minimize a divergence on what remains. Renormalization is assumed to account for the missing mass. We prove that it removes the visibility of tail mass, not the freedom to move it. The renormalized objective depends only on log-odds among the retained tokens, so at each position its gradient along the directions that control tail mass is exactly zero; on a real teacher that component measures $2$-$3\times10^{-15}$ of the gradient. The full-vocabulary gradient equals the truncated gradient plus a residual weighted by the tail masses, and training acts only on the first term. Without the cross-entropy term, a top-k student's tail mass drifts away from the teacher's while a full-vocabulary control moves slightly toward it; with it, the gap shrinks about fourfold but persists. On seven open-weight teachers, the retained set determines the teacher's distribution only from $k=d+1$ ($d$ the hidden dimension): recovery is exact from there on all seven, and commonly cached sizes sit below it. In our off-policy runs the practical effect is real but smaller than the choice of teacher-student pair. Renormalization is a normalization, not a safeguard. The code is available at https://github.com/KurbanIntelligenceLab/top-k-distillation.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 2: Presents a new statistical analysis of top-k distillation gradients, revealing subtle effects on tail mass and gradient components. This relates to understanding training dynamics and could impact membership inference or contamination detection methods indirectly.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 200. [Variance-Optimal State Resampling for Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15888) <a id="link200"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15888
**Authors:** Yibo Wang, Yuanxin Liu, Zhaoran Wang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15888v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards spends much of its rollout budget on states that yield no learning signal. We recast this as a question about the sampling distribution itself: under a fixed rollout budget, which distribution over states minimizes the variance of the policy-gradient estimator? The answer admits a closed-form Neyman allocation, $\mu_{\mathrm{var}}^\pi(s) \propto d^\pi(s)\,\sigma_\pi(s)$, which connects naturally to GRPO's group-relative advantage and induces an emergent reverse curriculum as the policy improves, with no externally imposed schedule. Building on this principle, we propose VSR (Variance-driven State Resampling), a plug-in modification to GRPO using a prefix replay buffer and a zero-cost variance proxy. On mathematical reasoning benchmarks and long-horizon agentic tasks (Sokoban, BabyAI), VSR outperforms GRPO and GiGPO on most benchmarks with fewer generated tokens per update.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes a variance-optimal state resampling method for reinforcement learning that improves policy-gradient estimation efficiency, which relates to methodological improvements in RLHF or instruction-following fine-tuning steps by optimizing sampling distributions for better learning signals.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 6

## 201. [REVEAL: Reverse Engineering Agentic Latents from Frontier-Model Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16288) <a id="link201"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16288
**Authors:** Yuetian Du, Meng Wang, Yucheng Wang, Zhangzhe Chen, Wei Wang, Boyu Yang, Weiqi Zhai, Bing Zhao, Ming Kong, Zhan Zhou, Jie Liu, Qiang Zhu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16288v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Frontier LLM agents perform complex interactive tasks, but their inference costs motivate transferring their experience to smaller open-source agents. Distillation and latent communication typically require target-model training or access to source-model internals. We study how textual frontier-model trajectories can provide reusable local controls for a frozen agent. Inspired by protocol reverse engineering, we introduce $\textit{Agentic Latents}$, comprising anchored states and dynamic profiles of feedback--response cycles elicited by replay in the target model. Our training-free framework, $\textbf{REVEAL}$ ($\textbf{REV}$erse $\textbf{E}$ngineering $\textbf{A}$gentic $\textbf{L}$atents), uses these profiles and trajectory outcomes to match and select local events from successful and failed rollouts of the same task and source model. Their response-transition contrasts yield a target-specific steering direction, applied before each response. Within-benchmark evaluations on Terminal-Bench 2.1 and Humanity's Last Exam with tools report higher accuracy than unmodified agents across three targets. Representation analyses and behavioral case studies support reusing frontier-model experience as local activation control.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 16: The paper studies reverse engineering of agentic latents from frontier LLM trajectories, aiming to uncover latent local controls and reusable activation patterns in frozen agents, which relates to uncovering latent reasoning or hidden computational processes in neural networks.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 6

## 202. [Long-Horizon-Terminal-Bench 2.0: Automating Diverse Task Construction and Measuring Sustained Agent Progress](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14146) <a id="link202"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14146
**Authors:** Zongxia Li, Zhongzhi Li, Yucheng Shi, Ruhan Wang, Junyao Yang, Chengsong Huang, Zhichao Liu, Xiyang Wu, Anhao Li, Yue Yu, Ergan Shang, Fuxiao Liu, Tong Zheng, Ninghao Liu, Lichao Sun, Haitao Mi, Leowei Liang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14146v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Long-horizon terminal benchmarks measure whether an agent can sustain progress through extended workflows with process rewards, not only whether its final state passes a binary check. We present Long-Horizon-Terminal-Bench 2.0 (LHTB 2.0), an extension of Long-Horizon-Terminal-Bench that preserves its 46 original tasks while correcting task and evaluation-infrastructure defects and extending 31 tasks. LHTB 2.0 contains 77 tasks across 13 domains and eight task types. The extended tasks are produced through a systematic reconstruction-task pipeline. We start from a working scientific or engineering artifact, then use a parameterized reference implementation to generate multiple scenarios, reveal six as behavioral evidence, hold out three for evaluation, and use dense progress ladders to reward partial progress while preventing memorization of public outputs. We also extend the evaluation horizon from 90 minutes to three hours and record verifier checkpoints every 30 minutes, enabling direct measurement of sustained progress within a run. Across 1,145 scored model-task runs from 15 frontier models, GPT-6-astra leads with dense mean reward 0.69 and 37 tasks at R at least 0.95, followed by GPT-5.6-sol with dense mean reward 0.67 and 32 tasks at the same threshold. On the 30 reconstruction tasks, binary verifier scores average 0.221, whereas dense ladder scores average 0.653, showing that outcome-only evaluation discards substantial intermediate progress. LHTB 2.0 provides a broader, hardened, and process-aware testbed for studying reliable agent execution over hours.

**Comment:** Partially relevant to criterion 4: proposes a new benchmark for long-horizon agent evaluation with dense progress ladders to better measure sustained progress, which is a new paradigm for evaluating open-ended generation and agent behavior.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 7

## 203. [Imprinting: Knowledge Distillation Can Shift Student Behavior](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18517) <a id="link203"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18517
**Authors:** Yu-Chien Tang, An-Zi Yen

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18517v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Knowledge distillation trains student models on teacher-generated responses to transfer task capability, but those responses may also carry behavioral tendencies of their source. We investigate whether the source teacher can shape student behavior beyond the capability that distillation is intended to transfer. We construct harm-accepting and harm-avoiding variants of the same base teacher, use them to generate responses to ordinary reasoning and knowledge questions unrelated to safety, and distill those responses into students with different base models. Students generally acquire task capability while their downstream decisions shift toward the source teacher's safety-pragmatism disposition, a phenomenon we call imprinting. Distillation from the harm-accepting teacher reduces harm avoidance, whereas distillation from the harm-avoiding teacher increases it. Imprinting varies across teacher-student pairs and does not extend uniformly across different forms of safety behavior. Excluding a sparse set of divergence tokens reduces imprinting, whereas response rewriting preserves the source-dependent effect and can introduce additional rewriter-dependent shifts. Post-distillation preference optimization raises harm avoidance but does not eliminate the source-dependent difference. These results show that behavioral tendencies can be intentionally imprinted through the source teacher, even with ordinary task supervision.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1 partially: The paper studies how knowledge distillation can imprint behavioral tendencies from teacher to student models, affecting instruction-following behavior and safety pragmatism. While not about RLHF or instruction-tuning datasets per se, it relates to fine-tuning steps that influence instruction-following behavior and alignment.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 6
