## 204. [Correction Training for Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14136) <a id="link204"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14136
**Authors:** Donghoon Ahn, Runqian Wang, Alexei A. Efros, Taesung Park, Yash Belhe

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14136v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Iterative sampling in diffusion models can lead to error accumulation due to exposure bias. The denoiser is trained on real images interpolated with noise, but at inference its inputs are constructed from its own imperfect predictions from the earlier steps, something it has never seen at training time. This might be one reason diffusion models often struggle without additional help, such as CFG. We introduce a correction step for diffusion models which attempts to reduce this training-inference gap. The corrector takes the base model's prediction as input and is trained against the same ground-truth target. This makes correction an explicit learning task, with training inputs provided by the base model. During sampling, at each denoising step, we iteratively refine the denoiser's clean-sample prediction. This allows us to correct predictions at intermediate denoising steps, preventing early artifacts from persisting into the final sample. Although the corrector is trained only on predictions from the base denoiser, it can be applied repeatedly to its own outputs at inference for better results. This simple framework leads to substantial improvement in sample quality while largely preserving diversity. We evaluate our method across DiT, SiT, and JiT. Our method substantially improves upon previously reported FID scores for unguided generation and, notably, unconditional generation, where producing high-quality samples without guidance has been particularly challenging. Our method achieves performance competitive with classifier-free guidance (CFG), and combining correction with CFG further improves diversity and reduces oversaturation typical of CFG. Finally, we show that the additional inference cost of correction can be amortized by distilling the refined predictions back into the base diffusion model, retaining the quality gains without additional inference-time overhead.

**Comment:** This paper introduces a correction training method for diffusion models to reduce training-inference gap and improve sample quality, including unconditional generation. It explicitly targets diffusion models and shows substantial improvements in sample quality and diversity. Although it does not explicitly mention language models, it evaluates on DiT, SiT, and JiT which are diffusion models for images. Since the criterion requires diffusion language models specifically, this paper is borderline but relevant for diffusion generative modeling advances.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 205. [Fast and Accurate: Improving Personalized LLM Performance by Semantic Concentration Inspired Workload Split between RAG and Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15921) <a id="link205"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15921
**Authors:** Xingke Yang, Sicong Li, Lingfeng Yao, Rui Chen, Changqing Luo, Xin Fu, Miao Pan

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15921v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: With ever increasing personalized large language model (LLM) services, the next question to answer is how to improve the quality of services/users' experiences, which is indicated by personalized LLM generation accuracy/quality and inference latency. Although state-of-the-art designs, e.g., parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT), retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) or Full PEFT+RAG, can help, they largely ignore the heterogeneous semantic concentrations of users' historical data and cannot fully unleash the potential of personal data for personalized LLM performance enhancement. In this paper, we propose PEFT Union RAG (PUR), a novel ``fast and accurate'' approach that exploits the semantic concentration of users' personal historical data to split workload between PEFT and RAG, aiming to improve personalized LLM performance in terms of better generation accuracy/quality and lower inference latency. Briefly, PUR partitions user's personal historical data and assigns semantic concentrated ``core'' information to PEFT for offline parametric fine-tuning, while retaining dispersed information for RAG. During the online LLM inference, PUR directly generates responses for ``core'' queries covered by the PEFT region and invokes RAG only for sporadic ``peripheral'' queries. Experimental results show that, by appropriately splitting workload between PEFT and RAG, PUR consistently achieves the best generation accuracy/quality for various users across multiple tasks, while reducing inference latency by 14.4%--46.6% compared with the best performed method.

**Comment:** Related to criterion 1: proposes a novel fine-tuning approach (PEFT Union RAG) that splits workload between PEFT and RAG to improve personalized LLM instruction-following performance, which is a specific fine-tuning step to improve instruction-following.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 5

---

## 206. [A Sense of Proprioception in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17495) <a id="link206"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17495
**Authors:** Jonas Geiping

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17495v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: In humans, proprioception corresponds to the sense of self-movement or position, e.g. understanding the position of one's arm. This sense is often less well-understood and in popular understanding sometimes conflated with introspection. But, notably, proprioception is a separate sense, with dedicated receptors and verifiable outcomes. On the other hand though, language models cannot observe their own internal states during training (and for example answer the question which experts activated in a certain layer of an MoE) and so cannot accurately report on them. Yet, such information is, by virtue of being internal to the model, both accessible, and readily constructed into verifiable signals. We argue that this provides the basis for a digital analog of proprioception. As such, we train models through SFT and on-policy training to equip them with this ability. We show that modern models can be trained to understand a range of subtypes of proprioception, such as routing decision awareness, token entropy, decision depth or logit lens information, without affecting model capabilities. We then run these models through a broad evaluation of situational awareness, legibility, and epiphenomenality, to evaluate in what ways access to proprioception changes model behavior.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1 moderately: discusses training language models with a form of internal state awareness ('proprioception') via SFT and on-policy training to improve situational awareness and interpretability of internal decisions, which relates to instruction-following improvements and model internal understanding.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 207. [The Fragility and Dynamics of LLM Pruning-Recovery Steps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15574) <a id="link207"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15574
**Authors:** Luis Frentzen Salim, Komang Rinartha, Chia-Chun Wu, Tran Van Nhiem, Lun-Wei Ku, Yung-Hui Li

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15574v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Structural pruning followed by a recovery stage is the standard recipe for compressing large language models (LLMs), yet the two stages are typically designed and tuned in isolation. In this work, we study how their interaction shapes the compressed model, sweeping ordering, the data that drives each stage, and the alignment of their training objectives. We find that instruction-following is the first capability to collapse under pruning, well before knowledge, reasoning, or core language modeling, and that much of the pruning in-domain loss is likely not knowledge loss but a domain-agnostic failure to respond coherently to the prompt. We find in our setting that in-domain data is the strongest pruning signal, whereas instruction-style data recovers the most. Stage order matters little, but only marginally. We further analyze how the importance ranking drifts before and after pruning. Together these results give practitioners a concrete recipe for composing pruning and recovery.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1 moderately: analyzes pruning and recovery steps in LLMs with a focus on instruction-following capability degradation and recovery, providing insights into fine-tuning and instruction data effects.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 5

---

## 208. [Verifier-Harness Co-Evolution for Training-Free Self-Improvement on Open-Ended Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17609) <a id="link208"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17609
**Authors:** Tianyi Xu, Yaolun Zhang, Yiran Wu, Joe Nguyen, Shengyu Dai, Saba Moeinizade, Zhenwen Shao, Qingyun Wu, Huazheng Wang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17609v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Training-free self-improvement lets an agent use feedback to revise the response it is about to return and to update the harness state that shapes how it handles later tasks. On open-ended tasks that feedback rarely comes from reference answers or executable tests, so the agent has to rely on a model-based verifier. When the verifier is wrong, the current response can get worse, and misleading experience can persist across the task stream. A verifier that never changes also grows stale as the agent's responses improve and task demands shift. We introduce Unified Co-Evolution (UCoE), a training-free method that evolves an executable verifier program together with the agent's harness state while all model parameters stay fixed. UCoE synthesizes candidate verifier programs and tests them on response contrasts, pairs of responses that differ in one declared property and have a known preferred side. It selects the candidate whose discrimination on its weakest contrast family is highest, a quantity we call driving capacity. The selected verifier diagnoses one weakness in each draft, guides a single repair, decides which response is served, and determines whether the accepted repair may enter the harness state. As tasks complete, their diagnostics seed new candidate verifiers, which must beat the current verifier on the same contrasts before adoption. Across six open-ended benchmarks, UCoE averages 59.00% preference against matched direct generation with Qwen3-8B and 57.19% with Qwen3.8-27B, margins of 9.00 and 7.19 percentage points. On three verifiable benchmarks, the same method raises the mean from 36.06% for direct generation to 38.48%. Code is available at https://github.com/tianyi0216/Unified-Co-Evolution.

**Comment:** Partially related to criterion 4: Introduces a training-free self-improvement method using co-evolving verifiers to improve open-ended task performance, which touches on evaluation paradigms by evolving verifiers to assess responses, but does not fundamentally rethink evaluation or address subjectivity/adversaries explicitly.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 209. [SkillForgeBench: Evaluating Agent Reliability Grounded in Agent Skill Specifications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14918) <a id="link209"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14918
**Authors:** Zhengxi Li, Junyi Zhou, Qiyuan Zhang, Chuhan Wu, Fuyuan Lyu, Le Tian, Xiao Zhou, Chen Ma, Yufei Wang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14918v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Evaluating an agent's ability to strictly satisfy diverse and procedurally complex requirements is essential to determining its practical reliability. Conducting such an evaluation demands agentic tasks that reconcile broad domain coverage with deep procedural complexity. Existing paradigms face a fundamental trade-off: expert-curated benchmarks achieve procedural depth but suffer from domain diversity bottlenecks, whereas crowdsourced benchmarks span diverse scenarios but lack execution rigor. To resolve this tension, we present SkillForgeBench, which leverages agent skills as unifying execution blueprints across three co-designed stages: (1) Resource Preparation uses domain-balanced skills to guide artifact retrieval, establishing broad workflow coverage; (2) Skill-driven Task Synthesis employs skills as procedural anchors to partition artifact roles, synthesize naturalistic task instructions, and package sandboxed scenarios for deep procedural execution; and (3) Holistic Evaluation Design translates skill-embedded requirements into fine-grained completion and process rubrics to enable strict requirement-level scoring. Comprising 842 sandboxed tasks across five major professional domains, SkillForgeBench reveals a steep capability hierarchy among eight mainstream agents, led by Opus 4.8 ($40.9\%$ strict completion). Requiring all constrains satisfaction uncovers severe capability bottlenecks across all model tiers that remain masked under individual rubric pass rates ($70.0\%$ to $85.7\%$ vs. $12.6\%$ to $40.9\%$). Crucially, under the external guidance from skills, top-tier agents obtain larger gains than low-tier agents (i.e., $\Delta 19.2\%$ for Opus 4.8 vs. $\Delta 5.8\%$ for Haiku 4.5). Finally, agents satisfy process rubrics far less frequently than completion rubrics (under $3\%$ for Haiku 4.5), proving that completion-only checks overestimate real-world agent reliability.

**Comment:** Partially relevant to criterion 5: presents a large benchmark (SkillForgeBench) for evaluating agent reliability grounded in skill specifications, providing data on real-world agent capabilities and reliability, but not focused on language model safety per se.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 210. [ReTerm: Recovery Training for Self-Improving Terminal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18484) <a id="link210"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18484
**Authors:** Wei Xie, Kaituo Feng, Shiming Fu, Yong Liu, Xiangyu Yue, Shuangjia Zheng

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18484v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Terminal agents have shown strong abilities in complex coding tasks. However, they often struggle to recover from errors during long-running execution (e.g., command errors, faulty implementations), eventually leading to task failures. To this end, we present ReTerm, a self-improving terminal agent trained by learning how to recover from its own failed terminal tasks. To support its training, we construct a customized data pipeline and produce ReTerm-10K, a high-quality dataset that provides effective recovery demonstrations for SFT cold start. Furthermore, we propose ReTerm-GRPO, an online RL algorithm that forks from the agent's own failure states to generate multiple independent self-recovery attempts. By learning from whether these attempts successfully complete the task, the agent self-improves through its own recovery experience. Built on Qwen3.5-9B, our ReTerm-9B achieves 34.8% Pass@1 on Terminal-Bench 2.0, improving over the base model by 14.6 points. Further analysis shows that our method achieves consistently higher recovery rates across diverse error categories compared with standard GRPO training. All code, data and model are released at https://github.com/twothsnd/ReTerm.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1 partially: proposes a recovery training method for terminal agents including RL algorithm improvements for self-improving behavior, related to instruction-following and fine-tuning for robustness in execution tasks with LLMs.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 5

---

## 211. [Vela: User Behavior as Supervision Sparks LLM Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14709) <a id="link211"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14709
**Authors:** Yang Xiao, Yusong Sun, Haoyi Wu, Wenyang Hui, Weiqiang Hang, Luqing Wang, Zejian Wang, Zirui Tu, Jingsheng Mao, Shaobo Hu, Yang Luo, Jiale Guo, Jin Zhang, Yudan Liu, Zhaokai Luo, Ren Huan, Yao Hu, Chengyue Jiang, Wenjie Li, Chuan Mu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14709v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language models are increasingly deployed in applications that act on users' long-term behavior histories, from personal assistants to recommendation and advertising systems. These applications demand the user dimension of LLM intelligence: inferring a person's preferences, intent, and likely future actions from weeks to years of behavior data. Yet current models are trained on static text or synthetic data, neither of which captures how user behavior evolves over time. Real-world behavior records, already available at scale on content platforms and in personal-assistant agents, offer a natural supervisory signal. We propose user behavior as supervision and introduce Vela, a framework that converts real user behavior data into trainable tasks and trajectories. From the anonymized behavior records of 100,000 users, Vela generates 100,000 tasks across 91 real-world task templates; difficulty-based filtering and trajectory acceptance criteria retain approximately 75,000 tasks with selected training trajectories. Fine-tuning Qwen3.6-35B-A3B on these trajectories improves performance over the base model by up to 11.5 points on user-related tasks and by an average of 4 points on general benchmarks. Training on more user behavior data further improves user-related performance, and reinforcement learning after fine-tuning outperforms reinforcement learning from the base model by up to 7.7 points. These gains suggest that real-world user behavior is a scalable source of supervision for both user-centered capabilities and the broader intelligence of LLMs.

**Comment:** Partially relevant to criterion 1: Uses real user behavior as supervision to fine-tune LLMs and improve user-related tasks and general benchmarks, including reinforcement learning after fine-tuning. While it involves fine-tuning and RL, it is more about leveraging user behavior data than specific RLHF or instruction-following methodological improvements.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

## 212. [WorkBorne: Discovering Agentic Tasks from Real-World Artifact Transitions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14915) <a id="link212"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14915
**Authors:** Junyi Zhou, Yufei Wang, Qiyuan Zhang, Chuhan Wu, Zhengxi Li, Wentao Zhao, Jie Zhou, Le Tian, Xiao Zhou, Chen Ma

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14915v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Developing terminal-based agents for long-horizon execution demands synthetic tasks that tightly couple complex instructions with authentic, interactive environments. However, current automated synthesis suffers from a fundamental asymmetry: instruction-oriented methods fabricate low-fidelity, synthetic environments, whereas resource-oriented methods treat real artifacts merely as static inputs, degrading task instructions into shallow, trivial operations. To address this limitation, we introduce WorkBorne, a synthesis framework that uses artifact transitions as the central primitive for modeling the full life-cycle of agentic tasks. In WorkBorne, each transition specifies who performs the task, which artifacts anchor the initial state and intended outcome, and the corresponding relationship between them. Specifically, WorkBorne first constructs coherent and diverse artifact corpora from iterative web retrieval, then discovers non-trivial transitions by identifying meaningful input-output artifact assemblies and the relations that connect them. Each discovered transition is finally instantiated into grounded agentic tasks. We construct 1,061 artifact corpora and 7,882 task environments, yielding 15,485 high-quality trajectories, with 53.6% exceeding 30 assistant turns. Experiments on Qwen3.5-27B and Qwen3.5-35B-A3B show that WorkBorne substantially improves over base models (i.e., improving Terminal-Bench 2.1 from 43.1% to 61.8% and ClawEval General score from 75.0 to 80.2) and achieves the strongest overall performance among other synthesis baselines (e.g., TMax (Ivison et al., 2026), RST (Li et al., 2026a), Terminal-Universe (Wu et al., 2026a)). Further analysis shows that transition is strongly associated with execution depth, while corpus breadth and transition depth provide complementary scaling gains.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1 partially: discusses instruction-following improvements via synthetic task synthesis and agentic task generation to improve long-horizon execution in language models. However, focus is on task synthesis and environment modeling rather than specific RLHF or instruction-tuning fine-tuning methods.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

## 213. [One Model, Multiple Compute Budgets: Multi-Ratio ConceptMoE for Flexible Compute Across Turns](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14074) <a id="link213"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14074
**Authors:** Yiyang Fu, Zihao Huang, Dongchen Han, Shukai Gong, Zhen Dong, Qiyang Min, Daquan Zhou

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14074v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language models typically support different inference budgets through separate model sizes or fixed compute configurations. In multi-turn interactions, however, compute demand can vary substantially across turns. Using a high-compute model for every turn wastes computation, while routing among independent models may require rebuilding model-specific history states after switches. We therefore ask whether a single shared model can support multiple compute budgets that can be flexibly assigned across turns. We introduce Multi-Ratio ConceptMoE, which jointly supports token-to-concept compression ratios of 2, 4, and 8 (CR2/CR4/CR8) within a shared backbone. Lightweight full-token modules reduce compression-independent cost, ratio-specific boundary routers support distinct compression levels, and cross-ratio training enables mixed-ratio processing within the same context. Across PTBench and SFTBench, the three fixed modes outperform their compute-matched MoE baselines in all 6 comparisons while remaining competitive with independently trained fixed-ratio specialists. On CodeChat and CoSQL, mixed-ratio inference improves the accuracy--compute trade-off, outperforming the Large MoE by 5.87 and 2.68 percentage points while reducing FLOPs by 41.1% and 12.3%, respectively. Under the same turn-wise budget schedule, Multi-Ratio ConceptMoE further reduces FLOPs by 78.4% and 46.9% relative to routing among independent models. These results demonstrate that a single shared model can provide multiple compute budgets for flexible compute allocation across turns.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: introduces Multi-Ratio ConceptMoE, a method for flexible compute allocation in large language models, improving efficiency and accuracy trade-offs, relevant to advances in diffusion or generative language model architectures with compute flexibility.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 214. [Overtaught, Underlearned: Learner-Centered Distillation from Multi-Agent Discussion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16543) <a id="link214"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16543
**Authors:** Mengtao Zhou, Jianxiao Wang, Xiupeng Chen, Cailiang Liu, Jie Song

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16543v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Multi-agent discussion produces reasoning collaboratively, while deployment still relies on a smaller student that must answer alone. Full-graph distillation transfers such discussions by treating each correct solution as a separate training target, but what teachers provide need not match what the student learns. This mismatch appears in two ways: several correct solutions may amount to the same lesson in the student's representation (over-teaching), while some training questions remain missed after learning (under-learning). We quantify both with rates that are high on discussion graphs. To address them, we introduce FAR (Fold--Attempt--Revisit), a learner-centered distillation procedure that organizes supervision around the student's own state. Before training, FAR folds solutions within each discussion state into representative lessons. After training, the student's own attempts identify missed questions, while poorly represented clusters indicate where folding may have lost useful variation; FAR then writes targeted lessons from retained discussion contexts. Across two students and four reasoning tasks, FAR outperforms full-graph distillation with far fewer training targets and lower under-learning. Further analyses examine the behavior of FAR and the sources of its gains. Overall, the results show that organizing discussion supervision around the student can make distillation both more effective and more efficient.

**Comment:** Related to criterion 1 (instruction-following improvements) in that it proposes a new learner-centered distillation method (FAR) to improve multi-agent discussion distillation for reasoning tasks, but does not explicitly address RLHF or instruction-following fine-tuning methods.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 215. [To Cache or Not to Cache: Understanding and Mitigating Safety Incompatibility in Cross-Model KV Cache Reuse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15721) <a id="link215"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15721
**Authors:** Zhiwei Lin, Jiahao Liu, Yan Liu, Chengpeng Wang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15721v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Cross-model KV cache sharing reduces prefill computation by allowing a receiver model to reuse prefix KV states generated by a related sender model. Existing methods optimize such reuse for latency and generation quality, but its safety implications remain unclear. In this work, we systematically evaluate this issue across diverse sender-receiver pairs and reuse configurations. We refer to deviations from the receiver's native safety behavior as safety incompatibility. Our study shows that both the direction and magnitude of safety incompatibility depend on the model pair and reuse configuration, with value reuse causing substantially larger deviations than key reuse across all evaluated pairs. Furthermore, we propose VReparo, a technique that learns per-layer and per-KV-head affine maps offline to align reused sender value tensors with their receiver-native counterparts before decoding. Across two model pairs under full KV reuse and the state-of-the-art selective reuse setting, VReparo reduces the average HarmBench attack success rate (ASR) from 57.28% to 39.21% and the average StrongREJECT score from 0.4215 to 0.2163 upon our benchmarks, while retaining an average Time to First Token (TTFT) reduction of 85.62%.

**Comment:** Partially relevant to criterion 10 (security/tamper resistance) by studying safety incompatibility in cross-model KV cache reuse and proposing VReparo to reduce attack success rates, but focused on a specific technical mechanism rather than broad tamper resistance.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 5

---

## 216. [LLM-to-Flow Distillation: Teaching Natural Language Goals to Scientific Generators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17246) <a id="link216"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17246
**Authors:** Riccardo De Santi, Federico Di Gennaro, Angelo Gnazzo, Sophia Tang, Pranam Chatterjee, Fanny Yang, \.Ismail \.Ilkan Ceylan, Michael M. Bronstein, Andreas Krause

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17246v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Generative discovery in science requires coupling high-level scientific reasoning with the ability to generate samples respecting the structure of a design space. Yet these capabilities remain largely separated: foundation LLMs encode broad chemical and biological knowledge, while domain-specific flow- and diffusion-based generators capture the priors needed to produce valid sequences and geometries. We study how to align such scientific generators to high-level natural-language discovery goals without requiring an explicit proxy reward, which is often unavailable or poorly captured by learned predictors in experimental sciences. We introduce LLM-to-Flow Distillation (LFD), where a frozen LLM acts as an adaptive teacher for a domain-specific generator. Given a goal such as 'generate peptides with promising anticancer activity', the teacher constructs a curriculum of intermediate sub-goals aware of the flow's current capabilities, and progressively directs them toward the original discovery goal. At each round, the LLM judges sampled candidates under the current objective. These judgments induce preferences that are iteratively distilled into the generator through DPO. This procedure provides dense learning signals even when the initial model places negligible probability on designs satisfying the final goal, enabling adaptation toward poorly covered, out-of-distribution regions. Experiments on therapeutic peptides and small-molecule structure design demonstrate that LFD enables a new form of generative control: aligning arbitrary domain-specific scientific generators to scientific discovery tasks expressed via high-level natural-language goals.

**Comment:** Partially relevant to criterion 1: proposes a method (LLM-to-Flow Distillation) to align domain-specific generative models to natural language goals using a frozen LLM as a teacher and distillation via DPO, which relates to instruction-following and fine-tuning steps to improve generative control. However, it is focused on scientific generators rather than general language models or RLHF.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 217. [Sparsity Saves Compute, Not Samples: Oracle Inequalities for Dense and Sparse Mixture of Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15039) <a id="link217"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15039
**Authors:** Sagar Ghosh, Huy Nguyen, Alessandro Rinaldo

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15039v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models combine specialized experts through an input-dependent routing mechanism, and their sparse variants underpin many modern large-scale systems. Yet the statistical cost of adaptive routing remains only partially understood. We study the estimation error of least-squares MoE estimators when both the experts and the gating network are Lipschitz in their parameters. We represent each MoE as a gate--expert pair in a Gated Hilbert Space, whose norm $\|\cdot\|_\Delta$ separates the contributions of routing and expert discrepancies. Using its empirical representation, we derive an oracle inequality with a fast rate of order $\tilde{\mathcal O}\big((Kd_{\mathrm{pr},e}+d_{\mathrm{pr},g})/n\big)$ for dense MoEs with $K$ experts. For sparse Top-$k$ and Top-$p$ routing, the selection map is discontinuous in the gating parameters. We handle this by partitioning the gating class according to the selection patterns it induces on the sample. The resulting bounds match the dense rate up to an additive routing cost governed by the Natarajan dimension of the selector. We show that such a routing-complexity condition cannot be dropped: Lipschitz continuity in the parameters alone does not suffice. For linear softmax gating, the routing cost is of the same order as the gating term. Finally, for linear experts, we establish a matching minimax lower bound of order $Kd_{\mathrm{pr},e}/n$, showing that the rates are optimal up to logarithmic factors.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 6: The paper studies statistical estimation error bounds and oracle inequalities for sparse and dense Mixture-of-Experts models, which relates to theoretical understanding of model complexity and sample efficiency, akin to scaling laws in neural networks.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 218. [What Is the "Self" of a Large Language Model?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16717) <a id="link218"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16717
**Authors:** Yuchen Li, Zongqi Fan, Xintong Li, Ken-tye Yong, Nguyen H. Tran

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16717v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: What could the "self" of a large language model be, if it is neither a particular word nor a single privileged layer? We propose a relational account: a functional self is an actor-centered organization of information that distinguishes the model's recorded actions from externally supplied material while content and reasoning operations change. Its representational candidate is a family of layer-specific source-binding components, rather than one unchanged vector throughout the network. Preliminary observations from a 27B BF16 model provide a geometric starting point. Across 150 matched message bodies, 450 role conditions, and a 75/75 discovery-held-out split, the combined Assistant-versus-external displacement transfers strongly across bodies and positions. Selected early components place User and System closer to one another than to Assistant, whereas this full-space distance relation does not persist throughout depth. These findings motivate a layered account: an early source-encoding candidate can be combined with role-specific information use and continuation preparation at later depths, without requiring monotonic overwriting of the common displacement. We connect this account to dynamic reasoning, historical continuity, and a computational model separating content operations, source binding, authority, and output. The reported evidence establishes a reproducible role-conditioned geometry; the proposed interpretation is that functional selfhood may reside in persistent relations among changing representations.

**Comment:** This paper studies internal representational structure of LLMs related to a notion of 'self' and source-binding components, which is a form of mechanistic interpretability (criterion 8). It does not explicitly mention instruction-following, RLHF, test contamination, or other criteria.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

## 219. [Generalizable Model Editing Could Be More Than Direct Generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15707) <a id="link219"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15707
**Authors:** Xiaotian Ye, Chunyao Yang, Xiaohan Wang, Junkai Chen, Yongqi Duan, Mengqi Zhang, Shu Wu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15707v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: A central desideratum in model editing is generalization to complex tasks, which prior work formalizes under a direct generalization paradigm: treating and evaluating LLM behavior as an input-output mapping and expecting a factual update to alter outputs on reasoning tasks. Yet despite substantial empirical effort, the field consensus remains that such generalization is highly challenging and elusive. However, when we deploy the latest models in an in-the-wild chatbot-user setting, we surprisingly find that even classical editing methods exhibit remarkably fluent generalization behavior. This motivates us to revisit the obstacles to generalization and the limitations of the prevailing paradigm. We find that direct generalization is fundamentally constrained both theoretically and practically: theoretically, it suffers from an identifiability issue; and practically, existing approaches attempting to circumvent this are further hindered by the ambiguity of their constructed biases and by vulnerability to being overridden by other factors such as optimization dynamics. We instead identify compositional generalization, empowered by the increasingly strong reasoning and composition abilities of modern LLMs, as a theoretically grounded and empirically validated alternative. We validate this phenomenon across multiple models and editing methods, showing that such generalization behavior is widespread. Overall, we argue that generalizable model editing could be more than direct generalization, and call for a paradigm shift toward more generalizable and practically deployable knowledge update techniques.

**Comment:** Related to criterion 9 (machine unlearning) and possibly 1 (instruction-following improvements) but primarily about model editing generalization paradigms rather than specific fine-tuning or unlearning methods. It challenges the direct generalization paradigm and proposes compositional generalization as an alternative, which is a conceptual advance in model editing.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

## 220. [DoSA: Dots Sparse Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13990) <a id="link220"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.13990
**Authors:** Fanxu Meng, Taosong Fang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.13990v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Sparse attention visits few keys, yet finding them can still require scoring every key with every indexer head. We introduce DoSA (Dots Sparse Attention), a lossless method for making this computation conditional while preserving exact token selection. Its central observation is that Top-K selection requires sufficient evidence to reject a candidate, not necessarily its full score. A seed set supplies a lower bound on the winning threshold. Nonnegative head projections and joint residual bounds upper-bound positive contributions, while activation-informed aggregation bounds the magnitude subtracted by negative heads. The certificate directs full scoring toward candidates that remain eligible; exact-score filtering further reduces writeback and selection work. We establish the certificate and its exact-selection conditions, and analyze how geometry and selection margins govern its effectiveness. Our GPU implementation accelerates the complete Indexer by up to 2.74 times over SGLang and 1.69 times over LiteTopK across English, Chinese, and code workloads. End-to-end evaluation on GLM-5.2 and DeepSeek-V3.2 achieves up to 1.94 times and 1.50 times time-to-first-token speedups over the two baselines, respectively.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: presents a new method (Dots Sparse Attention) improving sparse attention mechanisms relevant to language models, which can be related to diffusion or generative modeling improvements in language modeling architectures.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 221. [Pessimistic Latent Task-aware Optimization for Robust Offline Meta-Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15428) <a id="link221"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15428
**Authors:** Chang-Hoon Jeong, Kisung Shin, Hanwool Sul, Byoung-Tak Zhang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15428v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Context-based offline meta-reinforcement learning (COMRL) has advanced almost entirely through better task representations, which separate task identity from artifacts of the behavior policy so that the latent reflects the task alone. The implicit expectation has been that a clean representation will transfer to out-of-support (parameter-extrapolation) tasks. On such a task, however, the encoder produces a latent outside the training support, where the policy has never been trained. We address this gap with Pessimistic Latent Task-aware Optimization (PLATO), which exposes the policy to off-support latents during training. PLATO assumes a mild manifold hypothesis: task latents lie on a low-dimensional manifold, and out-of-support tasks lie farther from the training centroid. It exploits this geometry by perturbing inferred latents outward from the centroid to synthesize counterfactual tasks. A learned decoder ensemble then rolls out short trajectories at each perturbed latent, and the policy is updated on the imagined transitions under a pessimism weight that down-weights steps on which the ensemble disagrees. We prove that the perturbation leaves the training support by a controllable margin and that the out-of-support generalization gap is bounded by the same perturbation reach, and we give explicit conditions, verified empirically, under which the epistemic disagreement at the perturbed latent stays bounded away from zero. On eight MuJoCo continuous-control benchmarks, PLATO consistently improves out-of-support returns over strong COMRL baselines while remaining competitive on in-distribution tasks. Our code is available at https://github.com/deephoony/plato.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1 partially: proposes a new method (PLATO) for robust offline meta-reinforcement learning with latent task-aware optimization, which relates to reinforcement learning methods improving generalization. However, it is not specifically about RLHF or instruction-following fine-tuning for language models.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

## 222. [Perfect Reconstruction Cannot Show Whether Two Codebooks on a VAE's Latent Space Agree](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14012) <a id="link222"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14012
**Authors:** Yusuke Hayashi

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14012v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Many systems read the latent space of a variational autoencoder (VAE) as discrete codes, and rely on each latent being read back as the code it was written as. Shannon (1948) took such a shared codebook as given; inside a VAE nothing enforces it. We prove that no item of a standard training report can reveal whether the written and read codes agree on encoded data. We construct two VAEs that reconstruct every input perfectly, infer exactly, and match on every per-example ELBO and every code histogram, yet agreement is one in the first and zero in the second. We then show that the ELBO's own terms bound how far a measured agreement can move: the variational gap bounds the shift to exact inference, and the rate bounds the shift to generation from the prior. For Gaussian posteriors, a mean-field encoder pays an exact price in the gap that depends on its choice of axes. On probabilistic PCA the gap certifies a trained encoder in all 75 settings we test, at agreement between 0.76 and 0.97. On 240 trained VAEs, a floor set by the rate and the dataset size rules out rate-side certification in advance for 66 models, and none of them certifies. The results yield a simple protocol, Certify-or-Measure, for any pipeline that reads a VAE as codes.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 7 partially: discusses theoretical properties of VAE latent code agreement and proposes a certification protocol. While not about sparse autoencoders per se, it relates to latent space coding and representation verification, which is close to sparse coding and interpretability.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

## 223. [Taming Softmax at the Limits of Muon: A Unified Approach through Hilbert Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17588) <a id="link223"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17588
**Authors:** Aditya Somasundaram, Charles Guille-Escuret, Alexander Moreno, Zhengzhong Liu, Eric Xing

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17588v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Muon controls weight updates through the RMS movement of a linear layer's output. This implicitly treats the linear output as a proxy for the full module, including the nonlinearity that follows. This proxy can be misleading for softmax, where the RMS shift in activations does not faithfully measure the change in the output distribution. We propose Hilbert distance as a natural metric for bounding changes in these output distributions. Hilbert distance bounds changes in cross-entropy loss and limits probability redistribution. For softmax, it takes the form of the variation seminorm of the preactivation perturbation, allowing probability constraints to be expressed as parameter constraints. Formulating the steepest descent problem with a Hilbert distance constraint brings two recently proposed methods under a unified mathematical framework. For the LM head, solving a row-wise approximation to the resulting diameter-constrained problem leads us to RowNorm \citep{pethick2025trainingdeeplearningmodels}. For scaled dot-product attention, the same principle yields the first-order query--key steepest descent problem underlying Compositional Muon \citep{keigwin2026compositional}. These connections motivate using Hilbert distance as a guiding principle for optimizer design in softmax modules.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 10: proposes a new metric (Hilbert distance) for bounding changes in softmax output distributions, unifying optimizer design approaches. This relates to tamper resistance and robustness in training dynamics of language models.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

## 224. [Avoiding What Your Teacher Already Knows: Entity Familiarity for Deep-Search Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16251) <a id="link224"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16251
**Authors:** Yifei Liu, Zhixiang Liang, Wentao Tan, Yicheng Wang, Jiaqi Wang, Nan Duan, Qiong Cao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16251v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Deep-search agents are commonly trained by distilling tool-use trajectories from stronger teacher models. However, structurally complex questions may remain relatively easy for teachers familiar with key entities in the underlying entity graph, resulting in lower search demand and weaker supervision. We refer to this teacher-specific variation in question difficulty as teacher-relative difficulty and investigate entity familiarity as an early predictor. We first introduce Evidence-Verified Entity Familiarity (EVF), which estimates familiarity by verifying a teacher-generated factual brief against online evidence. Across multiple teachers, EVF is significantly correlated with teacher-relative difficulty with AUCs of 0.60--0.70. However, its high cost makes it impractical for constructing entity graphs at scale. To address this limitation, we propose Self-Reported Entity Familiarity (SEF), a lightweight gating signal that directly asks a teacher whether it is familiar with an entity before admitting that entity into the graph. Across multiple teachers, trajectories from SEF-gated synthesis exhibit greater observed search demand and yield higher downstream student accuracy than those from ungated synthesis. Because familiarity is teacher specific, we further study cross-teacher reuse. We screen graphs collected for other teachers using Kimi-K3's SEF judgments and use the retained graphs to produce 5,411 additional trajectories. Combined with 2,200 trajectories from newly constructed, SEF-gated graphs, these data improve Qwen3.8-27B's accuracy from 44.10% to 66.41% on a BrowseComp subset, and achieving state-of-the-art performance among models of comparable size on BrowseComp, SEAL-0, HLE-text, and xbench. Overall, SEF provides a low-cost way to synthesize more informative supervision for deep-search distillation.

**Comment:** This paper proposes Self-Reported Entity Familiarity (SEF) to improve deep-search distillation by gating entity inclusion based on teacher familiarity. This is a data-centric method to improve instruction-following supervision but does not directly propose new RLHF or instruction-tuning fine-tuning methods (criterion 1).
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

## 225. [Do LLMs differentiate linguistic traits internally?: A layer-wise analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15203) <a id="link225"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15203
**Authors:** Samhita Bollepally, Takashi Yamauchi, Manju Muralee, Motunrayo Adebimpe

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15203v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language models often produce judgments that resemble human responses, but it remains unclear whether they internally capture linguistic nuances such as conventional versus idiomatic meaning. A layer-wise representational analysis was conducted on two equal-sized pre-trained models, Llama-3.2-1B and Gemma-3-1B-PT, to examine whether and where these models differentiate linguistic traits from input. The analysis combined layer-wise probing with geometric distance measures, using the top embedding dimensions ranked by principal component loadings for each layer. The results show that even with equal capacity, models organize linguistic information in fundamentally different ways, suggesting the need to analyze internal representational geometry, examining not just behavioral outputs but semantic space alignment when evaluating how closely human and model language interpretation correspond.

**Comment:** Partially relevant to criterion 8: conducts layer-wise representational analysis of LLMs to understand internal differentiation of linguistic traits, contributing to mechanistic interpretability though not full circuit-level reverse engineering.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 5

## 226. [RSIPO: Recursive Self-Improving Policy Optimization for Agentic Reinforcement Learning via Skill-Policy Co-Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15772) <a id="link226"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15772
**Authors:** Fan Zhang, Hao Wu, Jinyang Wu, Hongyu Qu, Zheng Lian, Xiao Luo, Yefeng Zheng, Bo Zhang, Haoxuan Li, Pheng-Ann Heng

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15772v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Recent focus in agentic reinforcement learning (RL) seeks to improve credit assignment in long-horizon tasks. One promising direction combines reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) and on-policy self-distillation (OPSD), integrating trajectory-level advantages with dense token-level supervision. This line of work often conditions a teacher model on privileged context, such as agentic skills, to score tokens generated by the student policy. Despite their progress, they largely overlook the on-policy evolution of agentic skills and the reliability of teacher supervision. Towards this end, we introduce Recursive Self-Improving Policy Optimization (RSIPO), a self-evolving agentic RL framework that enables agentic skills and the policy model to co-evolve throughout the training process. Specifically, RSIPO enables the policy itself to extract skills from completed trajectories and explicitly optimizes its skill-generation capability through trajectory replay. It further employs empirical Bayes shrinkage to dynamically calibrate the student's trust in teacher scores, accounting for the fact that skill-enhanced teacher guidance can sometimes be less reliable. Experiments with Qwen-family models on embodied interaction and search-based agentic tasks demonstrate that RSIPO outperforms all the compared baseline approaches. With Qwen2.5-7B as the base model, RSIPO achieves an average success rate of 98.6\% on ALFWorld, yielding absolute improvements of 2.5\% and 17.4\% over the second-best baseline and standard GRPO, respectively.

**Comment:** Partially relevant to criterion 1 (new methodological improvements to RLHF or instruction-following) since it proposes a new agentic RL framework with self-improving policy optimization and teacher-student dynamics, but it is focused on embodied interaction and agentic RL rather than instruction-following in language models. No explicit mention of RLHF or instruction tuning.
**Relevance:** 4
**Novelty:** 6

## 227. [LeHami: Symplectic Latent Dynamics for World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15267) <a id="link227"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15267
**Authors:** Romina Yalovetzky, Randall Balestriero, Yann LeCun

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15267v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Joint-embedding predictive architectures (JEPAs) learn world models directly in latent space, but unconstrained autoregressive predictors may contract the latent distribution over long rollouts. We introduce Symplectic Hamiltonian JEPA (LeHami), which replaces the standard predictor with an action-conditioned Hamiltonian flow over a structured latent state (q, p) integrated using an exactly symplectic leapfrog step. This construction preserves phase-space volume at every rollout step and rules out uniform contraction, asymptotically stable fixed points, and merging of distinct latent states, independently of the learned parameters or action sequence. We obtain the momentum-like component from temporal derivatives of the learned position-like representation, providing an explicit second-order state for prediction and control. On conservative and actuated visual pendulum environments, LeHami substantially improves long-horizon velocity prediction over an unconstrained JEPA world model and empirically maintains constant latent volume where the baseline contracts. The learned flow also supports accurate integration at unseen sub-frame step sizes and numerical time reversal. These results show how geometric structure can provide inference-time guarantees that are complementary to representation regularization in latent world models.

**Comment:** This paper introduces LeHami, a symplectic latent dynamics model for world models, improving long-horizon prediction by preserving phase-space volume. It is a generative modeling advance in latent dynamics but does not focus on language models or diffusion language models specifically.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 7

## 228. [Coarse-Graining Recursive Expert Computation: Continuation-Relative Interfaces and Failure Modes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14413) <a id="link228"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14413
**Authors:** Shihua Yu, Yanzhi Wang, Tianchi Chen

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14413v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Computational coarse-graining aims to replace a sequence of updates with a cheaper module while preserving task performance. We present a continuation-relative evaluation framework that assesses replacements through the computation that follows them, using downstream task quality and total execution cost as joint criteria. The framework distinguishes boundary representations, summaries of skipped computation, executed operations, and downstream loss. It makes interfaces and permitted deployment information explicit, combines fine-path references with directly trained controls using the same shortened architecture, and accounts for selection overhead. An exact loss decomposition separates replacement effects at a fixed downstream action from effects of changing that action. A finite construction shows how retained information can reduce the stipulated cost of zero-error prediction under fixed information and controller restrictions. Experiments in two frozen pretrained language models show that representation fidelity and task loss can favor different fitting objectives. In a prespecified comparison on a 1.7-billion-parameter model, next-token-loss training lowers in-distribution negative log-likelihood by 0.1901 nats per scored token compared with reconstructing the skipped blocks' output, using the same optimizer and update count. This ranking reverses in all nine paired comparisons under distribution shift. In a recursive expert prototype, the jointly adapted candidate lowers task error relative to frozen-bank and router-only baselines, but trails directly trained controls, and the tested input-only selectors fail the joint quality-cost criteria. Together, the protocol and studies provide explicit criteria and diagnostics for distinguishing useful computational replacement from local approximation gains within specified interfaces and evaluation settings.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criteria. The paper proposes a computational coarse-graining framework for recursive expert computation in pretrained language models, focusing on efficiency and replacement effects rather than instruction-following, contamination, or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 4
**Novelty:** 6

## 229. [Terrarium: Growing Personal Assistants via Scalable Synthesis of App Worlds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14860) <a id="link229"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14860
**Authors:** Mengzhou Wu, Yuzhe Guo, Jiawen Tao, Xiaokun Yuan, Yuan Cao, Gang Cao, Yuetang Deng, Zhi Gong, Dezhi Ran, Linyi Li, Wei Yang, Tao Xie

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14860v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Advances in large language models (LLMs) have brought the vision of a general-purpose personal assistant closer to reality. Such assistants could provide a unified natural-language interface to a broad ecosystem of applications. However, real-world applications do not readily support training at scale because access is often restricted and their state is difficult to reset. To address this challenge, we introduce Terrarium, a framework that synthesizes executable App Worlds from real-world application information, generates training task packages with evaluator-side rubrics from these worlds, and iteratively validates and refines both through agent rollouts. For App World synthesis, Terrarium organizes public Web information and in-application observations into structured app specifications that guide the construction of self-contained runtimes with callable operations and mutable, resettable state. For task synthesis, Terrarium constructs tasks grounded in App World state together with evaluator-side outcome rubrics that assess success without prescribing reference trajectories. We construct a training suite of 275 App Worlds and 13,344 tasks for outcome-based reinforcement learning across three Qwen3 model scales (4B, 8B, and 14B parameters). Across these scales, models trained with Terrarium outperform the corresponding Qwen3 models by an average of 18.34 percentage points on the $\tau^2$-bench and 10.94 percentage points on the AppWorld bench. These results suggest that app-grounded world and task synthesis offers a scalable path toward general-purpose assistants capable of operating across broad application ecosystems.

**Comment:** Related to instruction-following and RLHF-like training but focuses on synthesizing app environments and tasks for training personal assistants; does not explicitly discuss RLHF or instruction-tuning methods per se.
**Relevance:** 4
**Novelty:** 6

## 230. [Sequent-Prover: Training Agents for Formal, Checkable SMT-based Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15258) <a id="link230"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15258
**Authors:** Debargha Ganguly, Weicong Chen, Mohsen Hariri, Vikash Singh, Sreehari Sankar, Shouren Wang, Wang Yang, Biyao Zhang, Chaoda Song, Alexander Nemecek, Kai Ye, Osama Zafar, Erman Ayday, Joe Stubbs, Srinivasan Iyengar, Vipin Chaudhary

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15258v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: We introduce Sequent-Prover, a family of verifier-using language-model agents, and the post-training framework that produces them. Agents reason in an executable SMT-LIB scratchpad: they write complete SMT-LIB programs, query Z3 across turns, inspect solver feedback, repair formalizations, and terminate with an explicit answer. Our recipe, verifier-conditioned trajectory distillation, bootstraps from a noisy teacher proposal policy by sampling multi-turn trajectories, retaining the successful tail that is correct, non-trivial, grounded, and executable, then applying supervised fine-tuning and trajectory-level preference optimization. Because solver success alone does not guarantee semantic faithfulness, we evaluate task accuracy, k-sample reliability, and SMT program quality. Across 37 benchmarks and 7,254 problems per model family, post-training raises the 4B model's per-attempt accuracy from 85.6% to 97.0% and four-sample all-correct reliability (pass^4}) from 64.1% to 91.7%. It also reduces average SMT calls from 4.10 to 2.52 and tokens per attempt from 18.97k to 12.72k. Sequent-Prover agents give answers backed by executable formal artifacts.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criteria. Paper presents a method for training language model agents to perform formal SMT-based reasoning with verifier-conditioned trajectory distillation, which is a reasoning and formal verification approach but not about RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 4
**Novelty:** 6

## 231. [Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon Agentic Environments with Dense Progress Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18868) <a id="link231"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18868
**Authors:** Wenbo Hu, Shraman Pramanick, Krishna Kumar Singh, Nanyun Peng, Kai-Wei Chang, Yong Jae Lee

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18868v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Long-horizon agents have drawn growing attention for their ability to work autonomously -- from hours to days -- on economically valuable tasks, a capability essential to automated discovery and recursive self-improvement (RSI). Yet open-source models still struggle on long-horizon tasks, largely for lack of both training data and a working recipe, and scaling both is hard: long, complex tasks are difficult to curate and verify, and their rewards are sparse. We address both. First, we introduce Tev-Gym, the first long-horizon agent training dataset with verifiable, dense, progressive rewards -- 1,170 verified task environments across 7 domains. It is built on a latent-world-first approach that first constructs a latent world: a complete and correct hidden specification of the task domain, workflow, and expected state after each step. Each task is then progressively rendered from this specification into an agent-facing environment, enabling controllable complexity and a natural progress metric. Second, we turn this metric into a training recipe: via progress-potential reward shaping (PPRS), we convert progress into a dense per-step reward that improves training efficiency and makes otherwise-intractable tasks trainable. Training with this recipe yields Tev-27B, which achieves substantial gains on the Agents' Last Exam computing and math sciences domains (ALE-CM), matching GPT-6 Astra and Claude Opus 5 at Pass@5. Despite training only on ALE-related data, it generalizes to the Terminal-Bench (TB) 3.0 and 4.0 subsets, surpassing GLM 5.2 and Gemini 3.7 Flash, and reaches Claude Opus 4.8-level performance on middle-horizon tasks such as TB 2.1.

**Comment:** Partially relevant to criterion 1: introduces a training recipe with dense progress rewards for long-horizon agents, improving training efficiency. However, focus is on agentic environments and task-specific training rather than general instruction-following or RLHF improvements.
**Relevance:** 4
**Novelty:** 6

## 232. [To Defer or Not to Defer? Online Learning with LLMs and Congested Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15128) <a id="link232"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15128
**Authors:** Vasileios Saketos, William Reveillard, Alexandre Proutiere, Sebastian Dalleiger, Ming Xiao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15128v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Modern AI systems increasingly combine large language models (LLMs) with human experts, using the LLM to handle routine queries while deferring uncertain cases for expert review. When expert capacity is limited, however, deferral decisions must account not only for the reliability of the LLM but also for congestion in the expert queue. We formulate this as an online sequential decision-making problem in which, for each query, the system observes the confidence signal of the LLM's answer and must decide whether to output the answer or defer the query to a capacity-constrained expert. The reliability of the confidence signal is initially unknown and must be learned from expert feedback, which is both selectively observed (only deferred queries are labeled) and delayed by queuing. We propose \texttt{KL-PBD}, a learning algorithm that uses Kullback--Leibler lower confidence bounds on the unknown reliability parameters to guide deferral decisions. For $K$ confidence levels and horizon $T$, \texttt{KL-PBD} achieves $O(K\log T)$ gap-dependent regret when the accuracy at every confidence level is bounded away from the deferral boundary, and $\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{KT})$ worst-case regret. We establish matching lower bounds, showing that these rates are optimal up to logarithmic factors. Finally, we validate our approach on synthetic instances and real-world question-answering datasets, namely GSM8K, NuminaMath, and MedMCQA, using Qwen LLMs.

**Comment:** Studies online learning for deferral decisions between LLMs and experts with regret bounds; interesting statistical learning but not about RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, contamination, or diffusion models.
**Relevance:** 4
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 233. [SoK: Rethinking Membership Inference in Tabular Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18486) <a id="link233"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18486
**Authors:** Cristina P\^era, T\^ania Carvalho, Maxime Cordy, Lu\'is Antunes

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18486v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Membership Inference Attacks (MIAs) are currently the dominant approach to evaluating privacy in machine learning applications. Despite their significance, several concerns remain unexplored, particularly with regard to tabular data. Existing evaluations focus predominantly on high-dimensional datasets, leaving the privacy behavior of small-to-medium datasets unexplored. In this paper, we present a new taxonomy for MIAs and conduct a comprehensive study of the factors influencing vulnerability. We evaluate four distinct state-of-the-art attacks across diverse tabular datasets and model architectures. Our results show that datasets with higher class granularity and majority class percentage are more susceptible to MIAs. We further demonstrate that advanced MIAs are highly dependent on adversarial knowledge of the target model, while reference model-free attacks often achieve statistically comparable results. Most importantly, we show that records with unique signatures (single-outs) remain highly vulnerable, even when common defenses are applied. These findings indicate that current defenses reduce average leakage, but fail to adequately protect the most sensitive individuals.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 2: membership inference attacks (MIAs) with new taxonomy and empirical findings on vulnerability factors in tabular ML models. Although focused on tabular data, it studies membership inference methods relevant to language models in principle.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 5

---

## 234. [Sparse-TD-JEPA: Improving Zero-Shot Reinforcement Learning With Sparse Latent Task Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14400) <a id="link234"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14400
**Authors:** Duaa Nakshbandi, Zhiyu An, Wan Du

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14400v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Successor-feature-based zero-shot reinforcement learning methods pre-train a task encoder and successor-feature predictor on offline, reward-free data, then recover a policy at test time by solving a closed-form linear regression over a small labeled dataset. We identify an underexplored limitation of this approach: when the task encoder produces dense representations, the inferred reward signal becomes diffuse across the state space, reducing the contrast between goal-relevant states and the background and providing a weaker signal for the policy to follow. We address this limitation by introducing an l1 sparsity penalty that concentrates reward-predictive structure into a smaller effective subspace of the task encoder's latent space. The penalty is applied only during training and requires no architectural change or modification to the closed-form inference procedure. We instantiate this approach as Sparse-TD-JEPA, a single-line of code addition based on Temporal-Difference Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture (TD-JEPA) that significantly improved its performance in zero-shot reinforcement learning. We evaluate Sparse-TD-JEPA on 65 tasks across the DeepMind Control (DMC) Suite and OGBench, covering locomotion, navigation, trajectory stitching, puzzle solving, and robotic manipulation. Sparse-TD-JEPA improves over TD-JEPA in all 13 domain-level comparisons. On DMC, it increases the average return from 661 +- 6 to 714 +- 3. On OGBench, it increases the average success rate from 38.0 +- 0.8 to 48.4 +- 2.3, with improvements of 13.4-18.0 percentage points on the evaluated antmaze navigation and stitching domains. Overall, Sparse-TD-JEPA achieves the highest mean performance among established zero-shot baselines in nine of the 13 domains. Analysis further shows that sparsity produces a sharper and more task-informative inferred reward landscape.

**Comment:** Related to criterion 1 in a broad sense: proposes a sparsity penalty to improve zero-shot RL performance by concentrating latent task representations, but this is reinforcement learning and not instruction-following fine-tuning for language models. No explicit mention of RLHF or instruction tuning.
**Relevance:** 4
**Novelty:** 6

## 235. [Acceptable Misalignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16881) <a id="link235"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16881
**Authors:** Maud van Lier, Ludovica Marinucci, Vieri Giuliano Santucci, Raja Chatila, St\'ephane Doncieux, \'Emiland Garrab\'e, Richard Duro

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16881v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: There is a specific challenge designers face in developing autonomous robots capable of open-ended learning. This challenge is how one can develop such systems in an ethically sound way when there are many unknowns during design time about their manner of deployment, what they can and will learn, and how these systems will act on the basis of what they have learned. One response to this challenge is to align, as far as possible, the activities and goals of autonomous robots with human values, raising the question of how to make this alignment possible, a question known as the value alignment problem. We argue that the value alignment problem faces a number of challenges that show that full alignment is both theoretically and practically impossible, meaning that misalignment incidents will keep occurring. This undermines current alignment approaches, as the attempt to maximize alignment cannot properly deal with the possibility that the risk of misalignment that remains after alignment methods have been implemented might still be unacceptable to society. To overcome this difficulty, we argue that a shift in perspective is required from a focus on the maximization of alignment to the conditions under which misalignment incidents become acceptable. In this paper, we explore what this shift in perspective entails. We introduce the concept of acceptable misalignment, formulate normative requirements for alignment mechanisms aimed at bringing misalignment incidents to an acceptable level, and we conclude by presenting a new methodological framework that consists of alignment mechanisms that can meet these requirements.

**Comment:** This paper discusses value alignment and proposes a new methodological framework for alignment mechanisms, which relates to AI alignment techniques (criterion 11). However, it is more conceptual and philosophical rather than specific fine-tuning or RLHF improvements.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 5

---

## 236. [Mixture of GFlowNets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17007) <a id="link236"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17007
**Authors:** Omar G. Younis, Joseph D. Viviano, Chirayu Anant Haryan, MD Vasimuddin, Sanchit Misra, Yoshua Bengio

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17007v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Generative Flow Networks (GFlowNets) learn a policy that samples compositional objects in proportion to an unnormalized reward $R(x)$. This makes them useful wherever many diverse, high-reward candidates are needed and oracle queries are expensive. In practice, a single GFlowNet often fails to cover all modes of a complex distribution, and the obvious ways to scale compute saturate: larger batches and data-parallel gradient averaging draw every trajectory from one, possibly biased, policy, so extra rollouts rediscover known modes instead of finding new ones. We explore whether this mode-bias problem (mode collapse in the extreme) can be addressed with a population of asynchronously trained policies which divide the state space between them. Mixture of GFlowNets (MoG) replaces the single forward policy with a community of $K$ independent GFlowNets coupled through one jointly trained soft classifier, which assigns each member its own region of the state space through reward shaping. Mixing the members under their learned partition functions provably recovers the target $p(x)\propto R(x)$. MoG exchange (MoGX) also lets the classifier route sampled trajectories: a trajectory the classifier assigns to another member is sent to that member and trained on there, with no importance sampling. We evaluate both methods by keeping the environment-interaction budget fixed while scaling the community size, turning parallel compute into mode coverage without extra oracle calls. On HyperGrid, across six reward landscapes and communities of up to $K{=}64$, MoG and MoGX keep finding new modes as $K$ grows, while data-parallel training saturates. On a genomic ideotype-design task, MoGX covers $39%$ of the $1024$ optima at $K{=}64$, against $31\%$ for independent agents and $20\%$ for data-parallel training. MoG also has a lower per-iteration synchronization cost than data-parallel training.

**Comment:** This paper proposes Mixture of GFlowNets, a method to improve mode coverage in generative flow networks. While it is a generative modeling advance, it is not about language models or diffusion language models, nor about instruction-following or contamination detection, so it does not match the criteria.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 7

## 237. [D2: High-Capability Full-Duplex Spoken Language Models from Half-Duplex Backbones](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17124) <a id="link237"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17124
**Authors:** Cheng-Kuang Chang, Alexander H. Liu, Brian Kingsbury, Hilde Kuehne, Rogerio Feris, Samuel Thomas, James Glass, Kai-Wei Chang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17124v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Full-duplex spoken language models (FD-SLMs) support simultaneous listening and speaking, but often lag behind half-duplex spoken language models (HD-SLMs) in general capabilities. We propose Dialogue-to-Duplex (D2), a framework that converts pretrained HD-SLMs into high-capability FD-SLMs at fine temporal granularity. D2 adapts the original audio encoder to streaming through decoder-free representation distillation and learns full-duplex interaction through supervised fine-tuning. We systematically study the effects of chunk size on full-duplex interaction, general capabilities, and inference computation. We apply D2 to two distinct HD-SLM architectures, Qwen3-Omni and LLaMA-Omni 2. At a 12.5Hz update rate, Qwen3-Omni-D2 achieves 99.2% turn-taking success with 521-ms latency, 99.5% interruption success, and a state-of-the-art VoiceBench score of 70.1.

**Comment:** Not matching criteria. Focuses on spoken language models and full-duplex interaction, not on RLHF, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms for text generation.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 238. [Uncertainty Evaluation: Bridging Machine Learning and Measurement Science](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18066) <a id="link238"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18066
**Authors:** Dario Petri, Luca Mari

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18066v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reliable uncertainty evaluation is essential for assessing the trustworthiness of machine learning (ML) predictions, particularly in safety-critical applications. This paper explores conceptual analogies between uncertainty evaluation in ML and measurement science, drawing on the evolution of metrology from error-based approaches to the framework established by the Guide to the Expression of Uncertainty in Measurement (GUM). Particular attention is devoted to the distinction between process-level and result-level quality and to the limitations of the conventional classification of ML uncertainty into aleatoric and epistemic components. It is argued that this dichotomy, similarly to the traditional distinction between random and systematic measurement errors, is inherently model-dependent and provides an insufficient basis for a unified uncertainty evaluation framework. Further analogies between machine learning and measurement field of knowledge are identified concerning distribution shifts, anomalous operating conditions, and the validity of uncertainty evaluation. Building on the proposed considerations, the paper advocates a metrology-inspired approach based on the systematic identification, quantification, and propagation of uncertainty contributions. By extending the methodology already established in measurement science to ML, this approach could offer a potentially rigorous, consistent, and transferable framework for evaluating the trustworthiness of individual predictions.

**Comment:** Related to criterion 18: discusses uncertainty evaluation in ML with a metrology-inspired approach, which is a data-centric methodology for assessing trustworthiness of predictions, but does not specifically address instruction-following, RLHF, or language models.
**Relevance:** 4
**Novelty:** 5

## 239. [Narrative Paradoxes in LLM Inference: Shaping Reasoning Trajectories toward Misalignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16312) <a id="link239"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16312
**Authors:** Haoming Yang, Ke Ma, Yangbangyan Jiang, Ligong Zhang, Xiaojun Jia, Longtao Huang, Yingfei Sun, Qianqian Xu, Qingming Huang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16312v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Explicit reasoning in large language models (LLMs) is often associated with reliability, yet it can still pose risks when a model infers harmful goals under a benign context. We identify a distinct challenge: ensuring the safety of the \emph{multi-step reasoning process} itself, where locally coherent steps may still lead to globally harmful outcomes. To explore this vulnerability, we propose PDRA (Paradox-driven Reasoning-based Attack), a novel attack method that programs a harmful reasoning trajectory. PDRA first constructs a paradoxical narrative core by pairing a malicious actor (e.g., a terrorist) with a benign task (e.g., writing a safety bulletin). It then embeds this core into a structured analytical framework that forces the model through a mandatory three-stage analysis -- surface purpose, latent intent, and operational reconstruction -- guiding it to produce detailed harmful content while maintaining narrative coherence. Mechanistically, we show PDRA steers the model's internal representations away from refusal-related patterns, suppressing safety-triggering lexicons while activating planning-oriented terms. Empirically, PDRA achieves state-of-the-art attack success rates across diverse LLMs with high efficiency. \textcolor{red}{\textbf{Warning:} This paper may contain sensitive content.}

**Comment:** Not relevant to any criteria: Describes a novel attack method (PDRA) to induce harmful reasoning trajectories in LLMs, related to safety and adversarial attacks but not matching criteria on RLHF improvements, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6
