## 240. [Uncovering the Generative Landscape of Diffusion Models: A Spectral-Guided Quality-Diversity Diffusion Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15724) <a id="link240"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15724
**Authors:** Hannah Janmohamed, Joao Correia, Penousal Machado, Antoine Cully

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15724v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Models have emerged as powerful generative priors across domains ranging from molecular design to high-resolution image synthesis. However, standard diffusion sampling is inherently biased toward high-likelihood regions, often limiting the diversity of outputs. In many domains, a single, high-likelihood sample is insufficient. Instead, diverse portfolios of high-performing solutions are required, motivating methods that uncover a model's full expressive range. Here we show that integrating Quality-Diversity (QD) optimisation with diffusion models enables the discovery of diverse, high-performing outputs. We first propose Quality-Diversity Diffusion, a framework that adapts QD to diffusion models by directly optimising the model's latent noise and conditioning embeddings and leveraging embedding models to quantify diversity. Second, we introduce a novel h-space Spectral Mutation operator, which guides search along directions of maximal semantic sensitivity by analysing principal singular vectors of the model's input-to-activation Jacobian. We evaluate our combined approach, Spectral-Guided Quality-Diversity Diffusion, on text-to-image generation. Against 12 baselines, our method consistently achieves the lowest FID score, demonstrating an excellent balance of image quality and diversity.

**Comment:** Partially relevant to criterion 3 (advances in diffusion language models) since it proposes a Quality-Diversity Diffusion framework and spectral-guided mutation for diffusion models. However, the application is text-to-image generation, not language modeling specifically, so less relevant to language models in NLP.

## 241. [Online LLM Personalization from Pairwise Preferences Across Users](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17203) <a id="link241"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17203
**Authors:** Romain Puech, Georgy Salakhutdinov, Dimitris Bertsimas

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17203v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language models can answer the same question in different styles, but users' preferred styles are hidden and vary across users and questions. We study inference-time personalization through online selection from a fixed set of prompts, each representing a persona. Users arrive sequentially, each asks a short sequence of questions, and the system displays two responses per question and observes one pairwise choice. We formulate this interaction as a variant of the contextual dueling bandit problem in which the learner starts without preference data, adapts within each session, and exploits information shared across users. The objective is to minimize cumulative strong regret, summing the utility gaps of both displayed responses relative to the user's best response among the available options. We make two contributions. First, we introduce the problem and release a reproducible preference benchmark and dataset built around a tutoring case study, containing educational questions, LLM-generated answers in six teaching styles, and pairwise judgments from LLM-simulated students with hidden preference profiles. We fit Bradley-Terry models to these judgments to obtain reference utilities for regret evaluation. Second, we propose a baseline that learns a mixture prior over users from completed sessions and adapts to each new user within the session. On students and topics never used in development, it removes 44-48% of random pairs' excess regret and achieves over half the performance of a per-user linear oracle fitted in hindsight. We release code, prompts, and the benchmark.

**Comment:** This paper studies online LLM personalization from pairwise preferences, formulating it as a contextual dueling bandit problem and releasing a benchmark dataset. It focuses on inference-time personalization and preference learning rather than instruction-following fine-tuning or RLHF. It is relevant to real-world usage and user preference modeling but does not match the specific criteria for instruction tuning or RLHF improvements.
**Relevance:** 4

## 242. [Drafting Agents, Not Just Tokens: Speculative Recursive Self-Improvement for Agentic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16491) <a id="link242"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16491
**Authors:** Xin Xu, Ke Cheng, Hao Wang, Yangru Huang, Yanghao Xiao, Zhichao Chen, Zhisheng Zhong, Xun Liu, Yi Li, Qian Li, Haibin Lv, Jun Zhang, Jie Jiang, Zhouchen Lin

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16491v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Improving an agent through successive revisions requires balancing exploration and evaluation under a limited budget. A narrow search can miss useful sequences of edits, while fully evaluating every intermediate version can exhaust the budget before those sequences yield a verified improvement. We introduce Speculative Recursive Self-Improvement (SpecRSI), which drafts executable successor agents before their predecessors are fully evaluated, while requiring complete verification before adoption. Verification-Aware Tree Search (VATS) allocates the budget between further edits and outstanding checks using the predicted verified return of a continuation. Group Verification Optimization (GVO) learns the improver from completed searches while keeping the real evaluator fixed. In the main scientific comparison, terminal gains exceed Janus by 33.3%--40.0%. Additional evaluations show that SpecRSI outperforms Janus in all 50 scientific backbone--task pairs and exceeds MetaRSI-v1 by 1.85%--2.38% in macro-average system score across twenty backbones. Coding-validation success reaches 69.4% versus HGM's 64.3% at a 20.8% lower target-cost index.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criterion. The paper proposes Speculative Recursive Self-Improvement for agentic systems, focusing on agent improvement and evaluation budget allocation, but does not address RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms for LLMs.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 243. [DeltaNet-PC: Test-Time Memory Without Backpropagation Through Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15185) <a id="link243"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15185
**Authors:** Todd Morrill, Richard Zemel

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15185v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Test-time memorization of sequential information is a foundational capability for any intelligent system that needs to rapidly store and retrieve data, as demonstrated by the attention mechanism in large language models. However, Transformers and linear attention variants are trained via backpropagation through time (BPTT), which has drawbacks such as its enormous memory requirements. Predictive coding (PC), an algorithm that optimizes a prediction-error energy function with local updates, offers a compelling solution but still lacks test-time memory for sequential data. We develop a solution to this problem by treating the per-step memory update $\Delta S_t$ as a latent variable, as opposed to a slow weight to be learned. By taking the log probability of a standard PC-based probabilistic model, we derive a PC-native solution for verbatim sequence memory, and relate our update rule to common choices in the linear attention literature (e.g., DeltaNet). We present an extensive set of empirical tests to probe our system's properties, including its scaling capacity, memory footprint, and ability to infer a context from evidence (e.g., bank (river) vs. bank (money)). We show how DeltaNet-PC can be used for storing single sequences of data, multiple sequences of data, and key-value associative memory pairs with perfect recall accuracy.

**Comment:** Relevant to general sequential memory and predictive coding in language models, but does not match any specific criteria such as RLHF improvements, test set contamination, or interpretability. It proposes a test-time memory update method without backpropagation through time, which is interesting but not directly aligned with the requested criteria.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 244. [When Skills Remain But Access Fails: Factorized Skill Memory for Continual Skill Internalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15492) <a id="link244"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15492
**Authors:** Haomiao Qiu, Yining Tang, Hongyi Wan, Changfeng Sun, Ziyue Qiao, Miao Zhang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15492v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Skill internalization aims to convert externally specified procedural skills into persistent model capabilities, allowing language-model agents to execute them without retrieving or reinjecting skill descriptions at inference time. Yet when new skills are learned continually, whether previously internalized skills remain usable is still poorly understood. We study forgetting in continual skill internalization and uncover a clear hierarchical structure. Early in subsequent training, a model may lose the ability to invoke an old skill from the raw request, while supplying only the correct canonical skill name substantially restores the learned behavior; with further optimization, this conditional execution also degrades. We call these two stages skill-addressing forgetting and skill-execution forgetting. Guided by this finding, we introduce Factorized Skill Memory (FSM), which separates continual skill memory into a plastic Address Memory and a stable Execution Memory. At each stage, FSM trains a new LoRA executor using only current-stage data, freezes it after acquisition, and appends it to Execution Memory; in parallel, Address Memory incrementally learns to map current requests to executors without replaying historical requests or execution trajectories. At inference time, Address Memory selects one executor from the raw request, and only that frozen executor performs a single language-model generation. On a five-stage benchmark built from 100 TaskBench skills, FSM achieves 65.95% mean exact match and 93.38% addressing accuracy across two task orders, improving over the strongest replay-free parameter-isolation baseline by 14.3 percentage points while remaining within 1.9 points of Replay, which retains historical training examples.

**Comment:** This paper studies continual skill internalization in language-model agents, focusing on forgetting phenomena and proposing Factorized Skill Memory (FSM) to mitigate forgetting. While it relates to language model capabilities and continual learning, it does not explicitly address RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, or test contamination.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 245. [Same Function, Different Trajectory: No Standard Optimizer Is Equivariant to Transformer Reparameterization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18085) <a id="link245"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18085
**Authors:** Bum Jun Kim

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18085v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: We show that two Transformers representing the same function can follow different training trajectories. For example, doubling a query matrix and halving its key matrix preserves the model's function but changes the gradient scales seen by the optimizer. Our theoretical analysis establishes that standard block-local first-order optimizers fail to scale updates with parameters under a shared learning rate. For AdamW, transforming the first- and second-moment estimates alone does not correct the mismatch between parameter and update scales. To make runs restarted from equivalent checkpoints follow the same trajectory, we balance matrix pairs and transform their moment estimates together, bringing distinct but equivalent training states to the same starting point. To correct scale imbalance between paired Transformer matrices without changing predictions, we propose Symmetry-Aware Gradient Geometry Stabilization (SAGGS), which periodically equalizes the Frobenius norms of exact symmetry pairs, and its damped variant, Soft SAGGS. SAGGS reduces perplexity (PPL) by $0.374\pm0.026$ at 101M parameters on WikiText-103, and Soft SAGGS reduces PPL by $0.780\pm0.040$ in a 31M modern-architecture replication, both under scale imbalance. In a 123.8M checkpoint-resume experiment, reciprocally rescaling matrix pairs barely changes validation loss at restart but increases final PPL by $3.78\pm0.42$; hard and soft rebalancing recover to within $0.02$ PPL of the control.

**Comment:** This paper studies optimizer behavior and training trajectory equivalences in Transformers, proposing Symmetry-Aware Gradient Geometry Stabilization (SAGGS) to improve training stability and perplexity. It is a methodological improvement in training Transformers but does not address RLHF, instruction-following, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 246. [When Should Enterprise Policy Decisions Be Learned, and When Should They Be Reasoned?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17643) <a id="link246"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17643
**Authors:** Hyojung Han, Ryangkyung Kang, Youngeun Kim, Seung-Hun Jeon

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17643v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: An enterprise agent that answers under a written policy must choose an action before it says anything: answer, ask for a missing fact, ask for confirmation, call one or several tools, escalate, or refuse. Holding one 27B Korean enterprise model fixed, we vary whether the decision is a generated JSON object or a single constrained token, and whether the model may reason first.   On 2800 in-distribution items we did not detect an accuracy difference between the two deployed paths (93.46% vs 94.14%), while they differ about tenfold in restart stability (3.00% of actions flip vs 0.29%); they also differ in reasoning, so this is an operational comparison.   On a sealed benchmark of 300 items whose labels come from a hidden executable policy, reasoning is worth +17.3 points [+12.3, +23.0] on exact action. With reasoning off, the format difference was 0.0 points [-4.3, +4.0], a failure to detect an advantage for either interface, not equivalence; with reasoning on it is unresolved. Against a token-matched random control, boundary-targeted supervision moves the two covered boundaries by +17.5 and +20.0 points and leaves a held-out boundary unchanged. In a 3-seed training x reasoning factorial, the sealed-rendering interaction is -4.9 [-8.1, -1.7], consistent with partial substitution.   All supervision was Korean. On independently rendered English versions, the model is 0.82 points lower [-2.68, +0.93] with reasoning on, the same order as re-rendering in Korean; the reasoning-off gain from targeted supervision is not stable in either language, so we do not claim transfer.   The original text stays private as the confirmatory set. An open-model re-rendering made after these results reproduced the reasoning effect and the English verdict; the interaction kept its sign but did not replicate under item-clustered intervals. We release the model and that re-rendering with gold labels.

**Comment:** This paper studies reasoning and decision-making formats in a large Korean enterprise model, comparing reasoning on/off and supervision effects. It involves reasoning capabilities and operational evaluation but does not focus on instruction-following fine-tuning or new evaluation paradigms. It partially relates to criterion 12 (LLM reasoning capabilities) but not strongly.

## 247. [Belief Embedding Tree Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15046) <a id="link247"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15046
**Authors:** Christopher Solinas, Radovan Halu\v{s}ka, Martin Schmid, Michael Buro, Nathan R. Sturtevant, Michael Bowling

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15046v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Beliefs in a Partially Observable Markov Decision Process (POMDP) are posterior distributions over the hidden state given the agent's history of actions and observations. They summarize everything the history says about the state, so planning in a POMDP is planning in a Markov decision process (MDP) over beliefs. Belief MDPs have continuous state spaces even for finite POMDPs, and exact planning in them is intractable in general. Planners must therefore approximate beliefs and estimate values. Monte Carlo planners do both inside a search tree, with an approximate belief at every node and a value estimate at every leaf. Approximations usually consist of sets of sampled states, with leaf values coming from rollouts, hand-designed bounds, or learned networks. Policies and values computed from these approximations are limited by their quality, and prior methods with learned networks rely on hand-chosen summaries of the sampled states. A belief representation should be computable from the parent node's representation through the POMDP's dynamics, let the search sample from it, and be learned so that it provides the information that policies and value functions need. We present Belief Embedding Tree Search (BETS), a Monte Carlo planner that meets these requirements by representing each node's belief with a generative distribution embedding (GDE) while updating it through the POMDP dynamics. BETS trains its belief model alongside its policy and value networks through experience, with the realized hidden state of the POMDP as the belief model's training target. We evaluate BETS on six configurations of RockSample, LightDark, and PocMan against BetaZero and three planners without learned components. BETS is competitive or leading on all six, while every baseline falls far behind on at least one. Where beliefs are highest-dimensional, it outperforms them by a large margin, even without search.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criteria. The paper presents a Monte Carlo planner with belief embeddings for POMDPs, which is a reinforcement learning/planning method but not related to RLHF or instruction-following in language models.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 248. [PersonaFactor: Preference Factors with Dynamic Selection for LLM Personalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16372) <a id="link248"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16372
**Authors:** Hang Su, Zequn Liu, Jianghan Chao, Qiuyu Tian, Xuesong Lu, Yingce Xia

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16372v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Although users may express different personalization needs across tasks, these task-specific behaviors can reflect shared underlying preference mechanisms. Existing LLM personalization methods often focus on surface-level interactions or undifferentiated histories, overlooking the shared mechanisms underlying these behaviors. We introduce PersonaFactors, a framework that distills reusable and interpretable personalization factors from heterogeneous user interactions. We construct a library of thirteen PersonaFactors by extracting evidence-grounded rationales and clustering recurring patterns across tasks, and develop a dynamic factor-guided generation framework that selects factors relevant to each user's history and current request. Across four personalization benchmarks and four backbone models, Dynamic PersonaFactor achieves the best score on every reported metric. Representation analyses further show that PersonaFactors and user history induce partially overlapping low-dimensional representations, and that their joint use yields complementary shifts. Taken together, these findings suggest that factor-based and history-based personalization can mutually reinforce each other.

**Comment:** Partially relevant to criterion 1: Introduces PersonaFactors for LLM personalization by distilling reusable preference factors and dynamically selecting them for generation. This is a method for personalization rather than general instruction-following or RLHF improvements.

## 249. [Characterizing the Separability Gap in Prior-Data Fitted Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14989) <a id="link249"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14989
**Authors:** Julius Kobialka, Manuel H\"ulskamp, Thomas Nagler, David R\"ugamer

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14989v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Prior-data fitted networks (PFNs) are transformers pretrained on simulated datasets to approximate Bayesian posterior predictive distributions. Standard PFN theory assumes a separable task prior, under which the feature distribution and the mechanism generating labels from features are independent. We show that common PFN priors are non-separable, even in simple settings. Consequently, standard pretraining targets a predictive distribution based on a task posterior updated using both the labeled context and the query features. We characterize its difference from the predictive based on the context posterior alone and show that this difference vanishes under posterior concentration. Under misspecification (i.e., when the true joint distribution lies outside the prior's support), however, prior non-separability can favor better feature fit at the expense of conditional label fit. We illustrate the consequences for feature attribution, Bayesian optimization, and martingale checks of Bayesian behavior. The same dependence also enables PFNs to learn from additional unlabeled observations. We demonstrate this with SemiTabICL, a PFN explicitly pretrained to use these observations, which improves accuracy over a matched supervised baseline. Our findings highlight the need for explicit rules on unlabeled data use in PFN evaluation and establish the dependence between feature and label distributions as a consequential prior design choice.

**Comment:** This paper analyzes prior-data fitted networks (PFNs) pretrained on simulated datasets, focusing on the separability of task priors and implications for Bayesian posterior predictive distributions. It discusses theoretical properties of PFNs and their use of unlabeled data. While it relates to generative modeling and Bayesian methods, it does not address RLHF, instruction tuning, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or interpretability as specified. It is more about prior design and Bayesian learning theory.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 250. [Probabilistic Multi-Source Data Selection via Learnable Categorical Priors: Variance Reduction and Guaranteed Derandomization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14942) <a id="link250"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14942
**Authors:** Shizhan Cai, Qixin Zhang, Siyuan Ma, Siyuan Liang, Yongcheng Jing, Dacheng Tao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14942v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Bilevel data selection is a fundamental task with numerous applications in machine learning and statistics. A common strategy for tackling this problem is to relax the original bilevel combinatorial optimization into a continuous one. However, existing methods, including 0-1 relaxation and probabilistic relaxation, still suffer from several limitations, such as unstable gradient estimation and infeasible rounding. To address these issues, this paper proposes a new probabilistic relaxation for the multi-source bilevel data selection problem. Our key idea is to introduce multiple learnable categorical priors to parameterize a probability distribution supported only on feasible data subsets, thus naturally yielding a lossless rounding scheme. We further show that our proposed relaxation admits an effective two-point gradient estimator, which can simultaneously avoid the inverse-Hessian computation required by existing 0-1 relaxation and the unbounded score term appearing in prior probabilistic relaxation. Furthermore, we develop a reducible representation of this estimator, which substantially reduces the number of lower-level retrainings required for gradient computation. Built upon this reducible two-point estimator, we then design a novel stochastic gradient algorithm, termed ReSGD, for multi-source bilevel data selection problem, and establish a rigorous approximation guarantee for the subset returned by ReSGD under weak submodularity. Finally, extensive experiments are conducted to validate the effectiveness of our proposed ReSGD.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criterion. The paper proposes a new probabilistic relaxation method for bilevel data selection with variance reduction and derandomization guarantees, but it is not specific to language models, RLHF, contamination, or generative modeling.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 251. [Monarch-Muon: Structure-Aware Muon for Fast and Memory-Efficient Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17429) <a id="link251"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17429
**Authors:** Alexander Miasnikov, Ruslan Kabirov, Gleb Molodtsov, Dmitrii Vlashchuk, Vladimir Aletov, Nail Bashirov, Daniil Tikhonov, Maxim Krutikov, Aleksandr Shestakov, Aleksandr Beznosikov

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17429v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Training large language models is constrained by both wall-clock time and memory footprint, and recent matrix-aware optimizers sharpen this tradeoff: Muon can improve optimization but its dense polar update remains expensive. We propose Monarch-Muon, a structured matrix-aware optimizer for weights parameterized by Monarch factors. The resulting structure reduces weights, gradients, and optimizer state, and exposes small independent update subproblems in place of a single dense orthogonalization; we formalize this through a blockwise trust-region interpretation. In quality runs, Monarch-Muon improves over Monarch-AdamW at fixed structure: 3.06 vs. 3.09 in the dense 257M model and 2.68 vs. 2.77 in the 1B MoE model; the two-block MoE variant also matches dense AdamW while using 15% fewer active and 25.4% fewer total parameters. In 6.89B-parameter systems measurements, Monarch-Muon reduces peak memory from 68.9 to 52.9 GB, Muon optimizer state from 14.60 to 6.77 GB, and the Muon optimizer step from 1288 to 304 ms.

**Comment:** Presents a new structured optimizer (Monarch-Muon) for efficient training of large language models; does not address instruction-following, RLHF, test contamination, or evaluation paradigms.

## 252. [Learning Adaptive Deliberation Control for Reliable Recognition with Multi-Agent Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17840) <a id="link252"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17840
**Authors:** Yuan Liu, Haoyue Peng, Fanliang Hu, Lei Huang, Wenli Cai

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17840v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Multi-agent deliberation can improve recognition by criticizing and revising candidate answers, but fixed controls cannot respond to changes in disagreement or reasoning quality. We present an adaptive multi-agent controller (ADMA) that learns continuous policies for proposal, challenge, arbitration, and coordination while keeping the task model frozen. Shared trace features describe quality, agreement, and progress, while role-specific features support decentralized control during inference. One controller trained on legal and financial reasoning is transferred without policy updates to four textual and multimodal benchmarks and two successive task-model versions. With Qwen3.5 held fixed, ADMA-Full improves accuracy by 6.2-14.2 percentage points over Direct and by 2.8-3.0 points over ADMA-Fixed. Paired bootstrap ranges for the Full-Fixed comparison are positive on three tasks. Outcome decompositions show that recoveries exceed introduced errors, while process assessment records fewer false-consensus events and more supported correct outcomes. Transfer to Qwen3.6 remains task dependent, which bounds the generality of the learned policy. Median token and latency ratios are 14.3-18.7 and 7.8-16.5 relative to Direct. These results show that adaptive deliberation can improve fixed-model recognition when its task-dependent gains justify the additional computation.

**Comment:** Partially relevant to criterion 12 (reasoning capabilities) as it presents adaptive multi-agent deliberation control improving recognition accuracy, but no explicit focus on instruction-following fine-tuning or RLHF.
**Relevance:** 4
**Novelty:** 5

## 253. [PVFH-Bench: Benchmarking Premise-Induced Visual Fixation Hallucinations in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14635) <a id="link253"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14635
**Authors:** Xinhao Wang, Junbo Cui, Zhenghao Liu, Yuan Yao, Xiaohua Li, Yu Gu, Ge Yu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14635v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) have achieved remarkable progress across a wide range of multimodal tasks. However, object hallucination remains a persistent challenge, undermining model reliability in applications such as visual question answering, embodied agents, and human-AI decision support systems. In this paper, inspired by cognitive science studies on perceptual bias, we investigate a new failure mode termed Premise-Induced Visual Fixation Hallucination (PVFH). Specifically, PVFH refers to a premise-induced reasoning error in which models become anchored to visually confusable evidence, accept a false textual premise, and subsequently generate responses about an absent yet visually plausible object. To systematically study this phenomenon, we introduce PVFH-Bench, a benchmark that automatically constructs visually confusable but absent targets and pairs the core hallucination setting with diagnostic subsets to trace how models move from object recognition to Premise-Induced Visual Fixation Hallucination. Experimental results on 10 state-of-the-art LVLMs demonstrate that all evaluated models are highly vulnerable to Premise-Induced Visual Fixation Hallucination. Moreover, we find that explicit chain-of-thought reasoning can further exacerbate this failure mode by encouraging models to overinterpret weak visual cues as supporting evidence for hallucinated objects.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper studies hallucination failure modes in vision-language models, introducing a benchmark for premise-induced visual fixation hallucinations. It does not focus on language-only diffusion models, RLHF, contamination, or evaluation paradigms for open-ended text generation.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 254. [Counterfactual Regret for Reflective Agents with Lean-Verified Guarantees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14790) <a id="link254"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14790
**Authors:** Zhongnian Li, Shi Xu, Mengting Xu, Peng Ying, Xinzheng Xu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14790v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reflective language agents reuse feedback and memory to adapt across tasks without updating model weights. However, existing guarantees for policy selection and action generation do not by themselves establish when local reflection yields long-term performance guarantees for the complete agent under resource constraints. In this paper, we address this gap by deriving sufficient conditions for sublinear counterfactual regret from local generation and deployment guarantees, while retaining the native controller. Specifically, we establish a resource-sensitive transfer that bounds full-state deployment discrepancies along actual execution paths and preserves the own-history laws of both learner and comparator. Besides, local generation reliability yields a cumulative generation opportunity bound, linking policy acquisition to long-term performance through an explicit quality, coverage, and hard-budget tradeoff. To verify these guarantees, we formalize the conditional proof chain for all thirteen numbered theoretical results in Lean, including a native instance with repeated policy growth and imperfect validation.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper focuses on theoretical guarantees for reflective language agents reusing feedback and memory without weight updates, involving counterfactual regret bounds and formal verification in Lean. It does not discuss RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms explicitly.

## 255. [PreSO: Privileged Representation Isolation for On-Policy Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14001) <a id="link255"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14001
**Authors:** Yuhang Yang, Mingxuan Xia, Chao Ye, Shuai Zhu, Shouqing Yang, Haobo Wang, Shenzhi Yang, Zhengqing Zang, Chenglong Wang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14001v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) uses a self-teacher conditioned on privileged information to turn the sparse outcome signal of reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) into dense token-level supervision. Yet the teacher's advantage may depend on information unavailable to the student at inference, raising a central question: which privileged changes are worth distilling? We use cross-problem recurrence as an empirical cue for potentially reusable guidance. Comparing the representation shifts induced by full solutions and compact hints, we find that hint-induced shifts are more concentrated and directionally consistent across problems, and yield more sustained downstream gains despite revealing less information. Motivated by this association, we propose Privileged Representation Isolation for On-Policy Self-Distillation (PreSO). At the source, PreSO uses compact hints to limit the solution-specific detail entering the privileged shift. At the target, it estimates a leading low-rank subspace from shifts pooled across related problems, projects the privilege-induced correction onto this subspace, and adds it to the student's unprivileged representation to form the representation-level distillation target. The selected subspace serves as an operational proxy for recurring structure, rather than a guaranteed separation of transferable and instance-specific information. On five competition-level mathematics benchmarks, PreSO consistently outperforms strong baselines across Qwen3-1.7B, 4B, and 8B, improving average accuracy over the base model by 6.8, 9.0, and 10.0 points. Beyond peak accuracy, PreSO attains the highest final-checkpoint average at every model scale, with improvements that persist through the end of training.

**Comment:** This paper proposes a new on-policy self-distillation method (PreSO) for reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards. It involves fine-tuning and representation learning but does not explicitly address RLHF or instruction-following for language models (criterion 1).
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 256. [Size Generalization for Diffusion Routing Solvers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17192) <a id="link256"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17192
**Authors:** Barak Itzchak Davidovitch, Eli Meirom, Ethan Fetaya, Gal Chechik

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17192v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Routing problems, like the Traveling Salesman Problem (TSP) and the Capacitated Vehicle Routing Problem (CVRP), are NP-hard and slow to solve for large instances. Diffusion-based solvers can be trained in a supervised way using sets of optimal solutions, and then quickly solve new test instances. However, for large instances, optimal solutions are hard to find, and diffusion models fail to generalize to instance sizes outside their training distribution. Here we describe ResizeCO, a label free size-generalization approach that addresses both the change in statistical properties of larger instances, and the computational challenge of solving them quickly. It uses reinforcement learning on large-scale instances to adapt a diffusion model trained on small instances. Specifically, we adapt GRPO to this problem, using route length as the training signal. ResizeCO also formulates k-opt as a tree search, solved by a beam search on the GPU. In size-generalization experiments on TSP and CVRP instances with up to 10000 nodes, ResizeCO achieves a Pareto front over route quality and time that dominates learned solvers, and also outperforms other solvers at short run times, setting a new state of the art.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: This paper presents advances in diffusion-based solvers for routing problems, but it does not explicitly mention language models or text applications, so relevance to diffusion language models is limited.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 257. [PRIME: Frequency-Aware Unified Pruning and Merging for MoE Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13982) <a id="link257"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.13982
**Authors:** Shanfeng Huang, Rongqian Zhang, Zhou Fang, Song Xiao, Hai Du

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.13982v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models have emerged as an effective paradigm for scaling large language models, yet their massive parameter footprint poses severe memory challenges for deployment. Existing compression methods typically treat expert pruning and merging as disjoint operations and rely on greedy heuristics, often leading to irreversible information loss or representational pollution. In this paper, we propose PRIME, a unified optimization framework for MoE compression via adaptive PRunIng and MErging. PRIME casts the discrete prune-merge assignment step as a global Integer Linear Programming over a frequency-weighted surrogate objective, jointly selecting pruning and merging decisions while accounting for expert usage. We motivate the frequency-aware objective through an error-bound analysis under fixed representative assignments, and show that the formulation favors high-frequency experts as centroids, promoting routing stability. Extensive experiments on Qwen3-30B-A3B and Qwen3.5-122B-A10B demonstrate that PRIME achieves strong accuracy retention across knowledge, coding, math, and long-context tasks, while serving benchmarks of merged models quantify the memory and throughput benefits of expert compression in vLLM.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criteria. Focuses on MoE compression via pruning and merging with frequency-aware optimization, which is a model compression technique rather than instruction-following, contamination detection, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.

## 258. [Improving Neural Symbolic Regression with a Diverse Prior and Post-training Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17994) <a id="link258"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17994
**Authors:** David Otte, J\"org K. H. Franke, Frank Hutter

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17994v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Symbolic regression is the problem of finding an expression that fits a dataset. Solving it requires searching a combinatorial space of candidate expressions. Neural symbolic regression can amortize that search by training a model once on synthetic data and then predicting expressions for a new dataset. But existing models still underperform genetic programming on real, noisy data. We argue that the deficit lies in the training rather than in the paradigm, and present RASR (Reinforced Amortized Symbolic Regression), a method with two key changes to the training recipe. First, we design a synthetic prior that narrows the gap with real-world data: inputs come from the prior of a tabular foundation model; constants are sampled bottom-up, conditioned on the data; targets carry heteroscedastic and structured noise; and everything is standardized. Second, we split training into two stages. Supervised pre-training maximizes the token-level likelihood of a reference expression. This is followed by reinforcement-learning post-training that aligns the model with its inference-time use by rewarding held-out fit and penalizing expression size. On the black-box track of SRBench 2.0, RASR achieves the best mean test $R^2$ rank of all evaluated methods. Three RASR variants, trained with different size penalties, make up most of the accuracy-complexity Pareto front. On Strogatz, our method recovers more ground-truth expressions than any baseline, and on Feynman it is competitive with the best-performing methods.

**Comment:** Proposes a two-stage training method for neural symbolic regression combining supervised pretraining and RL post-training alignment. This involves reinforcement learning for alignment but is not about RLHF or instruction tuning for language models. No direct match to criteria.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 259. [GA-SVD: Generation-aware Singular Value Decomposition for LLM Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15398) <a id="link259"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15398
**Authors:** Shengkun Tang, Cong Zeng, Zongfang Liu, Dan Alistarh, Zhiqiang Shen

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15398v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) demonstrate strong generalization across a broad range of reasoning and generation tasks, but their large parameter counts impose substantial memory and computation costs, hindering deployment in practical resource-constrained settings. One-shot low-rank compression is a promising solution since it reduces model size and inference cost effectively. However, existing SVD-based methods primarily target multiple-choice benchmarks and often fail to preserve performance on generation tasks. Moreover, their rank allocation strategies are typically heuristic. We propose GA-SVD, a generation-aware low-rank compression framework for LLMs. First, we introduce Generation-Aware Weight Whitening (GAWW), which leverages gradients from on-policy knowledge distillation to construct whitening transformations aligned with generative behavior. Second, we develop an evolutionary global rank allocation method that searches for effective layer-wise rank allocation under a given global compression ratio. Across extensive experiments on Mistral-7B, Qwen3 series, and Llama-3.3 models, evaluated on both multiple-choice and generative benchmarks, GA-SVD consistently outperforms existing SVD-based baselines and achieves state-of-the-art (SoTA) results. We further demonstrate scalability by applying our method to models with up to 70B parameters.

**Comment:** Related to compression of LLMs for generation tasks but does not discuss RLHF, instruction tuning, test contamination, or evaluation paradigms. No direct match to criteria.

## 260. [A Scaled-Up Empirical Study of Syntactic Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16837) <a id="link260"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16837
**Authors:** Pengcheng Wang, Zhexuan Zhang, Chao Lou, Kewei Tu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16837v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Syntactic language models (SLMs) that generate sentences along with their syntactic tree structures have shown promise in both syntactic generalization and downstream tasks. Prior studies, however, have mostly evaluated smaller models trained on less pretraining data and tested on narrower task suites. In addition, the design space of tree linearization and attention masks remains underexplored. We conduct a large-scale empirical study of 12 controlled configurations---one terminal baseline and 11 SLM variants spanning different linearizations and masks---at scales up to 1B parameters and 100B training tokens, evaluated across 11 downstream tasks and two syntactic benchmarks. The experimental results show that SLMs with standard causal attention match or exceed structural-mask variants across downstream and syntactic benchmarks. They require no architectural modifications and remain compatible with FlashAttention. We also observe that when scaling up to the 1B-parameter scale, the top-performing SLM variant no longer shows a clear advantage over a compute-matched non-structural baseline, suggesting that SLMs may become less competitive with scale. Our code is available at https://github.com/qlwpc/TG-Interpolation.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criteria. The paper studies syntactic language models at scale, focusing on architectural variants and scaling effects, but does not address instruction-following, RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms explicitly.

## 261. [AReaL-RSI: Adaptive Scaling and Coordination of Recursive Self-Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18083) <a id="link261"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18083
**Authors:** Wenhao Zhou, Jiajun Song, Haitao Wang, Zipeng Qiu, Yingqi Peng, Ruida Xu, Jiale Li, Wenshuang Li, Shusheng Xu, Wei Dong, Hao Dai, Tongkai Yang, Yi Gao, Binhang Yuan

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18083v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Scaling recursive self-improvement requires additional computation to produce useful research progress in reaching persistent objectives. Intuitively, more researcher agents can explore more hypotheses, but early evaluation scores might misrepresent their promise, shared findings could lose their applicability conditions, and useful procedures could remain confined to completed investigations. In this paper, we present AReaL-RSI, a framework for organizing a persistent research team of agents without updating backbone LLM weights. We formulate the scaling problem in terms of evaluated outcomes and whole-system resource expenditure. Concretely, a leader agent selects task-appropriate research orientations and guides ongoing investigations; conditional memory sharing distinguishes unresolved problems from supported findings and enables recipient adaptation. During recursive evolution, after each campaign, evidence-based review revises researcher orientation, knowledge, and skills for later campaigns. Experiments compare AReaL-RSI with Codex-Goal on downstream harness optimization, GPU kernel optimization, and AI research tasks using two backbones. Under the reported resource allocations, AReaL-RSI improves Terminal Bench performance by 2.2 percentage points for each backbone and increases mean KernelBench speedup by 32.2-36.8%; further scalability comparisons favor the design of AReaL-RSI over native scaling: combining experience sharing with specialized researcher roles yields 1.9-27.5% lower kernel latency than independent exploration and 1.3-33.9% lower latency than sharing alone across all seven comparable task-backbone pairs.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criterion. Proposes a framework for recursive self-improvement of research agents without updating LLM weights, but does not address instruction-following fine-tuning, contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 262. [ACED: Reference-Anchored Aggregation of Natural-Language and Executable Evidence for Mathematical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16635) <a id="link262"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16635
**Authors:** Yiyi Lu, Jiaxiang Geng, Bing Luo

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16635v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Natural-language self-consistency and executable programs provide complementary test-time evidence for mathematical reasoning, yet either channel may support the same semantic error. Thus we propose ACED, a reference-anchored framework that treats the self-consistency answer as the default and executable outputs as supporting evidence rather than interchangeable votes. ACED converts source-specific answer counts into smoothed supports, combines them through a weighted logarithmic pool, and overrides the reference only when a calibrated relative-evidence margin is exceeded. The same rule supports both candidate selection and admission of executable-only answers. Across five math word-problem benchmarks, ACED matches or outperforms the corresponding baselines. On GSM8K, ACED-CANDIDATE recovers 89.7% of the attainable candidate-set gain with only three damaging overrides, while ACED-ALL reaches 86.9% accuracy versus 82.6% for direct Program-of-Thought aggregation. These results show that preserving evidence provenance enables more reliable integration of natural-language and executable reasoning.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criterion. Focuses on combining natural-language and executable evidence for mathematical reasoning, not on instruction-following fine-tuning, contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.

## 263. [Model-Based Online Decision Making via Generative Trajectory Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18677) <a id="link263"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18677
**Authors:** Haldun Balim, Yilun Du, Na Li

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18677v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Online decision-making faces a tension between efficient inference and long-horizon reasoning. Value-based methods rely on short-horizon bootstrapping that limits the propagation of long-term information, while trajectory optimization methods plan explicitly but must solve a high-dimensional optimization problem at every decision step. We propose Generative Trajectory Planning (GTP), a model-based framework that addresses this tension by learning a generative model as a proposal distribution over action sequences. Instead of optimizing sequences from scratch, GTP samples candidate sequences from a diffusion prior and refines them using learned dynamics, reward, and value models, enabling efficient trajectory-level reasoning. Across continuous control and long-horizon manipulation tasks, GTP matches or exceeds state-of-the-art model-free, diffusion-policy, and model-based baselines, and remains stable in regimes where strong baselines degrade. Our results suggest that generative models are best understood not as policies, but as structured proposal distributions for planning, offering a new perspective on how to integrate generative modeling with model-based decision-making.

**Comment:** Proposes a generative model-based planning method using diffusion priors for trajectory optimization. Although it uses diffusion generative models, it is applied to continuous control tasks, not language models. Does not match criterion 3 for diffusion language models.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 264. [Shaping Latent Mixture-of-Experts: From Expert Geometry to Layerwise Capacity Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14644) <a id="link264"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14644
**Authors:** Yaoxiang Wang, Kai Zhang, Ruobing Xie, Lanrui Wang, Guoliang Zhao, Junhui Li, Jinsong Su

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14644v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Fine-grained mixture-of-experts (MoE) models commonly increase expert count by narrowing each expert, treating expert shape as a by-product of routing granularity. LatentMoE moves expert computation into a lower-dimensional space, exposing the input dimension and intermediate width of each expert as explicit design choices. We show that an expert's local Jacobian rank is jointly bounded by these two dimensions, and that balancing them maximizes the rank ceiling at fixed per-expert parameters. Controlled shape sweeps across model scales and routing granularities consistently favor near-square experts. We further use layerwise intrinsic dimension (ID) to guide capacity allocation across depth, co-varying the number and shape of experts while keeping per-layer parameter count, activated FLOPs, and communication volume constant. Compared with layer-homogeneous baselines, our ID-guided allocation achieves lower validation loss and stronger downstream performance. These results establish expert shape and layerwise allocation as practical design axes for MoEs.

**Comment:** No direct match to criteria. The paper studies mixture-of-experts model design and capacity allocation, which is relevant to model architecture but not specifically to RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3

## 265. [Optimal Transport PAC-Bayes Bounds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16560) <a id="link265"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16560
**Authors:** Armin Beck, Peter Ochs, Jalal Fadili

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16560v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: PAC-Bayesian generalization bounds are among the strongest distribution-dependent guarantees in statistical learning and are typically expressed in terms of the Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence between a prior and posterior distribution. While analytically convenient, the KL divergence requires the posterior to be absolutely continuous w.r.t. the prior, imposing support constraints that can substantially restrict the choice of posterior. Moreover, the KL divergence does not account for the underlying geometry of the probability space. In this work, we derive new PAC-Bayesian bounds replacing the KL divergence with general optimal transport (OT) distances, including the total variation and Wasserstein-$p$ distances.

**Comment:** This paper presents new PAC-Bayesian generalization bounds using optimal transport distances instead of KL divergence. While it is a strong statistical learning theory contribution, it does not match any of the specific criteria related to RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, or interpretability. It is relevant to statistical machine learning but not to the specific criteria.

## 266. [DeepThink: A Conceptual Model for Knowledge-Addressable Recurrent Language Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16171) <a id="link266"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16171
**Authors:** Weiguo Chen

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16171v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: This manuscript presents DeepThink, a knowledge-addressable framework for recurrent language modeling. A Knowledge Bank stores typed Knowledge Units, a Knowledge Activator selects units from the current execution state, Knowledge Assembly produces an executable configuration, and a Shared Thinking Operator updates that state. An output stage then organizes generation. This organization exposes knowledge capacity, activation width, and recurrent depth as separate design variables, supporting both complete parameter packages and fine-grained expert or low-rank modules. We formalize the interfaces and their execution semantics, including state-dependent selection, parameter-space assembly, and thinking followed by generation. For compatible templates and complete runtime-state configurations, we establish an exact Fixed-Schedule Realization of an existing fixed-depth model. Exact shared-base reparameterization and pretrained output-tail reuse provide complementary initialization constructions. Together, these results define a route from pretrained models to dynamically selected and assembled recurrent computation. A synthesis of conditional computation, recurrent models, modular parameters, and knowledge interfaces positions DeepThink within the research landscape and identifies the design questions arising from their integration.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper proposes a novel conceptual model for recurrent language modeling with modular knowledge interfaces, but does not explicitly address RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, test contamination, diffusion LLMs, or evaluation paradigms.

## 267. [Learnable Low-Rank Polynomial Sketch for Effective Linear Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14219) <a id="link267"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14219
**Authors:** Zongyue Qin, Haoran Deng, Jason Cong, Yizhou Sun

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14219v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Softmax attention in Transformers suffers from quadratic complexity in sequence length, making it impractical for long-context applications. Linear attention alleviates this issue by replacing the exponential kernel with alternative functions that enable linear-time computation. Among existing linear attention approaches, recent studies have shown that polynomial kernels are particularly effective, as they exhibit sparse and spiky behavior similar to softmax attention, which emphasizes large dot products while suppressing irrelevant interactions. However, the exact computation of high-degree polynomial kernels is infeasible for high-dimensional representations. As a result, prior work relies on approximate polynomial kernels. This introduces a non-negligible approximation error. In this paper, we show that, from the perspective of polynomial kernel approximation, existing linear attention methods are still suboptimal. We propose \textbf{L}earnable \textbf{Lo}w-Rank \textbf{P}olynomial \textbf{S}ketch (LLoPS), a principled and flexible framework for approximating polynomial kernels with linear attention. Our method learns a low-rank polynomial sketch that provably achieves a strictly smaller approximation error than existing approaches. Experiment results show that LLoPS achieves the highest performance across extensive benchmarks, comparing with various linear attention baselines.

**Comment:** No direct match to criteria. The paper proposes a new learnable low-rank polynomial sketch method for linear attention, improving efficiency and approximation error, but does not address instruction-following, RLHF, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 268. [Showing versus Doing: Teaching by Demonstration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14004) <a id="link268"></a>

**Authors:** Mark K. Ho, Michael L. Littman, James MacGlashan, Fiery Cushman, Joseph L. Austerweil

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14004v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: People often learn from others' demonstrations, and inverse reinforcement learning (IRL) techniques have realized this capacity in machines. In contrast, teaching by demonstration has been less well studied computationally. Here, we develop a Bayesian model for teaching by demonstration. Stark differences arise when demonstrators are intentionally teaching (i.e. showing) a task versus simply performing (i.e. doing) a task. In two experiments, we show that human participants modify their teaching behavior consistent with the predictions of our model. Further, we show that even standard IRL algorithms benefit when learning from showing versus doing.

## 269. [Omni-Modal LLMs Know More Than They Say](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16135) <a id="link269"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16135
**Authors:** Na Min An, Taekyung Kim, Jaemyung Yu, Hyunjung Shim, Dongyoon Han

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16135v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Omni-modal large language models (Omni-LLMs) extend LLMs to process heterogeneous modalities (e.g., vision, audio, and IMU signals) alongside language, enabling a broader range of multimodal tasks. Yet we find that these models often fail to express information from non-textual modalities even when they perceive it correctly. We term this disconnect knows-but-cannot-say. To investigate this phenomenon, we introduce Token-level Modality Alignment (TMA), which measures the alignment between individual modality representations and response representations. Our analysis shows that non-textual representations are less aligned with response tokens and receive less attention than textual representations, suggesting a potential explanation for uneven modality preferences. Motivated by these findings, we propose OmniCatalyst, a simple perceive $\rightarrow$ infer strategy that complements the original modality inputs with self-generated text, making non-textual knowledge more accessible. Across seven Omni-LLMs, OmniCatalyst consistently improves performance (+7.29%) and yields more balanced modality preferences, with further gains from diverse training strategies. Further analysis demonstrates that the knowledge extraction phases increase TMA, suggesting that improved alignment makes non-textual information more accessible during response generation. In summary, our findings uncover how Omni-LLMs can better express modality knowledge through language. Our code is available at https://github.com/naver-ai/OmniLLM.

**Comment:** This paper studies modality alignment in omni-modal large language models and proposes a method (OmniCatalyst) to improve expression of non-textual knowledge. Although it relates to LLMs and modality fusion, it does not address instruction-following fine-tuning, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms as specified.

## 270. [Approximation in Contrastive Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15388) <a id="link270"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15388
**Authors:** Yuanfan Li, Zihan Zhang, Yiming Ying, Ding-Xuan Zhou

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15388v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Contrastive representation learning (CRL) is a fundamental paradigm for   learning transferable representations. Recent work   identifies its population target as the pointwise mutual information (PMI)   $\log\frac{p(x,y)}{p_X(x)p_Y(y)}$   between two modalities $X,Y$, where $p$ is the joint density function and   $p_X,p_Y$ are the marginals. However, it remains unclear why standard inner-product scores $f(x)^\top g(y)$ can effectively approximate a generally nonseparable population target PMI. In this paper, we address this question from the aspect of approximation for CRL. By linking PMI to a compact operator, we show that $D$-dimensional inner-product models perform rank-$D$ spectral approximation, with error controlled by the spectral tail. We then study neural network realizations: in general settings, we show that deep neural network scoring functions are universally consistent, and the approximation error rates depend on the smoothness of the PMI function; under structured low-rank data models we obtain improved rates attained by moderate-width encoders. Finally, we establish a fast calibration rate from contrastive excess risk to downstream retrieval error under a density-ratio Tsybakov noise condition.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criterion. The paper studies approximation properties of contrastive representation learning, focusing on PMI approximation and neural network scoring functions, but does not address RLHF, instruction tuning, contamination, or interpretability in language models.
**Relevance:** 3

## 271. [Two For The Price Of One: Curvature-Aware Pruning Preserves Influence Functions Too](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18516) <a id="link271"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18516
**Authors:** Mehdi Makni, Natalia Ponomareva, Rahul Mazumder

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18516v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Post-training pruning enables efficient deployment, but conventional methods do not explicitly preserve internal-gradient alignment between sparse and dense models. We study novel connections between downstream performance of compressed models and the ability to retain gradient information via influence-function preservation. Central to this connection is a curvature-aware objective that accounts for both layer activations and backpropagated gradients. Despite its promise, this objective remains underexplored in pruning due to a lack of efficient solvers. We introduce $\texttt{CACTUS}$, a novel ADMM-based optimizer supporting both pruning and sparse-plus-low-rank decompositions of pre-trained weights. Compared to LLM-Surgeon which targets the same objective, $\texttt{CACTUS}$ can achieve over an order-of-magnitude speedup. At $50\%$ pruning of Qwen3-4B, $\texttt{CACTUS}$ reduces the C4 perplexity gap to the dense model by $45\%$, improves compressed-to-dense per-sample gradient cosine similarity on WikiText-2 from $0.670$ to $0.755$, and runs over $28\times$ faster than LLM-Surgeon on substantially weaker hardware. By preserving influence functions, $\texttt{CACTUS}$ supports effective data selection. On Llama-3.2-3B, fine-tuning a fresh dense model on the $5\%$ of data selected by $\texttt{CACTUS}$ achieves $49.44\%$ accuracy on BIG-Bench Hard, compared with $47.22\%$ using ALPS and $49.54\%$ using dense-model selection. Finally, integrating $\texttt{CACTUS}$ with LoGRA, a recent gradient-projection strategy, on Qwen3-4B with MLP layers pruned to 2:4 sparsity and a rank-64 correction retains a $0.711$ cosine alignment with dense LoGRA-projected C4 gradients.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 18 (data-centric AI) partially via data selection using influence functions preserved by pruning, but no direct match to RLHF or language model contamination or diffusion models.

**Novelty:** 6

## 272. [Compression Through Time: An Interplay of Separability, Stationarity and Capacity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16660) <a id="link272"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16660
**Authors:** Nikolaus Kopp, Franz Pernkopf, Bernhard Geiger

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16660v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Information-theoretic and geometric compression have been widely studied as possible explanations for generalization. Yet their temporal dynamics in recurrent networks remain largely unexplored. Using discretized LSTMs, whose finite embedding space enables direct estimates of information quantities, we observe pronounced compression across the final timesteps of many datasets. We explain this effect through three competing mechanisms: (i) class separability permits stronger compression at the final timestep; (ii) shared recurrent dynamics propagate this pressure across timesteps with similar activation states, making its extent depend on temporal stationarity; and (iii) limited representational capacity opposes prolonged compression because it restricts information available for subsequent computation. We systematically test these mechanisms through controlled synthetic interventions and real-world sequence tasks. Our results identify class separability, temporal stationarity, and network capacity as key determinants of temporal compression, and suggest that compression acts as a regularizer-potentially beneficial when excess capacity is available, but harmful to generalization when it consumes memory needed for the task.

**Comment:** No direct match to criteria. The paper studies temporal compression dynamics in recurrent networks from an information-theoretic perspective, which is relevant to understanding neural networks but does not address instruction-following, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 273. [Scale, Drivers, and Limits of Representational Alignment Across Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14476) <a id="link273"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14476
**Authors:** Jialin Wu, Meenakshi Khosla

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14476v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Comparing large language models can reveal shared representational structure and systematic differences between them. However, aggregate similarity measures can obscure how agreement varies across geometric scales and which linguistic properties support it. We introduce two complementary methods to resolve where models agree and what supports that agreement. Standard representational similarity analysis (RSA) summarizes similarity across all stimulus pairs, potentially obscuring differences in alignment between nearby and distant inputs. Neighborhood-Weighted RSA (NW-RSA) addresses this limitation by weighting stimulus pairs according to their proximity in representational space, placing alignment from local neighborhoods to global geometry on a common scale. Counterfactual RSA (CF-RSA) tests which linguistic properties support alignment by comparing intact representations in one model with representations under controlled linguistic perturbations in another. Across diverse language models, we find strong agreement in coarse representational geometry but substantial divergence in local neighborhoods. Alignment is observed both across broadly sampled natural text and within linguistic equivalence classes that preserve particular aspects of meaning or structure. Counterfactual analyses reveal selective sensitivity: changes to logical scope reduce alignment substantially more than the tested changes to hierarchical syntax, event roles, or discourse structure. Alignment also decreases when sentence structure or lexical meaning is disrupted in word lists and jabberwocky sentences, suggesting that the regularities of natural language support stronger convergence. These findings refine claims of representational universality by showing that strong overall similarity need not imply agreement in how models represent finer linguistic distinctions.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specified criteria. The paper studies representational alignment across LLMs using new similarity analysis methods, which is interesting for understanding LLMs but does not address instruction-following fine-tuning, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.

## 274. [Virtual Head Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17959) <a id="link274"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17959
**Authors:** Xibo Ding, Guoxia Wang, Jinle Zeng, Jiabin Yang, Sirui Tao, Chao Yang, Dianhai Yu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17959v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: The design of attention mechanisms in long-context large language models faces an ``impossible triangle'' among KV cache size, training and prefill FLOPs, and model quality. GQA achieves strong quality but incurs high KV cache overhead; alternative approaches such as MLA and MFA can compress the cache, but MLA suffers from quality loss while MFA substantially increases training and prefill compute. We propose Virtual Head Attention (VHA), which simultaneously improves all three vertices through two lightweight linear operations: Q Premix applies a near-identity transformation to queries within each KV group to recover virtual head diversity from halved physical query heads, and Linear Postmix fuses inter-head features via a low-rank $I + AB^\top$ residual structure. Since VHA introduces no nonlinear operations, Premix can be folded into the query projection and Postmix into the output projection at inference time, reducing the model to a standard GQA-2 architecture that directly reuses all existing GQA inference optimizations. With only two KV heads, the group size in large models readily reaches 64, fully exploiting Tensor Core parallelism during decoding. Across four scales with 4K-context 50B-token pretraining, VHA consistently surpasses the standard GQA baseline at every scale ($+$0.05\% to $+$0.81\%), while reducing KV cache by $4\times$ vs.\ GQA-8 at dense scales and $2\times$ vs.\ GQA-4 at the MoE scale, and lowering training and prefill FLOPs by 7.9\% (1.7B, $S{=}4096$). On an industrial 30B-A3B MoE model trained on 500B tokens at 8K context, VHA outperforms both MLA and GQA-4 by $+$0.70\,/\,$+$2.60\,\% on a 12-benchmark base-model evaluation suite; in a separate matched 10B-A1B MoE comparison after long-context mid-training, VHA attains an average RULER score of 84.4 over 8K--128K versus 73.0 for GQA, while using two rather than four KV heads in the full-attention layers.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: advances in diffusion language models is not met, but this paper proposes a new attention mechanism (Virtual Head Attention) improving long-context LLM efficiency and quality, which is relevant to generative modeling in NLP but does not explicitly mention diffusion models or instruction-following fine-tuning. No direct match to the specified criteria.

## 275. [Stable-Plastic Subspace Construction for Differentially Private Continual LoRA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17904) <a id="link275"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17904
**Authors:** Jin Liu, Ning Xi, Yinbin Miao, Junkang Liu, Ke Cheng, Jianfeng Ma

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17904v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Continual learning (CL) adapts pretrained models to a sequence of tasks while retaining knowledge acquired from earlier tasks. When task data are sensitive, differential privacy (DP) provides record-level protection, but maintaining both adaptation and retention under privacy constraints remains challenging. We study this problem in continual low-rank adaptation (LoRA) and observe that: 1) private training increases forgetting primarily during subsequent adaptation rather than initial task acquisition; 2) jointly perturbing both LoRA factors introduces a non-negligible bilinear noise interaction; and 3) DP aggravates the stability-plasticity dilemma, hindering both knowledge retention and adaptation to new tasks. Based on these observations, we propose DP-SPA, which constructs a stable subspace from previously privatized updates and a plastic subspace from pretrained weights within its orthogonal complement. The method fixes these bases and privately optimizes only the LoRA factor, eliminating the bilinear noise interaction within each step. Before merging each task update, it rescales stable-direction coefficients using historical and current update energies. We establish a privacy guarantee and show that recalibration does not increase historical overlap and tightens a conditional upper bound on old-task loss increase. Experiments across five datasets, eight settings, and multiple privacy budgets show that DP-SPA improves average accuracy and reduces forgetting compared with the evaluated baselines.

**Comment:** Addresses differentially private continual learning with a novel stable-plastic subspace approach but does not match criteria related to RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 276. [Fewer Bits, More Tries: Recovering Capability Under Memory Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15372) <a id="link276"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15372
**Authors:** Tomer Galanti, Josh Susskind, Eran Malach

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15372v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language models are generally more capable than smaller ones, but their memory requirements can make local deployment difficult. We ask whether part of this capability gap can instead be recovered by running a smaller or more compressed model multiple times and selecting among its outputs using public or model-generated tests. Single-user local inference can leave hardware underutilized, allowing parallel candidate generation to trade additional test-time computation for accuracy at modest latency cost. Across four code-generation benchmarks, verified retry recovers substantial accuracy lost to model size or compression. A 7B Qwen model with eight parallel attempts essentially matches a 32B model on MBPP+ and exceeds it on the other three benchmarks, while using about $4\times$ less model memory, running up to $6\times$ faster, and using less energy, as measured on Nvidia GPU. We observe similar tradeoff across model sizes, compression levels and hardware choices, although exact benefit depends on the hardware profile and problem setting. These results show that model capacity can be traded for verified test-time computation when correct solutions remain reachable within a modest retry budget.

**Comment:** This paper presents a method to recover model capability under memory constraints by running smaller/compressed models multiple times and selecting outputs via tests. It relates to generative modeling and statistical tricks for language models but does not address RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms explicitly.

## 277. [Neural Dimensional Analysis: Foundations of Dimensionless and Scale-Consistent Computation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17969) <a id="link277"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17969
**Authors:** Bum Jun Kim, Makoto Kawano, Yusuke Iwasawa, Yutaka Matsuo

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17969v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Modern Transformers rely on many scale-control mechanisms, including normalization, residual scaling, attention temperatures, loss weighting, and adaptive updates. To explain how these mechanisms maintain scale consistency, we introduce Neural Dimensional Analysis (NDA), a unified framework based on dimensional consistency. We define neural units that specify how tensors rescale and which quantities can be added, just as physical units do in physics. Our rules match residual units, nondimensionalize attention and gate inputs, combine losses across units, and convert gradient units into parameter units. We implement NDA through dimensionless Unit Violation Indices (UVI) for diagnosing scale mismatches within Transformers, a unit-consistent multi-loss objective, and the Gauge-Canonical Transformer (GCT) with normalized coordinates and orthogonalized residual additions. In language-model experiments spanning 51M to 1B parameters, GCT remains stable during training across a broad sixteenfold learning-rate range, outperforming Pre-LN, LLaMA-style, and QK-Norm in 124M validation loss at tuned learning rates. GCT's normalized residual stream also shows improved robustness to quantization. At 1B parameters, calibrated per-tensor int8 quantization increases GCT's validation loss by only 0.009 nats, compared with up to 5.51 nats for the baselines. Vision and physics-informed neural network experiments further confirm NDA's benefits beyond language modeling. These results establish dimensional consistency as a neural network design principle.

**Comment:** This paper introduces Neural Dimensional Analysis, a new theoretical framework for scale-consistent computation in Transformers, improving training stability and robustness. While it relates to neural network design and training improvements, it does not specifically address RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, test set contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms. It is relevant to statistical machine learning and generative modeling but does not match any specific criteria.

## 278. [AgentStripes: An Evidence-Graded Maturity Model for AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18603) <a id="link278"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18603
**Authors:** Nutan Kumar Naik, Vijay Prasad Poudel, Aditya Kumar Saroj, Saurav Samantray, Abhishek Patel

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18603v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Benchmarks report what an Artificial Intelligence (AI) agent can do on a task suite, but not whether one released version has been engineered with the controls that make it reliable, safe and observable, or how strongly that is shown. This paper proposes AgentStripes, an evidence-graded maturity model for AI agents. It defines levels (0 to 7 stripes), process areas (12 areas holding 40 capabilities), three gates (basic, safety, proof) that hold an agent at a level however high its score, and evidence grades E0 to E5. Each applicable capability receives a rating from 0 to 3 and a separate evidence level; these are combined into a credit, aggregated into a severity-weighted score and converted into stripes that the gates limit. Instruments gather evidence, language-model judges interpret it under explicit criteria, and the arithmetic is deterministic. The model is compared with standards at topic level without a claim of conformance, and an unexecuted validation protocol is specified. An empirical evaluation (exploratory, non-confirmatory) of 30 public Python agent repositories, with static evidence only, is a demonstration and not a validation: static evidence caps every final stripe at 2, 26 repositories end at one stripe and 4 at two, and this distinction is unreliable, since re-judging changed the final stripe of 4 of 22 repositories (engine, dossier and panel changed), whereas none of 8 panel-only repeats, all at stripe 1, changed. The parameters are expert conventions without outcome based calibration, and the stripes have not been shown to predict reliability or safety.

**Comment:** AgentStripes proposes an evidence-graded maturity model for AI agents focusing on reliability, safety, and observability controls. This relates to AI system safety and evaluation but does not directly address instruction-following fine-tuning, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or open-ended text generation evaluation paradigms. It is somewhat relevant to criterion 5 (real-world usage and safety properties) but focuses on static evidence grading rather than data or surveys.
**Relevance:** 4
**Novelty:** 5
