## 279. [CacheLink: Functional KV Transport for Zero-Prefill Cross-Model Context Handoff](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15509) <a id="link279"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15509
**Authors:** Haomiao Qiu, Yining Tang, Zijia Song, Ruizi Han, Ziyue Qiao, Miao Zhang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15509v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: KV caches make long-context serving efficient by reusing past computation, but model switching breaks this reuse and forces the receiver to prefill the context again. We study \emph{zero-prefill cross-model context handoff}: translating a sender cache into a receiver-compatible state so that the receiver can continue directly without prefilling same context. Existing affine transport methods show that receiver KV states can be substantially reconstructed from sender caches. However, through extensive analyses, we find that reconstruction fidelity is a poor proxy for receiver utility. Similar K/V reconstruction errors can lead to substantially different attention patterns, predictive distributions, and downstream accuracy, because conventional reconstruction objectives overlook the query-dependent functional importance of K and V in receiver attention. We thus introduce CACHELINK, which learns a coupled K/V correction through the receiver behavior directly. Across four independently trained sender--receiver pairs and seven evaluation panels, CACHELINK improves the equal-weight mean over all methods for every pair. In matched diagnostics, CACHELINK changes K/V squared error by less than 0.4% while substantially changing receiver attention and next-token behavior. At 32K context, it reduces handoff latency by up to $7.27\times$ relative to receiver re-prefill. Our findings verify that reconstruction fidelity alone is insufficient and that preserving the receiver computation induced by the transported cache is important for effective cross-model KV transport.

**Comment:** This paper proposes CACHELINK, a method for zero-prefill cross-model context handoff by learning K/V corrections based on receiver behavior rather than reconstruction fidelity. It relates to efficient context reuse in LLM serving but does not address RLHF, instruction tuning, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or interpretability. It is relevant to generative modeling infrastructure but not to the specific criteria.

**Novelty:** 6

## 280. [Many Steps into One: Reasoning Contraction and the Limits of Safe Memory Reuse for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17544) <a id="link280"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17544
**Authors:** Jianghong Zhou, Hao Rong, Chen Lin, Daoan Zhang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17544v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Thought templates, skill libraries, agent memories and caches turn many reasoning steps into one. We call this reasoning contraction. A contracted memory saves reasoning steps and errors until its facts change, then quietly answers wrong. Most systems trust memories by counting successes and failures. We prove that such counters are badly tuned change detectors: a symmetric counter notices a partial change only after a power of its false-alarm horizon, and an unclipped one may never notice it. On five LLMs answering multi-hop questions over edited facts, ExpeL- and ACE-style counters serve 7.0-13.5x more stale answers than certified memories given the same information, and on code tasks with an exact verifier 35-73x more. Safety also has a price. When facts change and each memory is judged only by its own verified outcomes, one number, the certification load, sets how much reasoning any safe memory can skip, and under Zipf demand it yields a scaling law. Our recipe weighs each memory's failures by its own likelihood ratio, certifies its utility against live reasoning, and restarts the certificate when facts change, with the likelihood-ratio detector as the safety net for unannounced changes; for large edits it also invalidates the memory.

**Comment:** This paper studies reasoning contraction and safe memory reuse for LLM agents, providing a theoretical analysis of memory change detection and proposing a likelihood-ratio based certification method. It relates to reasoning and memory safety in LLMs but does not explicitly address RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, test set contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms. It has a theoretical flavor with a novel safety certification approach.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 281. [Running Fewer Loops Removes Refusal Behaviour in Recurrent-Depth Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15218) <a id="link281"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15218
**Authors:** Seyedarmin Azizi, Erfan Baghaei Potraghloo, Arya Fayyazi, Saeid Shokoufa, Massoud Pedram

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15218v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: A recurrent-depth language model reuses one block of weights several times in a row. The number of repetitions, which we call loops, is chosen when the model runs, not when it is trained, and running fewer is cheaper and faster, so a deployment may choose to do it. Running fewer loops also makes these models less safe, and the loss of safety is larger than the loss of anything else we measured. We study four released Ouro checkpoints and Huginn-0125. At half of its trained depth, Ouro-1.4B-Thinking still solves 0.692 of the GSM8K maths problems, against 0.775 at full depth, keeping 89% of its accuracy. Over the same reduction, the rate at which it helps with harmful requests rises from 0.038 to 0.205, 5.3 times higher. The information is not lost: a linear classifier reads whether a request is harmful from the model's internal state after a single loop, with an ROC AUC of 0.967 against 0.986 at full depth. What the missing loops remove is the refusal that should follow. We measure the effect with four instruments, one generating no text at all, and reproduce it on a second model family. Removing a single direction from Ouro-1.4B's internal state at full depth drops its refusal rate on harmful prompts from 0.950 to 0.130, identifying what the shallow model is missing. Two run-time repairs do not help: the models' early-exit rules do not spend more loops on harmful requests, and choosing a depth per request does not recover the loss. Changing the training does. Standard safety training makes the shallow model safe only by refusing 0.865 of harmless requests, while training at randomly chosen depths reaches a separation of +0.399 between harmful and harmless refusal at one loop, against +0.118. On Huginn-0125 it keeps harmful help at or below 0.040 at every depth from 1 to 32 loops, where standard training reaches 0.194, and it costs no accuracy at full depth.

**Comment:** Studies recurrent-depth language models and safety/refusal behavior related to running fewer loops; no direct connection to instruction-following fine-tuning or RLHF (criterion 1) or other criteria.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 282. [Big-Big-SycoBench: A Benchmark to Detect Sycophancy Through Model Responses to Valid vs. Invalid Arguments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15634) <a id="link282"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15634
**Authors:** Marthe Ballon, Arne Vanhoyweghen, Seorin Kim, Francesca Carlon, Lynn Delcon, Melika Mobini, Vincent Holst, Lukas Van de Voorde, Tibo Vanleke, Mathieu Louis, Bert Verbruggen, Brecht F. Verbeken, Andres Algaba, Vincent Ginis

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15634v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Sycophancy in large language models risks amplifying unsupported claims or beliefs across the knowledge ecosystem as these systems increasingly contribute to research synthesis, evaluation, and decision-making. A reliable model should revise its judgement when a user identifies a genuine flaw, but should resist pushback based on fallacies, emotion, or irrelevant appeals to authority. While sycophancy is usually measured on questions with a verifiable answer, the evaluative tasks in which it is most difficult to detect have no such ground truth. We introduce Big-Big-SycoBench, which measures sycophancy through argument discernment: the ability to revise an evaluation after valid criticism while resisting invalid pressure. The benchmark consists of 450 text artefacts with 5,400 arguments for raising or lowering their quality scores, each reviewed by one or more of twelve human annotators. An LLM scores each artefact twenty times to establish its baseline score and uncertainty. The model is then given a valid or invalid argument and asked to update its score. We only count a revision if it exceeds the model's own baseline variability. We use the resulting score delta to distinguish responsiveness to valid arguments from compliance with invalid ones. Across ten models, responsiveness is 88-95\% for seven, 76-77\% for Kimi K3 and GLM-5.3, and 32\% for Opus 5.5. Compliance ranges from 0.2\% for GPT-6 Astra to 95\% for GPT-4o. Big-Big-SycoBench is intended to guide the development of models whose judgements can be corrected by sound arguments, but not displaced by unsupported pressure.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criteria. The paper introduces a benchmark for sycophancy in LLMs, focusing on argument discernment and model responsiveness to valid vs invalid arguments. This is related to model behavior evaluation but does not fundamentally rethink open-ended evaluation or align with the specified criteria.

**Novelty:** 5

## 283. [From Existing Methods to New Research Ideas: Two Perspectives on Recursive Self-Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14625) <a id="link283"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14625
**Authors:** Leijun Zhou, Yanke Yu, Zhihao Liu, Qu Xiang, Tiancheng Hu, Yiru Dong, Yihan Wu, Yangxi Xiang, Xuejun Wu, Xi Chen, Buyue Qian, Yaowei Zheng, Junhao Hu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14625v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Recursive self-improvement (RSI) offers a route to AI systems that participate in their own continued development. Recent methods allow language agents to retain changes to their parameters, memory, skills, and code for subsequent execution and improvement. We examine how these methods can inform new research ideas through two complementary perspectives. First, we introduce a unified taxonomy with nine design dimensions organized into four groups: update targets, update organization, update schedule and scope, feedback and selection. This taxonomy places existing methods in a shared design space, where examining individual dimensions and their interactions can inspire new research ideas. Second, we examine how existing RSI methods draw on traditional deep learning, for example through optimization, regularization, data augmentation, continual learning, and adversarial learning. We develop further ideas by examining more specific learning mechanisms and drawing on evolutionary computation. We also review how existing benchmarks assess self-improvement under online, offline, and offline-to-online settings. Together, these perspectives help researchers develop new RSI research ideas from existing designs and learning mechanisms. RSI papers and resources are available at https://prism-shadow.github.io/awesome-rsi/.

**Comment:** No direct match to the specified criteria. The paper surveys recursive self-improvement (RSI) methods for AI systems, providing a taxonomy and discussing learning mechanisms. ## 284. [MoMaTune: Model, Match, Then Tune Language Models for Numerical Records](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15235) <a id="link284"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15235
**Authors:** Weimin Lyu, Jingfen Zhang, Lingdao Sha, Peng sun, Xiaowei Chen, Vincent Gao, Rui Song

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15235v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Despite remarkable progress in language understanding and generation, decoder language models continue to exhibit striking failures on numerical data. Numerical examples are typically serialized as text by concatenating feature names with their values (e.g., fat:3.4), while conventional task adaptation applies loss only to the downstream prediction target. This leaves two structures weakly supervised: within-value sensitivity, where digit position determines numerical magnitude, because token cross-entropy contains no explicit numerical metric; and across-field structure, where measurements in the same record are statistically dependent, because target-only tuning provides no direct loss on the measurements or their dependencies. We introduce MoMaTune (Model, Match, then Tune), a two-stage post-training strategy organized around these supervision gaps. Stage 1 applies Model, ordinary next-token prediction densely over label-free serialized records, and optionally adds Match, a same-record consistency objective that aligns representations of different partial views of the same example. Stage 2 applies Tune, supervised fine-tuning for downstream target prediction. We evaluate MoMaTune on 169,285 records from eight public datasets, with 339 of 340 input fields numerical. At 1.5B parameters, Model raises the eight-dataset masked-value imputation aggregate from 0.106 to 0.401, while the three-dataset correct-context gain rises from 0.058 to 0.167. On diagnostics, Model also increases sensitivity to numerically consequential digit changes, while Match improves robustness to informative partial views. Adding Tune yields higher target-supervised embedding aggregates for each tested Stage-1 parent, and the two-stage recipes obtain higher aggregate scores than Tune alone. The same pattern is observed at 4B, 9.6B, and 27B parameters when model embeddings are used for downstream prediction.

**Comment:** No direct match to the specified criteria. The paper proposes a two-stage post-training strategy (Model, Match, Tune) to improve numerical record understanding in language models, focusing on numerical sensitivity and robustness. It does not explicitly address RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, test set contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.

**Novelty:** 6

## 285. [Have You Been Convinced? A Neuro-symbolic Argumentation Reasoning Approach to Detecting Persuasiveness in ChangeMyView Discussions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15091) <a id="link285"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15091
**Authors:** Siddharth Bhargava, Sara Tonelli, Patricia Mart\'in-Rodilla

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15091v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Argument structures represent how individual arguments interact within a discourse. However, leveraging these structures to infer meaningful knowledge remains challenging, as it requires a formal representation of how argumentative influence propagates across the structure. We propose Neuro-symbolic Argumentation Reasoning System (NARS) for performing persuasiveness detection directly from online discussions. The neural component employs retrieval-augmented iterative LLM reasoning to extract bipolar argument structures, while the symbolic component applies gradual argumentation semantics to propagate argumentative influence over the structure.   Using the Webis-CMV-20 corpus, we construct CMV2BAS, a dataset of long, multi-threaded ChangeMyView discussions annotated with LLM-derived bipolar argument structures and labeled with delta events (view change vs.\ no change). We investigate whether gradual argumentation semantics can effectively detect persuasiveness, whether augmenting them with argument quality and graph-based centrality improves performance, and whether symbolic reasoning remains necessary in the presence of modern LLMs. NARS achieves F1=0.57 and AUC=0.56 under its best configuration, outperforming zero-shot Qwen3.6:27B and GPT-oss:20B. We identify support-dominant discussions and long argumentative chains as key failure modes limiting attack influence propagation. Our work demonstrates the feasibility of operationalizing formal gradual argumentation semantics over noisy real-world discourse for scalable macro-level persuasion analysis, while highlighting key challenges and directions for future research in neuro-symbolic argumentation reasoning. We release both CMV2BAS dataset and the NARS framework for public use.

**Comment:** Not matching criteria. Presents neuro-symbolic argumentation reasoning for persuasiveness detection, involving LLMs but not instruction-following improvements, test contamination, or evaluation paradigms for open-ended generation.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18159
**Authors:** Jean Seo, Pepa Atanasova, Isabelle Augenstein

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18159v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Sycophancy in Large Language Models, the tendency to align responses with user preferences over ground truth, has largely been studied using questions without a clear ground truth and with a single, plainly stated user opinion. We present a systematic study of which framings of a user's claim trigger sycophancy in factual question answering, where every answer is objectively verifiable without extra context. Evaluating 14 models on 42K questions, we express correct and incorrect user opinions through various claim framings over 8 dimensions and 21 types, and measure a broad set of opinion-driven behaviors. We find that all models are susceptible to user opinions regardless of size. Models are most influenced by how confidently an opinion is stated, whom it is attributed to, and whether it is presented as shared between the user and the model. In contrast to prior work, framings based on the user's expertise, authority over the model, or social pressure matter little in our factual setting. Larger models resist incorrect opinions better but are no better at recovering from their own errors. Our results call for sycophancy evaluations that probe how an opinion is framed, not merely whether one is present, and that measure multiple behaviors beyond a single flip rate.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criterion. The paper studies sycophancy in LLMs, a behavioral phenomenon, but does not propose new RLHF or instruction tuning methods, contamination detection, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 287. [BLADE: Auditing Information Use for Failure Attribution in LLM Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18780) <a id="link287"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18780
**Authors:** Xiaoguang Guo, Yaohao Long, Chuxu Zhang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18780v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language model multi-agent systems extend individual agents' capabilities through collaboration across specialized roles, tools, and workflows. Yet collaboration makes failures harder to diagnose: one agent may misinterpret a correct result while others carry the mistake forward. Failure attribution therefore seeks to identify the responsible agent and decisive error step. Recent methods trace dependencies, but knowing where information came from is not the same as knowing where it went wrong. We introduce BLADE (Blame Localization via Audited Dependency Evidence), a training-free framework that moves beyond tracing information to auditing its use. Using Jev for structured probabilistic judgments, BLADE uses dependency probabilities to select relevant source-decision handoffs for auditing. At these handoffs, it examines whether receiving decisions use their sources faithfully and within task requirements, distinguishing error introduction, propagation, and repair. These findings inform a global diagnosis that considers all execution steps. Without reference answers, BLADE achieves the best or tied-best step accuracy among eight strategies under a shared Jev decision interface on 1,228 failed runs across five Who&When and Who&When Pro subsets, outperforming All-at-once by 2.2-11.1 percentage points. Making responsibility evidence explicit offers a path toward more interpretable and reliable agentic systems.

**Comment:** No direct match to criteria; focuses on failure attribution in multi-agent LLM systems via auditing information use, related to interpretability but not mechanistic interpretability or other specified criteria.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 288. [CodeLift: Your Test Suite Is Secretly a Dense SWE Curriculum](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17224) <a id="link288"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17224
**Authors:** Junjielong Xu, Huachao Zhu, Alyssa Wu, Zhichao Hu, Pinjia He

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17224v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: The pass-all-test binary reward has become a standard recipe for training software engineering agents, but its sparsity eliminates difference of partially correct patches. The test pass-rate is a natural alternative. However, we find that it is not a reliable indicator of patch quality. A functionality being tested can be covered by either one challenging test or one hundred trivial tests, where the majority might be already satisfied pass-to-pass tests. We introduce CodeLift, a reinforcement learning method that dynamically calibrates pass-rate rewards, turning the test suite into an adaptive curriculum. At each iteration, CodeLift groups tests that kill the same patches and patches that pass the same tests, then gives more weight to test groups that fewer patch groups pass. This reduces the impact of majority simple tests and focuses more on what is still challenging. Within each rollout, CodeLift applies the same scoring rule to repository snapshots, assigning credit to useful actions even when later ones introduce regressions. Experiments on SWE benchmarks show that CodeLift consistently improves pass@1/2/4/8 over binary and pass-rate baselines. Analyses show that CodeLift provides dense, stable learning signals and unlocks the inherent curriculum in the test suite.

**Comment:** Does not match any criteria. The paper proposes a reinforcement learning method (CodeLift) for software engineering agents using test suites as adaptive curricula, which is an application-specific method rather than a fundamental advance in RLHF or language modeling.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 289. [ANCHOR: Assessing Naturalistic Constraint Hierarchies and Ordering in Repository-Grounded Agentic Coding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16859) <a id="link289"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16859
**Authors:** Wenqiang Zhu, Xinping Lei, Zhiyi Lai, Zhengshuo Gong, Tianzhuang He, Songwei Yu, Weihao Li, Jingxuan Xu, Zihang Xu, Gang Wu, Zhongqiu Wang, Yuhui Bai, Jie Li, Zhao Liu, Junhao Fan, Yan Wu, Yanan Wu, Jiaheng Liu, Ariel Deng

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16859v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: For repository-grounded coding agents, a functionally correct patch is not necessarily an acceptable one: developers also constrain how the task must be carried out-modification scope, workflow order, validation, tool use, and reporting. This capability, which we call engineering constraint following, remains largely invisible to current evaluations. We introduce ANCHOR (Assessing Naturalistic Constraint Hierarchies and Ordering in Repository-Grounded Agentic Coding), a benchmark built on naturalistic, multilingual repository tasks spanning eight language groups. ANCHOR combines (i) a hierarchical taxonomy of 10 categories and 78 leaf types distilled from real developer interactions, (ii) an automated pipeline that instantiates task-grounded atomic constraints and composes them into typed AND/THEN/PRIORITY relations, and (iii) a trajectory-grounded evaluation framework that checks both the code diff and the execution trajectory with rule-based and LLM judges. Across 19 agent configurations spanning 2 scaffolds and 10 foundation models on 358 tasks, the strongest measured system, Claude Opus 4.8 under Cline, reaches 70.1% atomic compliance and 63.7% relation compliance, while 19.0% of tasks pass every scored checklist item under the strict all-item FCCR criterion. A five-engineer audit of constraints from 50 instances reports 94% feasibility, 94% repository consistency, and 96% realism. In the primary paired set (14 configurations), the mean signed change in task completion after adding constraints is +0.7 percentage points; individual changes vary in both directions. These results distinguish functional success from constraint compliance, with failures concentrated in workflow ordering, tool-use discipline, and reporting, and substantial interactions between models and scaffolds.

**Comment:** Not relevant to any criterion. The paper introduces a benchmark for constraint compliance in repository-grounded coding agents, focusing on engineering constraints rather than instruction-following improvements or other criteria.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 290. [Uncovering Robustness Weaknesses in LLM Agents via Persona Evolution with Task-Integrity Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16155) <a id="link290"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16155
**Authors:** Yutao Xie, Jingbo Yang, Guanyu Yao, Xiaoyu Yang, Duo Ding, Shiyu Chang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16155v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Interactive-agent evaluations commonly rely on standardized LLM-based user simulators that exhibit broadly cooperative behavior, potentially overlooking failures that arise under diverse but realistic user interactions. This can lead to overly optimistic estimates of agent robustness and provide limited failure signals for training agents that generalize across users. Existing approaches largely rely on predefined user behaviors, which may miss agent- or domain-specific weaknesses, while failure-oriented search can reduce task success by invalidating the underlying task rather than exposing genuine robustness failures. To fill this gap, we introduce TIPER, a task-integrity-preserving persona evolution method that automatically discovers agent-specific user policies exposing robustness weaknesses while keeping the underlying task valid and solvable. TIPER evolves persistent user personas through reflection-guided behavioral mutations and task-wise Pareto selection under a task-integrity constraint. Across four interactive-agent benchmarks, TIPER yields lower held-out avg@3 scores in all 12 GPT-5.4 settings, with observed reductions of 5.8 to 33.7 percentage points, and in all 28 agent-domain pairs spanning seven agents, with a median observed reduction of 22.9 points. Further analysis yields a behavior-to-failure taxonomy that connects discovered user behaviors to recurring agent failure modes. Together, these results show that TIPER enables more diagnostic robustness evaluation of interactive agents and provides informative failure analysis for future agent robustness improvement. Code repository: https://github.com/UCSB-NLP-Chang/TIPER.

**Comment:** Not directly matching any criteria: focuses on robustness evaluation of LLM agents via persona evolution to find failure modes, but does not address instruction-following fine-tuning, contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms explicitly.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 291. [Harness^2: Recursive Agent Harnessing for an Open World](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14224) <a id="link291"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14224
**Authors:** Ruiyao Xu, Yanfei Chen, Zhongying CuiZhu, Bhavana Dalvi Mishra, Yifei Ming, Han Yu, Rujun Han, Chen-Yu Lee, Tomas Pfister

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14224v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: LLM agents' capabilities depend not only on their underlying models but also on their execution harnesses. Recursive self-improvement (RSI) offers a promising approach to automatically evolve these harnesses. However, deploying RSI in open-world settings still faces two critical challenges. First, open-world tasks are typically unseen during the harness design phase and novel at test time, leaving no ground-truth verifiers to reliably guide online harness edits. Second, the RSI process remains highly task-specific and fails to generalize to novel scenarios. We introduce Harness^2, a plug-and-play, recursive agent harnessing framework that addresses these challenges through two connected levels of recursion. Task-level recursion refines the current task's harness using a contrastive Propose-Probe-Compose step that reliably guides edits without ground-truth verifiers, and can be scaled further via parallel or sequential recursion. Harnessing-level recursion then extracts experience from these edits to update a continual improvement harness, enabling the agent to better adapt to future tasks. Across three professional benchmarks and four model-harness configurations, a single step consistently improves GLM-5 and Gemini 3.5 Flash over their base harnesses, yielding gains of up to 11.5 points on WorkBuddy-Bench, 4.5 points on JobBench, and 11.8 percentage points on LAB, with further gains as the task-level recursion budget increases.

**Comment:** This paper proposes a new recursive agent harnessing framework to improve LLM agent execution harnesses, but it does not explicitly discuss RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, or instruction-tuning datasets. It is more about agent harness design and recursive improvement rather than direct instruction-following methodology.

**Novelty:** 5

## 292. [Reading Right, Acting Wrong: Diagnosing Decision Failures of System One Models in Agent Loops](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18579) <a id="link292"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18579
**Authors:** Yubo Li, Ramayya Krishnan, Rema Padman

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18579v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: System One models choose an agent's next action by scoring a supplied set of options in a single pass. This makes them fast and cheap, but when they choose wrongly there is nothing to inspect: did the model misread what the agent has seen, or read it correctly and still act wrongly? We answer behaviorally. On a frozen benchmark of agent decision states from simulated environments, customer-service policies, tool use, web navigation and coding, we pair every next-action question with factual probes about the same state, each answered independently. Across seventeen fast systems and a reasoning model, the answer is consistent: most wrong actions follow correct reading, and the more accurate a system, the more of its errors take this form. These errors are not noise; they gather on the same states across systems of every kind. Where the probed facts provably determine the action, models that read every fact still fail to combine them, and their own answers, composed by code, repair the failure. On real states, what is missing is often not a fact but the next sub-goal.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criteria. The paper analyzes decision failures in agent loops and model reading vs acting errors, but does not address RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms for open-ended text generation.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 293. [RT-J: Large-Scale Pretraining of Relational Transformers for Context-Efficient Predictions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17867) <a id="link293"></a>

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17867v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Foundation models for tables and relational databases are increasingly used for predictive tasks across various scientific and industrial domains. However, adapting existing models to unseen datasets requires tens of thousands of examples. Here we introduce RT-J, an 85M-parameter relational foundation model (RFM) that makes accurate predictions even with hundreds of in-context examples or fewer. To achieve this, we first assemble the Join, a large collection of 639 web-crawled databases with 6,023 LLM-generated forecasting tasks. We pretrain with long contexts of up to 8,192 database cells, using in-context examples from a novel lightweight random-walk retriever. Finally, we leverage context tuning and ensembling to scale up test-time compute. On in-context regression tasks from RelBench, RT-J requires 10x fewer examples on average to match strong feature engineering pipelines with LLM-agent or RDBLearn featurizers and TabICLv2 or LightGBM predictors. Overall, our work pushes the frontier for context-efficient predictions on relational data and highlights the importance of real-world semantics, context engineering and large-scale pretraining for RFMs.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criteria. The paper focuses on relational transformers for tabular data prediction with large-scale pretraining and context tuning, but does not discuss RLHF, instruction-following, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or interpretability.

## 294. [Neither True nor False: Do LLMs Represent Truth-Value Gaps in Plural Predication?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14438) <a id="link294"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14438
**Authors:** Duaa Nakshbandi, Zhiyu An, Yiping Li, Sachin Giri, Wan Du

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14438v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Do Large Language Models (LLMs) genuinely grasp the truth conditions of plural descriptions, or do they force every sentence into true or false? We investigate the Homogeneity Gap, a semantic phenomenon where a plural definite (e.g., the kids laughed) and its negation (the kids didn't laugh) are both neither true nor false when only some of the kids laughed, whereas their quantified counterparts (all, not all) remain strictly bivalent. We introduce HomogeneityNLI, a diagnostic dataset of 1,000 minimal-pair items crossing determiner, polarity, and group composition across ten logical conditions. Evaluating seven open-weight models, we uncover a pervasive Bivalence Bias: models assign plural definites their homogeneous truth conditions, reading the kids as all of them and the kids didn't as none of them, yet collapse the truth-value gap into falsity. In the zero-shot setting, no model recognizes the gap for both polarities in more than 15% of scenarios, and negated definites are judged False in 84% of cases. Explicit linguistic rules, chain-of-thought, and enumeration prompts produce unstable, model-specific effects that often entrench the bias. Taken together, our findings indicate that current open-weight LLMs have acquired the truth conditions of plural predication but not its trivalent logic.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. Focuses on semantic understanding and truth-value gaps in LLMs, which is about linguistic phenomena rather than instruction-following, contamination, or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 295. [Are Multimodal Benchmarks Really Useful? Item-Level Multimodal Benchmark Diagnosis via Structure-Response Co-Calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14542) <a id="link295"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14542
**Authors:** Shiqi Zhang, Weixin Zeng, Ziheng Zhang, Jiuyang Tang, Xiang Zhao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14542v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Multimodal benchmarks are central to evaluating whether models can reason over information from multiple modalities. However, existing literature has revealed that some benchmarks may contain shortcut items that can be answered from a single modality or from answer-option regularities alone, thus failing to reflect models' true cross-modal inference ability. Existing methods for assessing benchmarks typically rely either on intrinsic item structure or extrinsic model response behavior, which are not adequate for a comprehensive diagnosis. To fill in this gap, we propose SRCoD, a structure-response co-calibrated diagnosis framework for item-level diagnosis of modality dependence in multimodal benchmarks. SRCoD estimates sample-wise multimodal information structure from benchmark content and target answers, and uses this signal as a soft structural anchor for modality-conditioned item response modeling. The learned model produces a calibrated item-level diagnostic profile that reflects how strongly an item depends on different forms of modality evidence, enabling fine-grained benchmark analysis beyond aggregate accuracy. SRCoD also provides interpretable attribution of the diagnosis results. Extensive experiments under two complementary protocols, i.e., benchmark-refinement oriented validation and human-labeled item validation, demonstrate that SRCoD outperforms state-of-the-art benchmark diagnosis methods.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criterion. The paper proposes a framework for diagnosing multimodal benchmark items for modality dependence, which is about benchmark analysis but not about contamination detection or new evaluation paradigms for open-ended text generation.

## 296. [ObsSpec: Accelerating Agentic Reinforcement Learning with Observation Speculation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15246) <a id="link296"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15246
**Authors:** Kyle Montgomery, Wang Zhang, Chenguang Wang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15246v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Tool-using agents alternate between model generation and tool execution, waiting for each tool call to complete before starting the next generation. This sequential execution is costly during agentic reinforcement learning (RL), which requires large batches of multi-turn rollouts. We introduce ObsSpec, an observation speculation pipeline for agentic RL that uses a small world model to predict tool outputs while the environment executes, allowing the policy to begin its next generation early. ObsSpec retains speculative work only when the predicted and actual observations match exactly, making rollout collection lossless, while co-training keeps the world model up to date as the policy learns. On terminal-use tasks, predictions from a 0.6B world model exactly match actual observations in 66.4% of attempts and arrive roughly 16 times faster. Using these predictions, ObsSpec reduces agent latency by 9.3% and 14.2% for 8B and 32B policies. Across controlled experiments, we characterize the regimes in which ObsSpec reduces agent latency under shared policy resources. Finally, because ObsSpec overlaps policy generation with tool execution, it is architecturally compatible with existing techniques for accelerating RL. Code is available at https://github.com/kylemontgomery1/verl-recipe/tree/obsspec/obsspec.

**Comment:** Related to accelerating agentic reinforcement learning via observation speculation; does not explicitly address RLHF or instruction-following fine-tuning methods.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 297. [JevGPT: Thinking Fast and Slow in Autonomous Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18272) <a id="link297"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18272
**Authors:** Yasheng Sun, Yanning Dai, Yimeng Chen, Nanbo Li, Firas Laakom, Yifan Xu, Piotr Pi\k{e}kos, Mateusz Ostaszewski, Wenyi Wang, Cheng Luo, Yuhui Wang, J\"urgen Schmidhuber

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18272v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Autonomous research agents must acquire and reason over evidence within a limited budget. Yet relevant sources can remain uninspected or unused, leaving a gap between retrieval coverage and the evidence that informs research. The challenge is to align fast local judgments with changing evidence needs and feed their findings back into research decisions. We introduce JevGPT, a dual-system architecture for autonomous research that couples fast evidence processing with deliberative research planning. System Two sets evidence objectives and integrates findings, while System One locates support, assesses relevance, and guides further inspection. Independent source judgments run in parallel; selected original passages inform the next research step. Experiments on fixed-corpus question answering and document ranking show that JevGPT achieves leading task performance, with faster completion and lower cost than comparably effective research agents. Evidence analysis shows broader source inspection and greater coverage of supporting documents in the research loop, highlighting efficient evidence processing as a route to more capable autonomous research.

**Comment:** Does not closely match any specific criterion; focuses on autonomous research agents combining fast and slow thinking but no explicit mention of instruction-following fine-tuning, contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.

## 298. [agent-risk: Risk Assessment for Changes to AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16280) <a id="link298"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16280
**Authors:** Yue Zhao, Zixiang Xu, Angela Huyue Zhang, S. Alex Yang, Xiyang Hu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16280v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Before changing a deployed AI agent (a stricter safety gate, a new model), organizations must approve the change, starting from existing logs. Extending classical risk analysis to partial records and partial changes to running systems, agent-risk replays logged episodes, assuming nothing else changes and the agent does not anticipate the change. The log settles episodes acting as before, a declared stop value settles stopped ones, and the rest need logged support, a saved-state rerun, or a bound. We prove what logs must record for exact replay and which saved states serve a gate family. For independent episodes, we bound the reruns a decision needs and tolerable drift from live behavior. In a bash sandbox, a 7,200-episode log alone decided neither of two retry gates, which let the agent act after refusals the log never shows. Rerunning only each gate's blocked episodes from saved states reached both live decisions at half the agent calls of a live test sized post hoc for them, given the log. Without per-step harm, a stopping gate's certified harm ceiling rose up to 2.5-fold, since the log hides whether harm preceded the stop. Approval thus needs evidence designed in advance: log fields, saved states, targeted reruns.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper studies risk assessment for changes to AI agents using logs and saved states to replay episodes and bound reruns, focusing on safety gates and approval processes. It does not address RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms explicitly.

## 299. [From Relation-Specific Heads to Shared Interventions: Contextual Entrainment in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18674) <a id="link299"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18674
**Authors:** Jinghan Zheng, Jiajun Shi

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18674v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language models rely on prompt context to perform in-context learning, follow formats, and adapt to local evidence. This ability, however, also raises a robustness concern: models may reuse irrelevant tokens from earlier context when answering a later query. Prior work identifies this behavior as contextual entrainment and finds attention heads whose ablation reduces it, arguing that these heads are largely task- or relation-specific. But are the mechanisms behind contextual entrainment truly isolated by relation, or do relation-conditioned searches recover reusable components shared across relations? Using factual-distractor relation-completion prompts across 19 lexical-semantic subtypes, we identify attention heads whose ablation reduces entrainment and test how the resulting head sets transfer recovery ability across exact relations, semantic families, and mixed relation distributions. Same-family transfer exceeds different-family transfer, family residuals weaken once globally shared heads are removed, and subtype-specific searches repeatedly rediscover many global heads. Clean-completion and context-use probes further suggest that this shared intervention can reduce harmful copying while preserving ordinary relation completion and simple in-context abilities. Our findings provide a more precise perspective on contextual entrainment and its mitigation: harmful context following is not best addressed by searching for isolated relation-specific circuits, but by using heterogeneous attack prompts to reveal reusable intervention targets whose effects are shaped by semantic relation structure.

**Comment:** This paper studies mechanisms of contextual entrainment in language models and interventions to reduce harmful copying in in-context learning. While it relates to instruction-following and in-context learning robustness, it does not explicitly propose new fine-tuning or RLHF methods for instruction-following (criterion 1).

**Novelty:** 5

## 300. [Graph Metanetworks for Transformer Based Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18471) <a id="link300"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18471
**Authors:** Yonathan Wolanowsky, Guy Bar-Shalom, Fabrizio Frasca, Haggai Maron

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18471v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Weight-space learning treats the weights of neural networks as data, processed by other models called metanetworks. A key challenge is that different weights can realize the same function, related by a group of symmetries the metanetwork should respect. While symmetry-aware metanetworks for simple architectures such as MLPs are well developed, a comprehensive treatment of transformers, and in particular transformer-based language models, is still missing. Importantly, their weight space has a richer symmetry group: beyond neuron permutations, attention introduces continuous general-linear symmetries of the attention matrices and permutations of entire heads. Existing methods for transformer weights either capture only part of these symmetries or are restricted to a single, fixed architecture, and none accounts for language-model-specific components such as the token embeddings. Building on graph metanetworks, we propose a graph-based approach that is both symmetry-aware and applicable across depths and widths. We formalize a weight-space symmetry group of complete decoder-only language models and provide a graph construction that is invariant to it. We further introduce two new transformer-population datasets with novel tasks, such as training-corpus prediction and fine-tuning detection, on which our approach notably outperforms existing baselines.

**Comment:** Related to neural network weight-space symmetries and metanetworks for transformers, but does not match any specific criteria related to RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, or interpretability as defined.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 301. [Judges Follow the Reference: The Derivation Gap in Verifying Tool-Call Arguments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18497) <a id="link301"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18497
**Authors:** Linhai Ma, Yan Wang, Lingfei Qian, Yupeng Cao, Victor Gutierrez Basulto

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18497v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Tool-call judges evaluate argument values proposed by LLM agents. We find that they accept a correct value less often when their input holds no reference for it, that is, no text that says what the value should be. This happens when the value is a default value from the function schema that the user did not mention, when it has to be inferred, or when the text it comes from was cut from the judge input. We call this drop in acceptance the derivation gap. It appears across all seven judges we evaluate, including System One models CLM and Jev. The gap is not only a truncation artifact: correct default values have the largest gap on a benchmark with no truncation. A controlled source-relocation test shows that judges follow the reference in their input: they favor a visible incorrect value over a correct value whose source is hidden. We introduce DeRef (Derived Reference): a separate model reads the full history without seeing the candidate and writes a reference for the judge. Across three benchmarks, DeRef improves overall argument AUROC for all judges tested. On the controlled test, it raises AUROC from chance or below to between 98.40 and 99.59.

**Comment:** No criteria matched. This paper studies tool-call judges evaluating LLM agent arguments and introduces a model to improve argument verification, but it does not address instruction-following fine-tuning, test contamination, or evaluation paradigms for open-ended generation.

## 302. [FlashMask-3: Efficient and Expressive Mask-Aware Distributed Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17901) <a id="link302"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17901
**Authors:** Guoxia Wang, Qianyue He, Siming Wu, Haoyang Xie, Qiwen Bao, Jinle Zeng, Jiabin Yang, Tian Wu, Dianhai Yu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17901v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Long-context training of large language and multimodal models couples structured attention masks (document, prefix-LM, share-question, sliding-window, block-sparse) with multi-node context parallelism, making the mask a dominant bottleneck. FlashMask's column-wise sparse mask compresses them from $O(n^2)$ to $O(n)$ via four query-axis boundary vectors, covering broad structured patterns. Implemented for Ampere GPUs, it neither exploits FlashAttention-3's TMA/WGMMA warp-specialized pipeline on Hopper nor supports distribution. The column-wise sparse mask is indexed by key positions but stores query-axis interval boundaries. Under any multi-chunk context-parallel assignment, it reduces per-rank mask localization to a provably zero-communication \emph{clip--shift--max} on the boundary vectors, compatible with the single-GPU kernel. FlashMask-3 introduces a mask-aware Hopper kernel with three-role warp specialization atop FlashAttention-3's TMA/WGMMA pipeline, plus a mask-aware distributed extension: zero-communication mask localization, a sparsity-aware load balancer (HCC-IPO), and a mask-aware compute-communication overlap with mask-driven communication pruning, whose forward all-gather uses topology-aware hierarchical routing. On a single H100 across 12 masks at 4K--128K context, it improves achieved TFLOPs over FlashMask, FlexAttention, and MagiAttention by 45--141%, 34--71%, and 2--44%. At context-parallel degrees 4--32 with 8K sequence per device, it attains 31--75% compute-communication overlap efficiency and improves throughput over Megatron-Core CP ring, Megatron-Core CP all-gather, and MagiAttention by $1.80\times$--$3.71\times$, $1.71\times$--$2.92\times$, and $1.09\times$--$1.46\times$. In end-to-end training of a 118B-A14B MoE at 128K on 128 H100 GPUs, it raises throughput by 7.3--11.0% over a Dual Chunk all-gather baseline, with attention forward+backward 24.5--44.9% faster.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criteria. Paper focuses on efficient distributed attention mask computation and scaling for long-context models, which is an engineering/systems contribution rather than methodological improvements to RLHF, contamination detection, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.

## 303. [Do Personalized LLMs Need the Whole Conversation? Matching the Full History with Only 5% of Its Text via Relevance-Filtered Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14952) <a id="link303"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14952
**Authors:** Erika Hayashi, Andrew Shin

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14952v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Personalized assistants accumulate long conversation histories, and passing the whole history to an LLM with every request is costly. We show that it is also unnecessary. On PersonaMem-v2 (180 questions), with GPT-5.6-terra and Claude Opus 5.5 as judges, a relevance-filtered context, built by sentence-level retrieval that ranks the history by similarity to the current request and stops at a fixed budget, matches full-history accuracy with only 5% of the text, cutting the judge's input about 20-fold. An LLM summary of the same length performs on par, but it needs an LLM to read the entire history, whereas retrieval needs only a small embedding model. While both sentence-level retrieval and LLM summary perform strongly, retrieval does so while placing the relevant evidence in the context for only 61% of the questions, and an oracle given only that evidence beats the full history by 15.6 points, making better context selection a promising path to surpassing the full history.

**Comment:** Focuses on context selection for personalized LLMs, using retrieval and summarization to reduce input size. This is about input efficiency and retrieval, not about RLHF, instruction tuning, contamination, or evaluation paradigms.

## 304. [Tiny Recursive Message Passing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18649) <a id="link304"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18649
**Authors:** Tommaso Salvatori, Matteo Forasassi, Alexis Toumi, Giovanni de Felice

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18649v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Message passing neural networks have shown remarkable performance in algorithmic and constraint reasoning, due to their ability to encode the structure of the task directly in the architecture. Recent work has shown that better results can be achieved using recursive transformers trained with deep supervision and truncated recursions, which make extremely deep loops stable to train, and equipped with test-time procedures such as discrete deduction lattices. In this work, we apply the same procedures to message passing networks, and show that the two ingredients are complementary, and lead to parameter efficient models: we obtain about $99\%$ test accuracy on Sudoku-Extreme with a model of $16$k parameters that trains in about $10$ minutes on an H100, or in $9$ hours on a CPU. We further show that the cost of truncating gradients grows with the number of rounds information needs to cross the graph, making the choice of wiring a trade-off between robustness to truncation and transfer across problem sizes. We conclude by showing that our models transfer zero-shot to larger instances than those seen during training, outperforming popular neural baselines on N-queens and Boolean satisfiability with a fraction of their parameters.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper focuses on message passing neural networks and recursive transformers for algorithmic reasoning tasks like Sudoku and SAT, which is interesting but not about RLHF, instruction tuning, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or interpretability.

## 305. [SAGE: Pushing 2-Bit Vector-Quantized LLMs toward Real-World Usability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14745) <a id="link305"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14745
**Authors:** Xianglong Yan, Zenan Wu, Kewei Zhang, Kaicheng Yang, Yulun Zhang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14745v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Extreme low-bit weight-only quantization is an effective approach to reducing the deployment costs of large language models. Below 3 bits, scalar quantization often suffers severe accuracy loss, while vector quantization (VQ) better preserves accuracy through more expressive joint encoding. However, existing VQ methods still exhibit substantial degradation on real-world tasks, and two design challenges remain underexplored: (1) protecting sensitive weight channels within jointly encoded vectors; and (2) allocating a fixed bit budget among representation components, such as direction and radius, to suit different weight matrices. To address these challenges, we propose SAGE: Split Adaptation and Grouping by Energy for weight-only vector quantization. First, Energy-Stratified Grouping (ESG) couples channel grouping with activation-weighted fitting, grouping channels from different energy tiers within each subvector to prioritize sensitive channels. Second, Adaptive Bit Split (ABS) balances direction-codebook capacity and radius precision per matrix, selecting the split with the lowest activation-weighted reconstruction error under a fixed budget. Across benchmarks covering knowledge, mathematics, code generation, instruction following, and tool use, SAGE achieves state-of-the-art performance in the 2-bit quantization setting. On Qwen3-30B-A3B, it retains 95.3% of the BF16 model's average benchmark score. With our custom-designed CUDA kernel, SAGE delivers up to a 2.55-fold non-streaming end-to-end speedup over FP16. The code is available at https://github.com/Zincwolf/sage.

**Comment:** No criteria matched. Paper focuses on low-bit quantization of LLM weights for efficiency, not on RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.

## 306. [World Modeling through Spectral Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15429) <a id="link306"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15429
**Authors:** Holger Molin, William Peng, Marco Bagatella, Randall Balestriero

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15429v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: How do we tell a world model what information to preserve? We study this question through the lens of spectral representation learning. We introduce SpecWM, a JEPA framework that explicitly specifies desired relationships between observations through a target similarity kernel and trains the encoder to reproduce those relationships in its embedding geometry. Different kernels provide different supervision, ranging from physical state information to a fully self-supervised temporal kernel requiring only the ordering of observations within trajectories. We characterize the global optima of the objective, establish conditions for linear recoverability and invariance to nuisance information, and connect temporal-kernel similarity to the behavioral successor measure under an idealized setting. Empirically, the choice of kernel strongly controls which physical quantities are recoverable from the representation. More importantly, temporal spectral alignment improves CEM planning success over LeWorldModel from 52% to 68% on OGBench Cube, 49% to 77% on OGBench Scene, and 29% to 51% on CALVIN. These results suggest that explicitly specifying representation geometry provides a simple way to steer what latent world models preserve for control.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criteria. The paper focuses on spectral representation learning for world modeling and control, which is related to representation learning and planning but does not address RLHF, instruction-following, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms explicitly.

## 307. [Do correct answers have stable latent knowledge support?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15277) <a id="link307"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15277
**Authors:** Chuancheng Shi, Shangze Li, Wenhua Wu, Kun Hu, Fei Shen, Zhiyong Wang, Tat-Seng Chua

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15277v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language models are typically evaluated in terms of the correctness of their output. However, output correctness alone does not confirm whether the latent knowledge supporting an answer has stable internal representations. We therefore propose latent state stability (LSS) and knowledge topology stability (KTS) to investigate the stability of individual knowledge states and their relational structure, respectively. Specifically, we first separately average the model's internal representations of the same knowledge under each elicitation condition. Using the mean representation under the baseline condition as a reference, we remove shared shifts to obtain corrected representations. We then formulate LSS as the distance between each corrected representation and its reference representation, normalized against the typical distance between knowledge items of the same type. We formulate KTS as the cross-condition consistency of the ordered pairwise representation distances among knowledge items of the same type. Next, we construct EpiStableBench, which comprises 4,411 items spanning factual knowledge and mathematical reasoning, with 76,766 controlled queries. Across 16 LLMs from four families, representation stability varies substantially even among items answered correctly under the base condition. Within models, higher stability generally corresponds to higher accuracy under held-out elicitation conditions; across models, stability does not consistently increase with model size or overall accuracy. Meanwhile, we present LatentDPO to learn a knowledge-editing policy that accounts for the stability of latent knowledge. Experimental results show that integrating LatentDPO with multiple existing editing methods consistently improves editing success and generalization across different expressions.

**Comment:** Related to criterion 8 (mechanistic interpretability) in a broad sense since it studies latent knowledge stability inside LLMs, but does not explicitly do mechanistic interpretability or circuit analysis. Also touches on knowledge editing but not unlearning. No direct match to RLHF or contamination or evaluation paradigms.

## 308. [Heterogeneous Structured Pruning Under Resource Constraints for Hardware-Efficient Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15781) <a id="link308"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15781
**Authors:** Cameron Barker, Andreas Paraskeva, Jan N. van Rijn, Elliot J. Crowley, Henry Gouk

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15781v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Training-free pruning removes weights from pretrained large language models to reduce their deployment cost. However, existing approaches typically consider only the total number of retained weights. Consequently, their pruning decisions do not account for how target hardware stores and processes those weights. These methods select a sparsity structure in advance and apply it uniformly to all projections, rather than accounting for how each structure trades pruning error against latency and memory across sublayers. We propose Heterogeneous Structured Pruning (HSP), a hardware-aware pruning framework that combines two components: (i) a heterogeneous search space of dense, unstructured, 2:4, block-sparse, and head / channel (H/C) pruning options and (ii) structure-regularising constraints based on latency and memory models. Our contribution generalises existing importance-based methods: a global solver selects one option per sublayer to minimise proxy pruning loss under a resource budget, thereby assigning each projection a structure and density. We evaluate HSP on OPT and LLaMA models for an NVIDIA Jetson Orin Nano Super edge device, where it improves the quality-resource frontiers over training-free baselines.   A project page and updated links for the related code is maintained at: cameronbarker.me/publications/hsp.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criterion. Paper is about hardware-aware structured pruning for large language models, focusing on deployment efficiency rather than instruction-following, contamination, diffusion, or evaluation.

## 309. [MDN-VJEPA: Multimodal Probabilistic Joint-Embedding World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13956) <a id="link309"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.13956
**Authors:** Yongchao Huang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.13956v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPAs) learn world models by predicting future representations rather than reconstructing observations. Variational JEPA (VJEPA) gives this prediction probabilistic semantics and permits flexible conditional predictive families. We propose MDN-VJEPA, a multimodal probabilistic JEPA that instantiates the latent predictor as a mixture density network (MDN), representing future states with a Gaussian mixture whose components encode alternative futures, their probabilities, and within-mode uncertainty. For sequential prediction, we address two additional structural requirements: multi-modal futures should remain temporally coherent across a trajectory, and predictive beliefs should remain consistent when propagated to the same horizon through different temporal path compositions, as motivated by the Chapman--Kolmogorov equation. Together, these considerations define a three-level hierarchy of multimodal prediction, coherent multimodal trajectories, and compositionally consistent multimodal dynamics. Controlled experiments show that MDN-VJEPA closely recovers known branching predictive laws, that retaining the complete multimodal belief rather than its conditional mean can lead to materially different and substantially better planning decisions under branching futures, and that trajectory-level coupling and distributional consistency improve temporal coherence and multi-horizon agreement. These results support a simple principle: a probabilistic JEPA should preserve not only how uncertain the future is, but which distinct futures constitute that uncertainty, how they form coherent trajectories, and how those beliefs compose through time.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. Focuses on multimodal probabilistic joint-embedding predictive architectures, which is related to generative modeling but not specifically about RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.

**Novelty:** 5

## 310. [Counting with Attention and LayerNorm: Norm Scaling and Representation Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16653) <a id="link310"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16653
**Authors:** Ayyub Shaffy, Manoj Dhakal, Safal Shrestha, Keith W. Ross

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16653v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Counting is a simple task on which transformers can nevertheless fail at long sequence lengths. We study the cost of counting in a single attention layer, up to a maximum length $L$, focusing on how softmax attention and layer normalization shape the representation from which the count is recovered. We mainly focus on regression read-out but also consider classification read-out. For regression, we show unnormalized linear attention counts exactly at parameter norms independent of $L$. For softmax attention, regression cannot count exactly with finite parameters; it recovers the count after rounding, but only when the vector parameter norms grow with $L$. We measure this cost by $B_{\min}$, the smallest bound that can be placed on every trainable vector to ensure exact counting after rounding, and derive tight rates. At fixed model dimension, $B_{\min}$ grows as $L$ without normalization, as $L^{3/2}$ under bare post-normalization, and as $L^{3/4}$ with learned post-normalization. The last rate is unexpected: post-normalization discards the magnitude that carries the count and might be expected to make counting harder, yet with a learnable gain it counts at a smaller norm than softmax without normalization. Bare pre-normalization is different: it bounds the attention scores, so beyond a finite length no parameter norm suffices. A classification read-out keeps every norm bounded under every placement, but a dense head stores one output vector per count and costs more in storage. Experiments on the post-normalized model reproduce this split between the two read-outs. Together, our theoretical and empirical findings characterize how self-attention, layer-normalization placement, and read-out choice shape the representation geometry and parameter cost of counting in the minimal single-head architecture studied here.

**Comment:** Relevant to general understanding of transformer mechanisms but does not match any specific criterion. The paper studies counting ability in transformers focusing on attention and LayerNorm effects, which is a mechanistic insight but not mechanistic interpretability per se (criterion 8) or sparse autoencoders (criterion 7). No direct match to instruction-following, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 311. [Agent Sociodrama: Social-Dynamics-Guided Persona Recasting for LLM Agent Teams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15788) <a id="link311"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15788
**Authors:** Yirui Qi, Xiaoming Zhang, Jaehong Yoon

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15788v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly deployed as teams, yet they fail in characteristically social ways: information holders stay silent, dominant speakers anchor groups on wrong answers, and norm violations go unsanctioned. We present Agent Sociodrama, a framework for modeling and validating evolving social dynamics in LLM agent teams. Under fixed personas, agents' emotional states and directed, asymmetric relationships are updated every round by a unified external LLM judge and shape their subsequent interactions. Across six LLM backbones and five social scenarios, we assess social fidelity through classical social-theory patterns and agreement with human judgments. The resulting trajectories exhibit structured social patterns and model-specific differences. We then introduce Trajectory-Guided Persona Recasting (TPR), which diagnoses group-dynamic failures from interaction transcripts and social trajectories and recasts agent personas before rerunning the task. Across four collaborative benchmarks, TPR improves performance from both heterogeneous and randomly initialized agent persona compositions, yielding up to 83.3%p of accuracy improvement over the initial casting. On representative distributed-information cooperation and mixed-motive bargaining tasks, we outperform SOTOPIA and AgentVerse in three of four comparisons, by up to 37.5%p in Pareto rate. These results show that validated social trajectories provide actionable signals for repairing LLM agent teams.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. Focuses on social dynamics modeling and persona recasting in LLM agent teams, which is about multi-agent interaction rather than instruction-following fine-tuning or evaluation paradigms.

**Novelty:** 5

## 312. [Mitigating Cross-Modal Hallucinations by Correcting Modality Misrouting in Omnimodal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18141) <a id="link312"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18141
**Authors:** Yu Mi, Licheng Pan, Jiayi Zhu, Qirui Liu, Yitong Qiao, Changtong Xu, Tao Wei, Fei Sun, Zhixuan Chu, Kui Ren

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18141v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Omnimodal large language models (OLLMs) are susceptible to cross-modal hallucinations under audio-visual interference. We find that, for a substantial fraction of incorrect joint predictions, the question-relevant unimodal branch can still recover the correct answer, suggesting that task-relevant evidence can remain recoverable from the relevant modality even when it is not reflected in the joint prediction. We refer to this recoverable failure pattern as \emph{modality misrouting}. We propose CaRe (Conflict-aware Residual Intervention), a training-free inference-time method for identifying and correcting modality misrouting. CaRe uses a text-only modality probe to identify a candidate modality-specific counterfactual branch, and flags potential misrouting when its prediction conflicts with the original joint prediction. For such cases, CaRe computes a residual direction from the discrepancy between the counterfactual and fused representations and uses it to steer the original multimodal state toward the relevant modality evidence, while retaining both modalities in the original audio-visual inference path. The intervention strength is adapted based on the confidence of the counterfactual branch. Experiments on CMM and AVHBench across two OLLMs show that CaRe consistently outperforms the evaluated inference-time hallucination-mitigation baselines.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specified criteria. Addresses cross-modal hallucination correction in multimodal LLMs via inference-time intervention, but does not focus on instruction-following fine-tuning, contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.

## 313. [Beyond Answer Sharing: TrueMAD for Targeted Peer Engagement in Multi-Agent Debate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18240) <a id="link313"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18240
**Authors:** Dezheng Bao, Sihang Du, Renhong Huang, Zhizhang Yuan, Yifei Sun, Yang Yang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18240v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Multi-agent debate (MAD) aims to improve large language model reasoning by exposing agents to alternative solutions and enabling iterative revision. However, standard broadcast-style MAD makes peer answers visible without requiring agents to engage with a particular peer claim. As a result, reliable reasoning may be suppressed by conflicting or majority responses, limiting achievable performance, while erroneous reasoning may persist and degrade final predictions without actionable feedback. We propose TrueMAD, a backbone-agnostic test-time plug-in that introduces one-to-one, free-form peer advice between answer broadcasting and self-revision. Each source agent responds to a target's current reasoning through support, rebuttal, refinement, or further questions, while the target retains control over how to revise. The same mechanism can augment standard MAD and its improved variants without parameter updates. Across AIME, GPQA-Diamond, and TruthfulQA with Qwen3-30B-A3B, TrueMAD improves agent-level and strict-majority accuracy over MAD by 3.62 and 3.52 percentage points on average, while TrueDMAD improves DMAD by 4.72 and 4.06 points, respectively. Analyses at the utterance, agent-transition, and group levels further show more peer-specific deliberation, more correction-oriented revisions, and more favorable consensus dynamics.

**Comment:** No criteria matched. The paper proposes a multi-agent debate method to improve reasoning in LLMs via peer advice, but it is an application-level improvement rather than a new paradigm for instruction-following, contamination detection, or evaluation frameworks.

**Novelty:** 5

## 314. [Beyond Task Success: A Theory of Representation Reuse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18375) <a id="link314"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18375
**Authors:** Zibo Meng, Andrew C. M. Austin, Alin Achim

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18375v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: A learned representation is useful beyond its source task only if later tasks can make use of what the encoder has already learned. Source-task performance, however, gives no guarantee that the representation itself will remain useful once the original head is removed. We study representation reuse with respect to a specified family of future tasks. For such a family, we define the task-relevant state as the coarsest deterministic state sufficient for all of its tasks. A larger task family can require a finer state, and losing a required distinction makes at least one future task unrecoverable on a target support containing that conflict. Exact reuse also depends on how the retained information is presented to the downstream rule. We separate this into structural accessibility, which concerns whether the required distinctions remain recoverable, and coordinate consistency, which concerns whether those distinctions can be interpreted by the same downstream rule across samples. Both failure modes appear separately in the experiments. Reuse improves as task-relevant distinctions become more accessible. Reversible sample-dependent coordinate changes can sharply reduce reuse even when the representation remains recoverable, and inverting the applicable coordinate change restores performance. The same accessibility-reuse ordering appears in prospectively specified CIFAR-100 experiments with both ResNet-18 and DenseNet-121. Finally, preserving a candidate state that contains the distinctions needed by the anticipated task family improves reuse on compatible held-out tasks, with the clearest benefit when target supervision is limited. Representation reuse is therefore relative to the tasks that follow source learning: a useful representation must retain the distinctions those tasks need and make them available in a form that a shared downstream rule can use.

**Comment:** No direct match to criteria. The paper studies representation reuse across tasks, which is related to transfer learning but not specifically about instruction-following fine-tuning, contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 315. [Generative Models as Adaptive Trust Regions for Discrete Bayesian Optimisation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16992) <a id="link315"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16992
**Authors:** Michael Dodds, Erik Bodin, Sebastian W. Ober, Tom Diethe, Carl Henrik Ek, Henry Moss

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16992v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Bayesian optimisation (BO) is a powerful framework for sample-efficient optimisation of expensive black-box functions, but its application to high-dimensional discrete scientific design remains challenging. Local trust-region BO methods such as TuRBO address high dimensionality by restricting search to adaptively sized neighbourhoods. However, a direct discrete analogue based on edit distance can be poorly suited to structured discrete objects: neighbourhoods grow combinatorially, and metric proximity does not capture the dependencies that determine whether a design is plausible or useful. Motivated by the growing availability of pretrained generative models, we introduce GenTuRBO, which replaces the geometric trust region with an adaptively scaled proposal distribution defined by a pretrained generator. The generator provides a learned prior over discrete transformations, encoding coordinated changes without explicitly enumerating edit-distance neighbourhoods. GenTuRBO performs strongly across all three domains and achieves the highest mean final scores on Ehrlich sequence optimisation and Sokoban coordination.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criteria. The paper proposes a method for Bayesian optimization using pretrained generative models, but does not address RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, test set contamination, diffusion language models, or evaluation paradigms for LLMs.

## 316. [Prior-Fitted and Frozen Scorers for Multimodal Contrastive Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14770) <a id="link316"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14770
**Authors:** Tillmann Rheude, Roland Eils, Benjamin Wild

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14770v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Multimodal contrastive learning typically relies on manually specified scoring functions, such as cosine similarity, to quantify the similarity between representations from different modalities. Several such functions have been recently proposed to extend contrastive learning beyond two modalities. In principle, however, any function that assigns high compatibility to matched samples and low compatibility to mismatched ones could serve as a scorer. Motivated by this broader view, we instantiate the scorer with a Transformer and find that even a randomly initialized model, when kept frozen, can outperform strong handcrafted multimodal contrastive baselines, whereas optimizing the scorer jointly with the encoders is less effective. Building on this observation, we introduce the Prior-Fitted Scorer (PFS): a Transformer scorer pre-trained on synthetic multimodal data generated from structural causal model (SCM) priors before transferring it to real-world data. The SCM exposes the scorer to diverse dependency structures, including shared, pairwise, and private information, providing a structured prior over higher-order cross-modal similarity. Across four real-world datasets, frozen Transformer scorers are competitive to contrastive baselines, and prior-fitting further improves the random initialization. More broadly, our results suggest that multimodal contrastive learning need not rely on manual similarity functions: Transformers can provide strong inductive biases, while causal pretraining can further shape these biases.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper proposes a new Transformer-based scorer for multimodal contrastive learning, which is interesting for representation learning but does not address RLHF, instruction tuning, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms for LLMs.
