## 317. [When Do Learned Blocks Preserve Causal Meaning? Block Identifiability and Constructive Causal Abstractions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18155) <a id="link317"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18155
**Authors:** Karim Zaghw, Wieland Brendel, Patrik Reizinger

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18155v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Standard identifiability theory assumes one-dimensional latent factors, but learned representations can encode latent information across multidimensional blocks whose internal coordinates are not uniquely determined. When such blocks are used to represent and intervene on higher-level concepts, it is unclear whether their causal meaning survives the transformations left unresolved by block-identifiability. We connect block-identifiability to constructive causal abstraction. We show that block-diagonal invertible transformations followed by compatible coordinate permutations form the maximal class that preserves every constructive causal abstraction built on those blocks. This guarantee assumes that the correspondence between blocks and concepts is updated with the representation. A small synthetic experiment with additive autoencoders shows that high-level interventions can be transferred between independently learned representations while approximately preserving their intended effects.

## 318. [Scaling Repeated-Data Pretraining: Batch Size, Regularization, and GPU-Time Tradeoffs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15965) <a id="link318"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15965
**Authors:** Sewon Min

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15965v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: We study pretraining when compute is abundant but unique training data are finite, requiring repeated training on the same data. We first revisit infinite-compute pretraining (Kim et al. 2026) and show that its frontier depends critically on choices previously fixed or studied only in isolation. In particular, optimizing batch size together with epoch counts reveals a U-shaped relationship between validation loss and batch size, favoring smaller batches than typical pretraining, likely because small-batch gradient noise provides implicit regularization. We further find that training recipes, previously optimized under a single-pass assumption, can be tuned, e.g., repacking documents into new sequences every epoch, weight tying, and positive embedding weight decay.   We then relax the infinite-compute assumption. In particular, smaller-than-typical batch sizes can require prohibitively long sequential training, motivating to treat GPUs and time as separate resources. Tighter deadlines favor larger batches, and latency-oriented parallelism can trade additional GPUs for shorter training. Nevertheless, even under a fixed time budget, a smaller batch with fewer GPUs can achieve lower validation loss than a larger batch with more GPUs, challenging the common practice of increasing data parallelism to maximize GPU utilization under a deadline. Together, our results identify underexplored factors in repeated-data training and provide concrete guidance on how training recipes and resource allocation should change from single-pass pretraining.

**Comment:** Related to training recipe optimization and repeated-data pretraining but does not specifically address RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, or contamination; no direct match to criteria.

**Novelty:** 5

## 319. [When Dynamic Scaling Fails: A Scale-State Feedback Governs Recurrent-State Quantization in Linear-Attention and SSM LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15139) <a id="link319"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15139
**Authors:** Yuhang Jiang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15139v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Linear-attention and state-space LLMs carry long context in a fixed-size recurrent state, and quantizing that state is the natural way to cut its cost during long decode. Unlike a weight tensor, the state is rewritten at every step, so a quantizer applied at each step sits inside the recurrence and its errors compound. INT4 that leaves synthetic recall perfectly intact at the start destroys it a thousand steps later. The mechanism is a feedback between the quantizer scale and the state it is derived from. Yoking that scale to a full-precision reference removes the resulting norm explosion entirely, yet recovers only 1.8 of the 5.4 nats lost, so numerical instability and functional degradation are separable failures. The remainder is a resolution problem that locating outliers does not solve, since every outlier-aware scheme here loses to plain persistent scaling. Allocating bits across layers does help, but under article-disjoint splits a learned allocation collapses onto the trivial rule of protecting the first few layers. Run out to 32K, group-wise INT4 is worth no more than discarding the state. Contain the scale rather than locate outliers, and report the decode horizon at which a bit-width was certified.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criterion. The paper studies quantization and numerical stability in linear-attention and state-space LLMs, which is relevant to model efficiency but not to instruction tuning, contamination, diffusion, or evaluation.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 320. [The Geometry of Language Model Logits: Generic Structure and Learned Departures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18520) <a id="link320"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18520
**Authors:** Swathi Shree Narashiman, Aravind Ramana Venkatramanan, Nambi Seshadri

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18520v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Autoregressive language models produce, at every prediction step, a vocabulary-sized logit vector whose structure determines the conditional next-token distribution. We empirically identify that the ordered vocabulary-head logits follow a remarkably consistent log-linear relation   \[ z_{(r)} \approx \mu - \alpha \log r. \] across prediction contexts, architectures, and scales, emerging progressively during pretraining. To our knowledge, this is the first systematic analysis of the logit geometry underlying the distributional assumptions used in the prior decoding work. While controlled order-statistic baselines spanning bounded, light-tailed, and heavier-tailed distributions can largely explain the observed log-linear geometry, they do not explain the systematic shift in the slope $\alpha$: trained language models exhibit $\alpha \approx 1.4$--$1.7$, sitting well above the initialization baseline ($\alpha \approx 0.2$) . Moreover, $\alpha$ correlates with predictive performance, suggesting that it captures learned structure beyond generic extreme-value effects.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper analyzes the geometry of language model logits, an empirical statistical property, but does not address instruction-following, contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 321. [ProgAgent: Learning Tool Orchestration through Programmatic Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14774) <a id="link321"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14774
**Authors:** Hao Li, Keqing He, Longtao Zheng, Junlong Li, Weihao Zeng, Qian Liu, Yuzhen Huang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14774v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly expected to orchestrate diverse tools over long-horizon tasks. Existing agents typically use Direct Tool Calling (DTC), where the model invokes tools sequentially, and tool responses accumulate in the model context, which can lead to context rot and orchestration breakdown. In contrast, Programmatic Tool Calling (PTC) invokes tools through executable code and stores intermediate results in runtime variables rather than appending them to the context. Prior work has explored PTC mainly as an inference-time optimization, but its benefits for agentic training remain unclear, and suitable verifiable training tasks are scarce. In this work, we introduce ProgAgent, a full PTC training stack, comprising a pipeline for synthesizing verifiable long-horizon tasks, a lightweight PTC runtime, and a two-stage training recipe. Using only 215 tasks instantiated at four environment scales, ProgAgent yields higher pass rates than the evaluated baselines of comparable size across four benchmarks. On LOCA-bench, ProgAgent-14B achieves a 30.7% pass rate at a 64k-token environment state, where these baselines score zero. Cross-harness experiments show that PTC-trained models outperform DTC-trained models under both evaluation interfaces, suggesting that the benefits of PTC training extend beyond the interface.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criterion. The paper proposes a training method for programmatic tool orchestration in LLMs, improving long-horizon task performance. This is about agent training and tool use, not about RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 322. [Efficient Multi-Agent Reasoning via Confidence-Guided Adaptive Debate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16343) <a id="link322"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16343
**Authors:** Seungdong Yoa, Ye Seul Sim, Suhee Yoon, Sanghyu Yoon, Dongmin Kim, Soonyoung Lee, Bumsoo Kim, Junhyun Lee

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16343v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Multi-agent debate has shown promise for improving the reasoning of large language models, yet recent theory suggests its benefits are highly regime-dependent. While interaction can amplify informative signals under corrective conditions, symmetric debate dynamics are neutral in expectation, often making majority voting preferable. We reconcile these views by arguing that debate is effective only when invoked at the right time and with appropriate structure. Based on this insight, we propose LASE: Leader-Adaptive Structured Engagement, a leader-centric multi-agent debate framework that selectively engages interaction only in non-neutral regimes. LASE introduces an asymmetric leader-supporter architecture that enables directed information flow and selective signal amplification, while defaulting to simple aggregation otherwise. Experiments across diverse reasoning benchmarks show that LASE achieves multi-agent-level performance with near single-agent token cost, substantially improving efficiency over static debate and voting baselines.

**Comment:** No criteria matched. Paper proposes a multi-agent debate framework to improve reasoning efficiency in LLMs, but does not focus on instruction-following fine-tuning, contamination detection, diffusion LMs, or new evaluation paradigms for open-ended generation.

## 323. [Measuring Learned Equivariance: An Information Theoretic Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17269) <a id="link323"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17269
**Authors:** Claire Schlesinger, Wolfgang Gatterbauer, Robin Walters

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17269v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Learning symmetries through data augmentation offers an alternative to architectural equivariance, but does not guarantee that a network respects those symmetries. Measuring learned equivariance is especially difficult for intermediate representations, whose group actions are not specified. In a finite discrete setting, we show that equivariance requires representations of original and transformed inputs to determine one another exactly, yielding a test based on normalized mutual information (NMI). For high-dimensional activations, we use centered kernel alignment (CKA) as a scalable test, with an exact guarantee for orthogonal feature transformations. Across architectures and tasks, augmentation usually increases transformation consistency, showing that learned equivariance can be probed directly from representations without recovering the hidden transformation rules that relate them.

**Comment:** This paper proposes a novel information-theoretic test for learned equivariance in neural network representations using normalized mutual information and centered kernel alignment. While it involves statistical methods and representation analysis, it does not address any of the specified criteria such as RLHF, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or interpretability in language models.

**Novelty:** 5

## 324. [Representation Drift in Continual Learning: LoRA versus N-gram Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14921) <a id="link324"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14921
**Authors:** Hao Wu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14921v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: A language model that keeps learning online must not slowly erode its own base capabilities. We ask which part of a modern LLM should absorb continual updates, by measuring how each choice changes the model's internal representations. On a 48-layer mixture-of-experts model with a native pretrained N-gram memory, we train a seven-task chain under three update mechanisms with identical data and budgets: updating only the N-gram memory table, continually training one shared LoRA adapter, and training a fresh LoRA per task that is merged into the backbone. We capture fixed-context representations at every layer and task boundary. Updating only the N-gram memory changes the base model's representations of general math and code inputs by 26%/15% (relative RMS), against 64%/35% for merged and 97%/39% for shared LoRA. This drift does not grow with the number of tasks: it reaches 25% after the first task and stays there for all seven, while both LoRA variants keep accumulating. Its generic behaviour is essentially unchanged (no code problem lost, math NLL -0.01), and every change is confined to inspectable, reversible table rows. The cost appears elsewhere: N-gram memory acquires new tasks more weakly and its stored tasks interfere with one another, losing as much old-task NLL as shared LoRA. We show that this forgetting is governed by drift on the learned tasks' own inputs, measured from the checkpoint at which each was learned, not by generic drift (Spearman rho=0.85; rho=0.71 on Llama-3-8B), and we derive the exact relations behind it: with a frozen head, every pairwise logit margin changes by the projection of drift onto a head-difference direction. These results support a design principle for long-running online learning: write new knowledge only into the memory table and keep the backbone fixed; the remaining problem, interference between stored tasks, then lies inside the memory.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criterion. The paper studies representation drift in continual learning comparing LoRA and N-gram memory, focusing on internal representation changes and forgetting, but does not address RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, or interpretability mechanistic methods.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 325. [BELT : BEhavior-Aligned Low-Rank KV Cache Compression with Learned Top-k Rank Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17966) <a id="link325"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17966
**Authors:** Yaoyang Liu, Shuwei Shen, Jincheng Tang, Jinguang Liu, Jinbao Xue, Hailong Yang, Ke Zhang, Yinjun Wu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17966v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Long-context inference makes the key-value (KV) cache a major memory bottleneck for large language models. We propose BELT, a low-rank KV cache compression framework. Its key-side objective targets attention distributions rather than raw keys or pre-softmax logits, yielding a quadratic surrogate with a closed-form low-rank solution. A learned global rank allocator uses differentiable soft selection during training and hard selection at deployment to meet a user-specified component budget. Experiments demonstrate improved model quality under aggressive compression and increased decoding throughput at comparable accuracy. Evaluated across three LLMs and multiple benchmarks, BELT achieves up to 70% low-rank KV cache compression while outperforming the strongest baseline by up to 23.3 points on LongBench, and up to 17.8x overall KV cache reduction when combined with quantization. With Triton-based GPU kernels and a quantized cache, BELT delivers up to 2.7x speedup for the attention module and 3.3x end-to-end decoding throughput.

**Comment:** Proposes BELT, a low-rank KV cache compression method improving long-context inference efficiency in LLMs. This is a system optimization and does not match criteria related to instruction-following, contamination, or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 326. [Accelerating Cross-Model LLM Inference via Attention Map Reuse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14688) <a id="link326"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14688
**Authors:** Zhan Jin, Yuze Luo, Bin Cui, Xupeng Miao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14688v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: LLM-augmented data workflows often involve multiple heterogeneous models operating over substantial shared context, such as schemas, tool specifications, interaction history, and intermediate results. Although prefix caching can reduce prefilling overhead, existing approaches are largely limited to reuse within the same model because KV states are tightly coupled to model-specific architectures. We present MapFlow, a system for efficient heterogeneous LLM serving via cross-model attention map reuse. Instead of reusing KV states, MapFlow reuses attention maps, which capture token dependency patterns and are more transferable across models. To enable efficient and accurate reuse, we combine partial recomputation with lightweight linear translation across layers and heads. To make attention reuse practical for long contexts, we further introduce block-level sparse compression and a fused retrieval kernel. Experiments show that MapFlow reduces prefilling latency and improves end-to-end serving efficiency while preserving generation quality across heterogeneous model pairs and multi-turn routing workloads. These results suggest that attention maps provide an effective reusable state for heterogeneous LLM serving, especially in prefix-heavy data workflows. Our code is available: https://github.com/PKU-DAIR/MapFlow.

**Comment:** Proposes a method (MapFlow) for cross-model attention map reuse to accelerate LLM inference. This is an efficiency improvement but does not match criteria related to instruction-following, contamination, or evaluation paradigms.

## 327. [FON: A Draft-Revise Dual-Organ Language Model without Sequence-Level Quadratic Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16301) <a id="link327"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16301
**Authors:** Helong Hu (Zhanjiang Campus, Guangdong Ocean University, Zhanjiang, Guangdong, China), Hongdan Pan (Maoming Administrative Service Center, Maoming, Guangdong, China), Shuiqing Hu (China Pacific Insurance)

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16301v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Self-attention assigns two functions to one mechanism-where to read and how to mix-at the price of O(L^2) training, an O(T) decode state, and position signals bound to the training length. Existing attention-free alternatives all optimize the single function of generation. We propose FON (Fusion-Organ Network), which differentiates one weight stack into a drafting organ (multi-tap learnable delays + content-adaptive recursion; causal, O(S) training, O(1) decode state, no sequence-level quadratic attention) and a revision organ that re-processes the written prefix through multi-scale ripple kernels injected via weak gates, never generating. At 30M parameters, iso-parameter and iso-token budget, FON reaches perplexity 131.03 on WikiText-103, beating Transformer (197.73) and Mamba2 (152.53); the advantage persists under all depth-aligned baselines and at the 70M tier (-20.8%). At 8x length extrapolation FON degrades by ~8-9% without positional-encoding engineering, between ALiBi and RoPE. The revision channel provides a structural pathway for in-domain infilling that causal architectures lack in principle (+19% over on-demand coupling), at a perplexity cost indistinguishable within measurement precision. We report all costs and boundaries: impaired long-range retrieval, synthetic-task disadvantages, weaker out-of-domain code infilling than a same-recipe Transformer, and a 6.5-12.3x wall-clock gap.

**Comment:** Related to generative modeling architectures for language models but does not match any specific criterion such as instruction-following improvements, test contamination, or evaluation paradigms. Focuses on architectural efficiency and perplexity improvements.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 328. [SP$^2$ec: Adaptive Self-Speculative Decoding for Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18735) <a id="link328"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18735
**Authors:** Yuqi Huang, Xingyao Li, Yunlong Hou, Fengzhuo Zhang, Jiachun Pan, Vincent Y. F. Tan

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18735v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Speculative Decoding (SD) has demonstrated its efficacy in accelerating large language model inference, but for Vision-Language Models (VLMs), the diverse input modalities make it difficult for a small trained drafter to achieve consistently high throughput. Self-speculative decoding reuses parts of the target model as a drafter which retains the multi-modal capability of the VLMs, yet its efficiency depends on choosing a suitable draft configuration for each prompt. We propose Single-Peak SPeculative decoding (SP$^2$ec), a training-free bandit algorithm that adapts the draft configuration during generation. SP$^2$ec exploits the single-peak throughput pattern observed in existing self-speculative methods, including SWIFT, KnapSpec, SpecVLM, and our simple cosSim. Our algorithmic design explicitly minimizes a novel notion of wall-time regret for each given query, corresponding to the empirical wall-time latency. Theoretical guarantees on the wall-time regret show that SP$^2$ec can exploit the optimal draft configuration, with negligible exploration cost. Extensive experiments on Qwen2.5-VL-7B-Instruct and LLaVA-NeXT-Mistral-7B across visual and textual tasks show consistent improvements of up to 11\% over the original self-speculative methods, demonstrating the wide applicability of the proposed SP$^2$ec algorithm.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criterion. The paper proposes an adaptive self-speculative decoding method for vision-language models, focusing on inference speedup rather than instruction-following, contamination, or interpretability.

## 329. [Budgeted KV-Cache Repair for Evolving Contexts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17338) <a id="link329"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17338
**Authors:** Han Yu, Wenhui Zhu, Jincheng Cao, Rui Cai, Yuanda Xu, Zhengze Zhou, Xiwen Chen, Hao Wang, Xuanzhao Dong, Han Shi, Menglin Zhou, Shayan Mohajer Hamidi, Lin Su, Mina Razghandi, Sayan Ghosh, Qing Lan, Rohit Jain, Alborz Geramifard

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17338v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Language-model agents revise their working context as information becomes obsolete, but deleting text does not erase its influence on cached computation. Later key-value (KV) states remain conditioned on the deleted content, while recomputing the entire retained suffix can be costly. We introduce budgeted KV-cache repair: recovering task performance by recomputing a user-chosen fraction of the retained suffix. Comparing repair allocations under identical relevance scores and token budgets, we find that refreshing coherent regions can substantially outperform refreshing isolated tokens. This finding motivates Semantic-Unit Refresh (SUR), which allocates the budget to query-relevant text regions using inexpensive embedding scores, without training or an auxiliary transformer forward. To execute these repairs efficiently, a layer-packed executor processes selected states together at each layer while preserving full causal cache access. Across five models, SUR achieves strong recovery in controlled deletion tasks while recomputing only 2-5% of the suffix. On four natural multi-hop QA datasets, it achieves near-full-recompute exact match at a 5% budget with lower post-edit latency. The executor accelerates fragmented refresh by up to 16.1x over interval-wise execution.

**Comment:** Some relevance to language model context management but does not match criteria for RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, or interpretability. Focuses on efficient cache repair for evolving contexts in language-model agents.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 330. [Instance-Adaptive Online Multiclass Multicalibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15954) <a id="link330"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15954
**Authors:** Zhiming Huang, Jamie Morgenstern, Aaron Roth, Claire Jie Zhang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15954v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: We study online multiclass multicalibration over $T$ rounds, and give an algorithm with beyond-worst-case guarantees. With $n$ possible outcomes, forecasts lie in a simplex of dimension $d=n-1$. In the worst case, cumulative $\ell_1$ multicalibration error scales with $\widetilde\Theta(T^{(d+1)/(d+2)})$, which becomes untenable once $d$ exceeds a small constant. Our algorithm adapts to the geometry of the realized sequence: if the sequence of adversarially selected outcome means lies in an affine subspace of dimension $s$, it obtains error $\widetilde O(n^{1/(s+2)}T^{(s+1)/(s+2)})$ with high probability. The algorithm adapts to this structure without knowing the subspace or its dimension. For $T\ge n\ge2$, this rate is minimax optimal up to polylogarithmic factors. For a stochastic instance with a fixed conditional mean, the bound specializes to $\widetilde O(\sqrt{nT})$, giving a square-root dependence on the horizon.

**Comment:** No direct match to criteria. Paper studies online multiclass multicalibration with adaptive guarantees, which is a statistical learning topic but not related to RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, or interpretability.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 331. [Aligning Faithfulness for Theory-Scale Autoformalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18498) <a id="link331"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18498
**Authors:** Yuming Feng, Tadd Mao, Dhruva Arekar, Tianjun Zhong, Jiani Huang, Xujie Si, Ziyang Li

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18498v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Autoformalization (AF) requires more than compilation: formal statements must preserve the meaning of their informal sources, a property known as faithfulness. An emerging frontier is the automated evaluation of this property, yet most existing methods operate at the statement level and produce whole-item verdicts, failing to reveal how individual expressions correspond or which contextual interpretations support the judgment. This omission is consequential because AF is inherently theory-scale: target statements derive their meaning from surrounding mathematical material and unstated conventions. We formulate faithfulness evaluation as a context-aware align-then-label problem: identifying phrase-level correspondences between informal and formal statements and assigning a faithfulness label to each under explicitly specified contextual interpretations and assumptions. We also introduce FaithAlign, the first theory-scale faithfulness benchmark comprising 460 items and 4,982 annotated phrase-level links, alongside FaithAlign-Gap, a companion set of 146 items with informal context and assumptions withheld. Each FaithAlign item includes the target statement pair, relevant context, formal dependencies, and, where needed, assumptions specifying one given interpretation. Across 8 models, GPT-6 Astra achieves the highest defect F1 at 47.8% and identifies all defects in only 4.5% of items with two or more defects. On FaithAlign-Gap, Astra identifies only 28.2% of undecidable items as such. These results show the difficulty of recovering context-dependent correspondences, establishing FaithAlign as a testbed for fine-grained, context-aware faithfulness evaluation.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specified criteria. The paper focuses on faithfulness evaluation for autoformalization, which is a specialized evaluation task but not about open-ended text generation evaluation or instruction-following fine-tuning.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 332. [SLEM: Spectrum-Aware Latent Expert Merging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16484) <a id="link332"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16484
**Authors:** Dohyeon Kim, Bedionita Soro, Bruno Andreis, Sung Ju Hwang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16484v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models scale model capacity efficiently by activating only a small subset of experts for each input, while still requiring all parameters to be stored in memory, resulting in substantial memory overhead. Expert merging techniques have recently emerged as a promising compression paradigm that reduces this overhead by consolidating multiple experts into a smaller set. However, existing approaches have two key limitations: (i) direct convex interpolation in parameter space is not generally guaranteed to yield meaningful interpolation of expert functions; and (ii) estimating expert similarity or merging coefficients often relies on calibration data, introducing dependence on the calibration distribution. To address these limitations, we propose Spectrum-Aware Latent Expert Merging (SLEM), a calibration-free expert merging framework that occurs in a structured latent representation of expert weights using a VAE. SLEM groups experts within and across layers based on latent similarities and derives merging coefficients from expert weight spectra. It then performs geometry-aware latent interpolation and decodes the resulting representations into merged expert weights. Compared with existing expert merging methods, SLEM achieves comparable or better performance on general knowledge benchmarks and higher average performance on generation benchmarks involving mathematical reasoning and code generation.

**Comment:** Related to generative modeling and model compression but does not match any specific criteria such as RLHF improvements, test contamination, or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 333. [Using Evidence-Centered Design to Guide Prompt Engineering in Measurement: An Illustration with Automated Essay Scoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15231) <a id="link333"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15231
**Authors:** Jiawei Xiong, Cheng Tang, Hong Jiao, Hanna Choi

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15231v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Generative artificial intelligence (GenAI) models, particularly large language models (LLMs), are increasingly used in educational settings for tasks such as item generation, automated scoring, and feedback delivery. The quality of these outputs depends not only on the AI model but also on how users construct the prompts that guide model behaviors. Current prompt engineering practices are largely ad hoc, relying on trial and error rather than principled design. This lack of structure introduces validity threats including construct misrepresentation, construct-irrelevant variance, and limited reproducibility. We propose that Evidence-Centered Design (ECD), a framework in educational measurement for structuring valid inferences from observed performance, can serve as a principled foundation for prompt engineering in educational applications. We map the three core ECD models, the student model, the evidence model, and the task model, onto the components of educational prompt design, and describe a workflow for ECD-based prompt engineering. We derive five design principles from this mapping and present an empirical study contrasting ECD-designed prompts with state-of-the-art (SOTA) prompting baselines on an automated scoring task. Our empirical study demonstrates the usefulness of this framework on automated scoring. Results suggest that treating prompting as assessment design rather than informal instructions strengthens the validity, reliability, and fairness of AI-generated educational outputs.

**Comment:** This paper applies Evidence-Centered Design to prompt engineering for educational applications, improving prompt validity and reliability. While it concerns instruction-following and prompt design, it does not propose new fine-tuning or RLHF methods, so it does not match criterion 1. It also does not address evaluation paradigms for open-ended generation beyond educational contexts.

## 334. [RadixFlow: Filling Execution Bubbles in Prefix-Cached Hybrid LLM Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18143) <a id="link334"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18143
**Authors:** Huihao Jing, Wenbin Hu, Shaojin Chen, Haochen Shi, Haoyu Huang, Sirui Zhang, Yuxuan Liu, Zhongwei Xie, Haoran Li

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18143v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Prefix reuse improves LLM inference by sharing the KV cache of common prompt prefixes. This helps only if the scheduler can execute a matched branch in the current worker round. Schedulers assume that a matched prefix is ready, which holds for full attention but fails for hybrid LLMs. There, a branch (the consumer) may need recurrent state that another branch (the producer) is still computing in the same round. The native scheduler stops its waiting-queue scan at this blocked consumer, and the resulting head-of-line blocking creates \emph{execution bubbles}: idle batch slots that independent work could fill. We introduce RadixFlow, which decouples prefix matching from state readiness. Its state-ready traversal turns a global scan barrier into a local wait: only the consumer is blocked until the next round, while feasible independent branches keep filling the batch under the native state check. On five hybrid models, RadixFlow lowers mean latency by 3\% to 24\% and worker rounds by 11\% to 44\% at almost unchanged reuse. On the larger Qwen3.5 models, ablations show that most of the gain comes from traversal, which adds little on a full-attention control. Heavier rounds limit how much of this saving reaches latency.

**Comment:** RadixFlow improves hybrid LLM serving by reducing execution bubbles via a novel scheduling method. This is an engineering improvement in LLM inference efficiency and does not address instruction-following fine-tuning, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms for open-ended generation.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 335. [UltraBERT: Beyond the Limit of BERT Pre-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15028) <a id="link335"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15028
**Authors:** Jungwoo Park, Taewhoo Lee, Jaewoo Kang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15028v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Recent bidirectional encoders have been modernized with innovations from causal large language models (LLMs). However, the pretraining recipe itself remains inherited from small-scale conventions, leaving encoders underoptimized, with no single model performing competitively across both classification and retrieval tasks. In this work, we investigate how far encoder performance can be pushed by revisiting core design choices in training, revealing three key findings: (1) Encoders saturate on downstream tasks beyond a few trillion tokens, indicating that additional compute past this point does not help. (2) Across model sizes, lower masking ratios benefit classification while higher ones benefit retrieval, contradicting prior work suggesting larger ratios for larger models. (3) Distillation with pruning improves classification at low masking ratios but degrades retrieval at high ones. Guided by these findings, we present UltraBERT, a single encoder that assigns separate LM heads to low and high masking ratios within a batch and restricts the distillation objective to the low ratio. To our knowledge, UltraBERT is the first encoder to achieve state of the art on both classification and retrieval tasks, reaching 91.0 on GLUE and 47.7 on BEIR. Together, these results show how far encoder performance can be pushed and provide a principled basis for compute allocation, masking ratio design, and knowledge transfer in bidirectional models.

**Comment:** This paper studies pretraining design choices for bidirectional encoders related to masking ratios and distillation, but does not explicitly address RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, or instruction-tuning datasets. It is relevant to generative modeling and language model training but does not match any specific criteria related to RLHF or instruction tuning (criterion 1) or other listed criteria.

**Novelty:** 5

## 336. [LYNX: Analytical Program Synthesis via Grounding and Closed-Loop Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14777) <a id="link336"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14777
**Authors:** Ruichen Xia, Xiao Han, Dingyu Yang, Luwei Yang, Sai Wu, Dongxiang Zhang, Binhui Yao, Gang Chen

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14777v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: LLM-powered data analytics aims to translate natural language instructions into executable analytical programs over underlying data. However, existing approaches, whether treating LLMs as intent-to-code translators or as standalone analytical engines, often fail to reliably align domain semantics with complex schemas and heterogeneous metadata, resulting in brittle execution and semantically inconsistent analytical outputs in real-world workloads. We present LYNX, a multi-agent framework that formulates LLM-driven analytics as a schema- and knowledge-constrained analysis problem rather than a single-pass generation task. LYNX organizes analytical synthesis into a closed-loop workflow of planning, generation, verification, and refinement through three role-specialized agents that collaboratively enforce schema grounding, semantic consistency, and execution correctness. To address domain ambiguity and schema misalignment, LYNX integrates Semantic-Structural Distillation and Stage-Aware Allocation (SSD-SAA). A delta-guided iterative revision continuously diagnoses and repairs semantic and execution-level failures, substantially improving synthesis robustness. We evaluate LYNX on two NL-to-SQL benchmarks, BIRD-SQL Mini-Dev and the more challenging Spider 2.0-Lite. Experimental results demonstrate strong performance and efficiency, achieving 10.66% to 59.54% execution accuracy across lightweight and commercial models, while reducing token consumption by 70.53% compared with the prior most efficient method. Our framework displays high generalization over different backbones, from open-source, lightweight models to the latest commercial models. Additional case studies on quantitative finance analytics and complex multi-relational SQL workloads further demonstrate the framework's robustness, scalability, and practical applicability in real-world analytical scenarios.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper is about LLM-powered data analytics and program synthesis with a multi-agent framework, focusing on schema grounding and iterative refinement. It does not address instruction-following fine-tuning, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.

**Novelty:** 5

## 337. [Predict Before You Modify: Learning Improvement Dynamics for Self-Evolving Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13991) <a id="link337"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.13991
**Authors:** Zixuan Xie, Chang Xu, Yuhua Jiang, Li Zhao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.13991v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Self-evolving agents improve through repeated cycles of modification and evaluation. However, existing methods receive feedback on modifications only after costly evaluations, and aggregate scores may obscure distinctions between failures that require different repairs. We introduce predictive self-evolution through an editable Improvement Dynamics Model (IDM). Before evaluation, the IDM predicts task-level outcomes of a proposed modification and guides an optional revision. After evaluation, past modifications and their observed outcomes are used to improve the IDM, allowing predictive knowledge to accumulate across generations. The system can thus improve how it solves tasks, proposes modifications, and predicts their effects. Building on HyperAgents, we evaluate this approach across multilingual coding, paper review, robotics reward design, and Olympiad-level mathematical grading. Our approach outperforms HyperAgents across all four domains, with up to 46% higher average performance over generations. Gains also persist under matched token budgets that include both agent evolution and task evaluation, and extend to held-out tasks. Forecast accuracy improves as the IDM evolves, and the learned predictive knowledge transfers to improve evolution in a new domain.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper focuses on self-evolving agents with predictive improvement dynamics, which is an interesting approach but does not specifically address RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, test set contamination, diffusion LMs, or other listed criteria. It is more about agent self-improvement and meta-learning across tasks.

## 338. [ECMR: Evidence-Aware Deliberation Control for Multi-Agent LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17869) <a id="link338"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17869
**Authors:** Yuan Liu, Haoyue Peng, Fanliang Hu, Lei Huang, Wenli Cai

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17869v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Multi-agent Large Language Model (LLM) systems typically commit to a deliberation protocol before inference. A fixed protocol cannot allocate computation differently to resolved and unresolved instances, and it may mistake repeated support for independent confirmation. We study this failure as semantic false consensus: a confident group answer supported by narrow, duplicated, or correlated rationales. We introduce ECMR, an evidence-aware controller over frozen role-conditioned LLMs. At each step, ECMR summarizes answer concentration, support diversity and independence, contradictions, role behavior, verification history, and remaining budget, then selects whether to propose, challenge, verify, arbitrate, diversify, correct a role, or stop. We formulate this decision as finite-horizon budgeted control and derive sufficient conditions under which state conditioning, risk-triggered verification, and role correction improve cost-adjusted utility. Across a declared held-out evaluation of 9,000 records from MedQA, CaseHOLD, and FinQA, ECMR-Full increases pooled accuracy from 62.1% to 84.9%, reduces false-consensus rate from 40.7% to 17.0%, and raises evidence-support rate from 58.0% to 88.7%. State shuffling loses 11.7 accuracy points with only 0.7 fewer rounds, while harder traces shift from early stopping toward arbitration. ECMR-Full uses the largest token budget, yielding the strongest absolute reliability rather than uniformly superior token efficiency.

**Comment:** No criteria matched. This paper proposes a multi-agent LLM deliberation controller to improve reasoning and reduce false consensus, but does not focus on RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms as defined.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 339. [PAIR: Memory-Efficient and Fast Inference with Reading--Reasoning Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16003) <a id="link339"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16003
**Authors:** Sejun Park, Haocheng Xia, Yongjoo Park

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16003v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: For context-based question answering, using one large model for both evidence extraction and answer reasoning requires substantial memory and computation. Existing approaches trade accuracy for efficiency within a single model only. We propose a multi-model approach for efficient inference: our method, PAIR, employs two differently sized models for evidence extraction and answer reasoning, respectively. A smaller Reader model processes and retains the full context, while a larger Reasoner model sees only the question and generated sequence without directly attending to the context. These capabilities are fused at the logit level: at each token, both Reader and Reasoner produce a prediction, and their logits are combined into one, behaving as a single next-token-prediction model. Fine-tuning further boosts their performance. On HotpotQA, PAIR with a 32B Reasoner retains 96% of that same 32B model's full-context accuracy while using roughly one quarter of its KV memory. At 45.7K-token contexts, PAIR achieves up to 3.2$\times$ higher end-to-end throughput than the full-context 32B model on the same GPU. PAIR also improves the accuracy-memory and accuracy-throughput trade-offs over evaluated token-reduction baselines. Its accuracy-preserving behavior generalizes across Qwen2.5, OLMo-3, and Gemma-3 model families.

**Comment:** This paper proposes a multi-model approach for efficient inference in context-based question answering by fusing reading and reasoning models at the logit level. However, it does not explicitly discuss RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, or instruction-tuning datasets, nor does it focus on test set contamination, diffusion language models, or evaluation paradigms. It is more about inference efficiency and model architecture trade-offs.

## 340. [Words to World: Benchmarking LLMs in Interactive Physical Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15228) <a id="link340"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15228
**Authors:** Xinnan Dai, Kaiqi Yang, Yaxin Li, Cheng Luo, Wanli Li, Mingqing Xiao, Xinyang Jiang, Daoan Zhang, Kaitao Song, Fengze Liu, Zefan Cai, Caihua Shan

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15228v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Words are increasingly becoming an interface for action: large language models (LLMs) can now invoke tools, operate devices, and make decisions that affect the physical world. However, current benchmarks largely evaluate LLMs through static text responses, offline tool calls, or simulated tasks, leaving open the question of how well they can act in real physical environments. We present a benchmark for measuring LLMs in closed-loop physical interaction, where models control a harness system connected to real-world components including robotic arms, cameras, and sensors. In each task, the model must issue instructions, receive physical feedback from the environment, interpret the resulting state, and adapt its next action accordingly. This setting evaluates capabilities beyond language understanding, including grounded perception, action planning, tool selection, spatial reasoning, feedback-driven correction, and robustness to execution uncertainty. Through experiments with representative LLMs, we characterize their ability to translate linguistic reasoning into physical actions and identify common failure modes such as hallucinated state changes, poor recovery from unexpected outcomes, and unstable long-horizon plans. Our benchmark aims to provide a systematic foundation for evaluating LLMs as interactive agents that connect words, tools, and the physical world.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criterion. The paper presents a benchmark for LLMs acting in interactive physical environments, focusing on embodied interaction and physical control rather than instruction-following improvements, test contamination, or evaluation paradigms for open-ended text generation.

## 341. [CoverSynth: Coverage-Guided Generation of Retrieval Evaluation Sets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15409) <a id="link341"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15409
**Authors:** Anahita Sharma, Apaar Shanker, Rishav Chakravarti, Sneha Srinivasan, Vijay Kalmath, Dylan Wilbur, Yuan Li, Sara Bolouki, Yuan Xue

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15409v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Enterprise agents need retrieval evaluation sets that expose domain-specific failures and guide developers towards efficient deployment tradeoffs. Neither expert-human-authored sets nor current synthetic generation methods provide a methodology for achieving the necessary coverage, reliability, efficiency, and discriminability required for such decision-making. Simultaneously, the field does not have an assessment framework for determining when an evaluation set has satisfied these properties. We introduce an evaluation set assessment framework for these criteria and CoverSynth -- a generator specifically designed to satisfy these criteria without incurring the high cost of human expert labeling. Starting from an unannotated corpus, CoverSynth targets coverage across retrieval demands and system capabilities. Deterministic checks locate evidence quotes in their sources, while model-based checks assess semantic validity to guarantee reliable ground truth. Across five public enterprise-style corpora, our method provides informative variation in difficulty, revealing unseen failure modes such as an inability of agents to handle underspecified or ambiguous requests. On the RepLiQA corpus, 91\% of human-generated queries can be answered by all members on a panel of 12 retrieval systems, compared to only 12\% of CoverSynth-generated queries. Finally, in a repeated TechQA/RepLiQA study, 12 of 12 within-harness model contrasts have positive nominal 95\% intervals on generated sets, compared with 4 of 12 on human sets. CoverSynth makes coverage and evidence quality explicit construction objectives, producing corpus-grounded evaluations that reveal retrieval headroom and differences between agent configurations.

**Comment:** Presents a coverage-guided generation method for retrieval evaluation sets; focused on retrieval evaluation rather than open-ended text generation evaluation or instruction-following improvements, so no direct match to criteria.

## 342. [Lexical Redefinition Reveals Context-Sensitive Instruction Following](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16952) <a id="link342"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16952
**Authors:** Roksana Goworek, Haim Dubossarsky

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16952v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: How do language models reconcile conflicting contextual evidence within a prompt? We study this question using lexical redefinition, where an explicit instruction specifies how a word should be interpreted and a subsequent sentence provides additional evidence through its usage. Across 870 word pairs, we find that lexical redefinitions are not applied as fixed rules. Instead, the behaviour depends systematically on the relationship between the explicit instruction and the intervening sentence: the contextualised usage can preserve, weaken, or completely reverse the instruction. These results suggest that the interpretation of an explicit instruction remains sensitive to subsequent contextual evidence. Lexical redefinition therefore provides a controlled behavioural paradigm for studying how language models integrate multiple sources of information within a prompt.

**Comment:** Related to instruction-following behavior but does not propose new fine-tuning or RLHF methods; studies contextual sensitivity of lexical redefinition in prompts, so not a direct match to criterion 1.

## 343. [Is the Future Predictable? Modeling Future Predictability for Reliable Temporal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14073) <a id="link343"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14073
**Authors:** Yongyi Huang, Huaping Zhang, Wenyao Cui, Qiuchi Li, Lei Li, Baohua Zhang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14073v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) remain unreliable when reasoning about the future. Existing models often overlook the difference among future states, committing to specific answers even when the queried facts are inherently volatile. To capture this challenge, we introduce ChronoRegime, a future-oriented temporal reasoning task that requires models to determine whether historical temporal evidence justifies commitment to a future answer. Furthermore, we propose Confidence-Aware Temporal Reasoning (CTR), a framework that estimates future predictability from temporal dynamics and regulates answer commitment accordingly. Rather than performing unconditional forecasting, CTR commits only when the reconstructed evidence supports the target future state. Across multiple LLMs, CTR improves strict overall accuracy by 6.7 percentage points and reduces hallucination rates by 18.4 percentage points on average over the plain QA baseline. These results demonstrate that explicit predictability assessment is an effective mechanism for safer and more reliable temporal reasoning under future uncertainty.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper proposes a framework for temporal reasoning with confidence estimation, improving reliability in future prediction tasks, but does not address instruction-following fine-tuning, contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms as defined.

## 344. [Is the Platform Part of the Measurement? A Protocol and Simulation Study for Cross-Platform Equivalence of Machine-Labelled Policy Sentiment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16757) <a id="link344"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16757
**Authors:** Asad Ikram, Ella Haig

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16757v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Comparing machine-labelled sentiment across social media platforms is now routine: differences in classifier scores between platforms are read as differences in public opinion. These comparisons rest on an assumption that the classifier measures the same construct, on the same scale, on every platform. Some studies check a classifier's validity platform by platform, but to our knowledge none has modelled the platform as a facet of the classifier's measurement. We adapt the psychometric equivalence toolkit (generalizability theory, measurement invariance and differential item functioning, and score linking) to machine-labelled policy sentiment, treating the platform as a survey mode, and specify a six-step protocol with anchor events fixed in advance, an anchor-density audit, and a two-classifier convergence control. A simulation with known ground truth illustrates what the assumption can cost in one generating configuration fixed in advance: across 500 replications, because the platforms measure through different functions, a pooled index that takes platform scores at face value reports the wrong sign of aggregate sentiment on nearly a quarter of days, whereas an index adjusted with each platform's known measurement function does so on under one day in sixty on average. A case study of the instrument from the first author's MSc dissertation, a fine-tuned BERT classifier over 255,446 UK economic-policy comments from Twitter/X, Reddit, YouTube, and Quora, motivates the design and illustrates the instrument-dependence the protocol addresses. The full four-platform application, under fresh collection with the pre-specified design, is the designated follow-up study. The protocol lets a researcher test whether pooling platforms is defensible in a given corpus, or whether platform scores must be linked before any average is taken.

**Comment:** This paper studies cross-platform equivalence of machine-labelled policy sentiment using psychometric equivalence tools, which is a data-centric AI approach focusing on measurement and data quality rather than RLHF or contamination or generative modeling. It does not match the specific criteria.

## 345. [Measuring the Inference Energy Efficiency of Open-Weight LLMs across Benchmarks, Reasoning Modes, and Hardware](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14389) <a id="link345"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14389
**Authors:** Zeyu Yang, Wes Armour

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14389v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Benchmark scores tell us what an LLM can do, but not how much energy is required to deliver that capability. We directly measure this trade-off for 86 open-weight model checkpoints, evaluated on seven benchmarks and six hardware platforms ranging from a consumer GPU to an eight-GPU server. We find large differences in energy efficiency even among models with similar capability. Most configurations lie away from the score--energy Pareto frontier: they use a median of 2.6--4.8$\times$ more energy than another configuration that scores at least as well, with differences reaching 27$\times$. Reasoning can improve capability, but enabling thinking increases energy by about 3.7$\times$ on average, and the benefit depends strongly on the model family and output budget. Generated-token count is also an imperfect proxy for energy, misordering 18--24\% of model pairs. In contrast, FP8 reduces energy by roughly a quarter with little change in score, while changing hardware largely rescales energy without changing the relative ranking of models. These results show that capability alone gives an incomplete picture of efficiency. We argue that benchmark scores should be reported together with measured energy consumption under a specified serving regime and output budget.

**Comment:** This paper measures inference energy efficiency of open-weight LLMs across benchmarks and hardware. It provides empirical insights into energy vs. capability trade-offs but does not propose new evaluation paradigms for open-ended text generation or contamination detection.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 346. [Preserving Updates in Low-Bit Recurrent State Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15709) <a id="link346"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15709
**Authors:** Haotian Li, Liran Dong, Kaicheng Xiao, Guoliang Xing

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15709v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Linear attention and recurrent models compress past tokens into a fixed-size state that is updated at every step. However, storing these states in high precision incurs substantial memory and bandwidth costs at large batch sizes, motivating low-bit state quantization. Unlike weights and key--value caches, a recurrent state is requantized after every update, so its quantization errors accumulate across steps. Our analysis shows that these errors are highly coherent across steps, so reducing per-step errors alone is insufficient. This coherence arises mainly from small updates that are lost in rounding. To preserve these updates, we intervene both before and at rounding. Before rounding, we propose Low-Rank Bypass (LRB), which accumulates small updates in a compact low-rank buffer and periodically merges its sufficiently large elements into the state. At rounding, we observe that a state element can even move opposite to its update, and such reversals prove particularly harmful. Therefore, we propose Bounded Directional Rounding (BDR), which rounds reversed elements toward their updates without additional storage. Although BDR enlarges the rounding error, it improves the model, suggesting that the direction of an update matters more than its rounding error at a single step. Across Gated DeltaNet, GLA, Mamba2, and RetNet, our training-free method substantially outperforms INT4, while reducing state memory by up to 5.3$\times$ and speeding up decoding by up to 1.9$\times$ relative to FP32 states.

**Comment:** This paper proposes new quantization methods for recurrent state compression in models with linear attention and recurrence. While it is a technical improvement in model efficiency, it does not address instruction-following fine-tuning, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 347. [HarnessTTT: Learn to Optimize Harness at Test-Time for Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17370) <a id="link347"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17370
**Authors:** Yingzi Ma, Yulong Cao, Nanxi Li, Fang Wu, Yizhe Zhang, James Zou, Yejin Choi, Chaowei Xiao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17370v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Scientific discovery with language models requires finding the best valid solution within a finite computational budget. We study how feedback from this search can improve the procedure used to conduct it. We introduce HarnessTTT, a test-time learning framework that trains a harness proposer while keeping the executor frozen. The proposer generates executable harnesses comprising system prompts, reusable skills, tools, middleware, and execution controls. Each harness guides a budgeted discovery rollout, whose outcome provides feedback for updating the proposer. A discovery-oriented policy-gradient objective combines gap-normalized rewards, leave-one-out advantages (RLOO), and max-sharpening to favor high-performing proposals. Generated components are validated before execution, and the search retains the best valid solution and selected harnesses across rounds. Across fourteen discovery tasks in mathematics, systems, GPU kernels, heuristic programming, astrodynamics, and biology, HarnessTTT with a frozen 9B executor improves on OpenEvolve with the same executor on all eleven benchmark tasks (e.g., from 1.17 to 2.64 on circle packing), improves on the discovery-fine-tuned Finch-9B by up to 92%, holds the best open-weight sub-10B result on nine, and reaches 0.380919 on Erd\H{o}s minimum overlap, better than the best human reference of 0.380927. Scaling the proposer and frozen executor to 27B improves on eleven of fourteen tasks, and transferring the learned harnesses to GPT-5.5 matches or exceeds the strongest reported system of any backbone on nine of fourteen tasks. Together, these results show that harness search can itself be learned at test time: a trained proposer lets a small frozen executor search effectively, and the harnesses it finds carry over to stronger executors.

**Comment:** No direct match to the specified criteria. The paper focuses on test-time learning to optimize harnesses for scientific discovery tasks, involving a frozen executor and proposer model. It does not explicitly discuss RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, test set contamination, diffusion language models, or evaluation paradigms as per the criteria.

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14736v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Optimization by PROmpting (OPRO) directly leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate candidate solutions through natural-language prompting. However, OPRO's optimization process is difficult to track, which prevents LLMs from capturing useful information accumulated over previous iterations, thereby limiting solution quality. To address this limitation, we propose a Tool-Augmented Strategic Agent (TASA) which summarizes and exploits trajectory to improve solution quality. Specifically, TASA plans the strategy for each step based on the optimization trajectory, which decomposes the optimization process into traceable strategy selections. Moreover, we leverage LLM to construct problem-specific tools and introduce a strategy-guided adaptive tool planner, which efficiently utilizes the information of trajectory to update solutions. We establish local convergence under persistent exploration and evaluate the complete framework under a shared candidate-evaluation budget. The results support its solution quality and the traceability of strategy-guided search.

## 349. [Random Fourier Features in Near-Linear Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14751) <a id="link349"></a>

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14751v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Random Fourier features approximate a shift-invariant kernel in $\mathbb{R}^d$, such as the Gaussian kernel $\exp(-\tfrac12\|x-y\|_2^2)$, using independent samples from its spectral measure to construct an unbiased estimator for the kernel value with subgaussian concentration. However, generating $m$ such features requires $O(md)$ time. A long line of work on structured random features has accelerated this computation for the Gaussian kernel and other radial kernels, albeit with weaker concentration guarantees. We close this gap by showing a random feature map that can be computed in near-linear time $O((d+m)\log d)$ and yields near-subgaussian concentration. We obtain it through a new and tighter analysis of the Fastfood feature map of Le, Sarl\'{o}s and Smola (ICML 2013), using Brascamp-Lieb inequalities and the Schoenberg representation of radial kernels.

## 350. [Which Geometry on Which Layer? Measured Sign-Spectral Assignment in Mixed-Optimizer Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17285) <a id="link350"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17285
**Authors:** Hiroki Naganuma, Laura Gomezjurado, Mahdi Ghaznavi, Ganesh Talluri, Atsushi Nitanda, Seng Pei Liew, Ryuichiro Hataya, Ioannis Mitliagkas

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17285v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Modern neural-network training uses mixed recipes, assigning AdamW-like or sign-based updates to embedding and output layers and spectral updates to hidden matrices. These assignments are usually fixed by layer type, without a clear criterion for selecting the appropriate geometry. We turn sign-versus-spectral assignment into a measurable, layerwise decision problem. For ideal memoryless steps, we derive a finite-batch ratio of conservative descent-lemma lower bounds for spectral and sign updates. Its closed form separates signal efficiency from a geometry-dependent global curvature factor. In the positive-signal regime, the same smoothness assumption gives two-sided bounds that determine which step has the larger conditional expected decrease when the ratio crosses explicit noise-dependent thresholds. We then derive a local directional quadratic score from gradient alignments and Hessian-vector products and use it in Generalized-Scion (G-Scion) to control layerwise routing. In a controlled input-rank experiment, including directional curvature matches the measured same-iterate ordering in all seven aggregate settings, whereas the curvature-free proxy selects the opposite geometry. On 31M GPT-2, the output score routes the output layer from spectral warmup to sign, and G-Scion improves validation loss over fixed Scion in memoryless and matched-momentum comparisons. ViT-B/16 results further show that a spectral edge assignment can outperform fixed Scion outside language modelling. These results suggest that optimizer geometry need not be hard-coded by layer type and are a first step toward understanding how it should be assigned.

**Comment:** No direct match to criteria. Paper studies optimizer geometry assignment in neural network training, focusing on sign vs spectral updates, not on RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, or interpretability.

## 351. [WUSPruner: Second-Moment-Optimal Transforms for Structured Pruning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15394) <a id="link351"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15394
**Authors:** Shengkun Tang, Dan Alistarh, Zhiqiang Shen

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15394v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Structured pruning is typically formulated as identifying which model dimensions to remove, often within the model's original representation. However, the effect of coordinate deletion fundamentally depends on the basis in which pruning is performed. This raises a more fundamental question: how should the basis be chosen specifically for coordinate deletion? We introduce WUSPruner, a transform-based framework for structured pruning of large language models. We formulate basis selection as a local linear reconstruction problem that directly minimizes the reconstruction loss induced by pruning. Inspired by the second-moment transformation in WUSH, we consider a WUS transform constructed jointly from activation and weight statistics. We show that under positive-definite second moments, WUS transform minimizes the local reconstruction loss over all invertible changes of basis. In the resulting basis, the activation and weight second moments are simultaneously diagonalized and balanced, causing the deletion error to decompose into independent spectral contributions and yielding a principled criterion for coordinate selection. Guided by this result, we develop a scalable pruning method based on blockwise WUS transformations and compact input projections. Experiments on Llama and Qwen models from 8B to 72B parameters show consistent improvements over structured pruning baselines across language modeling, knowledge, commonsense reasoning, mathematics, and code generation benchmarks, while also delivering practical reductions in inference latency and memory usage.

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15882v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: A stored fact may stop being valid when a user changes a preference or an event replaces an earlier one. We compare it with additive clock conditioning, prompting with the full dated history, and retrieving four dated records. We train six matched adapters across three seeds after fixing a sampler that could admit test-age examples into training. The training audit reports no such examples among 127,760 evaluated training inputs. On 62 pairs of questions asked before and after an update, at an elapsed age of seven days excluded from training, both adapter methods answer zero pairs correctly under the primary blinded assistant review with order code 1 held fixed. Full-history prompting answers 59/62 pairs correctly, and retrieval answers 62/62. Separate experiments show that adapters can store facts and that removable memory units can support local deletion. Combining fact selection with time-based version selection remains unsuccessful. These results separate learning a fact from learning when to use it.

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16654v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Flow matching models are typically trained to transport a Gaussian source to the data distribution, with source and target samples paired independently. This correction is a closed-form function of the fitted mixture plus a term that we learn with a small correction network. We test our model on published checkpoints for CIFAR-10 and CelebA-HQ. ## 354. [Pushback in a Haystack: Language Models Respond to Factual Errors Ambiguously and Ineffectively](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17703) <a id="link354"></a>

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17703v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: The sycophancy of language models, or their failure to push back against faulty user input, is now widely studied. However, if LMs do push back, is sycophancy solved? We show that LMs vary in how they present pushback, and thus vary in how effectively they correct user beliefs. We introduce a phrase-level, human-validated response labeling framework to uncover fine-grained insights into how LMs push against and/or affirm false inputs. We develop and apply our framework on model responses in five languages: English, German, Chinese, Hindi, and Yoruba. We find that when models do push back against users' false assertions, they tend to use indirect phrasing and mix in affirmation, with more indirectness in Hindi and Yoruba than in other languages considered. In a large-scale human study (n = 300), we find that the pushback ambiguity we identified may fail to correct and prevent users' factual misconceptions.

## 355. [MLAfication: Efficiently Enabling High-Fidelity MLA for Modern LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17202) <a id="link355"></a>

**Authors:** Zile Wang, Zhide Xie, Tao Ji

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17202v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Multi-Head Latent Attention (MLA) compresses the KV cache into a latent representation, reducing inference memory and bandwidth. Existing MHA2MLA/TransMLA methods do not support QKNorm and require costly recovery on modern LLMs, especially MoE. We present MLAfication, a QKNorm-compatible, parameter-efficient extension of MHA2MLA. On Qwen3-30B-A3B, MLAfication delivers up to $5.35\times$ the serving throughput of BF16 GQA and, combined with 4-bit KV quantization, reduces the KV-cache footprint by $94.73\%$. ## 356. [Executable Prefix Values for Locating Agent Failures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17633) <a id="link356"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17633
**Authors:** Bingyu Shen, Jiaqi Tang, Lan Wei, Yi Sheng, J. Jenny Li, Boyang Li

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17633v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Work on debugging LLM agents treats the "first mistake" marked by a human or LLM judge as ground truth for where a trajectory failed. We ask whether such labels point at causes or symptoms, and make the distinction executable: we restart the agent from reconstructed intermediate states of a failed trajectory and estimate its prefix value V(h_t), the probability of success from each step, with a certified point of no return (PNR) for a failure. On 150 SWE-bench Verified tasks with two frozen open-weight agents, we build such curves for 47 and 60 failed trajectories and probe every step labeled by four frontier judges under the Traverse rubric. We find that the labels do not mark what a controller needs. Judges of different lineages agree on the exact step in only 21/57 cases, and the newest judges flag mistakes in 8/10 and 10/10 successful runs. For 66% of the labels, the agent's success probability does not drop at the labeled step; only 7/188 answers name the decisive step. Restarting at the measured restart point raises expected success from 0.44 to 0.57 offline, and real restarts confirm the advantage, whereas restarting at a judge's label does not (-0.08 to -0.03). We release our code, curves, and labels.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper studies debugging LLM agents by estimating prefix values and analyzing failure points, which is interesting but does not match criteria related to RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, evaluation paradigms, or interpretability.

## 357. [SemTree: Judge-Aware Semantic Decision Trees for Inspectable and Repairable Rule Decisions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13999) <a id="link357"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.13999
**Authors:** Jincheng Gao, Wei Ding, Shuo Sun

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.13999v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Rule decisions such as content moderation, advertising compliance, and eligibility checks combine several conditions, exemptions, and missing-information cases, and an error can be fixed only once the failing condition is known. We introduce SemTree, a framework that represents a rule decision as a semantic decision tree: each node is a natural-language question with explicit Yes/No/Unknown criteria, a pluggable semantic judge answers it, and the branching structure is compiled on the answers that this judge actually gives. The executed path is the reasoning behind every decision, so errors are repaired where they occur: a reviewer corrects a single node and replays the rule, corrected nodes become training labels for the judge, and a tool-using LLM editor revises questions and subtrees while observing execution results. On all 8,276 ShARC test cases, a tree executed by a hosted judge reaches 67.70% accuracy with 2.99 questions per case, on par with asking the same judge for the final label (68.23%); an earlier tree compiled for a different judge scores 47.87% when executed by this judge, while a new tree compiled on this judge's answers to a 12-question initial pool already scores 64.39%. Node-level repair raises BANKING77 accuracy from 82.04% to 86.16%, an explicit exemption node cuts the false positives of a Chinese e-commerce moderation clause from 24 to 5, and a trained editor raises the development accuracy of edited trees from 64.47% to 73.35%.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper proposes SemTree, a semantic decision tree framework for interpretable and repairable rule decisions, which is about explainability and rule-based decision systems, not about RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, or mechanistic interpretability of neural networks.

## 358. [Stable Token-level Scheduling for Serverless LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17689) <a id="link358"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17689
**Authors:** Hardi Ashishbhai Desai, Balaji N K, Sourav Kanti Addya

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17689v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Serverless inference is emerging as an efficient deployment model for Large Language Models (LLMs) due to its ability to elastically scale resources, improve GPU utilization through resource sharing, and reduce infrastructure costs. However, existing approaches primarily optimize time to first token (TTFT) while overlooking inter-token latency (ITL), a key determinant of user experience during response generation. We observe that LLM inference comprises a computation-intensive prefill stage and a bandwidth-bound decode stage, where admitting new requests can disrupt ongoing decoding and increase latency. To address this, we propose Token Window Pacing (TWP), a closed-loop scheduling framework that leverages runtime feedback from ITL, queue depth, and workload characteristics to regulate request admission at the token level instead of the batch level. TWP dynamically adjusts a selfregulating token window and incorporates chunk-based prefill to minimize interference from incoming requests. Experiments on realistic serverless workloads show that TWP delivers stable TTFT, significantly reduces latency spikes, lowers P99 tail latency, minimizes ITL variation, and improves throughput by up to 45% over ServerlessLLM and 58% over conventional schedulers. These results demonstrate that feedback-driven tokenlevel scheduling enables stable, high-throughput serverless LLM inference without modifying the underlying model.

**Comment:** Not relevant to any criterion. The paper proposes a scheduling method for serverless LLM inference to improve latency and throughput but does not address instruction-following, contamination, diffusion models, or interpretability.

## 359. [MFG: Multi-turn Feedback Guided Molecular Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18851) <a id="link359"></a>

**Authors:** Trieu Nguyen, Frazier N. Baker, Ruoxi Gao, Daniel Adu-Ampratwum, Xia Ning

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18851v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Multi-objective molecular optimization is a critical process in drug discovery that aims to modify a given molecule to simultaneously improve multiple properties while maintaining a similar overall structure. This process often requires iterative modifications guided by computational or experimental feedback. Recent large language model (LLM)-based methods have begun to explore this iterative approach; however, these methods are not explicitly trained to leverage such feedback, while existing approaches primarily focus on single-turn molecular optimization. To address this gap, we introduce Multi-turn Feedback Guided (MFG) molecular optimization, a reinforcement learning framework that trains an LLM to progressively optimize molecules using feedback from previous turns. MFG introduces a feedback vector capturing both property improvements and source molecule similarity, alongside a feedback-aligned reward function that promotes progressive improvement across turns. We apply MFG over $\text{GeLLM}^{3}\text{O}$, a state-of-the-art LLM model for multi-objective molecular optimization, evaluate $\text{GeLLM}^{3}\text{O-MFG}$ on ten multi-objective molecular optimization tasks. The results demonstrate that $\text{GeLLM}^{3}\text{O-MFG}$ achieves the highest average similarity-constrained success rate ($81.50%$), outperforming the base model $\text{GeLLM}^{3}\text{O}$ ($70.81%$).

## 360. [Understanding Personalization Gains in Whisper](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17019) <a id="link360"></a>

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17019v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Whisper-style ASR models provide strong zero-shot transcription, but their residual errors are not evenly distributed across datasets, recording conditions, and metadata-based groups. Across Common Voice, LibriSpeech, and VoxPopuli, we compare zero-shot Whisper, pooled LoRA, and group-specific LoRA under matched within-group splits. We find that adaptation is not uniformly beneficial: gains are concentrated in difficult Common Voice groups, are consistent on VoxPopuli, and largely disappear on low-error LibriSpeech. Crucially, pooled LoRA often matches, and sometimes clearly outperforms, group-specific LoRA. Gains over zero-shot should therefore be tested against matched pooled adaptation before being interpreted as evidence of personalization.

## 361. [Towards Robust Calibration Dataset for Domain-General Mixture of Experts Pruning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15578) <a id="link361"></a>
