**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15578v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Post-training expert pruning is a promising deployment technique for Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models. The calibration set is an integral part of the pruning process, however, its correlation with evaluation performance is largely misunderstood and unintuitive. In this work, we present an empirical study of the robustness of calibration perturbations, specifically in MoE models. Showing how calibration size, language, and data distribution affect layer-wise expert pruning for gpt-oss-20b. Our findings reveal that (1) domain-general pruning quality receives minimal benefit from scaling calibration data beyond small sample sizes, (2) cross-lingual calibration transfers effectively, with English-only calibration maintaining strong performance across seven languages of varying typologies and scripts, and (3) a diverse calibration data resembling the pretraining distribution can yield comparable or superior performance to test-distribution data. Ultimately, we provide practical insights as a recipe for better understanding calibration for more effective domain-general expert pruning.

## 362. [Relational Learning as World Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16763) <a id="link362"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16763
**Authors:** Zheyu Zhang, Gjergji Kasneci

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16763v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Relational learning typically predicts from a database state, but decision making asks a different question: how will that state change under an action? In operational systems, the answer depends on system-specific dynamics that may not be visible in the database itself. We view each such system as a relational "world" and ask whether a model can infer how it behaves from observed executions. We introduce TabWM (Tabular World Model) and WorldRel, a generator of executable relational worlds for pretraining. Given executions from an unseen world, TabWM uses them as context to predict the sparse structured edits caused by a new action without parameter updates. Across 512 held-out worlds, complete-edit F1 rises from 0.016 with no observed executions to 0.685 with 64, where a correct edit must match its operation, relational location, and value. Because these predictions are explicit relational edits, they can be applied recursively to imagine candidate futures and compare actions before they are taken. Broader search over these predicted futures improves the realized return of selected action sequences. Together, these results suggest that relational dynamics can be learned in context, broadening relational learning from modeling system state to modeling how that state changes under action.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specified criteria. The paper focuses on relational learning and world modeling for predicting state changes under actions, which is not about RLHF, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.

## 363. [Structural Baselines Matter for Agent Failure Detection and Attribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16661) <a id="link363"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16661
**Authors:** Ramesh Arvind Naagarajan, Stefan Streif

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16661v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: An LLM agent using tools can fail silently. It stops before the task is done, raises no error, and answers fluently. Two kinds of monitor address this, one deciding whether a run failed and the other naming the step where failure became unavoidable. Much of the reported performance on both tasks comes from regularities that need no model. Failing runs are longer, and the step labelled decisive is usually the last. A rule that always names the last step is correct $67\%$ to $96\%$ of the time here, without reading the run at all. That ceiling reflects how the labels were written, not how agents break. On failures whose decisive step replay can confirm, it falls to near chance. Reporting gain over that rule therefore changes what a score means. Under that correction, methods tested here gain nothing on give-up failures, while several published systems keep a clear advantage on other splits. For detection, the agent's internal states beat transcript length and text similarity on the agent benchmark, and hold up where the text baseline does not. Precision stays low when failures are rare, so such a monitor should rank runs for review, not replace it.

**Comment:** Does not match any specific criteria related to RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms. Focuses on failure detection in LLM agents but mainly analyzes baseline heuristics rather than proposing new instruction-following or contamination methods.

## 364. [Lost in Implicit Routing: How LLM Agents Fail to Load Relevant Skills at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18522) <a id="link364"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18522
**Authors:** Jinlin Liu, Wei Chen, Xiaojin Zhang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18522v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reusable skills let LLM agents load task-specific instructions on demand, but they make execution depend on implicit routing over skill metadata. We test whether this design scales by holding SkillsBench tasks and their golden skills fixed while adding irrelevant skills. We find skill routing collapse: agents increasingly load fewer or no skills, even though the relevant skills remain available. With 1,000 irrelevant skills, loaded-skill recall falls from 73.6% to 27.4% and the zero-load rate rises from 9.3% to 51.6%, whereas explicit selection from the same library retains 73.9% recall. Loading other skills, including plausible alternatives to golden skills, is too rare to explain this decline. Masking only the descriptions of irrelevant skills largely restores skill loading, yet with the metadata intact, explicitly asking agents to plan skill use still yields lower recall than preselection. These findings point to semantic interference from descriptions and instruction interference when skill choice competes with task execution. We also evaluate explicit preselection as a practical mitigation, including its coverage and latency trade-offs. Our analysis informs skill protocols and evaluation for agents with growing skill libraries.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. Discusses skill routing failures in LLM agents but does not address instruction-following fine-tuning, contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15661v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Feedback presentation shapes how LLMs use evaluator judgments during translation refinement. On the 29,400-record rewriting subset, mean xCOMET scores multiplied by 100 are 83.30 for low-load feedback and 80.67 for high-load feedback; the full-matrix original-translation reference mean is 83.46. A no-feedback second pass yields a small improvement over the original translations, and direct evaluator prompting reproduces the low-load advantage without a separate rewriting stage. These results show that compact feedback limits the quality loss from evaluator-guided refinement in the evaluated high-resource MT settings.

## 366. [Socratic Skill Learning: Interleaving Teaching and Practice for Transferable Agent Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17860) <a id="link366"></a>

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17860v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Agent Skills improve LLM agents by making task knowledge reusable across sessions. We introduce Socratic Skill Learning (SSL), which interleaves expert teaching with Student practice. An Educator guides investigation while the Student remains the sole environment operator and Skill author, able to question, test, and adapt advice. To test reuse beyond the learning task, we construct SkillTransferBench, with 35 task families and 408 executable cases, pairing public variation specifications with held-out evaluation in fresh solver sessions. On the main 22-family, 155-case cohort, Fixed-Window SSL reaches 0.801 utility, versus 0.723 without a Skill, 0.761 for adapted Trace2Skill (trajectory distillation), and 0.751 for adapted SKILL-KD (contrastive distillation). Across three model pairs on shared families, interleaved guidance outperforms expert authoring and post-hoc teaching. Process analysis links guidance to Student writing and package revision; a case study shows the Student testing and refining a proposed procedure for later reuse. As demand for new lessons falls, the cost of fixed-window review grows. Issue-Guided SSL tracks unresolved questions across Skill revisions to select and focus review, reaching 0.805 utility with 61.7% fewer Educator tokens and 16.9% fewer total acquisition tokens than Fixed-Window SSL on the main cohort.

## 367. [Learning after Execution with Action-Specific Credit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14573) <a id="link367"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14573
**Authors:** Yufeng Tian, Jiahao Tang, Minghao Wu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14573v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Execution is often used as terminal feedback: it scores a generated program, but the resulting state is not used for another learning action. Learning after execution instead uses the execution result as an intermediate state that supports an additional learning interaction for the same policy. This creates a credit-assignment problem because the two actions share the same final objective but play different roles in the trajectory. Accordingly, action-specific credit assigns the initial action credit from the final trajectory outcome and the corrective action credit for improvement over the intermediate state. Image-to-TikZ serves as an executable visual testbed for this setting. On 945 held-out examples, learning after execution strengthens the shared policy under single-call inference and also produces a post-execution corrective capability. Single-call render success reaches 92.70\%, compared with 88.25\% for continued single-stage GRPO after the same number of additional training iterations. This corrective capability is not reproduced by continued single-stage training and depends on the intermediate state. Alternative credit definitions also induce different group-relative preferences over the same trajectories. Together, these results suggest that executable tasks contain useful learning structure beyond a terminal reward. Code is available at https://github.com/tianyufengcn/Learning-after-Execution.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specified criteria. The paper focuses on a credit-assignment method in reinforcement learning for executable tasks, but does not discuss RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or other listed criteria.

## 368. [SkillFusion: Towards Reliable Multi-Skill Composition for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15481) <a id="link368"></a>

**Authors:** Enhao Huang, Xinye Zhao, Yiming Li, Bingrun Yang, Nanjun Zhou, Chunshu Xia, Hongwei Yao, Zhan Qin

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15481v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Independently authored skills can impose incompatible requirements, competing preferences, and redundant instructions when jointly loaded by an LLM agent. We propose SkillFusion, an offline compiler that reconciles these interactions into a reusable merged skill. Its central idea is to make composition decisions explicit through typed, scoped constraints linked to their source directives. A type-scope gate selects candidate interactions, while selective fallback revisits pairs potentially missed by structural pruning. A classifier identifies interaction types, and an adversarial verifier re-examines uncertain judgments. The resolver applies conditional routing or explicit priorities to hard conflicts, verified abstraction to soft conflicts, and deduplication to redundant instructions. Source-linked checks guide synthesis and compression, and metadata synthesis supports discovery of the merged skill. The resulting artifact retains associated scripts and resources and can be reused across tasks and executor models. SkillFusion achieves the highest mean Success among evaluated non-oracle methods on all three agent benchmarks and on both transfer benchmarks across three executor models. At the largest tested instruction configurations, it achieves over 2.5 times the Success of unresolved multi-skill loading, highlighting the growing value of reconciliation as composition becomes more demanding. The code will be open-sourced after the review process concludes.

## 369. [SARATHI: Decoupled Data-Free Probing and Adaptive Reconstruction for Structured LLM Pruning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15514) <a id="link369"></a>

**Authors:** Sankar Behera, Dhruv Singh, Satyadev Ahlawat, Yamuna Prasad

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15514v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Structured pruning reduces the inference cost of large language models (LLMs) by permanently removing neurons and weight rows, yielding dense, hardware-agnostic speedup. Existing post-training methods couple neuron selection and weight reconstruction through a shared calibration dataset, introducing domain bias and out-of-memory failures at high sparsity. We propose SARATHI, a framework that decouples these two stages. A data-free \emph{NMF Residual Subspace Probe} scores neurons using intrinsic weight geometry, without accessing any calibration data, and carries a formal connection to Robust PCA. \emph{Adaptive OBS} then reconstructs the pruned weights with a localized calibration batch, operating in $\mathcal{O}(d^2)$ peak memory per layer and eliminating the global memory bottleneck of coupled methods. A \emph{Bias Shift Compensation} step corrects systematic activation drift in post-LayerNorm architectures that would otherwise cause perplexity collapse at high sparsity. Experiments across six models from two architecture families (gated and non-gated FFN) at 15--40\% sparsity show that SARATHI achieves competitive perplexity and matches or exceeds existing structured pruning baselines on zero-shot accuracy.

## 370. [Label-Centric Knowledge Retrieval for Zero-Shot Text Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16182) <a id="link370"></a>

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16182v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Flagship large language models (LLMs) offer rich general knowledge and strong reasoning capabilities, but their high inference cost and latency limit their use for large-scale data labeling. We study zero-shot text classification with semantically meaningful labels and task descriptions, without task-specific labeled examples or classifier feedback. We propose \textbf{SLKR} (Semantic Label Knowledge Retrieval), a label-centric retrieval-augmented generation framework that separates offline knowledge construction from lightweight online inference. Offline, a flagship LLM independently generates label definitions, retrieval keywords, and boundary rules for potentially confusable label pairs, forming a reusable label-knowledge store. Because this construction operates over the label space rather than the input corpus, its one-time flagship cost can be amortized across large-scale inference. Online, hybrid semantic and lexical retrieval first identifies the top-$K$ candidate labels and then selects up to $M$ input-relevant boundary rules connecting candidate-label pairs. A lightweight LLM performs the final classification in a single call using the retrieved definitions and rules. Across three public benchmarks and a real-world multilingual book-classification dataset, SLKR achieves the best results on all four datasets with Qwen3.5-4B and on three of four with Ministral-3-3B, while using compact input contexts and no task-specific labeled examples. Boundary ablations further show that retrieving a small set of input-relevant rules preserves performance close to using all boundaries with substantially less context, supporting efficient reuse of flagship-generated label knowledge for lightweight classification.

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18026v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Some observation-window-based KV cache compressors use prompt attention to identify important tokens but allocate uniform or predefined budgets across heads, overlooking input-dependent differences in budget demand. An abnormal-head mask and a floor-budget mechanism improve robustness under weak attention responses. On LongBench and RULER, both Champion variants consistently improve over their SnapKV and PyramidKV base compressors; the improvements are largest and statistically significant on Llama3.1-70B-Instruct, especially under tight budgets, whereas results on the tested 7B/8B models remain competitive but mixed. ChampionKV does not uniformly outperform Ada-KV, whose global cross-head Top-K selection retains an advantage in several settings. Controlled analysis further shows improved retained attention mass over the corresponding base compressors at negligible overhead.

## 374. [Correcting or Continuing? What On-Policy Distillation Learns from Student Errors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18011) <a id="link374"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18011
**Authors:** Shuo Yang, Zheyu Zhang, Mahito Sugiyama, Gjergji Kasneci

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18011v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student on its own rollouts under a frozen teacher, mitigating the distribution mismatch of off-policy supervision. While prior work has primarily trained on problems a student can largely solve, we ask whether OPD can improve a student when training is restricted to problems it currently fails. We evaluate five representative OPD mechanisms with Qwen-3 and Phi-4 models under a unified competition-mathematics protocol. We find that OPD does not improve accuracy on this hard subset, with most mechanisms instead degrading performance despite continued decreases in training KL divergence. All eight configurations show a decline, and the two-sided sign test gives p=0.0078. In contrast, the same pipeline improves accuracy by 4.6 percentage points when training is not concentrated on failures, showing that the failure is conditional on the training regime rather than systematic. These findings suggest that OPD is effective when supervision is dominated by correct student prefixes, but can reinforce existing errors when training is concentrated on problems the student currently fails.

**Comment:** Related to reinforcement learning methods but does not specifically address RLHF or instruction-following fine-tuning improvements; focuses on on-policy distillation and student error dynamics.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 4

---

## 375. [Correct Choices, Failed Sequences: Choice2Seq Benchmarks Correctness Retention Across Output Interfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16191) <a id="link375"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16191
**Authors:** Tianyou Lai, Wentao Yue, Zhiying Deng, Qilei Li

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16191v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: A model may select the correct answer yet fail to construct it through a sequence of local decisions. We introduce Choice2Seq, a diagnostic benchmark that measures correctness retention across complete-answer selection and sequential construction in independent runs on the same problems and candidate sets. Sequence Composition Rate (SCR) measures the fraction of correctly selected items that are also answered correctly through local construction. Jev achieves 97.1% and 99.5% selection accuracy on arithmetic and lexical retrieval, yet retains only 33.0% and 2.1% of those correct choices under serial construction, respectively. Substantial gaps also emerge for StartLux and Qwen, including when Qwen emits characters directly. Restricting local options to candidate-supported characters improves construction, while explicit position indexing improves copying fidelity, even for incorrect selections. These interventions distinguish task correctness, plan fidelity, and output compliance. Matched dialogue-state evaluations reveal that local field construction largely preserves Jev's correct states but reduces Qwen's mean-turn accuracy, highlighting task- and interface-dependent differences. Sequence-level confidence and request-cost analyses further characterize completed outputs.

**Comment:** Focuses on diagnostic benchmarks for correctness retention in sequence generation but does not fundamentally rethink evaluation paradigms or match criteria related to instruction-following or contamination.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 4

---

## 376. [Disentangling Marginal Capacity from Geometry in Optimal-Transport Label Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14853) <a id="link376"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14853
**Authors:** Ali Khodabandeh Yalabadi, Azim Dehghani Amirabad, Artem Moskalev, Yun-Ching Chen, Mehdi Pirooznia, Scott Oloff, Rui Liao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14853v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Optimal transport (OT) is widely used to align distributions and transfer labels between unpaired source and target data, but source-target composition shifts can cause rare classes to be systematically under-predicted even with high transport confidence. We identify a source of this failure in the transport marginal: under the standard uniform weighting of source points, each class has transport capacity proportional to its source frequency, which can be insufficient to meet its target demand even when separated well in the embedding space. We show that displaced mass is preferentially absorbed by high-capacity classes rather than geometrically similar classes, producing confident errors that are difficult to detect from the transport plan. Downstream read-outs, entropic smoothing, and marginal relaxation provide limited recovery, whereas matching the transport marginal to the target composition substantially improves label transfer. On single-cell data, the oracle composition-matched marginal raises macro-F1 from 0.322 to 0.623, and the best deployable estimator closes 69% of the oracle gap. Correcting the marginal also separates a coverage-dependent component of error from residual error associated with class separability, showing that transport capacity and representation geometry are distinct sources of transfer error.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper focuses on optimal transport label transfer and distribution alignment, which is not related to RLHF, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or interpretability. It is more about statistical methodology in label transfer but not in language modeling or instruction-following.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 4

## 377. [Reasoning for Propaganda Detection via Label-Guided CoT Optimisation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17271) <a id="link377"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17271
**Authors:** Zeba Afroz, Harsh Vardhan, Md. Shad Akhtar

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17271v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Propaganda detection systems usually operate as black boxes, lacking explanations. To address this, we hypothesise that persuasive techniques contain definitional characteristics with respect to their social-political setting. We introduce ReCoT, a reasoning-conditioned detection framework that captures definitional cues of persuasive techniques via population-level refinement and performance-guided resampling, without requiring additional explanation annotations. On INDI-PROPspan, ReCoT achieves a macro F1_LCS of 0.364, surpassing baselines and reaching 88% of the SFT upper bound on Avg F1_LCS. Similarly, on PTC, ReCoT reaches 85% of the SFT upper bound on Avg F1_LCS, closing much of this gap while remaining interpretable. Importantly, the analysis of the reasoning pool's convergence and update pattern for ReCoT shows effective refinement of reasoning CoTs.

**Comment:** No specific criteria matched. The paper proposes a reasoning-conditioned framework for propaganda detection using chain-of-thought optimization, but this is an application and does not focus on RLHF or instruction-following improvements or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 4

## 378. [Estimating Surprisal from the Speech Signal](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18439) <a id="link378"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18439
**Authors:** Zachary Hopton, Samuel Kiegeland, Francesco Ignazio Re, Patrick Haller, Matthew B. Winn, Ryan Cotterell, Eleanor Chodroff

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18439v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Although language is predominantly spoken, empirical tests of surprisal theory rely almost entirely on written text. In practice, surprisal is estimated using language models (LMs) trained on text and applied to transcriptions, creating a text bottleneck that limits evaluation of the theory in the spoken modality. We remove this bottleneck by estimating surprisal directly from the speech signal using a speech LM. This raises a key question: should surprisal be defined over realization-specific acoustic inputs or over abstract linguistic units? Since a single unit can have many distinct acoustic realizations, each assigned a different probability by the model, defining surprisal over abstract units is difficult. To address this, we introduce an importance sampling estimator that approximates the probability of an abstract unit by marginalizing over realizations generated by a text-to-speech model. Using pupillometry data from a listening study, we evaluate surprisal theory in this text-free setting at multiple levels of granularity. Obtaining surprisal from several speech LMs, we consistently find that surprisal estimated via importance sampling better predicts pupil dilation than realization-specific surprisal for words. We also find that surprisal values for sub-lexical units are predictive of pupil dilation. While we find that surprisal can be estimated without recourse to text, and that marginalizing over distinct realizations of words is more predictive of lexical processing effort, surprisal as a whole is not a strong predictor of change in pupil size, regardless of its source.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper studies surprisal estimation from speech signals using language models, which is related to language modeling and surprisal theory but does not address instruction-following, RLHF, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms explicitly.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 4

## 379. [PANINI-DR: Controlled Deep Research through Generative Semantic Workspaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15208) <a id="link379"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15208
**Authors:** M. Yigit Turali, Shreyas Rajesh, M. Efe Lorasdagi, Chenda Duan, Kartik Sharma, Vwani Roychowdhury

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15208v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Deep research requires connecting multiple steps of search, reasoning, and computation, with intermediate findings guiding what to investigate next. As investigations grow, agents must maintain research state across model calls and check findings before errors propagate into subsequent work. We introduce PANINI-DR, a multi-agent harness that separates coordination of the overall investigation from the research and verification of individual subquestions. A shared workspace, built on the Generative Semantic Workspace representation, records subquestions, their dependencies, findings, and supporting evidence. Researchers investigate individual subquestions, while a separate verification agent checks proposed answers against those subquestions and their evidence. Verification feedback guides further investigation, and recorded findings inform subsequent assignments. We evaluate PANINI-DR on BrowseComp-Plus, FRAMES, and MoNaCo against seven state-of-the-art deep-research harnesses, all using a shared language-model backbone and frozen corpora. PANINI-DR achieves the strongest average performance across the three benchmarks. On BrowseComp-Plus, it achieves 73.3% accuracy, outperforming the next-best harness by 16.0 points. Its advantage is strongest on hard questions, where it answers nearly twice as many as the next-best harness. Ablations further show that structured planning and persistent state improve performance, as does checking each finding against its cited evidence before it is reused. Because a model can answer correctly from memory alone, we also analyze the evidence retained by each harness. PANINI-DR achieves the highest answer recoverability from saved evidence on all three benchmarks, while complementary claim-support audits show stronger evidence-backed answers. Accounting for this evidence changes how harnesses compare and further strengthens PANINI-DR's advantage.

**Comment:** Not directly matching any specific criterion; focuses on multi-agent research harness and verification for deep research tasks using language models, but does not address instruction-following fine-tuning, contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 4

## 380. [Query Your Way Out: Benchmarking Multi-Turn Knowledge Reasoning through Generalized Puzzle Solving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16133) <a id="link380"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16133
**Authors:** Xin Cheng, Junming Yang, Ning Xu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16133v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Puzzles offer a natural testbed for Large Language Models (LLMs) reasoning because they pose verifiable goals under partial information and require multi-step decisions to reach a solution. However, existing puzzle benchmarks typically focus on narrow environments with rigid mechanics and tightly constrained solution targets. To bridge this gap, we introduce the Active Probing Benchmark (APB), which generalizes puzzle-solving by casting concept identification as an interactive reasoning task that integrates specialized knowledge assessment into the puzzle setting. Specifically, APB evaluates whether models can acquire information via multi-turn active probing and inference to pinpoint 960 target concepts of specialized difficulty across eight diverse academic domains. Our evaluation reveals a counter-intuitive phenomenon: most open-source reasoning models significantly underperform their standard instruction counterparts. This finding suggests that in interactive settings, these models struggle to leverage procedural reasoning to reliably access and apply specialized domain knowledge. Beyond these empirical insights, APB provides a scalable pipeline for generating benchmarks that probe knowledge reasoning under interaction across diverse settings.

**Comment:** This paper studies multi-turn knowledge reasoning and instruction-following in interactive puzzle solving but does not propose new fine-tuning or RLHF methods for instruction-following (criterion 1). It also does not address test contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms explicitly.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 4

## 381. [SparPR: Runtime KV-Cache Recovery for Resource-Constrained LLM Inference on Consumer Hardware](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18140) <a id="link381"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18140
**Authors:** Jieyun Deng, Keming Hu, Yanghe Liu, Li Feng

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.18140v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Running large language models (LLMs) on consumer hardware is bottlenecked by the key-value (KV) cache, which grows with every decoded token and quickly exhausts the memory and bandwidth of a single consumer GPU. We present SparPR (Sparse Partial Recomputation), a runtime KV-cache recovery mechanism that does not modify model weights and requires no offline training. SparPR couples three online decisions: Query-Aware key-block selection scores historical blocks against the current query; dynamic budget control estimates per-block copy and recompute costs to decide how many blocks to retain; and on-demand local recomputation with cost-aware fallback merges retained blocks into continuous spans and switches between sparse recomputation and full recovery according to a runtime cost comparison. Implemented on FlexGen/FlexLLMGen, SparPR is evaluated on a single consumer laptop (RTX 3070 Ti 8GB, Ryzen 9 6900HX, 16GB RAM). It cuts end-to-end latency by 31%, 52%, and 63% at 1k, 2k, and 4k prompt tokens respectively, with 1.4x-2.7x higher throughput, and completes an 8k-token workload in 44 minutes where the FlexGen baseline times out. Under a fixed cache budget, SparPR retains a Needle Accuracy that stays well above StreamingLLM and H2O at every keep ratio, and ablation studies confirm that the three mechanisms act jointly.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criteria. Paper focuses on runtime KV-cache recovery for LLM inference efficiency on consumer hardware, not on RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, evaluation paradigms, or interpretability.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 4

## 382. [Improving the Evaluation of Biomedical Question Answering with Few Human Preference Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16008) <a id="link382"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16008
**Authors:** Shakila Mahjabin Tonni, Xiang Dai, Sarvnaz Karimi, Xiaomei Li, Samuel C. Andrew, Stephen Wan

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16008v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems are increasingly used for biomedical Question Answering (QA). However, evaluating their effectiveness remains challenging due to the lack of reliable reference resources and the frequent misalignment between automatic evaluation metrics and human judgments. One practical question is how evaluation results should be used to guide development decisions, especially when they correlate weakly with human judgments and may even lead to different conclusions. We explore how to use a small amount of human preference signals to calibrate direct assessment evaluation metrics, and show that calibrated metrics consistently outperform baselines that either treat all metrics equally or rely on a single best metric across multiple datasets.

**Comment:** Does not match any specific criterion: focuses on evaluation calibration for biomedical QA using human preference signals, not on open-ended evaluation paradigms or instruction-following improvements.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 3

## 383. [Should SLMs be in the Driver's Seat? A Study of Local Models as Interpreters in Cloud-Written Agent Programs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14209) <a id="link383"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14209
**Authors:** Boris Radovi\v{c}, Marco Canini, Veljko Pejovi\'c

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14209v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: The most accurate agents executing user-provided tasks are driven by large, cloud-hosted models, which solve them by reading the private data of the user. Agents powered by models small enough to run on edge devices keep that data local, but yield far worse results. A third option is to have cloud models write a program that devices execute: the data stays local, but as the program is written before any data is seen, it fails whenever a step depends on interpreting content that appears only at run time. We explore an intermediate architecture, in which the cloud-written program defers bounded questions over user data to a small local model through an ordinary function call. On AppWorld this improves the success rate of a code-only agent in all six combinations of backbone model and test partition we evaluate, by up to 14.9pp, recovering a substantial part of the gap to a cloud-hosted ReAct loop while keeping user data on the device.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper discusses architectures combining cloud and local models for agent programs, focusing on data locality and success rates, but does not address RLHF, instruction tuning, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or interpretability.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 3

## 384. [State Representation Shapes Long-Horizon Agent Learning and Transfer: An Empirical Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15407) <a id="link384"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15407
**Authors:** Dong Nie, Rui Lan, Yixin Nie, Lingzi Hong

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.15407v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: An end-to-end success rate alone cannot distinguish failure to learn an action from failure to apply it as task state changes. We study this distinction in deterministic tool workflows that provide the current artifact state and candidate action recipes. Using 1.7B, 8B, and 27B Qwen models, we compare supervised learning across workflow horizons and cross the state representation used for training with the one used at evaluation. Horizon-matched supervision yields high completion, but an H4-trained 1.7B model transfers poorly to longer workflows under full-state observations. In an H16 crossover, compact-trained 1.7B adapters complete 50/50 held-out tasks in compact view and 0/50 in full view for each of three paired training seeds; full-trained outcomes vary across seeds. A separate 8B adapter pair shows a similar asymmetry. The view dependence also appears in paired-choice tasks where both candidate calls are executable and the correct choice depends on table contents; here, the compact mask does not consult the target action. An explicit relevance cue improves full-view execution but does not close the gap for the tested checkpoint. In a separate 27B ScienceWorld study, compact-state training improves local accuracy under both views, indicating that the transfer pattern depends on the task and observation construction. Together, these experiments identify representation-sensitive transfer as a bottleneck in the controlled workflows, without isolating content reduction from other changes to the observation.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criterion. The paper studies state representation effects on long-horizon agent learning with LLMs but does not propose new instruction-following fine-tuning, contamination detection, diffusion LM advances, or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 3

## 385. [Empowering Edge Agents: A Systematic Analysis of Edge-Cloud Collaboration in LLM Agent Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17816) <a id="link385"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17816
**Authors:** Pengcheng Huang, Zhenghao Liu, Shixin Ren, Shi Yu, Sijia Yao, Yukun Yan, Chaojun Xiao, Maosong Sun

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.17816v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Edge-cloud collaboration combines the lower inference overhead and greater data locality of edge-side execution with the stronger capabilities of cloud-hosted models. However, as LLM agents increasingly operate in long-horizon scenarios, cloud participation becomes interleaved throughout execution rather than a one-shot decision for each query. Such interleaved participation introduces trajectory-level effects that remain insufficiently understood. To address this gap, we introduce EC2-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating edge-cloud collaboration in long-horizon agent execution. It provides executable digital-workspace agent tasks and a unified evaluation framework covering task performance, resource usage, and privacy exposure. Using this benchmark, we empirically study diverse forms of edge-cloud collaboration. Our study reveals three findings. First, collaboration achieves a favorable balance among task performance, resource usage, and privacy exposure compared with edge-only and cloud-only execution. Second, as edge models become stronger, collaborative execution approaches cloud-only performance, and performance differences across strategies shrink, while privacy exposure remains strongly strategy-dependent. Third, stronger edge capability shifts preferred collaboration from externally controlled cloud participation and direct cloud execution toward more edge-controlled collaboration. Further analyses identify cloud-invocation decision-making and instruction following as important edge-side abilities for effective collaboration. These findings provide a systematic understanding of edge-cloud collaboration in long-horizon agent execution and insights for designing future edge agents that effectively collaborate with cloud models. Code and resources are available at https://github.com/OpenBMB/EC2-Bench.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper studies edge-cloud collaboration for LLM agents and instruction following in a system context, but does not propose new RLHF or instruction-following fine-tuning methods, nor new evaluation paradigms or contamination detection.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 3

## 386. [Learn from Move: the Next Step for GUI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16720) <a id="link386"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16720
**Authors:** Jiahao Lu, Yize Shen, Chuanyuan Tan, Mianqiu Huang, Shicheng Fang, Xinchi Chen, Yining Zheng, Yuxin Wang, Xipeng Qiu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.16720v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Existing benchmarks for GUI agents primarily focus on increasing task difficulty by extending task horizons. Besides, enabling agents to explore unfamiliar environments and successfully complete tasks within them is also a critical yet challenging problem. We introduce LatentGUIWorld, a benchmark of six tasks in three matched pairs organized by the evidence needed for control: $I_0$ uses prior knowledge and available observations, $I_1$ requires interaction that directly reveals hidden semantics, and $I_2$ identifies dynamics from action-response relationships. Frontier models struggle especially on $I_2$ tasks. We introduce MOVE, which separates movement from button events and exposes intermediate visual feedback, together with demonstrations of iterative exploration and control. Ablations show that both action granularity and multi-step demonstrations improve performance. After SFT, LatentLearner-9B achieves 50.0% overall success on LatentGUIWorld, comparable to several frontier models. Joint RL further raises egocentric Drag and Ten-Choice success to 92.0% and 75.3%. Behavioral analysis reveals multi-step exploration. In egocentric Drag, RL increases Pearson correlation between error-normalized commands and ideal gain compensation from 0.610 to 0.803. Together, these behaviors show that models learn to explore through interaction and adapt their control to environment dynamics. The same format improves GUI grounding after RL, reaching 67.37% mean accuracy across five benchmarks versus 66.72% for conventional RL.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper focuses on GUI agent benchmarks and training methods, including RL and supervised fine-tuning, but does not address RLHF, instruction-following improvements, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or interpretability.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 3

## 387. [Unconstrained CTI-REALM with Open-Weights](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14180) <a id="link387"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14180
**Authors:** Sahil Garg, Ruhi Gottumukkala, Vishal Agarwal, Shubham Vishwakarma

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.14180v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Organisations that want language-model agents for security work face a choice between open-weight models, which they can run and audit themselves, and far more expensive closed frontier models. We study that choice on CTI-REALM (arXiv:2603.13517), a benchmark in which an agent turns a threat-intelligence report into a working detection by querying a live telemetry store. We evaluate twenty-six models, nineteen open-weight and seven closed, under a common harness, scorer and panel of LLM judges. Our main finding is that an open-weight model can match the strongest closed models at less than a tenth of their cost, provided it is given enough turns. The benchmark's published limit on agent messages is a confound: a task that exhausts the limit scores zero on every checkpoint, including those it had already passed, so results at that limit partly measure which models finish in time. At the published limit the leading open-weight and closed models cannot be distinguished. With the limit relaxed, the models it was truncating recover, and the highest rewards belong to open-weight models. Among the leading models, price is a poor guide to capability: models with similar rewards differ in cost by more than an order of magnitude. Because the benchmark's checkpoint scores largely record what an agent did or mentioned, we also introduce stage-level metrics that score what the agent submits, by executing its detection queries in the telemetry store and judging its outputs against the answer key. Rather than combining them into one more overall reward, we show that different models lead different stages, which suggests routing each stage of the workflow to the model that performs it best rather than choosing a single model by its overall score. We also report how many tokens each model generates, and find that the share of output spent on thinking does not track the reward.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper evaluates open-weight vs closed-weight LLM agents on a security benchmark but focuses on benchmarking and cost-performance tradeoffs rather than methodological improvements or new evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 3

## Paper selection prompt
 1. New methodological improvements to RLHF or instruction-following which are specific fine-tuning steps that are taken to make language models better at following user instructions across a range of tasks.
    - Relevant: papers that discuss specific methods like RLHF, or instruction-tuning datasets, improving these methods, or analyzing them. Usually these papers will explicitly mention RLHF, instruction-following or instruction-tuning.
    - Not relevant: papers about adaptation to some task. Simply following instructions or inputs are not sufficient.
 2. Shows new powerful test set contamination or membership inference methods for language models. Test set contamination is the phenomenon where a language model observes a benchmark dataset during pretraining.
    - Relevant: test statistics that can detect contamination of benchmarks in language models. statistics that can provide guarantees are more interesting. membership inference methods that are general enough to apply to language models are also relevant.
    - Not relevant: any papers that do not consider language models, or that do not consider test set contamination.
 3. Shows a significant advance in the performance of diffusion language models.
    - Relevant: papers that study language models that are also diffusion models. Continuous diffusions are even more relevant, while discrete diffusions are less so.
    - Not relevant: papers about image diffusions like DALL-E or Stable Diffusion, or papers that do not explicitly mention language models or applications to text.
 4. Describes new paradigms to evaluating open-ended text generation. Evaluating the outputs of language models is hard, especially in open-ended settings like for chatbots.
    - Relevant: papers that fundamentally rethink language model evaluation -- especially by accounting for subjectivity or using adversaries.
    - Not relevant: specific evaluations for specific tasks, identifying new properties or flaws of language models, or simply collecting new data.
 5. Conducts surveys or provides data into real-world usage and safety properties of language models.
    - Relevant: papers that create new datasets or surveys on real-world usage of language models.
    - Not relevant: papers that apply language models to new real-world tasks.
 6. Studies 'scaling laws' in the context of neural networks. Scaling laws refer to the very clear power-law relationship between the size or computational power used to train a model and the performance of that model.
    - Relevant: theoretical or conceptual explanation behind scaling laws for language models.
    - Not relevant: papers that have experiments at different model scales (but do not explicitly fit a scaling law) or papers that mention scaling laws, but the scaling laws are not the central subject of the paper
 7. Presents new methods or improvements for sparse autoencoders in neural network interpretability. Sparse autoencoders are unsupervised learning techniques that learn sparse representations of neural network activations to understand what features networks detect.
    - Relevant: papers that explicitly mention sparse autoencoders, sparse coding, or sparse feature extraction applied to understanding neural networks, language models, or other AI systems.
    - Not relevant: papers about general autoencoders without sparsity constraints, or sparse methods not applied to interpretability.
 8. Demonstrates new mechanistic interpretability techniques or discoveries about how neural networks work internally. Mechanistic interpretability seeks to understand the algorithms and circuits that neural networks learn.
    - Relevant: papers about circuit analysis, feature visualization, activation patching, causal interventions on neural networks, or reverse-engineering specific algorithms learned by models.
    - Not relevant: papers that only do black-box analysis or correlation studies without mechanistic understanding.
 9. Shows advances in machine unlearning techniques for removing specific knowledge or behaviors from trained models.
    - Relevant: papers on forgetting specific data, removing harmful capabilities, or selectively editing model knowledge while preserving other capabilities.
    - Not relevant: papers about general model editing or fine-tuning without specific unlearning focus.
 10. Presents methods for tamper resistance, robustness against adversarial modifications, or secure AI systems.
    - Relevant: papers on preventing model poisoning, backdoor attacks, or making AI systems resistant to tampering or manipulation.
    - Not relevant: papers only about general adversarial robustness without security/tamper resistance focus.
 11. Studies AI alignment techniques, value learning, or methods to ensure AI systems behave according to human values and intentions.
    - Relevant: papers on reward modeling, constitutional AI, value alignment, or techniques to make AI systems more aligned with human preferences.
    - Not relevant: papers focused only on performance improvement without alignment considerations.
 12. Advances in LLM reasoning capabilities, chain-of-thought reasoning, or understanding how language models perform complex reasoning tasks.
    - Relevant: papers on step-by-step reasoning, logical inference in LLMs, reasoning chain analysis, or methods to improve reasoning capabilities.
    - Not relevant: papers about reasoning in non-LLM contexts or that don't focus on reasoning mechanisms.
 13. Presents new methods for AI control, oversight, or monitoring of AI system behavior during deployment.
    - Relevant: papers on AI governance techniques, monitoring systems, control mechanisms, or methods to maintain oversight of deployed AI systems.
    - Not relevant: papers focused only on training-time controls without deployment considerations.
 14. Shows advances in chain-of-thought monitoring, reasoning transparency, or methods to understand AI decision-making processes.
    - Relevant: papers on monitoring reasoning chains, making AI reasoning more transparent, or techniques to track how models arrive at conclusions.
    - Not relevant: papers about general explainability without specific focus on reasoning processes.
 15. Studies ControlArena, Inspect framework, or similar evaluation platforms for AI safety and control.
    - Relevant: papers using or extending these evaluation frameworks, comparative studies using these platforms, or methodological improvements to these tools.
    - Not relevant: papers that mention these tools only in passing without substantial use or analysis.
 16. Advances in latent reasoning, implicit reasoning processes, or understanding hidden computational processes in neural networks.
    - Relevant: papers on uncovering implicit reasoning, analyzing hidden computational steps, or methods to make latent reasoning processes more explicit.
    - Not relevant: papers about explicit reasoning without focus on hidden or latent processes.
 17. Studies data poisoning attacks or defenses in machine learning systems.
    - Relevant: papers on poisoning training data to manipulate model behavior, detecting poisoned data, or defending against data poisoning attacks.
    - Not relevant: papers about general adversarial examples at inference time without training data manipulation.
 18. Advances in data-centric AI approaches that focus on improving data quality, curation, or data-driven methods for ML.
    - Relevant: papers on data quality assessment, data selection, data augmentation strategies, curriculum learning based on data properties, or systematic approaches to dataset creation and curation.
    - Not relevant: papers that simply use standard datasets without focusing on data-centric methodology.
 19. Studies security of LLMs and methods to protect language models from attacks or exploitation.
    - Relevant: papers on prompt injection defenses, jailbreak prevention, securing LLM deployments, protecting against extraction attacks, or hardening LLM systems against adversarial users.
    - Not relevant: papers about using LLMs for security applications, or general robustness without security focus.

 In suggesting papers to your friend, remember that he enjoys papers on statistical machine learning, and generative modeling in natural language processing.
 Your friend also likes learning about surprising empirical results in language models, as well as clever statistical tricks.
 He does not want to read papers that are about primarily applications of methods to specific domains.
